## I / 一
===Original===

Miriam paused with her heavy bag dragging at her arm. It was a disaster. But it was the last of Mornington Road. To explain about it would be to bring Mornington Road here.
“It doesn’t matter now,” said Mrs. Bailey as she dropped her bag and fumbled for her purse.
“Oh, I’d better settle it at once or I shall forget about it. I’m so glad the things have come so soon.”
When Mrs. Bailey had taken the half-crown they stood smiling at each other. Mrs. Bailey looked exactly as she had done the first time. It was exactly the same; there was no disappointment. The light coming through the glass above the front door made her look more shabby and worn. Her hair was more metallic. But it was the same girlish figure and the same smile triumphing over the badly fitting teeth. Miriam felt like an inmate returning after an absence. The smeariness of the marble-topped hall table did not offend her. She held herself in. It was better to begin as she meant to go on. Behind Mrs. Bailey the staircase was beckoning. There was something waiting upstairs that would be gone if she stayed talking to Mrs. Bailey.
Assuring Mrs. Bailey that she remembered the way to the room she started at last on the journey up the many flights of stairs. The feeling of confidence that had come the first time she mounted them with Mrs. Bailey returned now. She could not remember noticing anything then but a large brown dinginess, one rich warm even tone everywhere in the house; a sharp contrast to the cold harshly lit little bedroom in Mornington Road. The day was cold. But this house did not seem cold and when she rounded the first flight and Mrs. Bailey was out of sight the welcome of the place fell upon her. She knew it well, better than any place she had known in all her wanderings﻿—the faded umbers and browns of the stair carpet, the gloomy heights of wall, a patternless sheen where the staircase lights fell upon it and in the shadowed parts a blurred scrolling pattern in dull madder on a brown background; the dark landings with lofty ceilings and high dark polished doors surmounted by classical reliefs in grimed plaster, the high staircase windows screened by long smoke grimed lace curtains. On the top landing the ceiling came down nearly level with the tops of the doors. The light from above made the little grained doors stare brightly.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆停住脚，沉重的提包坠着她的胳膊。那是一场灾难。可那是莫宁顿路（Mornington Road）的最后一笔。要把它解释清楚，就等于把莫宁顿路搬到这儿来。
“这会儿不急着给。”贝利太太说。米丽亚姆放下提包，摸寻钱袋。
“哦，还是马上结清的好，省得忘了。东西这么快就送到，我真高兴。”
贝利太太收下那半克朗，两人站着相视而笑。贝利太太的模样同头一回分毫不差。一切都原样原样；没有叫人失望之处。前门上方玻璃透进的光，让她显得更寒素、更憔悴了些，头发更见金属般的光泽。可还是那少女似的身段，还是那个笑起来赛过那口不齐牙齿的笑容。米丽亚姆觉得自己像个外出过的房客又回来了。大厅里大理石桌面那些油腻的污痕并不惹她厌。她按住自己。开头怎样，往后就怎样，这样最好。贝利太太身后，楼梯在向她招手。楼上有什么在等着，若在这里同贝利太太多说下去，它就会溜走。
向贝利太太保证自己记得去房间的路之后，她终于踏上那层层楼梯的旅程。头一回随贝利太太登上这些楼梯时生出的那份笃定，此刻又回来了。她记得当时什么也没留意，只觉出一片浓重的褐色昏暗，一种丰厚温暖的均匀色调布满全宅；同莫宁顿路那间冷冷亮着的斗室是何等悬殊的对照。天是冷的。可这宅子仿佛不冷，待她转过第一段楼梯、贝利太太不见了踪影，这地方的欢迎便整个落到她身上。她熟悉它，比她漂泊以来认识的任何地方都熟——楼梯毯褪色的暗褐与棕，高得幽暗的墙面，楼梯灯照到处是一片无花纹的光泽，背光处则在棕底上显出暗茜红的模糊卷草纹；幽暗的楼梯平台，高高的天花板，高而黑亮的门，门楣上覆着积垢的古典浮雕，高高的楼梯窗垂着烟熏火燎的长蕾丝帘。顶层平台上，天花板低得几乎与门顶齐平。上头照下来的光，把几扇小小的木纹门照得亮晃晃的。

===Original===

Patches of fresh brown and buff shone here and there in the threadbare linoleum. The cracks of the flooring were filled with dust and dust lay along the rim of the skirting. Two large tin trunks standing one upon the other almost barred the passageway. It was like a landing in a small suburban lodging-house, a small silent afternoon brightness, shut in and smelling of dust. Silence flooded up from the lower darkness. The hall where she had stood with Mrs. Bailey was far away below and below that were basements deep in the earth. The outside of the house with its first-floor balcony, the broad shallow flight of steps leading to the dark green front door, the little steep flight running sharply down into the railed area seemed as far away as yesterday.
The little landing was a bright plateau, under the skylight, shut off by its brightness from the rest of the house, the rooms leading from it would be bright and flat and noisy with light compared with the rest of the house. From above came the tap-tap of a door swinging gently in a breeze and behind the sound was a soft faint continuous murmur. She ran up the short twisting flight of bare stairs into a blaze of light. Would her room be a bright suburban bedroom? Had it been a dull day when she first called? The skylight was blue and gold with light, its cracks threads of bright gold. Three little glaring yellow grained doors opened on to the small strip of uncovered dusty flooring; to the left the little box-loft, to the right the empty garret behind her own and in front of her her own door ajar; tapping in the breeze. The little brass knob rattled loosely in her hand and the hinge ran up the scale to a high squeak as she pushed open the door and down again as it closed behind her neatly with a light wooden sound. The room was half dark shadow and half brilliant light.
---
She closed the door and stood just inside it looking at the room. It was smaller than her memory of it. When she had stood in the middle of the floor with Mrs. Bailey she had looked at nothing but Mrs. Bailey, waiting for the moment to ask about the rent. Coming upstairs she had felt the room was hers and barely glanced at it when Mrs. Bailey opened the door. From the moment of waiting on the stone steps outside the front door everything had opened to the movement of her impulse.

===Chinese===

磨秃的油地毡上，这里那里闪着新补的棕色和牛皮色的块。地板缝里积着灰尘，护墙板沿口也躺着一线灰。两只大洋铁箱上下叠着，几乎堵死了过道。这像郊区小客栈的一个楼梯平台，一小片寂静的午后亮光，封闭着，带着尘土的气味。寂静从底下的黑暗里涌上来。她同贝利太太站过的门厅远在下面，再往下是深深陷进地下的地下室。这宅子的外观——二楼的小阳台，通向墨绿色前门的宽而浅的一段台阶，陡陡地扎进围着栏杆的地下采光井的那几级——都远得像昨天的事了。
小小的平台是一方明亮的髙地，在天窗底下，被它的亮同宅子的其余部分隔开；从这儿通向的几个房间，比起宅子其余的地方，会是明亮的、平展的、被光吵嚷着的。上头传来一扇门在微风里轻轻摆动的嗒、嗒声，那声响背后是一阵绵软低微的持续的絮语。她跑上那段短短的、拐弯的裸楼梯，一头扎进一片光焰。她的房间会是一间明亮的郊区卧室吗？她头一回来时，莫非是个阴天？天窗蓝着、金着，满是光，裂缝成了一道道金线。三扇小小的、亮得刺眼的黄木纹门开向一小条没有铺地毯的积灰地板；左边是小搁楼，右边是她自己那间背后的空阁楼，正前方，她自己的门虚掩着，在风里嗒嗒地响。小铜旋钮在她手里松松地哗啦；她推开门，门轴升上一个高高的吱呀，又随着门在她身后轻轻带拢、发出一声轻巧的木头声响而降下去。房间一半是浓重的影子，一半是炽亮的光。
---
她关上门，站在门内端详这间屋子。它比记忆里的小。当刦她同贝利太太站在地板中央时，她眼里只有贝利太太，只等着开口问房租的时机。上楼时她已觉得这房间是她的了，贝利太太开门时她几乎没看它一眼。从前门外的石阶上等候的那一刻起，一切都朝着她心念的动向敞开了。

===Original===

She was surprised now at her familiarity with the detail of the room﻿ ﻿… that idea of visiting places in dreams. It was something more than that﻿ ﻿… all the real part of your life has a real dream in it; some of the real dream part of you coming true. You know in advance when you are really following your life. These things are familiar because reality is here. Coming events cast *light*. It is like dropping everything and walking backwards to something you know is there. However far you go out you come back.﻿ ﻿… I am back now where I was before I began trying to do things like other people. I left home to get here. None of those things can touch me here. They are mine.﻿ ﻿…
… The room asserted its chilliness. But the dark yellow graining of the wallpaper was warm. It shone warmly in the stream of light pouring through the barred lattice window. In the further part of the room darkened by the steep slope of the roof it gleamed like stained wood. The window space was a little square wooden room, the long low double lattice breaking the roof, the ceiling and walls warmly reflecting its oblong of bright light. Close against the window was a firm little deal table covered with a thin brightly coloured printed cotton tablecloth. When Miriam drew her eyes from its confusion of rich fresh tones the bedroom seemed very dark. The bed drawn in under the slope showed an expanse of greyish white counterpane, the carpet was colourless in the gloom. She opened the door. Silence came in from the landing. The blue and gold had gone from the skylight. Its sharp grey light shone in over the dim colours of the threadbare carpet and on to the black bars of the little grate and the little strip of tarnished yellow grained mantelpiece, running along to the bedhead where a small globeless gas bracket stuck out at an angle over the head of the bed. The sight of her luggage piled up on the other side of the fireplace drew her forward into the dimness. There was a small chest of drawers battered and almost paintless but with two long drawers and two small ones and a white cover on which stood a little looking glass framed in polished pine﻿ ﻿… and a small yellow wardrobe with a deep drawer under the hanging part and a little drawer in the rickety little washstand and another above the dusty cupboard of the little mahogany sideboard.

===Chinese===

此刻她惊异于自己对这房间细节的熟稔……像梦里重游旧地的那种情形。可比那还要多些……你生活里真实的那部分，里面有一个真实的梦；你那真实梦境的一部分正在成真。当你真正循着自己的生命走时，你事先就知道。这些东西熟悉，因为现实就在这里。将临之事投下的是*光*。就像放下一切，倒退着走向一件你明知就在那里的东西。无论走出去多远，你总会回来。……如今我回来了，回到我还没开始学别人那样做事之前所在的地方。我离家，为的就是到这儿来。那些东西，在这里碰不到我。它们是我的。……
……房间申明它的寒意。可墙纸那暗黄的木纹是暖的，在被格栅窗透进来的光流里暖暖地发亮。在房间深处、被屋顶的陡坡罩暗的那一半，它像着色的木头般幽幽放光。窗子那块地方是一间小小的方形木屋，长长的矮矮的双扇格栅窗截开屋顶，天花板和墙壁暖暖地映着它那一方亮光。紧挨着窗是一张结实的小松木桌，铺着一块印花的薄棉桌布，颜色鲜亮。米丽亚姆把眼睛从那一桌子浓艳新鲜的颜色里拔出来，卧室便显得很暗。塞在屋坡底下的床露出一大片灰白的床罩，地毯在昏暗里没有颜色。她打开门。楼梯平台的寂静涌进来。天窗上的蓝与金没有了。它那清冷的灰光照进来，掠过磨秃的地毯的暗色，落在小壁炉的黑铁条上，落在那条失去光泽的黄木纹壁炉板上，一路沿到床头；一只没有灯罩的小煤气壁架，在床头斜斜伸出。看见自己的行李堆在壁炉另一边，她不由朝昏暗里走去。有一只小五斗柜，磕碰得几乎没了漆，却还有两个长抽屉、两个小抽屉，柜上蒙着白布，摆着一面亮松木框的小镜子……还有一只黄色的小衣柜，挂衣部分底下带一个深抽屉；摇摇晃晃的小脸盆架里有一个小抽屉，桃花心木小柜那积灰的柜橱上又有一个。

===Original===

I’ll paint the bright part of the ceiling; scrolls of leaves.﻿ ﻿… Shutting the quiet door she went into the brilliance of the window space. The outside world appeared; a long row of dormer windows and the square tops of the larger windows below them, the windows black or sheeny grey in the light, cut out against the dinginess of smoke grimed walls. The long strip of roof sloping back from the dormers was a pure even dark grey. She bent to see the sky, clear soft heavy grey, striped by the bars of her window. Behind the top rim of the iron framework of the bars was a discoloured roll of window blind. Then the bars must move.﻿ ﻿… Shifting the table she pressed close to the barred window. It smelt strongly of rust and dust. Outside she saw grey tiles sloping steeply from the window to a cemented gutter beyond which was a little stone parapet about two feet high. A soft wash of madder lay along the grey tiles. There must be an afterglow somewhere, just out of sight. Her hands went through the bars and lifted the little rod which held the lattice half open. The little square four paned frame swung free and flattened itself back against the fixed panes, out of reach, its bar sticking out over the leads. Drawing back grimed fingers and wrists striped with grime she grasped the iron bars and pulled. The heavy framework left the window frame with a rusty creak and the sound of paint peeling and cracking. It was very heavy but it came up and up until her arms were straight above her head and looking up she saw a stout iron ring in a little trap door in the wooden ceiling and a hook in the centre of the endmost bar in the iron framework.
Kneeling on the table to raise the frame once more and fix it to the ceiling she saw the whole length of the top row of windows across the way and wide strips of grimy stucco placed across the house fronts between the windows.
The framework of the freed window was cracked and blistered but the little square panes were clean. There were four little windows in the row, each with four square panes. The outmost windows were immovable. The one next to the open one had lost its bar, but a push set it free and it swung wide. She leaned out holding back from the dusty sill and met a soft fresh breeze streaming straight in from the west. The distant murmur of traffic changed into the clear plonk plonk and rumble of swift vehicles.

===Chinese===

我要把天花板亮的那一半画上画；画上叶子的卷须。……她轻轻带上门，走进窗前那片明晃晃的光里。外面的世界出现了；长长一排老虎窗，和它们底下那些大窗的方顶，窗子在光里或黑或泛着灰光，衬在烟气熏黄的墙的昏暗前。从老虎窗向后斜下去的那长条屋顶是一种纯净均匀的深灰。她俯身去看天，清朗、柔软、沉甸甸的灰，被窗栅分成一条条。窗栅铁框上沿后面，搁着一卷变了色的窗帘。这么说窗栅是可以动的。……她挪开桌子，紧贴到栅栏窗前。窗强烈地散发着铁锈和灰尘的气味。她看见外面灰色的瓦从窗口陡陡地斜向一条水泥檐沟，沟外是一道两尺来高的矮石沿。一层茜红的柔光洗在灰瓦上。别处什么地方准有一抹晚照，恰恰看不见。她的手穿过栅栏，抬起那根支着半开格栅窗的小杆。那四格的小方框脱开摆动，向后平贴到固定的窗扇上，够不着了，小杆在铅条上方探出去。她抽回沾了条纹污渍的手指和手腕，攊住铁栅用力拉。沉重的铁框带着锈响、带着漆皮剥落开裂的声音离开了窗框。它很沉，可它一点点上来了，直上到她双臂在头顶伸直；仰头看去，木天花板的一扇小天窗里有一个粗铁环，铁框最末一根横杆的中央有一个钩子。
跪在桌上再把窗框抬起、挂到天花板上时，她望见了街对面那排窗顶上一整列的窗，和横钉在临街墙面窗户之间的一条条积垢的拉毛水泥。
自由了的这扇窗，铁框开裂起泡，小小的方玻璃却干净。这一排共有四扇小窗，每扇四格玻璃。最外两扇推不动。开着那扇旁边的一扇丢了窗杆，可一推便开了，敞敞地荡出去。她避开积灰的窗台探身出去，迎面一道新鲜柔和的微风，从正西方直直流进来。远处交通的低语变成清脆的噗通噗通和疾驰车辆的辘辘。

===Original===

Right and left at the far end of the vista were glimpses of bare trees. The cheeping of birds came faintly from the distant squares and clear and sharp from neighbouring roofs. To the left the trees were black against pure grey, to the right they stood spread and bunched in front of the distant buildings blocking the vista. Running across the rose-washed façade of the central mass she could just make out “Edwards’s Family Hotel” in large black letters. That was the distant view of the courtyard of Euston Station.﻿ ﻿… In between that and the square of trees ran the Euston Road, by day and by night, her unsleeping guardian, the rim of the world beyond which lay the northern suburbs, banished.
From a window somewhere down the street out of sight came the sound of an unaccompanied violin, clearly attacking and dropping and attacking a passage of half a dozen bars. The music stood serene and undisturbed in the air of the quiet street. The man was following the phrase, listening; strengthening and clearing it, completely undisturbed and unconscious of his surroundings. “Good heavens,” she breathed quietly, feeling the extremity of relief, passing some boundary, emerging strong and equipped in a clear medium.﻿ ﻿… She turned back into the twilight of the room. Twenty-one and only one room to hold the richly renewed consciousness, and a living to earn, but the self that was with her in the room was the untouched tireless self of her seventeenth year and all the earlier time. The familiar light moved within the twilight, the old light.﻿ ﻿… She might as well wash the grime from her wrists and hands. There was a scrap of soap in the soap dish, dry and cracked and seamed with dirt. The washstand rocked as she washed her hands; the toilet things did not match, the towel-horse held one small thin face towel and fell sideways against the wardrobe as she drew off the towel. When the gas was on she would be visible from the opposite dormer window. Short skimpy faded Madras muslin curtains screened a few inches of the endmost windows and were caught back and tied up with tape. She untied the tape and disengaged with the curtains a strong smell of dust. The curtains would cut off some of the light. She tied them firmly back and pulled at the edge of the rolled up blind. The blind streaked and mottled with ironmould came down in a stifling cloud of dust.

===Chinese===

长街尽头的左右两侧，隐约露出光秃的树。鸟的啁啾从远处的广场微弱地传来，又从邻近的屋顶清亮而尖锐地传来。左边，树衬在纯粹的灰上成了黑的；右边，它们张着、簇着，立在堵住视野的远处楼宇前。横过中央那片被玫瑰色洗过的立面，她勉强辨认出几个黑色大字：爱德华家庭旅馆。那边就是尤斯顿车站院子的远景。……在它和那方块树丛之间，流着尤斯顿路，日里夜里流着，她那不眠的守卫者，世界的边缘，边缘之外是放逐了的北部城郊。
从街上望不见的哪个窗口，传来一把无伴奏小提琴的声音，清清楚楚地攻下又落下、再攻下一段五六小节的乐句。那乐音立在安静街道的空气里，安详而不受扰动。那人正跟着乐句走，倾听着；把它加强、理清，浑然不觉四周的一切。“天哪，”她静静地呼出，感到一种极度的松快，跨过了某道界线，强壮而齐备地现身在一片清朗的介质里。……她转回房间的暮色中。二十一岁，只有一间屋来盛这丰富焕然的意识，还有生计要挣；可同她一起在这间屋里的，是她十七岁以及更早所有时光里那个未曾磨损的、不知疲倦的自己。熟悉的光在暮色里动着，旧时的光。……她不妨把手腕和手上的污迹洗掉。肥皂碟里有一小块肥皂，干裂的，缝里嵌着脏。她洗手时脸盆架直摇晃；盥洗用品没有一件配套，毛巾架上挂着一条薄薄的小面巾，她一抽毛巾，架子便朝衣柜那边歪倒。点起煤气灯，对面老虎窗里就看得见她了。几幅短短窄窄褪了色的马德拉斯平纹细布帘，遮着最边上几寸窗，用带子拢住系起。她解开带子，一股浓重的灰尘味随帘子散出来。帘子会遮掉一些光。她又把帘子牢牢系回去，拉了拉卷起的窗帘的下沿。那条浸着铁锈渍、斑斑驳驳的窗帘，裹着一团呛人的灰尘落下来。

===Original===

She rolled it up again and washed once more. She must ask for a bath towel and do something about the blind, sponge it or something; that was all.
---
A light had come in the dormer on the other side of the street. It remained unscreened. Watching carefully she could see only a dim figure moving amongst motionless shapes. No need to trouble about the blind. London could come freely in day and night through the unscreened happy little panes; light and darkness and darkness and light.
---
London, just outside all the time, coming in with the light, coming in with the darkness, always present in the depths of the air in the room.
---
The gas flared out into a wide bright flame. The dingy ceiling and counterpane turned white. The room was a square of bright light and had a rich brown glow, shut brightly in by the straight square of level white ceiling and thrown up by the oblong that sloped down, white, at the side of the big bed almost to the floor. She left her things half unpacked about the floor and settled herself on the bed under the gas jet with the Voyage of the Beagle. Unpacking had been a distraction from the glory, very nice, getting things straight. But there was no *need* to do anything or think about anything﻿ ﻿… ever, here. No interruption, no one watching or speculating or treating one in some particular way that had to be met. Mrs. Bailey did not speculate. She knew, everything. Every evening here would have a glory, but not the same kind of glory. Reading would be more of a distraction than unpacking. She read a few lines. They had a fresh attractive *meaning*. Reading would be real. The dull adventures of the Beagle looked real, coming along through reality. She put the book on her knee and once more met the clear brown shock of her room.
---
The carpet is awful, faded and worn almost to bits. But it is right, in this room.﻿ ﻿… This is the furnished room; one room. I have come to it. “You could get a furnished room at about seven shillings rental.” The awful feeling, no tennis, no dancing, no house to move in, no society. The relief at first when Bennett found those people﻿ ﻿… maddening endless roads of little houses in the east wind﻿ ﻿… their kind way of giving more than they had undertaken, and smiling and waiting for smiles and dying all the time in some dark way without knowing it.

她把它重新卷上去，又洗了一遍。得跟人家要一条浴巾，还有这窗帘得想个办法，擦一擦还是怎么的；就这样了。
---
街对面的一扇老虎窗里亮起了灯。灯后没有帘。留神看去，只能看见一个模糊的身影在一堆不动的形状间移动。窗帘不用费心了。伦敦（London）可以昼夜自由地从这些无遮无拦、欢欢喜喜的小窗格里进来；光和暗，暗和光。
---
伦敦，一直就在外面，随光进来，随暗进来，时时都现在房间里空气的深处。
---
煤气灯噴地绽出一蓬宽宽的亮焰。发暗的天花板和床罩变白了。房间成了一方亮光，泛着浓艳的棕光，被平展白色天花板那笔直方正的一圈亮亮地围住，又被那张大床侧边白白斜下来、几乎直抵地板的斜方一衬，托得更高。她把东西解开一半摊在地板上，在煤气灯下、在床上安顿下来，就着《“贝格尔”号航行记》。解行李原是那荣光里的一桩分心，很惬意，把东西摆顺。可在这里，做什么、想什么都没有*必要*……永远。没有打扰，没有人盯着、揣度着、或以某种你不得不应付的特别方式待你。贝利太太不揣度人。她什么都知道。在这里的每个夜晚都会有它的荣光，只是荣光各不相同。读书会比解行李更分心。她读了几行。它们有一种新鲜动人的*意味*。读书会是真事。“贝格尔”号那乏味的历险看着是真事，一路从真实里穿过来。她把书搁在膝上，又一次迎上她房间那清澈的棕色的一击。
---
地毯糟透了，褪得磨得几乎成了碎片。可在这间屋里，它是*对的*。……这就是那间带家具的房间；一间屋。我来到它这里了。“七先令上下可以租到一间带家具的房间。”那可怕的感觉，没有网球，没有舞会，没有可以搬进去的宅子，没有社交。贝内特（Bennett）找到那户人家时，起初的松快……东风里那些气人的、没完没了的小房子排成的长街……他们那副好心肠，给的比应承的还多，微笑着，等着别人的微笑，同时不知怎么正暗暗地死去而自己不知道。

Filling the rooms and the piano and the fern on the serge table cloth and the broken soap dish in the bath room until it was impossible to read or think or play because of them, the feeling of them stronger and stronger till there was nothing but crying over the trays of meals and wanting to scream. The thought of the five turnings to the station, all into long little roads looking alike and making you forget which was which and lose your way, was still full of pain﻿ ﻿… the *relief* of moving to Granville Place still a relief, though it felt a mistake from the first. Mrs. Corrie’s old teacher liking only certain sorts of people knew it was a mistake, with her peevish silky old face and her antique brooch. But it had been the beginning of London.﻿ ﻿… Bond Street that Sunday morning in the thick fog; these sudden pictures gleaming in a window, filmy﻿ ﻿… von Hier. Adelina Compayne, hanging out silk stockings on the top balustrade. “I *love* cawfy”﻿ ﻿… that was the only real thing that had been said downstairs. There was no need to have been frightened of these two women in black silk evening dresses. None of these clever things were real. They said young Asquith is a really able man to hide their thoughts. The American Academy pupils talked together to keep everybody off, except when they made their clever jokes﻿ ﻿… “if anyone takes that top bit there’ll be murder Miss Spink.” When they went out of the room they looked silly. The young man was real somewhere else.
The little man talking about the wonders of the linotype in the smoking room.﻿ ﻿… How did I get into the smoking room? Someone probably told Miss Spink I talked to him in the smoking room and smoked a cigarette. Perhaps his wife. If they could have seen. It was so surprising to hear anybody suddenly talking. Perhaps he began in the hall and ushered me into the smoking room. There was no one there and I can’t remember anything about the linotype, only the quiet and the talking face and suddenly feeling in the heart of London. But it was soon after that they all began being standoffish; before Mr. Chamberlayne came; before Adela began playing Esther Summerson at the Kennington. They approved of my going down to fetch her until he began coming too. The shock of seeing her clumsy heavy movements on the stage and her face looking as though it were covered with starch.﻿ ﻿…

把那些房间、那钢琴、堅呢桌布上的蕨草、浴室里破裂的肥皂碟填得满满的，直到因为他们在场便无法读书、无法思想、无法弹琴，他们在场的感觉越来越强，直到只剩下对着一盘盘饭菜哭、只想尖叫。去车站要拐五个弯，全是长长的小巷，条条相像，叫你记不清哪条是哪条、迷了路——这念头至今还满是痛……搬去格拉纳维尔坊的*松快*至今仍是松快，尽管打一开始就觉得是个错。科里太太的那位老教师，只喜欢某几类人，也知道这是个错，带着她那张愠怒的、丝绸似的老脸和她那枚古旧的胸针。可那是伦敦的开端。……那个大雾的星期天早晨的邦德街；橱窗里突然闪亮的这些画，薄薄的……von Hier。阿德莉娜·康佩恩，在最顶层的栏杆上晾丝袜。“我*爱*舒服（cawfy）”……那是楼下跌进过的唯一一句真话。本不必怕那两个穿黑绸晚礼服的女人。这些聪明话没有一句是真的。他们说小阿斯奎斯是个真正能干的人，好遮住自己的心思。美国学院的那些学生彼此交谈，好把所有人挡开，只在做聪明的俏皮话时例外……“谁要拿最上头那一块，就出人命啦，斯平克小姐（Miss Spink）。”她们走出房间时样子显得蠢。那年轻人在别的什么地方是真的。
那个在小会议室里谈论莱诺铸排机之奇的小个子男人。……我是怎么进的小会议室？多半有人告诉了斯平克小姐，说我在会议室里同他说了话、抽了烟。兴许是他太太。要是她们看见了该多好。听见谁忽然开口说话，竟那么叫人吃惊。兴许他是在门厅里起的头，把我让进会议室。那里一个人也没有，莱诺机的事我一句也不记得，只记得那份安静、那张说话的脸，和忽然觉得置身于伦敦的心脏。可就是那以后不久，她们全开始冷淡起来；在钱伯莱恩先生来之前；在阿德拉开始在肯宁顿演埃丝特·萨默森之前。她们原本赞成我下去接她，直到他也开始同来。看见她在台上那笨重迟拙的动作、看见她那张仿佛上了一层浆的脸时的震惊。……

I can *think* about it all, here, and not mind.
---
She *was* beautiful. It was happiness to sit and watch her smoking so badly, in bed, in the strip of room, her cloud of hair against the wall in the candlelight, two o’clock﻿ ﻿… the Jesuit who had taught her chess﻿ ﻿… and Michael Somebody, the little book *The Purple Pillar*. He was an *author* and he wanted to marry her and take her back to Ireland. Perhaps by now she was back from America and had gone, just out of kindness. She was strong and beautiful and good, sitting up in her chemise, smoking.﻿ ﻿… I’ve got that photograph of her as Marcia somewhere. I must put it up. Miss Spink was surprised that last week, the students getting me into their room﻿ ﻿… the dark clean shining piano, the azaleas and the muslin shaded lamp, the way they all sat in their evening dresses, lounging and stiff with stiff clean polished hair.﻿ ﻿… “Miss Dust here’s going to be the highest soprano in the States.”﻿ ﻿… “None of that Miss Thicker.”﻿ ﻿… “When she caught that top note and the gold medal she went right up top, to stay there, that minute.”
She was surprised when Mrs. Potter took me to hear Melba. I heard Melba. I don’t remember hearing her. English opera houses are small; there are fine things all over the world. If you see them all you can compare one with the other; but then you don’t see or hear anything at all. It seems strange to be American and at the same time stout and middle-aged. It would have got more and more difficult with all those people. The dreadful way the Americans got intimate and then talked or hinted openly everywhere about intimate things. No one knew how intimate Miss O’Veagh was. I shall remember. There is something about being Irish Roman Catholic that makes *happiness*. She did not seem to think the George Street room awful. She was *surprised* when I talked about the hole in the wall and the cold and the imbecile servant and the smell of ether. “We are brought up from the first to understand that we must never believe anything a man says.” She *came* and sat and talked and wrote after she had gone﻿ ﻿… “goodbye﻿—sweet blessed little rose of Mary”﻿ ﻿… she tried to make me think I was young and pretty. She was sorry for me without saying so.
I should never have gone to Mornington Road unless I had been nearly mad with sorrow﻿ ﻿…

在这里，这一切我都*能想*，不在乎。
---
她*是*美的。坐在那里看她抽那拙劣的烟是一种幸福，在床上，在那一窄条房间里，她那一团云发衬着烛光里的墙，凌晨两点……教她下棋的那个耶稣会士……还有迈克尔什么什么，那本小书《紫柱》。他是个*作家*，他想娶她，带她回爱尔兰。兴许如今她已经从美国回来，又走了，纯粹出于好心。她是强壮的、美的、好的，支着身子坐在衬裙里抽烟。……我有她扮玛西娅（Marcia）的那张照片，不知收在哪儿了。我一定得把它挂起来。斯平克小姐上礼拜吃惊不小，那些学生把我拉进她们屋里……那架深色干净发亮的钢琴，杜鹃花和薄纱罩的灯，她们一个个穿晚礼服坐着的样子，慵懒又僵硬，头发僵挺光洁。……“这儿的达斯特小姐要成为全美最高的女高音。”……“少来这套，蒂克小姐。”……“她一唱上那个最高音、拿了那块金牌，就直上顶尖，就在那一刻，留在那儿了。”
我居然在波特太太带我去听梅尔巴的时候吃了一惊。我听过梅尔巴。我不记得听过她。英国的歌剧院都小；世上处处有好东西。若是什么都见识了，便可以彼此比较；可那样一来你就什么也看不见、听不见了。既是美国人、又胖又上了年纪，想想也是怪事。同那一群人在一起，会越来越难。美国人那套可怕的先亲热、随后到处公开谈论或暗示私事的做派。没人知道奥维小姐亲热到了什么地步。我会记得的。爱尔兰罗马天主教里有一种东西，能造出*幸福*。她似乎并不觉得乔治街那间屋子可怕。我说起墙上的洞、寒冷、白痴的女仆和乙醚的气味，她竟*吃惊*。“我们从一开始受的教养就是：男人说的话，永远一句不要信。”她*来*了，坐着，谈着，走后又来信……“再见——玛丽的小小的、蒙福的玫瑰”……她想让我相信我又年轻又漂亮。她可怜我，只是不说出口。
要不是伤心得几乎疯了，我本来绝不会去莫宁顿路……

if Miss Thomas disapproved of germs and persons who let apartments why did she come and take a room at George Street? She must have seen she drove me nearly mad with sorrow. The thought of Wales full of Welsh people like her, makes one mad with sorrow.﻿ ﻿… Did she think I could get to know her by hearing all her complaints? She’s somewhere now, sending someone mad.
I was mad already when I went to Mornington Road.
“You’ll be all right with Mrs. Swanson”﻿ ﻿… the awful fringes, the *horror* of the ugly clean little room, the horror of Mrs. Swanson’s heavy old body moving slowly about the house, a heavy dark mountain, fringes, bulges, slow dead eyes, slow dead voice, slow grimacing evil smile﻿ ﻿… housekeeper to the Duke of Something and now moving slowly about heavy with disapproval. She thought of me as a business young lady.
---
Following advice is certain to be wrong. When you don’t follow advice there may be awful things. But they are not arranged beforehand. And when they come you do not know that they are awful until you have half got hold of something else. Then they change into something that has not been awful. Things that remain awful are in some way not finished.﻿ ﻿… Those women are awful. They will get more and more awful, still disliking and disapproving till they die. I shall not see them again.﻿ ﻿… I will never again be at the mercy of such women or at all in the places where they are. That means keeping free of all groups. In groups sooner or later one of them appears, dead and sightless and bringing blindness and death﻿ ﻿… although they seem to like brightness and children and the young people they approve of. I run away from them because I must. They kill me. The thought of their death is awful. Even in heaven no one could explain anything to them if they remain as they are. Wherever people advise you to go there is in the end one of those women.﻿ ﻿…
---
When she turned out the gas the window spaces remained faintly alight with a soft light like moonlight. At the window she found a soft bluish radiance cast up from below upon the opposite walls and windows. It went up into the clear blue darkness of the sky.
When she lay down the bed smelt faintly of dust. The air about her head under the sharply sloping ceiling was still a little warm with the gas. It was full of her untrammelled thoughts. Her luggage was lying about, quite near.

托马斯小姐既然看不惯细菌和出租房间的人，为什么又上乔治街来租房？她一定看得出她把我逼得伤心欲狂。一想到威尔士满是她那样的威尔士人，就让人伤心欲狂。……她莫非以为，听完了她那一肚子怨言，我就能认识她了？她此刻正在什么地方，把什么人逼疯呢。
我去莫宁顿路的时候，已经是疯了。
“跟着斯旺森太太你会好好的”……那些可怕的流苏，那间干净丑陋的小屋的*恐怖*，斯旺森太太那沉重的老身子在宅子里慢慢挪动的恐怖，一座沉重的黑山，流苏，臃肿，迟钝的死眼睛，迟钝的死嗓子，迟钝地做怪的邪恶的笑……给什么公爵当过管家，如今怀着满腔的不以为然慢慢地挪来挪去。她把我看作一位做事务的年轻淑女。
---
听人指点行事，注定是错的。不听指点，也许有可怕的事。可那些事不是预先安排好的。而且事到临头，你并不知道它们可怕，直到你已抓到别的什么的一半。随后它们就变成并不曾可怕的东西。始终可怕的事，总有什么地方没个完结。……那些女人是可怕的。她们会越来越可怕，一路嫌弃着、不以为然着，直到死去。我不会再见她们了。……我永远不再任凭这样的女人摆布，永远不再待在她们在的地方。那就要避开一切团体。在团体里，迟早她们中的一个会出现，死的、瞎的，带来瞎与死……虽然她们似乎喜欢亮光、喜欢孩子和她们看得上的年轻人。我从她们身边逃开，因为我必须。她们杀我。想到她们的死，也是可怕的。即便在天堂，若她们还是这样，也没有谁能给她们讲明白任何事。凡有人劝你去的地方，到头来总有这样一个女人。……

她关掉煤气，窗那块地方还微微亮着，一种月亮似的柔光。在窗前，她发现一道柔和的淡蓝的辉光，从底下照上对面的墙和窗。它升上去，融进天空清澈的蓝色的夜里。
躺下时，床单淡淡地有灰尘的气味。陡斜的天花板下、她头部四周的空气还带着煤气的一点余温。空气里满是她无拘无束的念头。她的行李摊在近旁。

She thought of washing in the morning in the bright light on the other side of the room﻿ ﻿… leaves crowding all round the lattice and here and there a pink rose﻿ ﻿… *several pink roses*﻿ ﻿… the lovely air chilling the water﻿ ﻿… the basin quite up against the lattice﻿ ﻿… dew splashing off the rose bushes in the little garden almost dark with trellises and trees, crowding with Harriett through the little damp stiff gate, the sudden lineny smell of Harriett’s pinafore and the thought of Harriett in it, feeling the same, sudden bright sunshine, two shouts, great cornfields going up and up with a little track between them﻿ ﻿… up over Blewburton﻿ ﻿… *Whittenham Clumps*. Before I saw Whittenham Clumps I had always known them. But we saw them before we knew they were called Whittenham Clumps. It was a surprise to know anybody who had seen them and that they had a name.
---
St. Pancras bells were clamouring in the room; rapid scales, beginning at the top, coming with a loud full thump on to the fourth note and finishing with a rush to the lowest which was hardly touched before the top note hung again in the air, sounding outdoors clean and clear while all the other notes still jangled together in her room. Nothing had changed. The night was like a moment added to the day; like years going backwards to the beginning; and in the brilliant sunshine the unchanging things began again, perfectly new. She leaped out of bed into the clamorous stillness and stood in the window rolling up the warm hair that felt like a shawl round her shoulders. A cup of tea and then the bus to Harriett’s. A bus somewhere just out there beyond the morning stillness of the street. What an *adventure* to go out and take a bus without having to face anybody. They were all out there, away somewhere, the very thought and sight of them, disapproving and deploring her surroundings. She listened. There they were. There were their very voices, coming plaintive and reproachful with a held-in indignation, intonations that she knew inside and out, coming on bells from somewhere beyond the squares﻿—another church. She withdrew the coloured cover and set her spirit lamp on the ink-stained table. Strong bright light was standing outside the window. The clamour of the bells had ceased. From far away down in the street a loud hoarse voice came thinly up. Referee﻿—Lloyd’s﻿—*Sunday Times*﻿—People﻿—*pypa*.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她想到清晨在房间另一头的亮光里洗漱……叶子挤挤挨挨围满格栅窗，这里那里一朵粉玫瑰……*好几朵粉玫瑰*……可爱的空气把水激得凉凉的……脸盆就抵着格栅窗……露水从小花园里几乎被藤架和树木遮暗的玫瑰丛上溅落，同哈丽特挤过那扇小小的湿而硬的园门，哈丽特围涎突然的那股麻布味，和想着哈丽特穿着它的心情，是一样的，忽然阳光大亮，两声欢呼，大片麦田夹着一条小径向上、再向上……翻过布鲁伯顿……*惠滕汉姆树丛岗*（Whittenham Clumps）。在我见到惠滕汉姆树丛岗之前，我一直就认得它们。只是见到时还不知道它们叫惠滕汉姆树丛岗。知道居然有人见过它们、知道它们有个名字，是一件叫人惊奇的事。
---
圣潘克拉斯的钟在房间里喧响；急速的音阶，从最高音起，沉沉满满地一声砸在第四音上，末了冲向最低音，最低音还没沾着，最高音又悬在空气里了；它在户外清清亮亮地响，别的音还都挤在她房间里当啷。什么都没有变。夜像添在白日上的一瞬；像岁月倒退回开头；在灿烂的阳光下，那些不变的事物重新开始，崭新如初。她跳下床，跳进那喧响的寂静里，站在窗前挽起温暖的头发，头发像一条披肩绕着她的肩。喝杯茶，然后坐车去哈丽特家。一辆车就在那儿，就在清晨安静的街道的那一头。不用面对任何人就能出门、就能上一辆车，这是一场怎样的*奇遇*啊。他们全都在外面，在别的什么地方，只要一想到他们、一看见他们，就是对她的环境的不以为然和痛心疾首。她侧耳听。他们就在那儿。他们那真真切切的嗓音来了，哀哀的、责备的、憋着义愤的调子，她里里外外熟透了的腔调，越过一个个广场从什么地方乘着钟声而来——又一座教堂。她掀开彩色的盖布，把酒精灯搁在墨渍斑斑的桌上。明晃晃的光立在窗外。钟的喧响停了。从老远的街上，一个粗哑的大嗓门细细地传上来。《裁判报》——劳埃德——《星期日泰晤士报》——《人民报》——帕帕儿。……

===Original===

A front door opened with a loud crackle of paint. The voice dropped to speaking tones that echoed clearly down the street and came up clear and soft and confidential. Referee? Lloyd’s? The door closed with a large firm wooden sound and the harsh voice went on down the street.
St. Pancras bells burst forth again. Faintly interwoven with their bright headlong scale were the clear sweet delicate contralto of the more distant bells playing very swiftly and reproachfully a five finger exercise in a minor key. That must be a very high-Anglican church; with light coming through painted windows on to carvings and decorations.
---
As she began on her solid slice of bread and butter St. Pancras bells stopped again. In the stillness she could hear the sound of her own munching. She stared at the surface of the table that held her plate and cup. It was like sitting up to the nursery table. “How frightfully happy I am,” she thought with bent head. Happiness streamed along her arms and from her head. St. Pancras bells began playing a hymn tune in single firm beats with intervals between that left each note standing for a moment gently in the air. The first two lines were playing carefully through to the distant accompaniment of the rapid weaving and interweaving in a regular unbroken pattern of the five soft low contralto bells. The third line of the hymn ran through Miriam’s head a ding-dong to and fro from tone to semitone. The bells played it out, without the semitone, with a perfect satisfying falsity. Miriam sat hunched against the table listening for the ascending stages of the last line. The bells climbed gently up, made a faint flat dab at the last top note, left it in the air askew above the decorous little tune and rushed away down their scale as if to cover the impropriety. They clamoured recklessly mingling with Miriam’s shout of joy as they banged against the wooden walls of the window space.

===Chinese===

一扇前门带着油漆的一串脆响打开了。那嗓音落成说话的声气，清清楚楚地沿街荡下去，又清又软、贴己似的传上来。《裁判报》？劳埃德？门带着一大声结实的木头声响关上，粗哑的嗓音沿街去了下去。
圣潘克拉斯的钟又轰然响起。隐约织进它们明亮奔放的音阶里的，是更远处几口钟清甜细致的女低音，飞快地、带着责备地弹着一支小调的五指练习。那一定是一座高教会派的教堂；光透过彩绘的窗，照在雕饰上。
---
她开始啃那厚厚一片黄油面包时，圣潘克拉斯的钟又停了。寂静里她听得见自己咀嚼的声音。她盯着桌上摆盘子和茶杯的那块桌面。像坐在育儿室的桌边。俯着头，她想：我可真是快活极了。快活顺着她的手臂流，从她的头顶流。圣潘克拉斯的钟用一声声沉稳的敲击奏起一首赞美诗，敲击之间留着间隙，让每个音都轻轻地在空气里独立片刻。头两行被仔细地奏完，远处伴奏着的是那五口柔婉低音的钟，飞快地穿梭交织、织成规律的图案。第三行在米丽亚姆脑子里来来回回，从全音到半音地叮当。钟把这一行奏完，没有那个半音，带着一种令人满足的完美的错谬。米丽亚姆弓着背坐在桌边，听那最后一行的层层上升。钟轻轻爬上去，在最高的那个音上轻轻按了一个发闷的扁音，让它歪歪地悬在那支端整的小曲上方，便急急冲下自己的音阶，仿佛要遮过这份失礼。它们无所顾忌地喧响，同米丽亚姆迸出的欢呼搅在一起，撞在窗间小木屋的板壁上。

## II / 二
===Original===

“Been to church?” said Gerald digging his shoulders into his chair.
“No. Have you?”
“We’ve not been for weeks.﻿ ﻿… Everybody thinks us awful heathens.”
“P’raps you are.”
“It’s Curls. She says she’s hanged if she’s going any more.”
“I can’t stand the vicar,” said Harriett. “He doesn’t believe a word he says.”
Fancy Harriett!﻿ ﻿…
“Besides, what’s the good?”
“Oh, there you are.”
“There’s nothing the matter with church once in a way to my way of thinking if it’s a decent high musical service.”
“Even Eve hardly ever goes now﻿—and nobody could possibly be more goody than she is.”
This was disquieting. It was one thing to be the agnostic of the family﻿—but Eve and Harriett. Miriam pondered resentfully while Gerald smoked and flicked his clothing and Harriett sat upright and pursed and untroubled in her great chair. She wondered whether she ought to say something about Unitarianism. But after all there might not be anything in it and they might not feel the relief of the way it cleared up the trouble about Christ. Besides there was no worry here in the room. A discussion would lead nowhere. They could all three look at each other if they wanted to and laugh everything off. In the middle of a sleepy Sunday afternoon with nothing to do sitting in three huge chairs and looking at each other they were all right. Harriett’s strength and scorn were directed against everything in the world but not against herself﻿ ﻿… never against herself. Harriett often thought her grumpy and ill-tempered, but she approved of her. She was approving now.
“After all Frills it’s good form to go,” Gerald said idly. “Go on. Smart people go to show their clothes.”
“Well, we’ve shown ours.”
Harriett flew out of her chair and daintily kicked him.
He grabbed and missed and sank back wailing, his face hidden in a cushion. Her dainty foot flew out once more and he smothered a shriek.
“Shut up,” said Harriett curling herself up in her chair.
Gerald wailed on.
“Do we smoke in here?” said Miriam, wanting the scene to drop or change while it was perfect. She would tell them now about her change of lodgings.
“Yes,” said Harriett absently with an eye on Gerald.
“I’ve changed my diggings,” began Miriam formally, fumbling for her packet of cigarettes. Harriett was hurling a cushion. Gerald crumpled into the depths of his chair and sobbed aloud, beating with his arms.

===Chinese===

“去做礼拜了吗？”杰拉尔德说，肩膀往椅子里蹭了蹭。
“没有。你们去了？”
“我们好几个星期没去了。……人人都当我们是吓死人的异教徒。”
“没准儿你们就是。”
“都怪卷毛儿（Curls）。她说她要再去才怪呢。”
“我受不了那个牧师，”哈丽特说，“他自己说的话一个字也不信。”
想不到是哈丽特！……
“再说，去了又有什么用？”
“哦，你倒说这话了。”
“依我看，隔三差五去一趟教堂没什么不好，只要是一场像样的、高高在上的、有音乐的礼拜。”
“连伊芙如今都难得去一回——再没有谁比她更贤惠的了。”
这可叫人心绪不宁。家里的不可知论者是她，这是一回事——可伊芙和哈丽特也这样。米丽亚姆忿忿地琢磨着，由杰拉尔德抽烟、掸着衣裳上的烟灰，哈丽特笔直地坐在她的大椅子里，抿着嘴，安安稳稳。她寻思自己是不是该说点一位论。可说到底，那里面也许并没有什么，他们也未必体会得到它把基督那桩悬案一下廓清时的松快。何况这屋里没有烦恼。争论不会有什么结果。他们三个高兴的话尽可以面面相觑，把一切一笑了之。一个困倦的星期天下午，无事可做，坐在三张其大无比的椅子里面面相觑，这样就很好。哈丽特的力量与轻蔑指向世上的一切，却不指向她自己……从不指向她自己。哈丽特常常觉得她乖僻、脾气坏，可是喜欢她。她此刻就喜欢着。
“说到底，花边儿（Frills），去教堂是体面事儿，”杰拉尔德懒懒地说。“去吧。时新人物上那儿展示衣裳。”
“嗯，我们的衣裳已经展示过了。”
哈丽特从椅子里飞身而起，娇俏地踢了他一脚。
他伸手抓、没抓住，倒回椅子里号啕，脸埋进靠垫。她的纤足又飞出一下，他闷住一声尖叫。
“闭嘴。”哈丽特说着蜷回她的椅子里。
杰拉尔德号啕如故。
“这儿能抽烟吗？”米丽亚姆说，想让这场戏趁着正好收场，或者换个样。她这就要告诉他们她换了住处。
“能。”哈丽特心不在焉地应着，眼睛盯着杰拉尔德。
“我换了住处了。”米丽亚姆郑重其事地开口，摸索她的烟盒。哈丽特正抡起一只靠垫。杰拉尔德瘫进椅子深处放声抽泣，胳膊乱拍。

===Original===

“Stop it silly,” piped Harriett blushing.
“I’ve changed my diggings,” repeated Miriam uncomfortably. Harriett’s face flashed a response. Gerald’s loud wailings were broken by beseeching cries. Real, absolutely real and satisfying. Miriam answered them from some far deep in herself as if they were her own cries. Harry was embarrassed. Her bright strength was answering. She was ashamed at being seen answering.
Miriam got up conversationally and began looking about for matches in the soft curtained drawing-room light. There were swift movements and Harriett’s voice busily chiding. When she turned Gerald was sitting on the floor at Harriett’s knee beating it gently with his head.
“Got a match, G?” she said seeing in imagination the flare of the match in the soft greenish glare of the room. There was bright light all round the house and a glare of brightness in the garden, beyond the curtains. “Rather,” said Gerald, “dozens.” He sat up and handed out a box. Leaning back against Harriett’s knee he began intoning a little poem of appeal. There was a ring at the front door bell. Miriam got herself to the piano putting cigarettes and matches behind a vase on the mantelshelf. “That’s old Tremayne,” said Gerald cheerfully, shooting his linen and glancing in the strip of mirror in the overmantel. The door opened admitting the light from the hall. The curtains at the open French windows swayed forward flooding the room with the bright garden light. Into the brightness stepped Mr. Tremayne, grey-clad and with a pink rose in his buttonhole.
Over tea they heard the story of his morning and how it had been interrupted by the man on the floor above who had come down in his dressing gown to tell him about a birthday party﻿ ﻿… the two men sitting telling each other stories about drinks and people seeing each other home. After tea he settled back easily in his chair and went on with his stories. Miriam found it almost impossible to follow him. She grew weary of his bantering tone. It smeared over everything he touched and made him appear to be saying one thing over and over again in innuendo. Something he could not say out and would never get away from.

===Chinese===

“住手，傻小子，”哈丽特尖着嗓子说，脸红了。
“我换了住处了。”米丽亚姆又别扭地重复了一遍。哈丽特的脸倏地有了回应。杰拉尔德响亮的号啕里夹进了哀告。真的，千真万确地真、叫人心满意足。米丽亚姆从自己内心深处的某个远方应答它们，仿佛那是她自己的哭喊。哈里不好意思了。她那明亮的力量正在应答。被人看见自己在应答，她臊得慌。
米丽亚姆装作闲谈的样子站起身，在拉了窗帘的客厅柔光里四下找火柴。一阵急促的动静，哈丽特的声音忙忙地呵斥着。待她转过身，杰拉尔德正坐在地板上，偎着哈丽特的膝头，拿脑袋轻轻地磕它。
“有火柴吗，杰？”她说，想象着火柴的火苗在屋子幽绿的柔光里一亮。房子四周一片亮，窗帘那头的花园里一片白晃晃的亮光。“有的是，”杰拉尔德说，“多着呢。”他坐直了，递过一只盒子。他倚着哈丽特的膝盖，开始吟诵一首小小的求告诗。前门门铃响了。米丽亚姆挪到钢琴边，把香烟和火柴藏到壁炉架上一只花瓶后面。“是老特里梅因，”杰拉尔德喜洋洋地说，抻了抻他的亚麻衣领，往炉架上方镜子的一条窄影里照了照。门开了，门厅的光泻进来。敞开的落地长窗前的窗帘向前鼓动，花园的亮光漫进屋里。特里梅因先生踏进这片亮光，一身灰衣，扣眼里簪着一朵粉玫瑰。
喝茶的时候，他们听他讲他这一早晨的事，讲楼上那人如何穿着晨衣下来打断他，跟他讲一个生日聚会……两个男人坐着，你一段我一段，讲喝酒的故事，讲人们如何送彼此回家。喝完茶，他舒舒服服往椅背上一靠，接着讲他的故事。米丽亚姆发觉几乎跟不上他。他那打趣的腔调叫她厌倦。它把他碰到的一切都涂抹一遍，弄得他像是在旁敲侧击、翻来覆去说同一件事。一件他不能说出口、也永远摆脱不掉的事。

===Original===

He made little pauses and then it gleamed horribly about all his refinement of dress and bearing and Gerald laughed encouragingly and he went on, making a story that was like a play, that looked like life did when you looked at it, a maddening fussiness about nothing and people getting into states of mind. He went on into a story about business life﻿ ﻿… people getting the better of each other. It made her feel sick with apprehension. Anybody in business might be ruined any minute unless he could be sure of getting the better of someone else. She had never realised that before.﻿ ﻿… It pressed on her breathing and made her feel that she had had too much tea.﻿ ﻿… She hated the exponent sitting there so coolly. It made the cool green-lit afternoon room an island amongst horrors. But it was that to him too﻿ ﻿… he felt the need of something beyond the everlasting innuendo of social life and the everlasting smartness of business life. She felt it was true that he spent Sunday mornings picking out hymn tunes with one finger and liked “Sabbath music” and remembered the things his mother used to play to him. He wanted a home, something away from business life and away from social life. He saw her as a woman in a home, nicely dressed in a quiet drawing room, lit by softly screened clear fresh garden daylight.﻿ ﻿… “Business is business.”﻿ ﻿… “Man’s love is of man’s life a thing apart﻿—’tis woman’s whole existence.” Tennyson did not know what he was saying when he wrote it in his calm patronising way. Mr. Tremayne would admire it as a “great truth”﻿—thinking it like a man in the way Tennyson thought it. What a hopeless thing a man’s consciousness was. How awful to have nothing but a man’s consciousness. One could test it so easily if one were a little careful, and know exactly how it would behave.﻿ ﻿…
Opening a volume of Mendelssohn she played from his point of view one of the Songs without Words quietly into the conversation. The room grew still. She felt herself and Mr. Tremayne as duplicates of Harriett and Gerald only that she was a very religious very womanly woman, the ideal wife and mother and he was a bad fast man who wanted to be saved. It was such an easy part to play.

===Chinese===

他略停一停，于是那东西便在他衣着的考究、举止的斯文四周骇人地闪烁，杰拉尔德笑着鼓劲，他便接着讲，编出一个像一出戏的故事，像生活隔着一层看过去的模样，一场为着虚无的令人发狂的小题大做，人们一个个钻进这样那样的心境里去。他讲进了生意场的故事……人们如何彼此占上风。这叫她忧虑得心里发呕。干生意的谁都可能转眼间倾家荡产，除非他有把握占某个别人的上风。这她从前不曾意识到。……它压着她的呼吸，叫她觉得茶喝得太多了。……她恨这位代言人，这么冷冷静静坐在那儿。它使这绿光的午后房间成了一座四面皆恐怖的小岛。可对他来说它也是……他感到需要一点什么，超出社交生活没完没了的影射、生意场没完没了的精明之外的东西。她觉得那是真的：他星期天上午用一根手指挑赞美诗的调子，喜欢“安息日的音乐”，记得他母亲从前弹给他听的那些曲子。他想要一个家，一样远离生意场、远离社交场的东西。他把她们看作家中的女人，衣装雅洁地坐在一间安静的客厅里，被花园明净清新的天光透过柔纱照着。……“生意是生意。”……“男人的爱情是男人生命中的一段插曲——却是女人的整个生命。”丁尼生写下这句时用着他那份平静的居高临下，并不知道自己在说什么。特里梅因先生会把这当作一条“伟大的真理”来叹赏——像一个男人那样想它，正如丁尼生像个男人那样想它。一个男人的意识是一件多么无望的东西。倘若除了一个男人的意识之外一无所有，那是多么可怕。只要稍稍留心，便能轻易地考验它，准确知道它会怎样举动。……
她翻开一部门德尔松，站在他的观点上，把一首《无词歌》悄悄弹进谈话里。屋子静下来。她觉得自己和特里梅因先生是哈丽特和杰拉尔德的翻版，只不过她是个极虔诚、极有女人味的女人，理想的妻与母，而他是个浪荡的坏男人，渴望得救。这个角色多容易扮演。

===Original===

She could go on playing it to the end of her life, if he went on in business and made enough money, being a “gracious silence,” taking an interest in his affairs, ordering all things well, quietly training the servants, never losing her temper or raising her voice, making the home a sanctuary of rest and refreshment and religious aspiration, going to church.﻿ ﻿… She felt all these things expressing themselves in her bearing. At the end of her piece she was touched to the heart by the look of adoration in his eyes, the innocent youthfulness shining through his face. There was something in him she could have and guard and keep if she chose. Something that would die if there were no woman to keep it there. There was nothing in his life of business and music halls to keep it there, nothing but the memory of his mother and he joined her on to that memory. His mother and his wife were sacred﻿ ﻿… apart from life. But he could not be really happy with a woman unless he could also despise her. Any interest in generalities, any argument or criticism or opposition would turn him into a towering bully. All men were like that in some way. They each had a set of notions and fought with each other about them whenever they were together and not eating or drinking. If a woman opposed them they went mad. He would like one or two more Mendelssohns and then supper. And if she kept out of the conversation and listened and smiled a little he would go away adoring. She played the Duetto; the chords made her think of Beethoven and play the last page carelessly and glance at Harriett. Harriett had felt her response to the chords and knew she was getting away from Mendelssohn. Mr. Tremayne had moved to a chair quite close to the piano, just behind her. She found the Beethoven and played the first movement of a sonata. It leapt about the piano breaking up her pose, using her body as the instrument of its gay wild shapeliness, spreading her arms inelegantly, swaying her, lifting her from the stool with the crash and vibration of its chords.﻿ ﻿… “Go on,” said Harriett when it came to an end. The Largo came with a single voice deep and broad and quiet; the great truth behind the fuss of things. She felt her hearers grow weary of its reiterations and dashed on alone recklessly into the storm of the last movement.

===Chinese===

这角色她可以一直演到生命尽头，只要他接着做生意、挣到足够的钱，做一个“优雅的静默”，关切他的事务，把一切打点妥帖，悄悄调教仆人，从不发脾气、从不高声，把家变成一座安息、滋养与宗教憧憬的圣殿，上上教堂。……她感到这一切都在她的仪态里流露出来。一曲终了，他眼中倾慕的神情叫她心头一热，一脸天真的少年气透过那张脸放着光。他身上有一样东西，只要她愿意，便可以拥有、看护、保有。一样倘若没有女人替他守住、便会死去的东西。他的生意和音乐厅的生活里没有什么能留住它，除了对母亲的记忆，而他把她们也续接在那段记忆上。他的母亲和他的妻子是神圣的……在生活之外。可是，倘若不能同时轻视一个女人，他便无法真正同她幸福。对一般道理的任何兴趣，任何争论、批评、顶撞，都会叫他变成一个盛气凌人的恶霸。男人全都这样，各有各的路数。人人都揣着一套主张，只要凑在一起、不在吃吃喝喝，便为这些主张争斗。女人一顶嘴，他们就发疯。他愿意再来一两部门德尔松，然后是宵夜。只要她不插话，听着、微笑一点点，他便会怀着倾慕离去。她弹《二重唱》；那些和弦叫她想起贝多芬，末一页便弹得潦草，眼睛瞟向哈丽特。哈丽特觉出了她对和弦的感应，知道她要从门德尔松里跑掉了。特里梅因先生已挪到紧挨钢琴的一把椅子上，就在她身后。她找到那部贝多芬，弹起一首奏鸣曲的第一乐章。它在钢琴上蹦跳，拆了她的架势，把她的身体当乐器使唤，成全它那欢畅狂野的俊逸，把她双臂张得不雅，摇撼着她，和弦轰鸣震颤，把她从琴凳上掀起来。……“接着弹。”乐章终了时哈丽特说。广板来了，单独一个声音，深沉、宽厚、安静；万事的小题大做背后那条伟大的真理。她觉出听的人对它的反复厌倦起来，便独自不管不顾，一头扎进末乐章的风暴。

===Original===

Through its tuneless raging she could hear the steady voice and see the steady shining of the broad clear light. Daylight and gaiety and night and storm and a great song and truth, the great truth that was bigger than anything. Beethoven. She got up, charged to the fingertips with a glow that transfigured all the inanimate things in the room. The party was wrecked﻿ ﻿… a young lady who banged the piano till her hair nearly came down.﻿ ﻿… Mr. Tremayne had heard nothing but noise.﻿ ﻿… His eyes smiled and his uneasy mouth felt for compliments.
---
“Why didn’t you ask him to supper La Fée?”
“The Bollingdons are coming round, silly.”
“Well?”
“With one small chicken and a blancmange.”
“Heaven help us.”
When they sat down to play halfpenny nap after supper Miriam recovered her cigarette from its hiding place. She did not know the game. She sat at Harriett’s new card-table wrapped in the unbroken jesting of the Bollingdons and the Ducaynes, happily learning and smoking and feeling happily wicked. The Bollingdons taught her simply with a complete trustful friendliness, Mrs. Bollingdon leaning across in her pink satin blouse, her clear clean bulging cheeks and dark velvet hair like a full blown dark rose. Between the rounds they poured out anecdotes of earlier nap parties, all talking at once. The pauses at the fresh beginnings were full of the echoes of their laughter. Miriam in the character of the Honourable Miss Henderson had just accepted Lord Bollingdon’s invitation to join the Duke and Duchess of Ducayne and himself and Lady Bollingdon in an all-day party to Wembley Park in a break and four on Easter Monday and had lit a second cigarette and accepted a small whisky and soda when Mr. Grove was announced. Harriett’s face flushed jocular consternation.
When the party subsided after Mr. Grove’s spasmodic handshakings Miriam got herself into a chair in a far corner, smoking her cigarette with burning cheeks. Sitting isolated with her cigarette and her whisky while he twice sent his low harsh clearly murmuring voice into the suddenly empty air to say that he had been to evensong at the Carmelites and was on his way home, she examined the relief of his presence and the nature of her farewell. Mr. Bollingdon responded to him remarking each time on the splendour of the evening.

===Chinese===

穿过那无调性的怒号，她听得见那个平稳的声音，看得见宽阔澄澈的光平稳地闪亮。白昼与欢快，黑夜与风暴，一支浩大的歌与真理，比一切都大的大真理。贝多芬。她站起身，周身充盈着一种光，直透指尖，把屋里一切无生命的东西都点化了。茶会完了……一个把钢琴敲得头发都快散下来的小姐。……特里梅因先生什么也没听见，只听见了响。……他眼睛笑着，那张不安的嘴摸索着恭维话。
---
“你怎么不留他吃宵夜呀，小仙女（La Fée）？”
“博林顿一家要过来，傻瓜。”
“那又怎样？”
“就一只小鸡和一份牛奶冻。”
“老天爷救救我们。”
宵夜后坐下来玩半便士纳普牌，米丽亚姆从藏烟的地方摸回了她的香烟。这牌她不会玩。她坐在哈丽特的新牌桌边，裹在博林顿家和杜肯家不断的玩笑声里，快快活活地学牌、抽烟，觉得自己快快活活地坏。博林顿家两口子教她，教得简单、全然信任而友好，博林顿太太穿着粉缎衬衫俯过身来，她那干净丰盈的面颊和黑丝绒似的头发，像一朵盛开的深色玫瑰。牌局间隙他们倒出一箩筐早先纳普牌会的逸事，人人同时开口。新一局开牌的停顿里，满是方才笑声的回响。米丽亚姆以亨德森荣誉小姐的身份，刚刚接受了博林顿勋爵的邀请——同杜肯公爵夫妇及他本人和博林顿夫人一道，复活节后的星期一乘四马敞车去温布利公园玩上一整天——刚刚点上第二支烟、接过一小杯威士忌苏打，格罗夫先生到了。哈丽特的脸涨红了，一脸滑稽的惊愕。
格罗夫先生一阵痉挛式的握手之后，牌局散了，米丽亚姆缩进远处角落的一把椅子里，脸颊烧着，抽她的烟。她孤零零坐着，守着香烟和威士忌，他那低哑、清晰如喃喃的嗓音两次飘进骤然空落的空气里，说他方才在加尔默罗会做了晚祷，正回家的路上。她于是掂量他在场的那份松快、掂量她告别的性质。博林顿先生应着他，两次都就夜色的明丽赞叹了一番。

===Original===

---
Strolling home towards midnight along the narrow pavement of Endsleigh Gardens Miriam felt as fresh and untroubled as if it were early morning. When she had got out of her Hammersmith omnibus into the Tottenham Court Road she had found that the street had lost its first terrifying impression and had become part of her home. It was the borderland of the part of London she had found for herself; the part where she was going to live, in freedom, hidden, on her pound a week. It was all she wanted. That was why she was young and glad; that was why fatigue had gone out of her life. There was nothing in the world that could come nearer to her than the curious half twilight half moonlight effect of lamplit Endsleigh Gardens opening out of Gower Place; its huge high trees, their sharp shadows on the little pavement running by the side of the railings, the neighbouring gloom of the Euston Road dimly lit by lamps standing high in the middle of the roadway at long intervals, the great high quiet porched houses, black and still, the shadow mass of St. Pancras church, the great dark open space in front of the church, a shadowy figure-haunted darkness with the vague stream of the Euston Road running to one side of it and the corridor of Woburn Place opening on the other. The harsh voice of an invisible woman sounded out from it as she turned off into her own street.﻿ ﻿… “Dressed up﻿—he was﻿—to the bloody death.”﻿ ﻿… The words echoed about her as she strolled down the street controlling her impulse to flinch and hurry. The woman was there, there and real and that was what she had said. Resentment was lurking about the street. The woman’s harsh voice seemed close. Miriam pictured her glaring eyes. There was no pretence about her. She felt what she said. She belonged to the darkness about St. Pancras church﻿ ﻿… people had been garrotted in that part of the Euston Road not so very long ago.﻿ ﻿… Tansley Street was a soft grey gloaming after the darkness. When she rattled her key into the keyhole of number seven she felt that her day was beginning. It would be perpetually beginning now. Nights and days were all one day; all hers, unlimited. Her life and work at Wimpole Street were something extra, thrown in with her own life of endless day. Sarah and Harriett, their lives and friends, her own friends, the Brooms, the girls in Kennett Street, all thrown in.

===Chinese===

---
将近午夜，米丽亚姆沿着恩德斯利花园狭窄的人行道信步往家走，觉得神清气爽、毫无挂碍，仿佛正是清晨。她从哈默史密斯的公共汽车上下来、跨进托特纳姆考特路时，发现这条街已失了它最初那种叫人胆寒的印象，成了她家园的一部分。这是她自己为自己寻得的伦敦那一角的地界；她将要生活在那里，自由自在，藏而不露，靠一星期一镑过活。这就是她要的一切。所以她年轻、欢快；所以疲惫从她的生活里退了出去。世上再没有什么能比这灯下的恩德斯利花园——从高尔坊那儿豁然展开——更贴近她了，那半是暮色半是月色的奇妙光景；它高大参天的树，树影尖尖地投在围栏旁窄窄的人行道上；近旁尤斯顿路的幽暗，隔老远才有一盏灯，高高地立在路心，把它照得迷迷蒙蒙；那些高大的、带门廊的宅子，黑黢黢、静悄悄；圣潘克拉斯教堂黑沉沉的一团，教堂前大片开阔的暗地，一个魅影出没的幽暗，一边淌着尤斯顿路模糊的车流，另一边豁开沃本坊的走廊。她拐进自己那条街时，一个看不见的女人的粗厉嗓门从那里面传出来……“打扮得——他那是——他妈的绝了。”……这话在她四周回荡，她顺着街信步走去，按捺着想要退缩、想要疾走的冲动。那女人就在那儿，真真切切在那儿，这就是她方才说的话。怨恨潜伏在街上。那女人的粗嗓子仿佛很近。米丽亚姆想象出她怒目圆睁的眼睛。她毫无作假。她说的是她的感受。她属于圣潘克拉斯教堂四周的黑暗……就在不久前，尤斯顿路那一段还有人遭过勒杀。……坦斯利街在那一团黑暗之后，是一片柔和的灰色薄暮。她把钥匙哗啦捅进七号的锁孔时，觉得她的一天正在开始。往后它将永远在开始。黑夜白昼都是同一个日子；全是她的，无穷无尽。她在温波尔街的生活与差事是外快，搭在她自己那无尽白昼的生活里。萨拉和哈丽特，她们的生活和朋友，她自己的朋友，布鲁姆一家，肯尼特街的姑娘们，全是搭上的。

===Original===

She lit her table lamp and the gas and two candles, making her little brown room brilliant under a brilliant white ceiling and sat down eager to tell someone of her wealth and freedom.
---
Someone must know she was in London, free, earning her own living. Lilla? She would not see the extraordinary freedom; earning would seem strange and dreadful to her﻿ ﻿… someone who would understand the extraordinary freedom.﻿ ﻿… Alma. *Alma!* Setting forth the London address in a heavy careless hand at the head of a postcard she wrote from the midst of her seventeenth year, “Dear A. Where are you?”
---
Walking home along the Upper Richmond Road; not liking to buy sweets; not enjoying anything to the full﻿—always afraid of her refinements; always in a way wanting to be like her; wanting to share her mysterious knowledge of how things were done in the world and the things one had to do to get on in some clever world where people were doing things. Never really wanting it because the mere thought of that would take the beauty of the syringa and make it look sad. Never being able to explain why one did not want to do reasonable clever things in a clever brisk reasonable way; why one disliked the way she went behaving up and down the Upper Richmond Road with her pretty neat brisk bustling sidling walk, keeping her secret with a sort of prickly brightness. The Upper Richmond Road was heaven, pure heaven; smelling of syringa. She liked flowers but she did not seem to know.﻿ ﻿… *Syringa.* I had forgotten. That is one of the things I have always wanted to stop and remember.﻿ ﻿… What was it all about? What was she doing now? Anyhow the London postcard would be an answer. A letter, making her see Germany and bits of Newlands and what life was now would answer everything, all her snubs and cleverness and bring back the Upper Richmond Road and make it beautiful. She will know something of what it was to me then. Perhaps that was why she liked me even though she thought me vulgar and very lazy and stupid.

===Chinese===

她点亮桌灯，点上煤气灯，又点起两支蜡烛，让她棕色的小房间在一顶亮晃晃的白天花板底下灿然生辉，然后坐下来，急切地想把她的富足与自由讲给什么人听。
---
总得有人知道她在伦敦，自由了，自食其力。莉拉？她看不出这种非常的自由；自食其力在她眼里会显得古怪而可怕……得是一个能懂得这非常的自由的人。……阿尔玛。阿尔玛！她在一张明信片顶端，用重而随意的笔迹写下一行伦敦地址，从她十七岁那年的正中写道：“亲爱的阿。你在哪儿？”
---
沿着上里士满路走回家；舍不得买糖果；什么都不肯尽兴地享受——总忌着她的那些雅致；总在某种程度上想学她的样；想分享她那套世事如何打理的神秘知识，分享在那个聪明世界——人们都在做事的世界——里出人头地必得做的事。其实从没真想学，因为单这一个念头就会夺走山梅花的美丽，叫它显得悲哀。始终没法解释清楚，为什么自己不肯用聪明的、麻利的、讲道理的方式去做通情达理的聪明事；为什么看不惯她那副样子——在上里士满路上走上走下，步子俏丽、利落、匆匆、侧身，浑身带着一种扎人的亮劲儿，守着她的秘密。上里士满路是天堂，纯粹的天堂；飘着山梅香。她喜欢花，可她仿佛并不知道。……山梅花。我忘了。这是我一直想停下来记起的事情之一。……那都是怎么回事？她如今在做什么？不管怎样，这张伦敦的明信片会是一个回答。一封信，让她看见德国、看见纽兰兹的零星、看见如今的生活，便能回答一切，回答她所有的奚落与聪明，把上里士满路带回来，让它变美。她会约略懂得那一切于我的意义。也许正为这个她才喜欢我，尽管她觉得我粗俗、懒惰、愚蠢。

## III / 三
===Original===

There was a carriage at the door. West-end people, after late nights, managing to keep nine o’clock appointments﻿—in a north wind. Miriam pressed the bell urgently. The scrubbed chalky mosaic and the busy bright brass plate reproached her for her lateness during the long moment before the door was opened.﻿ ﻿… It must be someone for Mr. Orly; an appointment made since last night; that was the worst of his living in the house. He was in his surgery now, with the patient. The nine-fifteen patient would come almost at once. He would discover that his charts were not out before there was any chance of getting at his appointment book.﻿ ﻿… As the great door swung open she saw Mr. Hancock turn the corner of the street walking very rapidly before the north wind.﻿ ﻿… Mr. Orly’s voice was sounding impatiently from the back of the hall.﻿ ﻿… “Where’s Miss Hends.﻿ ﻿… Oh﻿—here y’are Miss Hends, I say call up Chalk for me will ya, get him to come at once, I’ve got the patient waiting.” His huge frock-coated form swung round into his surgery without waiting for an answer. Miriam scurried through the hall past Mr. Leyton’s open surgery door and into her room. Mr. Leyton plunged out of his room as she was flinging down her things and came in briskly. “Morning, Pater got a gas case?”
“Mm;” said Miriam. “I’ve got to call up Chalk and I haven’t a second to do it.”
“Why Chalk?”
“Oh I don’t know. He said Chalk,” said Miriam angrily, seizing the directory.
“I’ll call him up if you like.”
“You are a saint. Tell him to come at once﻿—sooner,” said Miriam dabbing at her hair as she ran back through the hall and upstairs. As she passed the turn of the staircase Mr. Hancock was let in at the front door. She found his kettle furiously boiling on its wrought-iron stand near the chair. The stained glass window just behind it was dim with steam. She lowered the gas, put a tumbler in the socket of the spittoon, lit the gas burner on the bracket table and swiftly pulled open its drawers one by one. The instruments were all right﻿ ﻿… the bottles﻿—no chloroform, the carbolic bottle nearly empty and its label soaked and defaced. Gathering the two bottles in her hand she turned to the instrument cabinet, no serviettes, no rubber dam, clamps not up from the workshop. The top of the cabinet still to be dusted.

===Chinese===

门口停着一辆马车。西区的人物，昨夜欢到深夜，居然还能赶九点钟的约——顶着北风。
米丽亚姆急急按铃。擦洗过的、白垩似的马赛克地面和那块亮闪闪的铜牌，在门开之前的漫长一刻里责备着她的迟到。……一定是找奥利先生的；昨夜之后新定的约；这就是他住在这宅子里的坏处。他这会儿正在诊室里，陪着病人。九点一刻的病人马上又要到。不等有机会翻他的预约簿，他就会发觉他的病历卡还没摆出来。……大门荡开时，她看见汉考克先生顶着北风从街角快步走来。……奥利先生的声音正从门厅后头不耐烦地响着……“亨德森小姐在哪儿。……哦——你在这儿，亨德森小姐。我说，替我打电话给查克，好么，叫他马上来，我这儿病人等着呢。”他那穿着大礼服的魁伟身影没等回答便一转身进了诊室。米丽亚姆匆匆穿过门厅，经过莱顿先生敞开的诊室门，进自己的房间。她正把东西往下一掊，莱顿先生从屋里冲了出来，快步进来。“早。老爸接了个煤气病例？”
“嗯，”米丽亚姆说。“我得给查克打电话，可我连一秒钟都没有。”
“干吗找查克？”
“哦，我不知道。他说查克，”米丽亚姆气冲冲地说，一把抓起电话簿。
“你乐意的话我来打。”
“你真是个圣人。叫他马上来——越快越好，”米丽亚姆边说边胡乱拍着头发，重新跑过门厅、跑上楼去。她经过楼梯拐角时，汉考克先生从前门被让了进来。她发现他的水壶在治疗椅旁的熟铁架上狂怒地滚开着。它正后方的彩色玻璃窗被蒸汽熏得模糊。她把煤气调小，在唾液孟的托圈里放上一只平底大玻璃杯，点燃边架台上的煤气灯，飞快地把抽屉一个个拉开。器械没问题……瓶子——没有氯仿，石炭酸瓶快空了，标签浸得透湿、字迹模糊。她把两只瓶子拢在手里，转身对着器械柜：没有治疗巾，没有橡皮障，固位夹还没从工场送上来。柜顶还待掸尘。

===Original===

Dust and scraps of amalgam were visible about the surfaces of the paper lining the instrument drawers. No saliva tubes in the basin. She swung round to the bureau and hurriedly read through the names of the morning’s patients. Mr. Hancock came quietly in as she was dusting the top of the instrument cabinet by pushing the boxes and bottles of materials that littered its surface to the backmost edge. They were all lightly coated with dust. It was everlasting and the long tubes and metal body of the little furnace were dull again. “Good morning,” they said simultaneously, in even tones. There were sounds of letters being opened and the turning of the pages of the appointment book. The chain of Mr. Hancock’s gold pencil case rattled softly as he made notes on the corners of the letters.
“Did you have a pleasant weekend?”
“Very,” said Miriam emphatically.
There was a squeak at the side of the cabinet. “Yes,” said Miriam down the speaking tube.﻿ ﻿… “Thank you. Will you please bring up some tubes and serviettes.”
“Mr. Wontner.”
“Thank you.”﻿ ﻿… “Mrs. Hermann is ‘frightfully shocked’ at the amount of her account. What did we send it in for?”
“Seventy guineas. It’s a reduction, and it’s two years’ work for the whole family.” The bell sounded again.﻿ ﻿… “Lady Cazalet has bad toothache and can you see her at once.”
“Con*found*.﻿ ﻿… Will you go down and talk to her and see if you can get one of the others to see her.”
“She won’t.”
“Well then she must wait. I’ll have Mr. Wontner up.” Miriam rang. Mr. Hancock began busily washing his hands. The patient came in. He greeted him over his shoulder. Miriam gathered up the sheaf of annotated letters and the appointment book and ran downstairs. “Has Mr. Leyton a patient Emma?” “Miss Jones just gone in, Miss.” “Oh, Emma, will you ask the workshop for Mr. Hancock’s rubber and clamps?” She rang through to Mr. Leyton’s room. “There’s a patient of Mr. Hancock’s in pain, can you see them if I can persuade them?” she murmured. “Right, in ten minutes,” came the answering murmur. Mr. Hancock’s bell sounded from her room. She went to his tube in the hall. “Can I have my charts?” Running into her room she hunted out the first chart from a case full and ran upstairs with it. Mr. Hancock’s patient was sitting forward in the chair urging the adoption of the decimal system. Running down again she went into the waiting room.

===Chinese===

器械抽屉里垫纸的表面看得见灰尘和汞齐碎屑。盆里没有唾液吸管。她旋身奔向写字台，匆匆把上午病人的名字过了一遍。她正把柜面上乱摊的材料盒、材料瓶推到最里边去、好掸柜顶的尘，汉考克先生悄悄走了进来。它们全都蒙着薄薄一层灰。灰尘是没完没了的，小熔炉的长管子和金属炉身又暗了。“早上好。”两人同时开口，语调平平。拆信的声音、翻预约簿页子的声音。汉考克先生的金铅笔套的链子轻轻作响，他在信角上做记号。
“周末过得愉快吗？”
“非常愉快。”米丽亚姆加重语气说。
柜边传来一声吱呀。“是，”米丽亚姆对着传话管说。……“谢谢。请送些吸管和治疗巾上来。”
“旺特纳先生。”
“谢谢。”……“赫尔曼太太见了账单的数目‘吓了一大跳’。我们寄它去做什么？”
“七十畿尼。还是减过的，这是给全家两年的活儿。”铃又响了……“卡扎莱特夫人牙疼得厉害，问您能不能马上看她。”
“见*鬼*。……你下去同她说说，看能不能让别位给她看。”
“她不肯。”
“那她就只好等。让旺特纳先生上来吧。”米丽亚姆按铃。汉考克先生忙着洗手。病人进来。他隔着肩膀同他打了招呼。米丽亚姆收起那一沓做了记号的信和预约簿，跑下楼去。“艾玛，莱顿先生有病人吗？”“琼斯小姐刚进去，小姐。”“哦，艾玛，你到工场替汉考克先生问问橡皮障和固位夹好吗？”她打内线电话到莱顿先生房里。“汉考克先生有个病人疼得厉害，要是我劝得动，你能看吗？”她低声道。“成，十分钟后。”那头低声应道。她房里汉考克先生的铃响了。她到门厅他那根传话管前。“能把我的病历卡给我吗？”她跑进自己房里，从满满一匣病历卡里翻出头一张，拿着跑上楼去。汉考克先生的病人坐在椅子里前倾着身子，力陈十进制的必要。再跑下来，她进了候诊室。

===Original===

The dark Turkey carpeted oak furnished length seemed full of seated forms. Miriam peered and Lady Cazalet, with her hat already off rose from the deep armchair at her side. “Can he see me?” she said in a clear trembling undertone, her dark eyes wide upon Miriam’s. Miriam gazed deep into the limpid fear. What a privilege. How often Captain Cazalet must be beside himself with unworthiness. “Yes, if you can wait a little,” she said dropping her eyes and standing with arms restrained. “I think it won’t be very long,” she added lingering a moment as the little form relapsed into the chair.
“Lady Cazalet will wait until you can see her,” she tubed up to Mr. Hancock.
“Can’t you make her see one of the others?”
“I’m afraid it’s impossible; I’ll tell you later.”
“Well I’ll see her as soon as I can. I’m afraid she’ll have to wait.”
Miriam went back to her room to sort out the remaining charts. On her table lay a broken denture in a faded morocco case; a strip of paper directed “five-thirty sharp” in Mr. Orly’s handwriting. Mr. Leyton’s door burst open. He came with flying coattails.
“Vi got to see that patient of Mr. Hancock’s,” he asked breathlessly.
“No,” said Miriam, “she won’t.”
“Right,” he said swinging back. “I’ll keep Miss Jones on.”
Mr. Hancock’s bell sounded again. Miriam flew to the tube.
“My clamps please.”
“Oh yes,” she answered shocked, and hurried back to her room.
Gathering up the broken denture she ran down the stone steps leading to the basement. Her cheap unyielding shoes clattered on the unyielding stones. The gas was on in the lunch room, Mrs. Willis scrubbing the floor. The voices of the servants came from the kitchens in the unknown background. She passed the lunch room and the cellar and clamped on across the stone hall to the open door of the workshop.
---
Winthrop was standing at the small furnace in the box-lined passageway. It was roaring its loudest. Through its open door the red light fell sharply on his pink-flushed face and drooping fair moustache and poured down over his white apron. “Good ph‑morning,” he said pleasantly, his eye on the heart of the furnace, his foot briskly pumping the blower. From the body of the room came sounds of tapping and whistling﻿ ﻿… the noise of the furnace prevented their knowing that anyone had come in.﻿ ﻿… Miriam drew near to the furnace, relieved at the shortness of her excursion.

===Chinese===

这条铺着深色土耳其地毯、摆着橡木家具的长屋仿佛坐满了人。米丽亚姆张望，卡扎莱特夫人已经摘了帽子，从她身旁的深扶手椅里站起来。“他能看我吗？”她用清亮而发颤的低声说，一双黑眼睛睁得大大的，望着米丽亚姆的眼睛。米丽亚姆深深望进那澄澈的恐惧里。何等的殊荣。卡扎莱特上尉该常常为自己配不上而发狂吧。“能，只要您稍等一等。”她垂下眼，克制地垂手站着。“我想不会很久的。”她流连了一刻又说，那小小的身影已缩回椅子里去了。
“卡扎莱特夫人愿意等到您能看她。”她对着传话管向汉考克先生说。
“你没法让她找别位看吗？”
“恐怕不可能；回头我告诉您。”
“好吧，我尽快看她。只怕她得等等。”
米丽亚姆回自己房里去理余下的病历卡。她桌上放着一只褪色的摩洛哥皮盒，里面是一副摔坏的假牙；一张纸条上是奥利先生的笔迹：“五点半整”。莱顿先生的门猛地开了。他衣摆飞扬地冲过来。
“我——能不能看汉考克先生那个病人？”他喘着气问。
“不行，”米丽亚姆说，“她不肯。”
“好，”他一转身甩回去。“琼斯小姐我留下了。”
汉考克先生的铃又响。米丽亚姆飞奔到传话管前。
“麻烦你，我的固位夹。”
“哦，对了。”她心惊地应道，赶忙回房。
她收起那副坏假牙，顺着石阶跑下地下室。她那双硬邦邦的便宜鞋在硬邦邦的石头上得得作响。午餐室里点着煤气灯，威利斯太太在刷地板。仆役们的声音从看不见的后头厨房里传来。她经过午餐室和地窖，橐橐地穿过石头过厅，到工场敞开的门前。
---
温思罗普站在纸箱夹道的过道里那座小熔炉前。炉子正吼到最响。红光从敞开的炉门里尖锐地落在他粉红的脸和垂着的淡色髭须上，又倾泻到他的白围裙上。“早——早上好，”他愉快地说，眼睛盯着炉心，脚下轻快地踏着风箱。屋子深处传来敲打声和口哨声……炉子的轰鸣使他们不曾察觉有人进来。……米丽亚姆凑近熔炉，庆幸自己这一趟跑得不远。

===Original===

She stared at the tiny shape blazing red-gold at the heart of the glare. Winthrop gathered up a pair of tongs and drew the mould from the little square of light. The air hissed from the bellows and the roaring of the flames died down. In a moment he was standing free with hot face and hot patient ironic eyes, gently taking the denture from her hands. “Good morning,” said Miriam. “Oh, Mr. Winthrop, it’s a repair for Mr. Orly. It’s urgent. Can you manage it?” “It’s ph‑ph‑sure to be urgent,” said Winthrop examining the denture with a shortsighted frown. Miriam waited anxiously. The hammering and whistling had ceased. “It’ll be all right, Miss Ph‑Henderson,” said Winthrop encouragingly. She turned to the door. The clamps.﻿ ﻿… Gathering herself together she went down the passage and stood at the head of the two stone steps leading down into the body of the room. A swift scrubbing of emery paper on metal was going on at the end of the long bench, lit by a long skylight, from which the four faces looked up at her with a chorus of good mornings in response to her greeting. “Are Mr. Hancock’s clamps ready?” she asked diffidently. “Jimmy﻿ ﻿…” The figure nearest to her glanced down the row of seated forms. The small bullet-headed boy at the end of the bench scrubbed vigorously and ironically with his emery stick. “He won’t be a minute, Miss Henderson,” said the near pupil comfortingly.
Miriam observed his spruce grey suit curiously masked by the mechanic’s apron, the quiet controlled amused face, and felt the burden of her little attack as part of the patient prolonged boredom of his pupillage. The second pupil, sitting next to him kept doglike sympathetic eyes on her face, waiting for a glance. She passed him by, smiling gently in response without looking at him while her eyes rested upon the form of the junior mechanic whose head was turned in the direction of the scrubbing boy. The head was refined, thin and clear cut, thatched with glossy curls. Its expression was servile﻿—the brain eagerly seeking some flowery phrase﻿—something to decorate at once the occasion and the speaker, and to give relief to the mouth strained in an arrested obsequious smile. Nothing came and the clever meticulous hands were idle on the board. It seemed absurd to say that Mr. Hancock was waiting for the clamps while Jimmy was scrubbing so busily. But they had obviously been forgotten.

===Chinese===

她盯着强光正中那小小的、烧成红金色的形状。温思罗普抓起一把钳子，把铸模从那一小方光亮里夹出来。风箱嘶地放着气，火焰的吼声低下去。转眼他已闲闲站着，脸热热的，一双耐心的、讥诮的眼睛，温柔地从她手里接过假牙。“早上好，”米丽亚姆说。“哦，温思罗普先生，这是奥利先生的活儿，急着要。您能赶一赶吗？”“那——那准是急的，”温思罗普说着，眯起近视的眉头察看假牙。米丽亚姆焦急地等着。锤打声和口哨声都停了。“误不了事，亨——亨德森小姐。”温思罗普鼓劲地说。她向门口转去。固位夹。……她打起精神，走下过道，站在通往屋身的那两级石阶上头。长工作台尽头正沙沙地用砂纸蹭着金属，头顶一条长天窗照亮，四张脸应着她的问好，齐声抬起头来朝她道早安。“汉考克先生的固位夹好了吗？”她怯怯地问。“吉米……”离她最近的那个人沿一排坐着的人影望过去。工作台尽头那个子弹头似的小伙子用砂棒起劲地、带点揶揄地蹭着。“他一会儿就好，亨德森小姐。”近旁的学徒安慰道。
米丽亚姆打量他那一身整洁的灰西装，被技工的围裙奇奇怪怪地罩着，那张安静、克制、觉得好笑的脸，感到自己这一小小的打扰，是他漫长的学徒生涯里那番耐心的无聊的一部分。坐在旁边的第二个学徒拿狗似的、满含同情的眼光守着她的脸，等她一瞥。她没理会他，不看他、只温婉地笑了笑作答，眼睛落在了那个年轻技工的身影上，他的头正转向蹭砂纸的少年那边。那脑袋清秀、瘦削、轮廓分明，覆着一头亮泽的卷发。神情是卑恭的——脑子急切地搜寻一句花哨话——好同时给这个场合和说话的人添彩，也好松一松那张嘴，它正僵在一个谄笑上。什么也没想出来，那双灵巧细致的手在台子上闲着。这时候说汉考克先生正等固位夹、而吉米又蹭得这么起劲，未免荒唐。可它们分明是被忘了。

===Original===

She fidgeted.
“Will somebody send them up when they’re done?”
“Jimmy, you’re a miserable sinner, hurry up,” said the senior pupil.
“They’re done,” said Jimmy in a cracked bass voice. “Thank goodness,” breathed Miriam, dimpling. Jimmy came round and scattered the clamps carefully into her outstretched hand, with downcast eyes and a crisp dimpling smile.
“Rule Bri*tan*nia,” remarked the junior pupil, resuming his work as Miriam turned away and hurried along the passage and through the door held open for her by Winthrop. She flew up to Mr. Hancock’s room three steps at a time, tapped gently at the door and went in. He came forward across the soft grey green carpet to take the clamps and murmured gently, “Have you got my carbolic?”
---
Miriam looked out the remainder of the charts and went anxiously through the little pile of letters she had brought down from Mr. Hancock’s room. All but three were straightforward appointments to be sent. One bore besides the pencilled day and date the word “Tape”﻿ ﻿… she glanced through it﻿—it was from a University settlement worker, asking for an appointment for the filling of two front teeth.﻿ ﻿… She would understand increasing by one thickness per day until there were five, to be completed two days before the appointment falls due so that any tenderness may have passed off. Mrs. Hermann’s letter bore no mark. She could make a rough summary in Mr. Hancock’s phraseology. The third letter enclosed a printed card of appointment with Mr. Hancock which she had sent without filling in the day and hour. She flushed. Mr. Hancock had pencilled in the missing words. Gathering the letters together she put them as far away as her hand would reach, leaving a space of shabby ink-stained morocco clear under her hands. She looked blindly out of the window; hand-painted, they are hand-painted, forget-me-nots and gold tendrils softly painted, not shining, on an unusual shape, a merry Christmas. Melly Klismas. In this countree heapee lain, chiney man lun home again, under a red and green paper umbrella in the pouring rain, that was not a hand-painted one, but better, in some *strange* way, close bright colours drawing everything *in*; a shock. I stayed in there. There was something. Chinee man lun *home* again. Her eye roamed over the table; everything but the newly-arrived letters shabby under the high wide uncurtained window.

===Chinese===

她焦躁起来。
“谁把它们弄好了给送上去行吗？”
“吉米，你个不可救药的小罪人，快点。”大师兄说。
“好了。”吉米用破了音的男低音说。“谢天谢地。”米丽亚姆轻声道，腮上现出笑靥。吉米绕过来，垂着眼、脆生生地一笑，把固位夹小心地散放进她摊开的手里。
“《统治吧，不——不列颠尼亚》。”小师弟说了一句，接着干活；米丽亚姆转身匆匆沿过道走去，穿过温思罗普替她拉开的门。她一步三级地飞上汉考克先生的房间，轻轻叩了门进去。他迎过柔软的灰绿地毯来接固位夹，柔声问：“我的石炭酸拿到了吗？”
---
米丽亚姆把余下的病历卡挑出来，又不安地翻检从汉考克先生房里拿下的一小沓信。除了三封，都是直截了当、照发的预约回信。有一封在铅笔写的星期和日子之外，还注着“胶布”两个字……她草草过了一遍——是大学居民区的一名工作人员，预约补两颗门牙。……她会懂的：每天加厚一层，加到五层为止，在预约日之前两天贴完，好叫可能出现的酸胀都过去。赫尔曼太太那封没有记号。她可以用汉考克先生的措辞拟一份大意。第三封里附着一张印好的与汉考克先生的预约卡，是她发出去时没填日子和钟点。她脸红了。汉考克先生用铅笔补上了缺的字。她把信拢到一起，推到手臂够得着的最远处，手底下空出一片墨渍斑斑的旧摩洛哥皮。她怔怔地望向窗外；手绘的，那是手绘的，勿忘我和金色卷须柔柔地画在一种别致的形状上，不发光，圣诞快乐。麦利·克利司马斯。这个国——国，雨落多多，中国佬跑回家，滂沱大雨里，红红绿绿一把纸伞下——那张不是手绘的，却更好，不知怎么，有一种奇异的妙，鲜明的颜色把一切都收了进去；叫人一震。我留在那里面。有一种什么。中国佬跑——跑回家。她的目光在桌上游走；高而宽、不挂窗帘的窗下，除了新到的信，样样都寒素。

===Original===

The table fitted the width of the window. There was something to be done before anything could be done. Everything would look different if something were done. The fresh letters could lie neatly on the centre of the table in the midst of something. They were on the address books, spoiled by them. It would take years to check the addresses one by one till the old books could be put away. If the daybooks were entered up to date, there would still be those, disfiguring everything. If everything were absolutely up to date, and all the cupboards in perfect order and the discounts and decimals always done in the depot-books to time there would be time to do something. She replaced the letters in the centre of the table and put them back again on the address books. His nine-forty-five patient was being let in at the front door. In a moment his bell would ring and something must be said about the appointment card. “Mr. Orly?” A big booming elderly voice, going on heavily murmuring into the waiting-room. She listened tensely to the movements of the servant. Was Mr. Orly in “the den” or in his surgery? She heard the maid ring through to the surgery and wait. No sound. The maid came through her room and tapped at the door leading from it. *Come* in sang a voice from within and Miriam heard the sound of a hammer on metal as the maid opened the door. She flew to the surgery. Amidst the stillness of heavy oak furniture and dark Turkey carpet floated the confirming smell. There it was all about the spittoon and the red velvet covered chair and the bracket table, a horrible confusion﻿—and blood stains, blood blotted serviettes, forceps that made her feel sick and faint. Summoning up her strength she gathered up the serviettes and flung them into a basket behind the instrument cabinet. She was dabbing at the stains on the American cloth cover of the bracket when Mr. Orly came swinging in, putting on his grey frock-coat and humming Gunga Din as he came. “Regular field day,” he said cheerfully. “I shan’t want those things﻿—just pop ’em out of sight.” He turned up the cupboard gas and in a moment a stream of boiling water hissed down into the basin filling the room with steam. “I say, has this man got a chart? Don’t throw away those teeth. Just look at this﻿—how’s that for twisted? Just look here.”

===Chinese===

这张桌子正好卡满窗宽。得先做一件事，才谈得上做任何事。只要那件事做了，一切看上去都会不同。新到的信便可以齐齐整整摆在桌子正中、摆在“秩序”当中。它们眼下压在地址簿上，被地址簿糟蹋着。要一条条核完地址、直到旧簿子能收起来，得花上几年。就算流水账记到了今天，那些东西还在，碍着一切的眼。倘若一切都绝对赶在了日子前头，所有的柜子井井有条，折扣和小数永远按时记进发货簿，那就腾得出工夫做点什么了。她把信挪回桌子中央，又把它们放回地址簿上。他九点三刻的病人正从前门被让进来。马上他的铃就要响，预约卡的事总得说一句。“奥利先生？”一个低沉隆隆的老人嗓音，一路重浊地喃喃着进了候诊室。她紧张地听女仆的动静。奥利先生在“窝”里还是在诊室？她听见女仆打了内线到诊室，等着。没有动静。女仆穿过她的房间，敲了敲从这儿通过去的门。进来——里面一个声音唱着；女仆开门时，米丽亚姆听见铁上敲锤子的声音。她飞奔进诊室。沉重的橡木家具和深色土耳其地毯的静穆中间，浮动着坐实了那件事的气味。唾液孟四周、红丝绒面的治疗椅四周、边架台四周，全都是它，一团可怕的狼藉——还有血迹、浸透了血的布巾、叫她恶心发晕的拔牙钳。她鼓起力气，把布巾拢起来，摔进器械柜后头的筐里。她正擦拭边架台漆布面上的血渍，奥利先生一阵风地进来，一面套他的灰色大礼服，一路哼着《贡格丁》。“简直是会操。”他喜洋洋地说。“那些东西我用不着了——把它们收得看不见就行。”他拧大柜下的煤气，转眼一股沸水嘶嘶冲进盆里，满屋子腾起蒸汽。“我说，这人有病历卡吗？别把那些牙扔了。瞧瞧这个——拧成这样，够绝吧？瞧这儿。”

===Original===

He took up an object to which Miriam forced reluctant eyes, grotesquely formed fangs protruding from the enclosing blades of a huge forceps. “How’s that eh?” Miriam made a sympathetic sound. Gathering the many forceps he detached their contents putting the relic into a bottle of spirit and the rest into the hidden basket. The forceps went head first into a jar of carbolic and Miriam breathed more freely. “I’ll see to those. I say has this man got a chart?” “I’ll see,” said Miriam eagerly making off with the appointment book. She returned with the chart. Mr. Orly hummed and looked. “Right. Tell ’em to send him in. I say, vi got any gold and tin?” Miriam consulted the box in a drawer in the cabinet. It was empty. “I’m afraid you haven’t,” she said guiltily. “All right, I’ll let y’know. Send ’im in,” and he resumed Gunga Din over the wash-hand basin. Mr. Hancock’s bell was ringing in her room and she hurried off with a sign to the little maid waiting with raised eyebrows in the hall. Darting into her room she took the foils from the safe, laid them on a clean serviette amongst the litter on her table and ran upstairs. Mr. Leyton opened his door as she passed “I say, can you feed for me,” he asked breathlessly, putting out an anxious head. “I’ll come down in a minute,” promised Miriam from the stairs. Mr. Hancock was drying his hands. He sounded his bell as she came in. The maid answered. “I’m so sorry,” began Miriam. “Show up Mr. Green,” said Mr. Hancock down the speaking tube. “You remember there’s Lady Cazalet?” said Miriam relieved and feeling she was making good her carelessness in the matter of the appointment card.
“Oh con*found*.” He rang again hurriedly. “Show up Lady Cazalet.” Miriam swept from the bracket table the litter of used instruments and materials, disposing them rapidly on the cabinet, into the sterilising tray, the waste basket and the wash-hand basin, tore the uppermost leaf from the headrest pad, and detached the handpiece from the arm of the motor drill while the patient was being shown upstairs. Hancock had cleared the spittoon, set a fresh tumbler, filled the kettle and whisked the debris of amalgam and cement from the bracket table before he began the scrubbing and cleansing of his hands, and when the patient came in Miriam was in her corner reluctantly handling the instruments, wet with the solution that crinkled her fingertips and made her skin brittle and dry. Everything was in its worst state. The business of drying and cleansing, freeing fine points from minute closely adhering fragments, polishing instruments on the leather pad, repolishing them with the leather, scraping the many little burs with the fine wire brush, scraping the clamps, clearing the obstinate amalgam from slab and spatula, brought across her the ever-recurring circle. The things were begun, they were getting on, she had half-done﻿ ﻿… the exasperating tediousness of holding herself to the long series of tiny careful attention-demanding movements﻿ ﻿… the punctual emergence when the end was in sight of the hovering reflection, nagging and questioning, that another set of things was already getting ready for another cleansing process; the endless series to last as long as she stayed at Wimpole Street﻿ ﻿… were there any sort of people who could do this kind of thing patiently, without minding?﻿ ﻿… the evolution of dentistry was wonderful, but the more perfect it became the more and more of this sort of thing there would be﻿ ﻿… the more drudgery workers, at fixed salaries﻿ ﻿… it was only possible for people who were fine and nice﻿ ﻿… there must be, everywhere, women doing this work for people who were not nice. They *could* not do it for the work’s sake. Did some of them do it cheerfully, as unto God. It was wrong to work unto man. But could God approve of this kind of thing﻿ ﻿… was it right to spend life cleaning instruments﻿ ﻿… the blank moment again of gazing about in vain for an alternative﻿ ﻿… all work has drudgery. That is not the answer.﻿ ﻿… Blessèd be Drudgery, but that was housekeeping, not someone else’s drudgery.﻿ ﻿… As she put the things back in the drawers, every drawer offered tasks of tidying, replenishing, and repapering of small boxes and grooves and sections. She had remembered to bring up Lady Cazalet’s chart. It looked at her propped against the small furnace. Behind it were the other charts for the day complete.

It was a busy day. He would hardly have half an hour for lunch.﻿ ﻿… She examined the names carefully, one by one, and wrote against one “ask address,” underlined and against another “enquire for brother﻿—ill.” Lady Cazalet drew a deep sigh﻿ ﻿… she had been to other dentists. But perhaps they were good ones. Perhaps she was about thirty﻿ ﻿… had she ever gone through a green baize door and seen a fat common little man with smooth sly eyes standing waiting for her in a dark stuffy room smelling of creosote? Even if she had always been to good ones they were not Mr. Hancock. They were dentists. Cheerful ordinary men with ordinary voices and laughs, thinking about all manner of things. Or apparently bland, with ingratiating manners. Perhaps a few of them, some of his friends and some of the young men he had trained were something like him. Interested in dentistry and the way it was all developing, some of them more enthusiastic and interested in certain special things than he was. But no one could be quite like him. No other patients had the lot of his patients. No other dentist was so completely conscious of the patient all the time, as if he were in the chair himself. No other dentist went on year after year remaining sensitive to everything the patient had to endure. No one else was so unsparing of himself﻿ ﻿… children coming eagerly in for their dentistry, sitting in the chair with slack limbs and wide open mouths and tranquil eyes﻿ ﻿… small bodies braced and tense, fat hands splayed out tightly on the too-big arms of the chair in determination to bear the moment of pain bravely for him.﻿ ﻿…
