When his room was cleared in preparation for his last patient it was nearly six o’clock. She began entering his daybook in the ledger. The boy coming up for the letters brought two dentures to be packed and despatched by registered post from Vere Street before six o’clock. “They’ll be ready by the time you’ve got your boots on,” said Miriam and packed her cases brilliantly in a mood of deft-handed concentration. Jimmy clattered up the stairs as she was stamping the labels. When he fled with them she gave a general sigh and surveyed the balance of her day with a responsible cheerful wicked desperation; her mind leaping forward to her evening. The daybooks would not be done, even Mr. Hancock’s would have to go up unentered; she had not the courage to investigate the state of the cash book; Mr. Leyton’s room was ready for the morning; she ran through to Mr. Orly’s room and performed a rapid perfunctory tidying up; many little things were left; his depleted stores must be refilled in the morning; she glanced at his appointment book, no patient so far until ten. She left the room with her everyday guilty consciousness that hardly anything in it was up to the level of Mr. Hancock’s room﻿ ﻿… look after Hancock, I’m used to fending for myself﻿ ﻿… but he knew she did not do her utmost to keep the room going. There were times when he ran short of stores in the midst of a sitting. That could be avoided.
---
When Miriam entered his room at half past six Mr. Hancock was switching off the lights about the chair. A single light shone over his desk. The fire was nearly out.
“Still here?”
“Yes,” said Miriam switching on a light over the instrument cabinet.
“I should leave those things tonight if I were you.”
“It isn’t very late.”
She could go on, indefinitely, in this confident silence, preparing for the next day. He sat making up his daybook and would presently come upon Mrs. Hermann’s letter. As long as he was there the day lingered. Its light had left the room. The room was colourless and dark except where the two little brilliant circles of light made bright patches of winter evening. Their two figures quietly at work meant the quiet and peace of the practice; the full, ended day, to begin again tomorrow in broad daylight in this same room. The room was full of their quiet continuous companionship. It was getting very cold. He would be going soon.

===Chinese===

等他的房间为最后一位病人收拾停当，已近六点。她开始把他的流水账登进总账。上来取信的男孩带来两副假牙，要六点前从维尔街挂号寄出。“等你穿上靴子它们就好了，”米丽亚姆说着，在一种手巧心专的心境里漂亮利落地打好包。她正往签条上盖戳，吉米噔噔噔跑上楼来。他抱着包裹飞奔而去，她长出一口气，怀着一种负责的、快活的、豁出去的心情掂量这一天的余数；心思早跳到晚间去了。流水账是赶不完了，连汉考克先生的也只能原样带上去；现金簿的情形她没胆量查；莱顿先生的房间已备好明早之用；她跑进奥利先生房里，风风火火敷衍了事地归置了一遍；许多零碎留下了；他耗尽的备料明早必须补上；她扫了一眼他的预约簿，到十点为止没有病人。她带着每天那份亏心走出房间——这屋里几乎没有一样够得上汉考克先生房间的水准……照应好汉考克，我自己惯于自己对付……可他知道，她没有尽最大力气让这间屋子转起来。有时他做到一半，备料断了档。那是可以避免的。
---
六点半米丽亚姆进他房间时，汉考克先生正关掉治疗椅四周的灯。书桌上孤零零一盏亮着。炉火将熄。
“还在？”
“在，”米丽亚姆说着，拧亮器械柜上方一盏灯。
“我是你，今晚那些东西就搁下了。”
“不算晚。”
这样的、笃定的静默里她可以一直做下去，无休无止，为明天备好一切。他坐着结他的流水账，一会儿就会撞见赫尔曼太太那封信。只要他在，这一天就流连不去。天光已离开房间。屋子无色而昏暗，只有两小圈灿亮的灯，点出两片冬夜。他们两个静静做事的身影，便是这诊所的安静与太平；圆满收梢的一天，明天大天白亮又在这同一间屋子里开始。满屋是他们静静的、连续的作伴。天很冷了。他快走了。

===Original===

He stood up, switching off his light. “That will do excellently,” he said with an amused smile, placing Mrs. Hermann’s letter on the flap of the instrument cabinet and wandering into the gloomy spaces.
“*Well.* I’ll say good night.”
“Good night,” murmured Miriam.
Leaving the dried instruments in a heap with a wash leather flung over them she gathered up the books, switched the room into darkness, felt its promise of welcome and trotted downstairs through the quiet house. The front door shut quietly on Mr. Hancock as she reached the hall. She flew to get away. In five minutes the books were in the safe and everything locked up. The little mirror on the wall, scarcely lit by the single globe over the desk just directed the angle of her hat and showed the dim strange eager outline of her unknown face. She fled down the hall past Mr. Leyton’s room and the opening to the forgotten basement, between the heavy closed door of Mr. Orly’s room and the quiet scrolled end of the balustrade and past the angle of the high dark clock staring with its unlit face down the length of the hall, between the high oak chest and the flat oak coffer confronting each other in the glooms thrown by Mrs. Orly’s tall narrow striped Oriental curtains; she saw them standing in straight folds, the beautiful height and straightness of their many coloured stripes, as they must have been before the outside stripe of each had been cut and used as a tie-up; and was out beyond the curtains in the brightly lit square facing the door. The light fell on the rich edge of the Turkey carpet and the groove of the bicycle stand. In the corner stood the blue and white pipe, empty of umbrellas. Her hand grasped the machine-turned edge of the small flat circular knob that released the door﻿ ﻿… brahma; that was the word, at last.﻿ ﻿… The door opened and closed with its familiar heavy wooden firmness, neatly, with a little rattle of its chain. Her day scrolled up behind her. She halted, trusted and responsible, for a long second in the light flooding the steps from behind the door.
The pavement was under her feet and the sparsely lamplit night all round her. She restrained her eager steps to a walk. The dark houses and the blackness between the lamps were elastic about her.

===Chinese===

他站起身，关掉他的灯。“这就很好，”他含着一点有趣的笑说，把赫尔曼太太的信搁在器械柜的翻板上，踱进屋子的暗处。
“*好了*。我道晚安了。”
“晚安，”米丽亚姆轻声道。
她把擦干的器械拢成一堆，搭上一块麂皮，收起账簿，把房间掣进黑暗，觉出它的欢迎的许诺，小跑着穿过静静的宅子下楼。她到门厅时，前门在汉考克先生身后轻轻合上。她飞奔着去脱身。五分钟里账簿进了保险柜，一切上了锁。墙上的小镜子，被书桌上方那盏孤灯照得将将可辨，恰好校正了她帽子的角度，照出她那张陌生的脸的朦胧、奇异而热切的轮廓。她飞奔着穿过门厅，经过莱顿先生的房门和那道通往被遗忘的地下室的豁口，夹在奥利先生沉重的关着的门与楼梯栏柱卷花的端头之间，又经过那座高大的黑钟的棱角——它用不亮的钟面顺着门厅长长地望着——再从两具高高的橡木柜中间穿过，它们在奥利太太那几幅又高又窄的条纹东方式帘子投下的幽暗里遥遥相对；她看见它们笔直地垂着，多色条纹那么美的高与直，一如它们未被裁下外侧一条、拿去当捆扎带之前的模样；而后她已在帘子外头、正对大门那方亮堂堂的光里。光照着土耳其地毯厚实的边和自行车架的凹槽。角落里立着蓝白瓷伞桶，空着，没有伞。她的手扣住那只小小的平圆旋钮的机纹棱边，旋钮一旋门就开……brahma；这个词，终于想起来了。……门开了又关，带着它惯常的沉实的木头的坚定，利利索索，链子轻轻一响。她的一天在她身后卷拢。她站住，被信任着、担着责任，在从门后涌下台阶的光里，立了长长的一秒。
人行道在脚下，四周是灯火稀疏的夜。她按住急切的脚步，改成行走。黑暗的房屋和灯与灯之间的黑，在她四周富有弹性。

## IV / 四
===Original===

When she came to herself she was in the Strand. She walked on a little and turned aside to look at a jeweller’s window and consider being in the Strand at night. Most of the shops were still open. The traffic was still in full tide. The jeweller’s window repelled her. It was very yellow with gold, all the objects close together and each one bearing a tiny label with the price. There was a sort of commonness about the Strand, not like the cheerful commonness of Oxford Street, more like the City with its many sudden restaurants. She walked on. But there were theatres also, linking it up with the west-end and streets leading off it where people like Bob Greville had chambers. It was the tailing off of the west-end and the beginning of a deep dark richness that began about Holywell. Mysterious important churches crowded in amongst little brown lanes﻿ ﻿… the little dark brown lane.﻿ ﻿… She wondered what she had been thinking since she left Wimpole Street and whether she had come across Trafalgar Square without seeing it or round by some other way. They were *fighting*; sending out suffocation and misery into the surrounding air﻿ ﻿… she stopped close to the two upright balanced threatening bodies, almost touching them. The men looked at her. “Don’t,” she said imploringly and hurried on trembling.﻿ ﻿… It occurred to her that she had not seen fighting since a day in her childhood when she had wondered at the swaying bodies and sickened at the thud of a fist against a cheek. The feeling was the same today, the longing to explain somehow to the men that they *could* not fight.﻿ ﻿… Half-past seven. Perhaps there would not be an A.B.C. so far down. It would be impossible to get a meal. Perhaps the girls would have some coffee. An A.B.C. appeared suddenly at her side, its panes misty in the cold air. She went confidently in. It seemed nearly full of men. Never mind, city men; with a wisdom of their own which kept them going and did not affect anything, all alike and thinking the same thoughts; far away from anything she thought or knew. She walked confidently down the centre, her plaid-lined golf-cape thrown back her small brown boat-shaped felt hat suddenly hot on her head in the warmth. The shop turned at a right angle showing a large open fire with a fireguard, and a cat sitting on the hearthrug in front of it. She chose a chair at a small table in front of the fire.

===Chinese===

待她回过神来，人已在斯特兰德街（the Strand）。她往前走了一小段，侧过身去端详一家珠宝店的橱窗，琢磨着夜里身在斯特兰德街这件事。店铺大都还开着。车马人流仍是一片涨潮。珠宝店的橱窗叫她反感。满窗的金子黄澄澄的，件件东西挤在一处，每一件都贴着标价的小签。斯特兰德街有一种俗气，不像牛津街那种快活的俗气，倒更像伦敦城，多的是突然冒出来的餐馆。她接着走。可这里也有戏院，把它同西区连起来，还有一些街巷从旁岔出，鲍勃·格雷维尔那样的人就在那些街上设着事务室。这里是西区的尾巴，是一种深沉幽暗的富丽的开头，那富丽从霍利韦尔一带开始。神秘的重要教堂挤在小棕巷中间……那条深棕的小巷。……她纳闷自己离开温波尔街以来都在想些什么，纳闷自己是不是没看见就穿过了特拉法尔加广场，还是绕了别的道。他们*打起来了*；把窒息和痛苦散进四周的空气里……她停在那两具直立的、僵持的、含威吓的身体跟前，几乎碰着他们。男人们望着她。“别打了。”她哀求道，发着抖匆匆走开。……她想起自幼时的一天起她再没见过打架——那天她惊诧于那摇晃的两具身子，一拳砸在腮上的闷响叫她恶心。今天的感觉一样，还是那阵冲动，想没法儿让男人们明白他们*不能*打架。……七点半。再往下去恐怕不会有 A.B.C. 了。吃不上饭了。姑娘们那儿也许有点咖啡。一家 A.B.C. 忽然出现在她身旁，玻璃窗在冷空气里蒙着雾。她自信地走进去。里面几乎坐满了男人。不要紧，伦敦城的男人；自有一套叫日子过得下去、又于世事无碍的聪明，个个一样，想的一样心思；离她想的一切、知道的一切都远。她自信地顺着店堂中间走下去，格子呢衬里的高尔夫斗篷往后一掊，那顶棕色小船形毡帽在暖气里忽然变得烫头。店堂拐了个直角，露出一大炉敞开的火，围着炉栏，炉前的地毯上蹲着一只猫。她挑了炉前一张小桌边的椅子。

===Original===

The velvet settees at the sides of the room were more comfortable. But it was for such a little while tonight and it was not one of her own A.B.C.s. She felt as she sat down as if she were the guest of the city men and ate her boiled egg and roll and butter and drank her small coffee in that spirit-gazing into the fire and thinking her own thoughts unresentful of the uncongenial scraps of talk that now and again penetrated her thoughts; the complacent laughter of the men amazed her; their amazing unconsciousness of the things that were written all over them.
The fire blazed into her face. She dropped her cape over the back of her chair and sat in the glow; the small pat of butter was not enough for the large roll. Pictures came out of the fire, the strange moment in her room, the smashing of the plaque, the lamplit den; Mr. Orly’s song, the strange rich difficult day and now her untouched self here, free, unseen and strong, the strong world of London all round her, strong free untouched people, in a dark lit wilderness happy and miserable in their own way, going about the streets looking at nothing, thinking about no special person or thing, as long as they were there, being in London.
Even the business people who went about intent, going to definite places were in the secret of London and looked free. The expression of the collar and hair of many of them said they had homes. But they got away from them. No one who had never been alone in London was quite alive.﻿ ﻿… I’m free﻿—I’ve got free﻿—nothing can ever alter that she thought, gazing wide-eyed into the fire, between fear and joy. The strange familiar pang gave the place a sort of consecration. A strength was piling up within her. She would go out unregretfully at closing time and up through wonderful unknown streets, not her own streets till she found Holborn and then up and round through the Squares.
---
On the hall table lay a letter﻿ ﻿… from Alma; under the shadow of the bronze soldier leaning on his gun. Miriam gathered it up swiftly. No one knew her here﻿ ﻿… no past and no future﻿ ﻿… coming in and out unknown, in the present secret wonder. Pausing for a moment near the smeary dimly-lit marble slab the letter out of sight she held this consciousness. There was no sound in the house﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

房间两侧的天鹅绒软榻更舒服。可今晚只坐这么一小会儿，况且这不是她自己那些 A.B.C. 之一。她坐下来，觉得自己像是伦敦城男人们的客人，怀着这份心气吃她的煮蛋、奶油面包卷，喝她那一小杯咖啡——望着炉火想自己的心思，对偶尔渗进思绪里的不入耳的谈话碎片也不动气；男人们那洋洋自得的笑声叫她惊奇；他们对写在自己浑身上下的东西那份惊人的浑然不觉。
炉火烧上她的脸。她把斗篷褪到椅背上，坐在红光里；那一小块黄油对付不了大面包卷。画面从炉火里出来：她房间里那奇异的一刻，陶板摔碎，灯下的“窝”；奥利先生的歌，那奇异、丰厚、难挨的一天，而此刻她那个未动的自己在这里，自由，无人看见，强健，四周是伦敦这个强健的世界，强健、自由、未动的人们，在一片亮着灯的黑暗旷野里按自己的方式又快乐又悲苦，走在街上什么也不看，不想任何特定的人或事，只要在那里就好，就在伦敦里。
就连那些行色匆匆、奔着明确去处办事的人，也分享了伦敦的秘密，看上去自由。他们中许多人的领子和头发说明他们有家。可他们从家里逃了出来。谁若不曾独自一人待在伦敦，就还不算完全活着。……我自由了——我挣到自由了——什么也改变不了这个，她瞪大眼睛望着炉火想，介于恐惧与欢喜之间。那阵熟悉的奇异的刺痛给这个地方添了一种祝圣。一种力量在她体内积起来。到打烊的时候她会毫无留恋地走出去，往上穿过奇妙的陌生的街——在找到霍尔本之前还不是她自己的街——然后再往上、绕行过一座座广场。
---
门厅桌上躺着一封信……阿尔玛来的；在那尊拄枪斜倚的青铜士兵的影子里。米丽亚姆一把拾起。这里没人认识她……没有过去，也没有将来……进进出出无人知晓，在眼下的秘密的奇妙里。她在那块油腻的、灯光昏暗的大理石板旁站了一刻，信藏起来，守着这份意识。宅子里没有一点声音……

===Original===

its huge high thick walls held all the lodgers secure and apart, fixed in richly enclosed rooms in the heart of London; secure from all the world that was not London, flying through space, swinging along on a planet spread with continents﻿—Londoners. Alma’s handwriting, the same as it had been at school only a little larger and firmer, broke into that. Of course Alma had answered the postcard﻿ ﻿… it had been an impulse, a cry of triumph after years of groping about. But it was like pulling a string. Silly. And now this had happened. But it was only a touch, only a finger laid on the secret hall table that no one had seen. The letter need not be answered. Out of sight it seemed to have gone away﻿ ﻿… destroyed unopened it would be as if it had never come and everything would be as before.﻿ ﻿… Enough, more than enough without writing to Alma. An evening paper boy was shouting raucously in the distance. The letter-box brought his voice into the hall as he passed the door. Miriam moved on up the many flights.
---
Upstairs she found herself eagerly tearing open the letter.﻿ ﻿… “I’ve just heard from an old schoolfellow,” she heard herself saying to the girls in Kennett Street. There was something exciting in the letter﻿ ﻿… at the end Alma Wilson (officially Mrs. G. Wilson)﻿ ﻿… strange people in the room﻿ ﻿… Alma amongst them; looking out from amongst dreadfulness. *Married.* She had gone in amongst the crowd already﻿—forever. How clever of her﻿ ﻿… deceitful﻿ ﻿… that little spark of Alma in her must have been deceitful﻿ ﻿… sly, at some moment. Alma’s eyes glanced at her with a new more preoccupied and covered look﻿ ﻿… she used to go sometimes to theatres with large parties of people with money and the usual dresses who never thought anything about anything﻿ ﻿… perhaps that was part of the reason, perhaps Alma was more that than she had thought﻿ ﻿… marrying in the sort of way she went to theatre-parties﻿—clever. The letter was full of excitement﻿ ﻿… Alma leaping up from her marriage and clutching at her﻿ ﻿… not really married; dancing to some tune in some usual way like all those women and jumping up in a way that fizzled and could not be kept up.﻿ ﻿…
“You dear old thing!﻿ ﻿… fell out of the sky this morning﻿ ﻿… to fill pages with ‘you dear old thing!’﻿ ﻿… see you at *once*! *Immediately!*﻿ ﻿… come up to town and meet you﻿ ﻿… some sequestered teashop﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

它那高大厚实的墙把所有的房客安稳地、各自隔开，钉在伦敦心脏里一间间四壁丰厚的屋子里；与整个不是伦敦的世界相隔——那世界在太空里飞驰，在铺满大陆的星球上一路荡过去——伦敦人。阿尔玛的字，同上学时一样，只是略大了些、稳了些，闯进这份心思里来。阿尔玛当然回了明信片……那本是一阵冲动，多年摸索之后一声得胜的呼喊。可它像牵动一根线。傻。而如今竟出了这样的事。可它不过一触，不过一根手指搭上了没人看见的秘密的门厅桌。这信可以不回。看不见了，它仿佛已经走远……不拆就销毁，它就等于从没来过，一切照旧。……不写信给阿尔玛也尽够了，绰绰有余。一个卖晚报的孩子在远处嘶哑地叫卖。孩子经过门口时，信箱把他的声音送进门厅。米丽亚姆继续上那一层层楼。
---
到了楼上，她发觉自己正急急地斯开信。……“我刚收到一位老同学的信，”她听见自己对肯尼特街的姑娘们说。信里有一种叫人兴奋的东西……末尾署着阿尔玛·威尔逊（正式名分是 G·威尔逊太太）……房间里那些奇怪的人……阿尔玛在他们中间；从一片可怕里望出来。*结了婚*。她已经走进人堆里去了——永远。她多精明……骗人……她心里那一星阿尔玛的火苗准是骗人的……狡猾，在某个时刻。阿尔玛的眼睛瞥她一眼，带着一种新的、更心事重重、更遮掩的神色……她从前有时跟着一大伙有钱的、穿寻常衣裳、对什么都不想的人去看戏……也许那就是一部分缘故，也许阿尔玛比她想的更是那种人……按她赴戏局子的那种路数结了婚——精明。信里满是兴奋……阿尔玛从她的婚姻里一跃而起，一把抓住她……并非真的结了婚；同所有那些女人一样，照某种惯常的调子跳舞，以一种持续不了、渐渐泄气的方式往上蹦。……
“你这亲爱的老家伙！……今早从天上掉下来……要用‘你这亲爱的老家伙’填满几页纸……*马上*见你！立刻！……进城来会你……哪家幽静的茶室……”

===Original===

our ancient heads together﻿ ﻿… tell you all that has happened to me since those days﻿ ﻿… next Thursday﻿ ﻿… let you know how really really rejoiced I am﻿ ﻿… break the very elderly fact that I am married﻿ ﻿… but that makes no difference.﻿ ﻿…” That would not be so bad﻿—seeing Alma alone in a tea shop in the west end; in a part of the new life, that would be all right; nothing need happen, nothing would be touched, “all I have had the temerity to do﻿ ﻿…” what did that mean?
---
Unpinning the buckram-stiffened black velvet band from her neck, she felt again with a rush of joy that her day was beginning and moved eagerly about amongst the strange angles and shadows of her room, the rich day all about her. Somebody had put up her little varnished oak bookshelf just in the right place, the lower shelf in a line with the little mantelpiece. When the gas bracket was swung out from the wall the naked flame shone on the backs of the indiscriminately arranged books﻿ ﻿… the calf-bound Shakespeare could be read now comfortably in the immense fresh dark night under the gas flame; the Perne’s memorial edition of Tennyson.﻿ ﻿… She washed her face and hands in hard cold water at the little rickety washstand, yellow-grained rich beloved, drying them on the thin holey face towel hurriedly. Lying neatly folded amongst the confusion of oddments in a top drawer was her lace tie. Holding it out to its full length she spread it against her neck, crossed the ends at the back bringing them back round her neck to spread in a narrow flat plastron to her waist, kept in place by a brooch at the top and a pin fastened invisibly halfway down. Her face shone fresh and young above the creamy lace﻿ ﻿… the tie was still fairly new and crisp﻿ ﻿… when it had to be washed it would be limp﻿ ﻿… but it would go on some time just for evenings transforming her harsh black John Doble half guinea costume into evening dress. For some moments she contemplated its pleasant continuous pattern and the way the rounded patterned ends fell just below the belt.﻿ ﻿…
---
The top-floor bell would not ring. After some hesitation Miriam rang the house bell. The door was opened by a woman in a silk petticoat and a dressing jacket. Miriam gazed dumbly into large clear blue eyes gazing at her from a large clean clear fresh face feathered with little soft natural curls, cut out sharply against the dark passage.

===Chinese===

咱们两颗古老的头凑到一处……把那些日子以后我遭遇的一切讲给你……下星期四……让你知道我真是真是多么欢喜……宣布一件非常老成的事实：我结了婚……可这毫无分别。……”这样倒还不坏——在西区一家茶室里单独见阿尔玛；在新生活的一隅，那没什么不行；什么也不会发生，什么也不会被碰着，“我斗胆做下的这一切……”那是什么意思？
---
她从颈间解下那条硬衬的黑丝绒饰带，又一次怀着一阵欢喜觉出她的一天正在开始，急切地在房间那些奇怪的角度和阴影间走来走去，丰厚的一天围着她。有人把她那小小的亮漆橡木书架钉在了恰恰好的位置，下层搁板同小壁炉架齐平。煤气灯架从墙边旋出来时，赤裸的火苗照在胡乱排列的书脊上……犊皮面的莎士比亚这会儿可以在煤气灯下、在浩大新鲜的夜里舒舒服服读了；珀恩纪念版丁尼生。……她在小歪脸盆架上用又冷又硬的水洗脸洗手——黄木纹的、丰厚的、心爱的——匆匆在那条薄薄的、有洞的洗脸毛巾上擦干。上层抽屉的杂物堆里，整整齐齐叠着她的蕾丝领带。她把它拉直了全长，贴着颈子铺开，两端在颈后交叉再绕回颈前，摊成窄窄一片胸衣直抵腰际，上头一枚胸针、半腰一根暗别针固定。她的脸在乳白蕾丝上方清新发亮……领带还相当新、挺括……等要洗的时候它就软塌了……可它还能撑一阵，专在晚上用，把她那身约翰·多布尔半几尼的黑粗呢衣裳点化成晚装。好一阵她端详它那宜人的连绵的花样，看那圆圆的花纹端角恰好垂在腰带下面。……
---
顶楼的门铃不响。迟疑了一下，米丽亚姆按了总铃。开门的女人穿着丝衬裙和家常短褂。米丽亚姆哑然望进一双又大又清亮的蓝眼睛，它们从一张宽大、干净、清朗、鲜妍的脸上望着她，四围覆着一圈柔软的天然小卷发，衬着暗暗的过道轮廓分明。

===Original===

“Are you for the top?” enquired the woman in a smooth serene sleepy voice.
“Yes,” announced Miriam eagerly coming in and closing the door, her ears straining to catch the placid words spoken by the woman as she disappeared softly into a softly-lit room. She went tremulously up the dark stairs into a thick stale odour of rancid fried grease and on towards a light that glimmered from the topmost short flight of steep uncarpeted winding stairs. “They’re in,” said her thoughts with a quick warm leap. “Hullo,” she asserted, ascending the stairs.
“Hullo,” came in response a quick challenging voice﻿ ﻿… a soft clear reed-like happy ring that Miriam felt to her knees while her happy feet stumbled on.
“Is that the Henderson?”
“It’s me,” said Miriam emerging on a tiny landing and going through the open door of a low-ceiled lamplit room. “It’s me, it’s me,” she repeated from the middle of the floor. An eager face was turned towards her from a thicket of soft dull wavy hair. She gazed vaguely. The small slippered feet planted firmly high up against the lintel the sweep of the red dressing-gown, the black patch of the Mudie book with its yellow label, the small ringed hand upon it, the outflung arm and hand, the little wreath of smoke about the end of the freshly lit cigarette, the cup of coffee on the little table under the lamp, the dim shapes about the room lit by the flickering blaze.﻿ ﻿…
Miriam smiled into the smiling steel blue of the eyes turned towards her and waited smiling for the silver reed of tone to break again. “I’m so glad you’ve come. I wanted you. Sit down and shut the door my child.﻿ ﻿… I don’t mind which you do first, but﻿—do﻿—them﻿—both,” she tinkled, stretching luxuriously and bringing her feet to the ground with a swing. Miriam closed the door. “Can I take off my things?”
“Of course child﻿ ﻿… take them all off; you know I admire you most draped in a towel.”
“I’ve got such awful feet,” said Miriam hugging the compliment as she dropped her things in a distant armchair.
“It’s not your feet, it’s your extraordinary shoes.”
“M.”
“How beautiful you look. You put on ties better than anyone I know. I wish I could wear things draped round my neck.”
Miriam sat down in the opposite wicker chair.
“Isn’t it cold﻿—my feet are freezing; it’s raining.”
“Take off your shoes.”
Miriam got off her shoes and propped them in the fender to dry.
“What is that book?”

===Chinese===

“你是找顶层？”女人用一种平滑、安详、睡意未消的嗓音问。
“是，”米丽亚姆热切地应道，进来带上门，耳朵紧张地捕捉女人消失在柔光房里时留下的几句平静话音。她哆嗦着摸黑上楼，一股陈腐的油煎哈喇味浓浓的，一路朝顶上那截没有地毯的陡陡旋梯透出的一点微光走去。“她们在家。”她的念头热热地一跳。“喂，”她壮着声上楼。
“喂，”应她的是一个干脆挑战的嗓音……一种清柔如簧、快活的鸣响，米丽亚姆一直感觉到膝盖里去，一双欢喜的脚磕磕绊绊往上走。
“是亨德森吗？”
“是我，”米丽亚姆说着登上一个小平台，走进那间天花板低低的、点着灯的房间的敞门。“是我，是我。”她在地板中央又重复了一遍。一张急切的脸从一蓬柔软的深色波发里转向她。她恍惚地望着。两只小小的套着拖鞋的脚高高抵着门楣立定，红晨衣的一摆，穆迪的书那一方黑、黄标签，搭在上面的戴着戒指的小手，伸展的胳膊和手，新点的烟卷一端缭绕的一小圈烟，灯下小桌上那杯咖啡，房间里被跳动的炉光照亮的一团团暗影。……
米丽亚姆迎着转向她的、含笑的钢蓝色眼睛微笑，笑着等那银簧般的嗓音再度响起。“你来了我真高兴。我正需要你。坐下，关上门，我的孩子。……先做哪样我都不介意，可是——两样——都要——做。”她叮咚响着，舒舒服服伸了个懒腰，双脚一荡落地。米丽亚姆关上门。“我能脱了外衣吗？”
“当然，孩子……都脱了；你知道你裹条毛巾的时候我最赏识你。”
“我的脚难看死了，”米丽亚姆说，把这句恭维揣进怀里，边把外衣丢到远处一把扶手椅上。
“不是你的脚，是你那双非凡的鞋。”
“嗯。”
“你多好看。你系领带比我知道的谁都好。但愿我也能往脖子上围点什么。”
米丽亚姆在对面的柳条椅里坐下。
“冷不冷——我的脚都冻僵了；在下雨。”
“把鞋脱了。”
米丽亚姆脱下鞋，支在炉栏边烤。
“那是什么书？”

===Original===

“Eden Philpotts’s *Children of the Mist*,” fluted the voice reverently. “Read it?”
“No,” said Miriam expectantly.
The eager face turned to an eager profile with eyes brooding into the fire. “He’s so wonderful,” mused the voice and Miriam watched eagerly. Mag read books﻿—for their own sake; and could judge them and compare them with other books by the same author﻿ ﻿… but all this wonderful knowledge made her seem wistful; knowing all about books and plays and strangely wistful and regretful; the things that made her eyes blaze and made her talk reverently or in indignant defence always seemed sad in the end﻿ ﻿… wistful hero worship﻿ ﻿… raving about certain writers and actors as if she did not know they were people.
“He’s so wonderful,” went on the voice with its perpetual modulations “he gets all the atmosphere of the west country﻿—perfectly. You *live* there while you’re reading him.”
With a little chill sense of Mag in this wonderful room alone, living in the west country and herself coming in as an interruption, Miriam noted the name of the novelist in her mind﻿ ﻿… there was something about it, she knew she would not forget it; soft and numb with a slight clatter and hiss at the end, a rainstorm, the atmosphere of Devonshire and the mill-wheel.
“Devonshire people are all consumptive,” she said decisively.
“Are they?”
“Yes, it’s the mild damp air. They have lovely complexions; like the Irish. There must be any amount of consumption in Ireland.”
“I suppose there is.”
Miriam sat silent and still watching Mag’s movements as she sipped and puffed, so strangely easy and so strangely wistful in her wonderful rich Bloomsbury life﻿—and waiting for her next remark.
“You look very happy tonight child; what have you been doing?”
“Nothing.”
“You look as happy as a bird.”
“Are birds happy?”
“Of *course* birds are happy.”
“Well﻿—they prey on each other﻿—and they’re often frightened.”
“How wise we are.”
Brisk steps sounded on the little stairs.
“Tell me what you have been doing.”
“Oh. I don’t know. Weird things have been happening.﻿ ﻿… Oh, weird things.”
“Tell your aunt at once.” Mag gathered herself together as the brisk footsteps came into the room. “Hoh,” said a strong resonant voice, “it’s the Henderson. I thought as much.”
“Yes. Doesn’t she look pretty?”
“Yes﻿—she has a beautiful lace tie.”
“I wish I could wear things like that round my neck, don’t you von Bohlen?”

===Chinese===

“伊登·菲尔波茨的《雾之子》，”那嗓音恭恭敬敬地吹道。“读过吗？”
“没有，”米丽亚姆期待地说。
那张急切的脸转向急切的侧影，眼睛沉思地探进炉火。“他真是了不起，”那声音冥想着说，米丽亚姆急切地望着。玛格（Mag）读书——为书本身而读；还能品评它们、把它们同同一作者的其他书比较……可这一切了不起的学问使她显得怅惘；对书啊戏啊无所不知，却又奇怪地怅惘、惋惜；那些叫她眼睛发亮、叫她出言虔敬或愤而辩护的东西，到头来似乎总是悲哀的……怅惘的英雄崇拜……对某些作家和演员赞不绝口，仿佛不知道他们也是人。
“他真是了不起，”那声音带着它永不停歇的抑扬说下去，“他抓住了西部乡野的全部气息——完完全全。读他的时候你就*生活*在那儿。”
想到玛格独自在这间了不起的房里、生活在西部乡野、自己闯进来是个打扰，米丽亚姆心头掠过一丝凉意，把小说家的名字记在心里……这名字有点什么，她知道自己忘不了它；柔软而钝重，末梢带一点轻响和嘶声，一场雷雨，德文郡的空气和水车轮。
“德文郡人都害痨病，”她断然说。
“是吗？”
“是，就是那温和潮湿的空气。他们肤色都可爱；像爱尔兰人。爱尔兰的痨病准多得不得了。”
“我猜也是。”
米丽亚姆静坐不动，望着玛格且啜且吸的一举一动——在她那丰美的布卢姆斯伯里生活里那么奇怪地安逸，又那么奇怪地怅惘——等她的下一句话。
“今晚你看起来很快活，孩子；你干什么来着？”
“没干什么。”
“你快活得像只鸟。”
“鸟快活吗？”
“鸟*当然*快活。”
“唔——它们互相捕食——还常常受惊。”
“我们多智慧。”
小楼梯上响起一阵轻快的脚步。
“告诉我你干什么来着。”
“哦。我说不上。怪事一直在发生。……哦，怪事。”
“马上告诉你姑妈。”玛格打叠起精神，轻快的脚步已进屋。“嚯，”一个洪亮的嗓门说，“是亨德森。我猜着了。”
“是。她好看吗？”
“好看——她有漂亮的蕾丝领带。”
“但愿我也能往脖子上围那样的东西，你说呢，冯·博伦？”

===Original===

“I *do*. She can stick *anything* round her neck﻿—and look nice.”
“Anything; a garter or a﻿—a *kipper*.﻿ ﻿…”
“Don’t be so cracked.”
“She says weird things have been happening to her. I say I didn’t make any coffee for you and the spirit lamp wants filling.”
“Damn you﻿—*Schweinhund﻿—verfluchte Schweinhund*.”
Miriam had been gazing at the strong square figure in the short round fur-lined cloak and sweeping velvet hat, the firm decisive movements and imagining the delicate pointed high-heeled shoes. Presently those things would be off and the door closed on the three of them.
“There’s some Bass.”
“I’m going to have some *suppe*. Have some *suppe*, Henderson.”
“*Non, merci.*”
“She’s proud. Bring her some. What did you have for supper, child?”
“Oh, we had an enormous lunch. They’d had a dinner-party.”
“What did you have for supper?”
“Oh lots of things.”
“Bring her some *suppe*. I’m not sure I won’t have a basin myself.”
“All right. I’ll put some on.” The brisk steps went off and a voice hummed in and out of the other rooms.
Watching Mag stirring the fire, giving a last pull at her cigarette end and pushing back the hair from her face﻿ ﻿… silent and old and ravaged, and young and animated and powerful, Miriam blushed and beamed silently at her reiterated demands for an account of herself.
“I say I saw an extraordinary woman downstairs.”
Mag turned sharply and put down the poker.
“Yes?”
“In a petticoat.”
“Frederika Elizabeth! She’s seen the Pierson!”
“Hoh! Has she?” The brisk footsteps approached and the door was closed. The dimly shining mysteries of the room moved about Miriam, the outside darkness flowing up to the windows moved away as the tall dressing-gowned figure lowered the thin drab loosely rattling Venetian blinds; the light seemed to go up and distant objects became more visible; the crowded bookshelf, the dark littered table under it, the empty table pushed against the wall near the window﻿—the bamboo bookshelf between the windows above a square mystery draped to the ground with a table cover﻿—the little sofa behind Mag’s chair, the little pictures, cattle gazing out across a bridge of snow, cattish complacent sweepy women. Albert﻿ ﻿… ? Moore?

===Chinese===

“我*但愿*。她脖子上围*什么*都行——都好看。”
“什么都行；吊袜带，或者一条——一条*熏鲱*。…”
“别这么疯。”
“她说怪事一直在她身上发生。我说我没给你煮咖啡，酒精灯也该添了。”
“该死的——*Schweinhund——verfluchte Schweinhund*（猪猡——天杀的猪猡）。”
米丽亚姆一直望着那裹在短短的圆毛里斗篷和高高扫过的丝绒帽里的、结实方正的身影，那利落果断的动作，想象着那双尖头细高跟的鞋。过一会儿这些东西都要脱下来，门要在他们三人身上关拢。
“还有巴斯啤酒。”
“我要来点*汤*。来点*汤*吧，亨德森。”
“*Non, merci*（不，谢谢）。”
“她骄傲。给她端点来。你晚饭吃的什么，孩子？”
“哦，我们午饭吃得可多了。他们头天请了客。”
“你晚饭吃的什么？”
“哦，好些东西。”
“给她端*汤*来。说不定我自己也要来一盆。”
“好。我去热。”轻快的脚步走开，一个嗓音在别的房间里进进出出地哼着。
望着玛格拨着炉火，最后吸一口烟卷、把头发从脸上撞回去……又沉默又苍老又憔悴，又年轻又生动又有力，米丽亚姆红着脸，对她三番五次要她交代自己行踪的要求默默含笑。
“我说，我在楼下看见一个非凡的女人。”
玛格霍地转头，放下火钳。
“是吗？”
“穿着衬裙。”
“弗蕾德里卡·伊丽莎白！她看见皮尔森了！”
“嚯！是吗？”轻快的脚步逼近，门关上了。房间里那些幽幽发亮的神秘围着米丽亚姆挪动，涌到窗前的屋外黑暗退了开去，那裹着晨衣的高高身影放下薄薄的、灰扑扑、松松作响的软百叶帘；灯光仿佛升高了，远处的东西看得更清了：挤挤的书架，架下堆满东西的暗色桌子，推到窗边墙根的空桌——两窗之间的竹架，架下罩着垂到地面的桌布的一团方方正正的神秘——玛格椅后的小沙发，那些小画，隔着雪桥望出来的牛，猫一样自得、裙裾曳扫的女人。阿尔伯特……？摩尔？

===Original===

the framed photographs of Dickens and Irving, the litter on the serge draped mantelpiece in front of the mirror of the bamboo overmantel, silver candlesticks, photographs of German women and Canon Wilberforce﻿ ﻿… all the riches of comfortable life.
“You are late.”
“Yes I am fear‑fully late.”
“Why are you late Frederika Elizabeth von Bohlen?”
The powerful rounded square figure was in the leather armchair opposite the blaze, strongly moulded brown knickered black stockinged legs comfortably crossed stuck firmly out between the heavy soft folds of a grey flannel dressing gown. The shoes had gone, grey woollen bedroom slippers blurred all but the shapely small ankles. Mag was lighting another cigarette, von Bohlen was not doing needlework, the room settled suddenly to its best rich exciting blur.
“Tonight I must smoke or die.”
“*Must* you, my dear.”
“Why.”
“To-*nate*﻿—a, ay must smoke﻿—a, or *daye*.”
“*Es ist bestimmt, in Gottes Rath.*”
“Tell us what you think of the Pierson, child.”
“She was awfully nice. Is it your landlady?”
“Yes﻿—isn’t she nice? We think she’s extraordinary﻿—all things considered. You know we hadn’t the least idea what she was when we came here.”
“What is she?”
“Well﻿—er﻿—you embarrass me, child, how shall we put it to her, Jan?”
“D’you mean to say she’s improper?”
“Yes﻿—she’s improper. We hadn’t the faintest notion of it when we came.”
“How extraordinary.”
“It is extraordinary. We’re living in an improper house﻿—the whole street’s improper we’re discovering.”
“How absolutely awful.”
“*Now* we know why Mother Cosway hinted when we left her to come here that we wanted to be free for devil’s mirth.”
“How did you find out?”
“Henriette told us; you see she works for the Pierson.”
“What did she tell you?”
“Well﻿—she told us.”
“Six”﻿—laughed Mag, quoting towards Jan.
“Six,” trumpeted Jan, “and if not six, seven.”
They both laughed.
“In one evening,” trumpeted Jan.
“I say are you going to leave?” The thought of the improper street was terrible and horrible; but they might go right away to some other part of London. Mag answered instantly but the interval had seemed long and Miriam was cold with anxiety.
“No; we don’t see why we should.”
Miriam gazed dumbly from one to the other, finding herself admiring and wondering more than ever at their independence and strength.
“You see the woman’s so absolutely self-respecting.”
“Much more so than we are!”

===Chinese===

装框的狄更斯和欧文相片，镜前竹制高架上那面罩着粗咇叽的壁炉台上摆的杂物，银烛台，德国女人们和威尔伯福斯教士的照片……舒适生活的全部富足。
“你来晚了。”
“是，我晚——得可怕。”
“你为什么来晚了，弗蕾德里卡·伊丽莎白·冯·博伦？”
那副结实的、滚圆的、方正的身影坐在炉火对面的皮扶手椅里，绷着灯笼裤、黑长袜的棕色腿壮实好看，舒服地交叠着，从灰色法兰绒晨衣厚软的衣褶间稳稳伸出来。鞋脱了，灰色羊毛卧室拖鞋糊住了那一双秀气的小脚踝以外的一切。玛格正点另一支烟卷，冯·博伦没在做针线，房间忽然沉进它最好的、丰厚撩人的朦胧里。
“今晚我不抽烟就死。”
“非抽不可吗，亲爱的。”
“为什么。”
“今——晚——啊，我必——须抽——啊，不然就——死。”
“*Es ist bestimmt, in Gottes Rath*（这是定数，在上帝的旨意里）。”
“跟我们说说你觉得皮尔森怎么样，孩子。”
“她好极了。是你们的房东太太？”
“是——她好吧？我们觉得她非凡——各方面看都是。你知道，我们搬来的时候完全不知道她是什么人。”
“她是什么人？”
“这个——呃——你把我问住了，孩子，咱们怎么跟她说，扬？”
“你是说人不正经？”
“是——她不正经。我们来的时候一点也没想到。”
“多奇怪。”
“是奇怪。我们住在一所不正经的房子里——整条街都不正经，我们正在发现。”
“那可真是糟透了。”
“*这下*我们懂了，为什么离开科斯韦妈妈上这儿来的时候，她话里有话，说我们想要自由自在地寻鬼乐。”
“你们怎么发现的？”
“亨丽埃特告诉我们的；你瞧，她替皮尔森干活。”
“她怎么说的？”
“这个——她跟我们说了。”
“‘六个’”——玛格笑着，朝扬引用。
“六个，”扬大声宣布，“没有六个，也有七个。”
两人都笑了。
“一晚上，”扬又大声宣布。
“我说，你们要搬走吗？”不正经的街道这念头又可怕又吓人；可她们也可能一走了之，搬到伦敦别的什么地方去。玛格立刻答了，可那一瞬显得很长，米丽亚姆焦虑得发冷。
“不搬；我们看不出为什么要搬。”
米丽亚姆哑然望望这个又望望那个，比以往任何时候更叹服她们的独立与力量。
“你瞧，那女人自尊得了不得。”
“比我们自尊得多！”

“Out of doors she’s a model of decorum and good style.”
“We’re ashamed when we meet her.”
“We are. We skip into the gutter.”
“We babble and slink!”
“Indoors she’s a perfect landlady. She’s been awfully good to us.”
“A perfect brick!”
“She doesn’t drink; she’s most exquisitely clean. There’s nothing whatever to﻿—to indicate the er﻿—nature of her profession.”
“Except that she sits at the window.”
“But she does not tire her hair and look forth.”
“Or fifth.”
“*Fool.*”
Miriam giggled.
“Really Miriam she *is* rather wonderful you know. We like her.”
“Henriette is devoted to her.”
“And so apparently is her husband.”
“Her *husband*?”
“Yes﻿—she has a husband﻿—he appears at rare intervals﻿—and a little girl at boarding school. She goes to see her but the child never comes here. She tells us quite frankly that she wants to keep her out of harm’s way.”
“How amazing!”
“Yes, she’s extraordinary. She’s Eurasian. She was born in India.”
“That accounts for a good deal. Eurasians are awful; they’ve got all the faults of both sides.”
“East is East and West is West and never the two shall meet.”
“Well, we like her.”
“So we have decided to ignore her little peccadilloes.”
“I don’t see that it’s our business. Frankly I can’t see that it has anything whatever to do with us. Do you?”
“Well I don’t know; I don’t suppose it has really.”
“What would you do in our place?”
“I don’t know﻿ ﻿… I don’t believe I should have found out.”
“I don’t believe you would; but if you had?”
“I think I should have been awfully scared.”
“You would have been afraid that the sixth.”
“Or the seventh.”
“Might have wandered upstairs.”
“No; I mean the whole idea.”
“Oh; the idea.﻿ ﻿…”
“London, my dear Miriam, is full of ideas.”
“I will go and get the *suppe*.”
Jan rose; her bright head and grey shoulders went up above the lamplight, darkening to steady massive outlines, strongly moving as she padded and fluttered briskly out of the room.
The rich blur of the room free of the troubling talk and the swift conversational movements of the two, lifted and was touched with a faint grey, a suggestion of dawn or twilight, as if coming from the hidden windows. Mag sat motionless in her chair, gazing into the fire.
“… Wise and happy infant, I want to ask your opinion.”
Miriam roused herself and glanced steadily across. The outlines of things grew sharp.

===Chinese===

“出了门，她是端整和风度的模范。”
“我们碰见她都害臊。”
“可不。我们一跳跳进阴沟。”
“我们叽叽喳喳、缩头缩脑！”

“她不喝酒；她干净得精致极了。什么也没有——丝毫看不出她那——呃——职业的性质。”

“可她并不描头发朝外张望。”

“*傻瓜*。”
米丽亚姆咯咯笑。
“真的，米丽亚姆，她确实相当了不起，你知道。我们喜欢她。”

“有——她有个丈夫——隔许久露一回面——还有个住寄宿学校的小女孩。她去看她，可孩子从不上这儿来。她相当坦白地告诉我们，她要让孩子远离是非。”
“真不可思议！”
“是，她非凡。她是个欧亚混血。生在印度。”
“这就解释了不少。欧亚混血糟透了；两边的毛病全占了。”
“东方是东方，西方是西方，两者永不能相合。”
“嗯，我们喜欢她。”
“所以我们决定不计较她那些小过犯。”
“我不觉得这是我们的事。坦白说，我看这同我们毫无关系。你呢？”
“嗯，我说不好；我想其实没有。”
“换作你们，你们怎么办？”
“我不知道……我相信我根本不会发现。”
“我相信你不会；可要是发现了呢？”
“我想我会吓坏的。”
“你会怕那第六个。”
“或者第七个。”

“不；我是说整个这件事。”
“哦；这件事。…”
“伦敦啊，亲爱的米丽亚姆，到处都是‘这件事’。”
“我去端*汤*来。”
扬起身；她那明亮的头颅和灰色的肩膀升到灯光之上，暗成沉稳厚重的轮廓，一面轻快地扑扑走出房间，那轮廓有力地动着。
这房间丰厚的朦胧，摆脱了扰人的谈话和两人敏捷的谈锋，浮起来，染上淡淡一层灰，一点黎明或薄暮的意味，仿佛来自那几扇藏起来的窗。玛格一动不动坐在椅里，凝望炉火。
“……又聪明又快活的小娃娃，我要征求你的意见。”
米丽亚姆回过神，定定地望过去。万物的轮廓清晰起来。

She could imagine the room in daylight and felt a faint sharp sinking; hungry.
“I’m going to state you a case. I think you have an extraordinarily sharp sense of right and wrong.”
“Oh *no*.”

“Go on.”
“Imagine a woman engaged to a man. Imagine her allowing﻿—another man﻿—to kiss her.”
Miriam sat thinking. She imagined the two, the snatched caress, the other man alone and unconscious.
“Would you call that treachery to the other person?”
“It would depend upon which she liked best.”
“That’s just the difficulty.”
“*Oh.* That’s awful.”
“Don’t you think a kiss, just a kiss﻿—might be﻿—well﻿—neither here nor there.”
“Well, if it’s nothing, there’s nothing in the whole thing. If there *is* anything﻿—you can’t talk about just kisses.”
“Dreadful Miriam.”
“Do you believe in blunted sensibilities?” How funny that Mag should have led up to that new phrase﻿ ﻿… but this was a case.
“You mean﻿—”
“Whether if a sensibility is blunted it can ever grow sharp again.”
“No. I suppose that’s it. How can it?”
“I don’t know. I’m not sure. It’s a perfectly awful idea, I think.”
“It is awful﻿—because we are all blunting our sensibilities all the time﻿—are we not?”
“That’s just it﻿—whether we ought.”
“Does one always know?”
“Don’t you think so? There’s a feeling. Yes I think one always knows.”
“Suppe, children.”
Miriam took her bowl with eager embarrassment﻿ ﻿… the sugar-basin, the pudding basin and the slop bowl together on a tray, the quickly produced soup﻿—the wonderful rich life the girls lived in their glowing rooms﻿—each room with a different glow.﻿ ﻿… Jan’s narrow green clean room with its suite and hair brushes and cosmetics and pictures of Christ, Mag’s crowded shadowy little square, its litter and its many photographs, their eiderdowns and baths and hot water bottles; the kitchen alive with eyes and foreheads﻿—musicians, artists philosophers pasted on the walls﻿ ﻿… why? Why?﻿ ﻿… Jan with wonderful easy knowledge of the world’s great people﻿ ﻿… and strange curious intimate liking for them﻿ ﻿… the sad separate effect of all those engraved faces﻿ ﻿… the perfectly beautiful blur they made all together in patches on the walls﻿ ﻿… the sitting room, Mag, nearly all Mag, except the photographs on the mantelpiece﻿ ﻿… the whole rooms from the top of the stairs﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她能想象这间屋子在白天的样子，感到一阵轻微尖锐的下沉；饿了。
“我要给你陈述一个案子。我想你有一种非凡敏锐的是非感。”
“哦*不*。”
“你有一种非凡敏锐的是非感。设想一个女人。你能设想一个女人吗？”
“说下去。”
“设想一个女人同一个月人订了婚。设想她允许——另一个男人——吻她。”
米丽亚姆坐着想。她想象那两个人，那偷来的一吻，那另一个男人，独自一人，毫不知情。
“你会管这个叫对另一个人的背叛吗？”
“那要看她最喜欢哪一个。”
“难就难在这里。”
“*哦*。那糟了。”
“你不觉得一个吻，就一个吻——也许——唔——无足重轻？”
“唔，如果它什么都不是，那整件事就什么都不是。如果*真*有什么——那就不能只谈吻不吻的。”
“可怕，米丽亚姆。”
“你相信感觉会磨钝吗？”多有趣，玛格竟引到了这个新词……可这是一桩案子。
“你是说——”
“一种感觉磨钝了，还能不能再锋利起来。”
“不能。我看就是这样。怎么能呢？”
“我不知道。我不敢肯定。我觉得这是个糟透了的念头。”
“是糟——因为我们一直都在磨钝自己的感觉——不是吗？”
“症结就在这儿——我们究竟该不该。”
“人总是知道吗？”
“你不这样想吗？有一种感觉的。是，我想人总是知道。”
“汤来啦，孩子们。”
米丽亚姆带着热切的窘迫接过她的碗……糖盆、布丁盆和泔水碗一起摆在托盘上，飞快端出来的汤——姑娘们在她们暖光融融的房间里过的那种美妙丰厚的生活——每个房间一种不同的光。……扬那间窄窄的、绿色的、洁净的房间，一整套家具、发刷、脂粉和基督像；玛格那间拥挤幽暗的小方屋，它的凌乱和它成堆的照片；她们的鸭绒被、澡盆、汤婆子；厨房四壁贴满活生生的眼睛和额头——音乐家、艺术家、哲学家……为什么？为什么？……扬对世上大人物有一种轻松非凡的熟稔……对他们又有一种古怪亲昵的喜爱……那一幅幅镌版的面孔各自散出悲哀的意味……它们一团团贴在墙上，合成一片美妙的朦胧……起坐间，玛格的，几乎全是玛格，除了壁炉台上的照片……整间整间的屋子，从楼梯顶上望下去……

her thoughts folded down; they were not going away; not; that was certain.

“Was she your favourite schoolfellow?”
Miriam’s jarred mind worked eagerly. The girls thought this was a revival of some great school friendship﻿ ﻿… they would not be in the least jealous; they were curious and interested, but they must understand﻿ ﻿… they must realise that Alma was wonderful﻿ ﻿… something to be proud of﻿ ﻿… in the strange difficult scientific way; something they knew hardly anything about. 她的念头折拢了；它们不走；不走；这是肯定的。

“不，说真的。”

“房间使用费两几尼。”
“知性交谈——”
“一镑十一先令三便士。”
米丽亚姆投去欢快钦慕的目光，放声大笑。玛格那副“这么点事就把孩子逗成这样”的神色一点也没减损她欢快的喜悦。她们的妙语——或者该说幽默？——总带来同样快活的震动……她们真逗；里头有一个秘密。
“这汤好极了。”
“脱水——”
“一便士一包。”
“豌豆粉勾的芡。”

---
“她是你最喜欢的同学吗？”
米丽亚姆受了震动的心思急急转动。姑娘们以为这是某段伟大同窗友谊的复活……她们一点也不会妒忌；她们好奇、感兴趣，可她们必须明白……必须认识到阿尔玛是了不起的……是件足以自豪的事……在那种奇异、艰深的科学路数上；这种路数她们几乎一无所知。玛格几乎全然不知，扬也只在她那整饬宽博的教养里知道个大概；绝不是阿尔玛那种路数——刻板、虔敬、事事亲身关切，以至于看待事物的任何别种方式都叫她生气。可她们必须明白，必须用什么十分可靠的办法赶快让她们同时明白：阿尔玛在外……是额外的……一种古怪、明亮、遥远的资源，同这美妙的当下毫无关系……伦敦的生活是神圣而秘密的，同世上其余一切隔绝。人若在外有了牵挂，它就会消失……除了假日和周末那些她们三人共有、从外面带回来谈论的东西之外，什么都添不得。这个前提说清楚了，去见阿尔玛就是又容易又刺激的事，回来还可以讲给姑娘们听。
“我觉得不是。”
两人都抬起头，用她们那种敏捷的方式一激灵，等着。
米丽亚姆不安地动了动头。这叫人痛苦。她们在用一种语言……麻烦就在这儿……你最喜欢的花……你最喜欢的颜色……这正是填忏悔册时那种痛苦。这段谈话转眼就要结束了。

她的恼火会把它掐断，她们会不明所以地把它收起来，玛格会转到别的事情上去。

玛格坐在椅子前沿，胳膊肘支在膝上，两只小手朝空中支棱着。一阵舒服的、叮咚的轻笑摇着她的肩膀。米丽亚姆挣扎着往外拽。

“哦——她结了婚。…”

“她多大？”
“跟我差不多。”
“哦——九岁上下；那么早就开始受庇护的生活，可不早。”
“受庇护的生活开始得再早也不嫌早；只要打算开始。”
“干吗要开始呢，扬？”
“呃，我亲爱的小米丽亚姆，我觉得受庇护的生活大有可说之处。”

这么说玛格是打算永远这样下去……哦，好，好……身边有好几个人惦着她……她的婚约该是多古怪的一桩烦恼……不相干……何况她还有她那套忠诚的观念。“你说是不是哇？”恼人——她干吗要这样——这是什么——不是乡音——是一种做作，仿佛在台上。听着干脆要紧——“哇”——仿佛她的心思已经走远，嘴唇还在敷衍应酬。随她们去、由它去不好吗……总有一样东西在外面等着，总在应酬的聒噪外头等着的东西。

===Original===

“I can’t imagine anything more awful than what you call the sheltered life,” said Miriam with a little pain in her forehead. Perhaps they would laugh and that would finish it and something would begin.
“For us yes. Imagine either of us coming down to it in the morning; the regular breakfast table, the steaming coffee, the dashes of rishers﻿ ﻿… dishers of rashes I mean, the eggs.﻿ ﻿…”
“You are alluding I presume to the beggs and acon.”
“Precisely. We should die.”
“Of boredom.”
“Imagine not being able to turn up on Sunday morning in your knickers with your hair down.”
“I love Sundays. That first cigarette over the *Referee*.”
“Is like nothing on earth.”
“Or in heaven.”
“Well, or in heaven.”
“The first cigarette anyhow, with or without the *Referee*. It’s just pure absolute bliss that first bit of Sunday morning; complete well-being and happiness.”
“While the sheltered people are flushed with breakfast table-talk﻿—”
“Or awkward silences.”
“The deep damned silence of disillusionment.”
“And thinking about getting ready for church.”
“The men smoke.”
“Stealthily and sleepily in armchairs like cats﻿—ever seen a cat smoke?﻿—like cats﻿—with the wife or somebody they are tired of talking to on the doormat﻿—as it were﻿—tentatively, I speak *ten*tatively﻿ ﻿… in a dead-alley﻿—Dedale﻿—Dedalus﻿—coming into the room any minute in Sunday clothes﻿—”
“To stand on the hearthrug.”
“No hanging about the room. If there’s any hearthrug standing it’s the men who do it, smoking blissfully alone, and trying to look weary and wise and important if anyone comes in.”
“Like Cabinet ministers?”
“Yes; when they are really﻿—er.”
“Cabinets.”
“Footstools; office stools; you never saw a sheltered woman venture on to the hearthrug except for a second if she’s shortsighted to look at the clock.” Miriam sprang to the hearthrug and waved her cigarette. “Con‑fu‑sion to the sheltered life!” The vast open of London swung, welcoming, before her eyes.
“*Hoch! Hoch!*”
“Banzai!”
“We certainly have our compensations.”
“Com‑pen‑*sa*‑tions?”
“Well﻿—for all the things we have to give up.”
“What things?”
“The things that belong to us. To our youth. Tennis, dancing﻿—er, irresponsibility in general.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“我想不出还有什么比你说的受庇护的生活更可怕的了，”米丽亚姆说，额角微微作痛。也许她们会笑，那样这事就完了，什么新的就要开始了。
“对我们是可怕。想想我们俩谁早上过那种日子；按时开的早饭桌，腾着热气的咖啡，几碟——碟几熏肉我是说，还有鸡蛋。…”
“您指的是‘蛋和熏肉’吧。”
“正是。我们会死掉的。”
“无聊死。”
“想想星期天早上不能穿着灯笼裤、披头散发地出现。”
“我爱星期天。就着《裁判》报的第一支烟。”
“人间无比。”
“天上也无比。”
“唔，天上也无比。”
“反正那第一支烟，有《裁判》报也罢没有也罢。星期天早上头一小会儿是纯粹的、绝对的至福；圆满的安康与幸福。”
“而受庇护的人们正让早饭桌上的谈话熏红了脸——”
“或是尴尬的沉默。”
“幻灭的深深的、该死的沉默。”
“一面惦记着打扮上教堂。”
“男人们抽烟。”
“在扶手椅里偷偷地、瞌睡沉沉地，像猫——见过猫抽烟吗？——像猫——跟那个说腻了的老婆或者随便谁一起蹲在门垫上——不妨说——试探性地，我说的是*试探*性地……在一条死巷里——德达勒——代达罗斯——穿着礼拜天的衣裳随时进门——”
“站到炉前地毯上去。”
“不许在屋里晃荡。要说谁有资格站炉前地毯，那是男人们，独自幸福地抽着烟，有人进来就竭力显得又倦怠、又智慧、又重要。”
“像内阁大臣？”
“是；当他们真正——呃。”
“内阁。”
“脚凳；办公凳；你从没见过受庇护的女人胆敢上炉前地毯，除非近视、站上一秒钟看看钟。”米丽亚姆跳上炉前地毯，挥着烟卷。“让受庇护的生活乱——掉！”伦敦那浩大的开阔在她眼前晃着、迎接着。
“*Hoch! Hoch!*（好！好！）”
“万岁！”
“我们当然有我们的补偿。”
“补——*偿*？”
“唔——为我们不得不放弃的一切。”
“什么一切？”
“属于我们的东西。属于我们青春的东西。网球，跳舞——呃，总之是不负责任。…”

===Original===

“I’ve never once thought about any of those things; never once since I came to town,” said Miriam grappling with little anxious pangs that assailed her suddenly; dimly seeing the light on garden trees, hearing distant shouts, the sound of rowlocks, the lapping of water against smoothing swinging sculls. But all that life meant people, daily association with sheltered women and complacent abominable men, there half the time and half the time away on their own affairs which gave them a sort of mean advantage, and money. There was nothing really to regret. It was different for Mag. She did not mind ordinary women. Did not know the difference; or men.
“Yes but anyhow. If we were in the sheltered life we should either have done with that sort of thing and be married﻿—or still keeping it up and anxious about not being married. Besides anyhow; think of the awful *people*.”
“Intolerant child.”
“Isn’t she intolerant. What a good thing you met us.”
“Yes of course; but I’m not intolerant. And look here. Heaps of those women envy us. They envy us our freedom. What we’re having is wanderyahre; the next best thing to wanderyahre.”
“Women don’t want wanderyahre.”
“I do, Jan.”
“So do I. I think the child’s quite right there. Freedom is life. We may be slaves all day and guttersnipes all the rest of the time but *ach Gott*, we are free.”
---
“What a perfectly extraordinary idea.”
“I know. But I don’t see how you can get away from it,” mused Miriam, dreamily holding out against Jan’s absorbed sewing and avoiding for a moment Mag’s incredulously speculative eyes; “if it’s true,” she went on, the rich blur of the warm room becoming as she sent out her voice evenly, thinking eagerly on, a cool clear even daylight, “that everything that can possibly happen does happen, then there must be somewhere in the world, every possible kind of variation of us and this room.”
“D’you mean to say,” gurgled Mag with a fling of her knickered leg and an argumentative movement of the hand that hung loosely dangling a cigarette over the fireside arm of the chair, “that there are millions of rooms exactly like this each with one thing different﻿—say the stem of one narcissus broken instead of whole for instance.”
“My dear Miriam, infinitude couldn’t hold them.”

===Chinese===

“那些事我一样也没想过；进城以来一次也没想过，”米丽亚姆说，一边同突然袭来的小小焦灼的刺痛搏斗；恍惚看见照在园中树上的光，听见远处的呼喊，桨架的声响，水拍着匀匀荡起的双桨。可那一切生活意味着人，意味着天天同受庇护的女人和自得可憎的男人厮混——他们一半时间在场，一半时间外出办自己的事，这给了他们一种下贱的优势——还意味着钱。实在没有什么可惋惜的。玛格不一样。她不介意寻常女人。不知道其中分别；男人也一样。
“是，可不管怎样。我们要是过受庇护的生活，要么早跟那种事一刀两断、结了婚——要么还在撑着，为没结婚发愁。再说不管怎样；想想那些可怕的*人*。”
“不宽容的孩子。”
“可不是不宽容么。遇见我们真是她的造化。”
“那当然；可我不折不宽容。再说你看。那一大堆女人里头有的是眼红我们。她们眼红我们的自由。我们过的是游学年；仅次于游学年的好日子。”
“女人不要游学年。”
“我要，扬。”
“我也要。孩子这一点完全对。自由就是生命。我们可以白天当奴隶、其余时间当街童，可是*ach Gott*（啊，天哪），我们自由。”
---
“一个绝顶奇怪的念头。”
“我知道。可我看你绕不开它，”米丽亚姆沉吟着说，一面做梦似的顶住扬专注的针线、躲开玛格那怀疑探究的目光片刻；“如果这是真的，”她接着说，匀匀地送出声音，心思急急向前，暖房里丰厚的朦胧变成一片清凉澄澈的均匀日光，“凡是可能发生的一切都确会发生，那么世上必定处处存在着我们和这间屋子的每一种可能的变体。”
“你是说，”玛格咯咯地笑，灯笼裤的腿一甩，那只松松搭在椅臂上、悬着烟卷的手做了个争辩的动作，“有几百万间一模一样的屋子，各有一点不同——比如一朵水仙的花茎是折断的而不是完好的。”
“我亲爱的米丽亚姆，无穷也装不下它们。”

===Original===

“Infinitude can hold anything﻿—of course I can see the impossibility of a single world holding all the possible variations of everything at once﻿—but what I mean is that I can think it and there must be something corresponding to it in life﻿—anything that the mind can conceive is realised, somehow, all possibilities must come about, that’s what I mean I think.”
“You mean you can see, as it were in space, millions of little rooms﻿—a little different,” choked Mag.
“Yes I can﻿—quite distinctly﻿—solid﻿—no end to them.”
“I think it’s a perfectly horrible idea,” stated Jan complacently.
“It isn’t﻿—I love it and it’s true﻿ ﻿… you go on and on and on, filling space.”
“Then space is solid.”
“It is solid. People who talk of empty space don’t think﻿ ﻿… space is more solid than a wall﻿ ﻿… yes﻿ ﻿… more solid than a diamond﻿—girls, I’m sure.”
“Space is full of glorious stars.﻿ ﻿…”
“Yes; I know but that’s such a tiny bit of it.﻿ ﻿…”
“Millions and trillions of miles.”
“Those are only words. Everything is words.”
“Well you *must* use words.”
“You ought not to think in words. I mean﻿—you can think in your brain by imagining yourself going on and on through it, endless space.”
“You can’t grasp space with your mind.”
“You don’t **grasp** it. You go through it.”
“I see what you mean. To me it is a fearful idea. Like eternal punishment.”
“There’s no such thing as eternal punishment. The idea is too silly. It makes God a failure and a fool. It’s a man’s idea. The men who take the hearthrug. Sitting on a throne judging everybody and passing sentence is a thing a man would do.”
“But humanity is wicked.”
“Then God is. You can’t separate God and humanity and that includes women who don’t really believe any of those things.”
“*But.* Look at the churches. Look at women and the parsons.”
“Women like ritual and things and they like parsons, *some* parsons, because they are like women, penetrable to light, as Wilberforce said the other day, and understand women better than most men do.”
“Miriam, are you a pantheist?”

===Chinese===

“无穷装得下任何东西——我当然看得出，单个世界一下子装下万物的全部可能变体是不可能的——可我的意思是，我能想到它，生活中就必有与之对应的东西——心智所能设想的一切都以某种方式实现着，一切可能性都必然发生，我想我指的是这个。”
“你是说你能在空间里看见，几百万间小屋——各有一点不同，”玛格呛着说。
“是，我能——清清楚楚——实实在在——望不到头。”
“我觉得这是个糟透了的念头，”扬自得地说。
“不糟——我爱它，它是真的……你一直一直走下去，填满空间。”
“那空间就是实心的。”
“它就是实心的。说空间空无一物的人不动脑子……空间比一堵墙更实在……是的……比钻石还实在——姑娘们，我确信。”
“空间满是灿烂的星。…”
“是；我知道，可那只是它极小的一角。…”
“千百万亿万里。”
“那些只是字。一切都是字。”
“可你*非*用字不可。”
“不该用字思想。我是说——你可以在头脑里想，想象自己一直一直穿行其间，无穷的空间。”
“你没法用心智把握空间。”
“你不是去**把握**它。你是穿行它。”
“我明白你的意思。于我这是个可怕的念头。像永罚一样可怕。”
“没有什么永罚。这念头蠢透了。它把上帝弄成一个失败者和傻瓜。那是男人的念头。占据炉前地毯的男人们想的。坐在宝座上审判众人、宣判，是男人干得出来的事。”
“可人类是邪恶的。”
“那上帝也是。你没法把上帝和人类分开，而这其中包括女人们，她们其实根本不信那些东西。”
“*可是*。瞧瞧那些教堂。瞧瞧女人和牧师们。”
“女人喜欢仪式和种种名堂，她们喜欢牧师，*某一些*牧师，因为他们像女人，如威尔伯福斯前几天说的，光可以透进来，比大多数男人更懂女人。”
“米丽亚姆，你是泛神论者吗？”

> “太阳、月亮和星辰
> “这些岂不都是，哦灵魂，

===Original===

“That’s the Higher Pantheism.”
“Nearer is he than breathing, closer than hands and feet. It doesn’t matter what you call it.”
“If you don’t accept eternal punishment there can’t be eternal happiness.”
“Oh punishment, happiness; tweedledum, tweedledee.”
“Well﻿—look here, there’s remorse. That’s deathless. It must be. If you feel remorseful about anything the feeling must last as long as you remember the thing.”
“Remorse is real enough. I know what you mean. But it may be shortsightedness. Not seeing all round a thing. Is that Tomlinson? Or it may be cleansing you. If it were *complete* Mag it would *kill* you outright. I can believe that. I can believe in annihilation. I am prepared for it. I can’t think why it doesn’t happen to me. That’s just it.”
“I should like to be annihilated.”
“Shut up von Bohlen; you wouldn’t. But look here Miriam child, do you mean to say you think that as long as there is something that keeps on and on, fighting its way on in spite of everything one has, well, a right to exist?”
“Well, that may be the survival of the fittest which doesn’t mean the ethically fittest as Huxley had to admit. We kill the ethically fittest at present. We killed Christ. They go to Heaven. All of us who survive have things to learn down here in hell. Perhaps this is hell. There seems something, ahead.”
“Ourselves. Rising on the ashes of our dead selves. Lord, it’s midnight﻿—”
The chill of the outside night, solitude and her cold empty room.﻿ ﻿…
“I’m going to bed.”
“So am I. We shall be in bed, Miriam, five minutes after you have gone.”
Jan went off for the hot water bottles.
“All right, I’m going﻿—” Miriam bent for her shoes. The soles were dry, scorching; they scorched her feet as she forced on the shoes; one sole cracked across as she put her foot to the ground﻿ ﻿… she braced the muscles of her face and said nothing. It must be forgotten before she left the room that they were nearly new and her only pair; two horrid ideas, nagging and keeping things away.
---
Outside in the air daylight grew strong and clear in Miriam’s mind. Patches of day came in a bright sheen from the moonlit puddles, distributed over the square. She crossed the road to the narrow pathway shadowed by the trees that ran round the long oblong enclosure. From this dark pathway the brightness of the wet moonlit roadway was brighter and she could see façades that caught the moonlight.

===Chinese===

“那是《高等泛神论》。”
“比呼吸更近，比手足更亲。随你管它叫什么。”
“你不接受永罚，就不会有永福。”
“哦，刑罚，幸福；半斤，八两。”
“唔——你看，还有悔恨呢。那是不死的。必得是。你对什么事起了悔恨，那感觉必得同记忆一样长久。”
“悔恨够真实。我明白你的意思。可它也许是近视。没把一件事四面都看到。那是汤姆林森吗？又也许它是在涤净你。它若是*完全的*，玛格，会当场*杀*了你。这个我信。我能相信寂灭。我做好了准备。我不明白它为何还没轮到我。症结就在这儿。”
“我倒想寂灭。”
“闭嘴，冯·博伦；你不想。可是你听着，米丽亚姆孩子，你是说，只要有什么东西一直一直向前，不管人拿它怎样，仍旧一路挣着往前，那它就有存在的权利？”
“唔，那也许是最适者生存，可那不是道义上最适者，赫胥黎也不得不承认。如今我们杀的是道义上最适者。我们杀了基督。他们上了天堂。我们这些活下来的，都还得在这下面的地狱里学东西。也许这就是地狱。前面似乎有——什么。”
“我们自己。在我们死去的自我的灰烬上升起。天哪，半夜了——”
外面黑夜的寒意，孤独，她那间冷冷的空屋。……
“我去睡了。”
“我也是。你走后五分钟我们就上床了，米丽亚姆。”
扬去拿汤婆子。
“好，我走了——”米丽亚姆俯身取鞋。鞋底干了，烫的；她硬把脚往里塞，鞋底烫着脚；一只脚落地，鞋底咔一声裂了……她绷紧脸上的肌肉，什么也没说。趁还没出这房间，得把它忘掉：鞋几乎是新的，而且是仅有的一双；两个讨厌的念头，聒噪着，把东西都挡在外面。
---
到了外面夜气里，日光在米丽亚姆头脑里变得强劲而清朗。一块块天光从月光照亮的积水里亮亮地闪出来，散布在广场上。她过街走到那条窄径上，树影幢幢，绕着那一长方围地。从这条暗径望出去，湿漉漉的月色车路更亮，她看得见迎着月光的一张张门面。

===Original===

There was something trying to worry her, some little thing that did not matter at all, but that some part of her had put away to worry over and was now wanting to consider. Mag’s affairs﻿ ﻿… no she had decided about that. It might be true about blunted sensibilities; but she had meant for some reason to let that other man kiss her, and people never ask advice until they have made up their minds what they are going to do and Mag was Mag quite apart from anything that might happen. She would still be Mag if she were old﻿ ﻿… or mad. That was a firm settled real thing, real and absolute in the daylight of the moonlit square. She wandered slowly on humming a tune; every inch of the way would be lovely. The figure of a man in an overcoat and a bowler hat loomed towards her on the narrow pathway and stopped. The man raised his hat, and his face showed smiling with the moonlight on it. Miriam had a moment’s fear; but the man’s attitude was deprecating and there was her song; it was partly her own fault. But why, why﻿ ﻿… fierce anger at the recurrence of this kind of occurrence seized her. She wanted him out of the way and wanted him to know how angry she was at the interruption.
“Well,” she snapped angrily, coming to a standstill in the moonlit gap.
“Oh,” said the man a little breathlessly in a lame broken tone, “I thought you were going this way.”
“So I am,” retorted Miriam in a loud angry shaking tone, “obviously.”
The man stepped quickly into the gutter and walked quickly away across the road. St. Pancras church chimed the quarter.
Miriam marched angrily forward with shaking limbs that steadied themselves very quickly﻿ ﻿… the night had become suddenly cold; bitter and penetrating; a northeast wind, of course. It was frightfully cold after the warm room; the square was bleak and endless; the many façades were too far off to keep the wind away; the pavement was very cold under her right foot; that was it; the broken sole was the worry that had been trying to come up; she could walk with it; it would not matter if the weather kept dry﻿ ﻿… an upright gait, hurrying quickly away across the moonlit sheen; just the one she had summoned up anger and courage to challenge was not so bad as the others﻿ ﻿… they were not bad; that was not it; it was the way they got in the way﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

有什么东西想来搅扰她，一件全然不要紧的小事，可她的某一部分早已把它收起来预备烦恼，这会儿要拿出来琢磨了。玛格的事……不，那个她已经拿定主意了。感觉磨钝也许是真的；可她当时是有意、为着什么缘故要让那另一个男人吻她的，而人从不征求别人意见，除非自己已经打定了主意要怎么做，何况玛格就是玛格，无论发生什么都隔不开。哪怕她老了……疯了，她也还是玛格。这是一件笃定的、实在的事，在月光广场的白昼里又实在又绝对。她缓缓往前逛，哼着一支调子；这一路的每一寸都会是可爱的。一个穿大衣、戴圆顶帽的男人影影绰绰从窄径上朝她走来，停住。男人举了举帽，月光照亮一张陪笑的脸。米丽亚姆有一瞬的害怕；可男人的姿态带着歉意，何况还有她的歌；这多少也怪她自己。可是为什么，为什么……这等事一再重演，一阵凶猛的怒火攫住了她。她要他走开，还要他知道，这一打搅她多么愤怒。
“嗯，”她怒气冲冲地进出一字，在月光的一隙里站定。
“哦，”男人有点气喘，语调又瘸又碎，“我当您也走这边。”
“我是走这边，”米丽亚姆大声还嘴，声音又气又抖，“这不明摆着。”
男人快步跨进阴沟，快步穿过马路走了。圣潘克拉斯教堂敲了刻钟。
米丽亚姆怒冲冲向前，四肢发抖，很快又稳住了……夜忽然冷了；又尖又钻；自然是东北风。从暖房里出来冷得吓人；广场荒凉而无边；那一排排门面隔得太远，挡不住风；右脚底下的路面很冷；对了；就是它；裂了的鞋底正是那个一直想冒头的烦恼；她还能踩着它走；只要天不放雨，就不碍事……一个笔直的身影，匆匆穿过月光的亮膜走远；方才她鼓起怒气和胆量顶回去的那一个，还不像别的那些坏……他们并不坏；不是坏不坏的事；是他们挡道的那个方式……

===Original===

figures of men, dark, in dark clothes, presenting themselves, calling attention to themselves and the way they saw things, mean and suggestive, always just when things were loveliest. Couldn’t the man see the look of the square and the moonlight?﻿ ﻿… that afternoon at Hyde Park Corner﻿ ﻿… just when everything flashed out after the rain﻿ ﻿… the sudden words close to her ear﻿ ﻿… my beauty﻿ ﻿… my sweet﻿ ﻿… you sweet girl﻿ ﻿… the puffy pale old face, the puffs under the sharp brown eyes. A strange﻿ ﻿… *conviction* in the trembling old voice﻿ ﻿… it was deliberate; a sort of statement; done on purpose, something chosen that would please most. It was like the conviction and statement there had been in Bob Greville’s voice. Old men seemed to have some sort of understanding of things. If only they would talk with the same conviction about other things as there was in their tone when they said those personal things. But the things they said were worldly﻿—generalisations, like the things one read in books that tired you out with trying to find the answer, and made books so awful﻿ ﻿… things that might look true about everybody at some time or other and were not really true about anybody﻿—when you knew them. But people liked those things and thought them clever and smiled about them. All the things the old men said about life and themselves and other people, about everything but oneself, were sad; disappointed and sad with a glint of far off youth in their faces as they said them﻿ ﻿… something moving in the distance behind the blue of their eyes.﻿ ﻿… “Make the best of your youth my dear before it flies.” If it all ended in sadness and envy of youth, life was simply a silly trick. *Life* could not be a silly trick. Life cannot be a silly trick. That is the simple truth﻿ ﻿… a certainty. Whatever happens, whatever things look like, life is not a trick.
Miriam began singing again when she felt herself in her own street, clear and empty in the moonlight. The north wind blew down it unobstructed and she was shivering and singing﻿ ﻿… “spring is *co*‑ming a‑and the *swa*‑llows﻿—have come *back* to te‑ell me *so*.” Spring could not be far off. At this moment in the dark twilight behind the thick north wind the squares were green.

===Chinese===

一个个男人的身影，黑的，穿着黑衣，自己凑上来，招人看他们，看他们看事情的那副样子，猥琐而含沙射影，偏偏总在事情最美的时候。那人难道看不见广场和月光的模样？……那天下午在海德公园角……雨后万物刚刚闪出光来……贴着她耳边突然的话……我的美人……我的甜心……你这甜姑娘……那张浮肿苍白的老人脸，一双锐利的棕色眼睛底下的眼泡。苍老的、发颤的嗓音里有一种奇异的……*笃信*……那是蓄意的；一种宣言；故意的，挑了一句最讨多数人喜欢的话。同鲍勃·格雷维尔嗓音里曾有过的笃信和宣言一个样。老男人们似乎对事情有某种领悟。但愿他们谈别的事情时也拿出说这些私语时的那种笃信来。可他们说的尽是些世故话——大而化之的概论，同书里读到的那些叫你找不着答案、把书弄得可怕的东西一样……那些话放在张三李四身上某个时刻也许像真，可对谁都不是真的——等你认识了那个人。可人们喜欢那些话，觉得它们聪明，为之微笑。老男人们谈生活、谈自己、谈旁人，谈一切、单单不谈你，谈出来都悲哀；失望而悲哀，说这些话时脸上一星遥远青春的光……他们眼睛的蓝后面，远处有什么在动。……“趁青春还没飞走，好好利用它吧，亲爱的。”倘若一切到头来是悲哀、是羡慕青春，那生命简直就是个愚蠢的把戏。*生命*不可能是个愚蠢的把戏。生命不可能是个愚蠢的把戏。这是简单的真理……一种确凿。无论发生什么，无论事情看似如何，生命不是一个把戏。
待觉出自己已走到自己那条街上——月光下又清朗又空阔——米丽亚姆又唱起来。北风顺街无遮无拦地刮，她一边抖一边唱……“春——天*要*——来了，燕——子飞*回*——来告诉——我。”春天不会远了。此刻，在厚厚的北风背后那黑暗的微明里，一座座广场正绿着。

===Original===

---
Her song, restrained on the doorstep and while she felt her already well-known way in almost insupportable happiness through the unlit hall and through the moonlight up the seventy-five stairs, broke out again when her room was reached and her door shut; the two other doors had stood open showing empty moonlit spaces. She was still alone and unheard on the top floor. Her room was almost warm after the outside cold. The row of attic and fourth floor windows visible from her open lattice were in darkness, or burnished blue with moonlight. Warm blue moonlight gleamed along the leads sloping down to her ink-black parapet. The room was white and blue lit, with a sweet morning of moonlight. She had a momentary impulse towards prayer and glanced at the bed. To get so far and cast herself on her knees and hide her face in her hands against the counterpane, the bones behind the softness of her hands meeting the funny familiar round shape of her face, the dusty smell of the counterpane coming up, her face praying to her hands, her hands praying to her face, both throbbing separately with their secret, would drive something away. Something that was so close in everything in the room, so pouring in at the window that she could scarcely move from where she stood. She flung herself more deeply into her song and passed through the fresh buoyant singing air to light the gas. The room turned to its bright evening brown. *Prayer.* Being so weighed down and free with happiness was the time﻿ ﻿… sacrifice﻿ ﻿… the evening *sacrifice* of praise and prayer. That is what that means. To toss all the joys and happiness away and know that you are happy and free without anything. That you cannot escape being happy and free. It always comes.
Why am I so happy and free? she wondered with tears in her eyes. Why? Why do lovely things and people go on happening? To *own* that something in you had no right. But not crouching on your knees﻿ ﻿… standing and singing till everything split with your joy and let you through into the white white brightness.
---
To *see* the earth whirling slowly round, coloured, its waters catching the light. She stood in the middle of the floor hurriedly discarding her clothes. They were old and worn, friendly and alive with the fresh strength of her body. Other clothes would be got somehow; just by going on and working﻿ ﻿… there’s so much﻿—eternally.

===Chinese===

---
她的歌，在门口台阶上按住，在她凭着熟门熟路、怀着几乎按捺不住的幸福穿过黑黑的门厅、踏着月光爬完七十五级楼梯时也按住，一到房间、门一关，便又迸出来；那两扇门都敞着，露出一间间空空的月光屋子。顶层依然只有她一人，无人听见。屋外寒气进来之后，这房间几乎是暖的。从敞开的窗格子望得见的那一排阁楼窗和四楼窗，都黑着，或被月光擦成蓝色。暖蓝的月光顺着斜下来的铅皮屋面一路闪，闪到她那乌黑的女儿墙。房间亮成白色和蓝色，带着一个月光甜美的清晨。她起过一阵祈祷的冲动，瞟了一眼床。走到那一步，扑跪下去，把脸埋进双手抵着床罩，手背的骨头抵着脸颊那圆圆的、熟悉的形状，床罩的灰尘味升上来，脸向着手祈祷，手向着脸祈祷，各自带着自己的秘密一齐搏动，会把什么东西赶走。那东西此刻贴着屋里的一切那么近，从窗口灌得那么满，她几乎挪不动脚。她更深地扎进歌里，穿过清新、欢腾的歌唱的空气去点煤气。房间转回它明亮的晚间棕色。*祈祷*。被幸福压得这样沉、又被幸福放得这样轻，正是那个时候……献祭……晚间赞美的*献祭*与祈祷。说的就是这个。把一切欢乐幸福抛出去，知道自己一无所有也幸福、也自由。知道你逃不开幸福与自由。它总会来。
我为什么这样幸福、这样自由？她眼里含着泪，纳闷。为什么？为什么可爱的事物和可爱的人总在发生？承认你身上那样东西没有权利。可不是蜷在膝头上……站着唱，唱到万物都被你的欢乐涨裂，放你进到那一片白而又白的亮里。
---
*看见*大地缓缓转动，有颜色，它的水面接住光。她站在屋子正中，匆匆褪衣裳。衣裳旧了、磨薄了，友好，被她身体新鲜的力量充得活泛。别的衣裳总会有的；只要走下去、做下去……天地间——永远有那么多。

===Original===

It’s stupendous. I’ve no right to be in it; but I’m in. Someone means me to be in. *I* can’t help it. Fancy people being alive. You would think everyone would go mad. She found herself in bed, sitting up in her flannellette dressing jacket. The stagnant air beneath the sharp downward slope of the ceiling was warmed by the gas. The gaslight glared beautifully over her shoulder down on to the page.﻿ ﻿…
---
*All* that has been said and known in the world is in *language*, in words; all we know of Christ is in Jewish words; all the dogmas of religion are words; the meanings of words change with people’s thoughts. Then no one *knows* anything for certain. Everything depends upon the way a thing is put, and that is a question of some particular civilisation. Culture comes through literature, which is a half-truth. People who are not cultured are isolated in barbaric darkness. The Greeks were cultured; but they are barbarians﻿ ﻿… why? Whether you agree or not, language is the only way of expressing anything and it dims everything. So the Bible is not true; it is a culture. Religion is wrong in making word-dogmas out of it. Christ was something. But Christianity which calls Him divine and so on is false. It clings to words which get more and more wrong﻿ ﻿… then there’s nothing to be afraid of and nothing to be quite sure of rejoicing about. The Christians are irritating and frightened. The man with side-whiskers understands something. But.

===Chinese===

这是惊天动地的。我没有权利置身其中；可我在其中。有人要我置身其中。*我*由不得自己。想想人竟然活着。你会以为人人都要发疯。她发觉自己已在床上，穿着法兰绒家常短褂坐起来。天花板陡然压下来的斜顶底下，停滞的空气被煤气烘暖了。灯光越过她的肩头，亮亮地照在页面上。……
---
世上说过、知道过的*一切*都在*语言*里，在字词里；我们对基督的全部所知都在犹太字词里；宗教的一切教条都是字词；字词的意思随人的思想变。那么没有人*确知*任何事。一切取决于说法，而说法是某种特定文明的问题。文化经由文学而来，这是一句半真半假的话。没有文化的人隔离在野蛮的黑暗里。希腊人有文化；可他们是野蛮人……为什么？无论你同不同意，语言是表达一切的惟一途径，而它使一切蒙翳。所以圣经不是真的；它是一种文化。宗教错在用它造出字词的教条。基督是实有其事的。可称祂为神等等的基督教是虚假的。它抱住一些字词，越抱越错……于是没有什么可怕的，也没有什么可以确知其可喜。基督徒们气人又胆怯。那位留连鬓胡的人懂得一点什么。但是。

## V / 五
===Original===

Then all these years they might have been going sometimes to those lectures. Pater talking about them﻿—telling about old Rayleigh and old Kelvin as if they were his intimates﻿—flinging out remarks as if he wanted to talk and his audience were incapable of appreciation﻿ ﻿… light, heat, electricity, sound-waves; and *never* saying that members could take friends or that there were special lectures for children﻿ ﻿… Sir Robert Ball﻿ ﻿… “a fascinating Irish fellow with the gift of the gab who made a volcano an amusing reality,” Krakatoa﻿ ﻿… that year of wonderful sunsets and afterglows﻿ ﻿… the air half round the world, full of fine dust﻿ ﻿… it seemed cruel﻿ ﻿… deprivation﻿ ﻿… all those years; all that wonderful knowledge just at hand. And now it was coming, the Royal Institution﻿ ﻿… this evening. She must find out whether one had to dress and exactly how one got in. Albemarle Street.﻿ ﻿… It all went on in *Albemarle* Street.
---
“We might meet,” said Mr. Hancock, busily washing his hands and lifting them in the air to shake back his coat sleeves. Miriam listened from her corner behind the instrument cabinet, stupid with incredulity; he *could* not be speaking of the lecture﻿ ﻿… he must be﻿ ﻿… he had meant all the time that he was going to be with her at the lecture.
“… in the library at half past eight.”
“Oh yes,” she replied casually.
---
To sit hearing the very best in the intellectual life of London, the very best science there was; the inner circle suddenly open﻿ ﻿… the curious quiet happy laughter that went through the world with the idea of the breaking up of air and water and rays of light; the strange *love* that came suddenly to them all in the object lesson classes at Banbury Park. That was to begin again﻿ ﻿… but now not only books, not the strange heavenly difficult success of showing the children the things that had been found out; but the latest newest things from the men themselves﻿—there would be an audience and a happy man with a lit face talking about things he had just found out. Even if one did not understand there would be that. Fancy Mr. Hancock being a member and always going and not talking about it﻿ ﻿… at lunch. He must know an enormous number of things besides the wonders of dentistry and pottery and Japanese art.
It was education﻿ ﻿… a liberal education.

===Chinese===

那么，这么多年，他们本可以偶尔去听听那些演讲的。爸爸谈起来——谈起老瑞利、老开尔文，仿佛是他的至交——抛出一句句议论，仿佛他有满腔的话要说而听众不配领会……光、热、电、声波；却从来不曾说过会员可以带朋友，也不说还有专为孩子们办的演讲……罗伯特·鲍尔爵士……“一个有摇舌之才的爱尔兰迷人家伙，把一座火山讲成了有趣的实在”，喀拉喀托……那一年奇妙的落日和霞彩……半个地球的空气，满是细尘……这似乎残忍……是剥夺……这么多年；那么多了不起的知识就在手边。而如今它来了，皇家研究院……就在今晚。她得打听清楚要不要着礼服、到底怎么进去。阿尔伯马尔街。……这一切都在阿尔伯马尔街里进行。
---
“我们兴许能碰上，”汉考克先生说着，忙着洗手，把手举到空中抖回衣袖。米里亚姆从仪器柜后她的角落里听着，因不敢相信而发懵；他不会是在说演讲……一定是在……他从头到尾的意思就是，演讲时他要在她身边。
“……八点半，在图书馆。”
“哦，好的，”她随口应道。
---
坐下来，听伦敦智识生活的精华、世上最好的科学；内圈突然敞开了……那种悄悄的、快活的笑意随“分解空气、水和光线”的想法传遍世界；那种奇异的爱，当年在班伯里公园的实物教学课上突然来到他们全体中间。那一切又要开始了……可如今不只是书本，不只是给孩子们看那些已经发现了的东西时那种奇异的、天国的、艰难的成功；而是最新的、最新鲜的东西，从那些男人们本人那里来——会有满堂的听众，一个容光焕发的快活的人，讲着他刚刚发现的东西。哪怕听不懂，也有这个在。想想汉考克先生竟是个会员，一直在去，却从不提起……在午饭桌上。除了牙科、陶瓷和日本艺术的种种奇妙，他一定还知道数不清的事情。
这就是教育……一种宽博的教育。

It made up for only being able to say one was secretary to a dentist at a pound a week﻿ ﻿… it sounded strange at the end of twelve years of education and five months in Germany and two teaching posts﻿—to people who could not see how wonderful it was from the inside; and the strange meaning and rightness there was; there had been a meaning in Mr. Hancock from the beginning, a sort of meaning in her privilege of associating with fine rare people, so different to herself and yet coming one after another, like questions into her life, and staying until she understood﻿ ﻿… somebody struggled all night with the angel﻿ ﻿… I will not let thee go until thou bless me﻿ ﻿… and there was some meaning﻿—of course, meanings everywhere﻿ ﻿… perhaps a person inside a life could always feel meanings﻿ ﻿… or perhaps only those who had moved from one experience to another could get that curious feeling of a real self that stayed the same through thing after thing.

“This is the library,” said Mr. Hancock leading Miriam along from the landing at the top of the wide red-carpeted staircase. It seemed a vast room﻿—rooms leading one out of the other, lit with soft red lights and giving a general effect of redness, dull crimson velvet in a dull red glow and people, standing in groups and walking about﻿—a quite new kind of people. Miriam glanced at her companion. He looked in place; he was in his right place; these were his people; people with gentle enlightened faces and keen enlightened faces. They were all alike in some way. If the room caught fire there would be no panic. They were gentle, shyly gentle or pompously gentle, but all the same and in agreement because they all knew everything, the real important difficult things. Some of them were discussing and disagreeing; many of the women’s faces had questions and disagreements on them and they were nearly all worn with thought; but they would disagree in a way that was not quarrelsome, because everyone in the room was sure of the importance of the things they were discussing﻿ ﻿… they were all a part of science.﻿ ﻿… “Science is always right and the same, religion cannot touch it or be reconciled with it, theories may modify or cancel each other but the methods of science are one and unvarying. To question that fundamental truth is irreligious”﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这就补回了那个——只能说自己是一个牙医的秘书、一星期一镑……受完了十二年的教育、在德国待过五个月、做过两份教职之后，这称呼听着离奇——对那些看不出它从里面看去多么奇妙的人；而其中自有一种奇异的意蕴和正当；汉考克先生从一开始就有一种意蕴，一种她得以亲近这些好、这些罕见的人的特权里的意蕴——他们同她那样不同，却一个接一个地来，像一个个问题进入她的生命，待到她懂得为止……有谁同天使搏斗了一整夜……你若不给我祝福，我就不容你去……而这里头有个意义——当然，意义到处都是……也许活在一种生命里面的人，总能觉出意义……又或许，只有那些从一段经历迁往另一段经历的人，才能得到那种古怪的感觉：有一个真的自我，穿过一件又一件的事，始终不变。
---
“这就是图书馆，”汉考克先生说，领着米里亚姆从宽宽的红毯楼梯顶上的楼梯台往里走。这似乎是一间极大的屋子——一间间屋子彼此相通，亮着柔柔的红灯，整体是一片红的效果，暗红的丝绒浴在暗红的光里，还有人们，成群站着、走来走去——一类全然陌生的人。米里亚姆瞥了一眼同伴。他在这里合宜；他在他该在的地方；这些是他的人；是些有着温文的、开明的脸和敏锐的、开明的脸的人。他们在某个方面全都相像。倘若这屋子着了火，也不会有慌乱。他们温文，羞怯地温文或神气地温文，但全都一样，全都一致，因为他们全都知道一切，那些真正的、重要的、艰难的事情。有几个正在讨论、争执；许多女人的脸上挂着疑问和异议，她们几乎全都被思考磨得憔悴；可她们的争执不会是吵闹的那种，因为屋里每一个人都确信他们讨论之事的重要……他们全是科学的一部分。……“科学永远正确、始终如一，宗教碰不着它，也无法同它调和；学说可以互相修正或取消，但科学的方法是唯一的、不变的。置疑这一根本真理，便是非宗教。”……

===Original===

these people were that in the type of their minds﻿—one and unvarying; always looking out at something with gentle intelligence or keen intelligence﻿ ﻿… this was Alma’s world﻿ ﻿… it would be something to talk to Alma about. There was something they were not. They were not﻿ ﻿… jolly. They could not be. They would never stop “looking.” Culture and refinement; with something about it that made them quite different to the worldly people, a touch of rawness, raw school harshness about them that was unconscious of itself and could not come to life. Their shoulders and the backs of their heads could never come to life. It gave them a kind of deadness that was quite unlike the deadness of the worldly people, not nearly so dreadful﻿—rather funny and likable. One could imagine them all washing, very carefully, in an abstracted way still looking and thinking and always with the advancement of science on their minds; never really aware of anything behind or around them because of the wonders of science. Seeing these people changed science a little. They were almost something tremendous; but not quite.
---
“That’s old Huggins,” murmured Mr. Hancock, giving Miriam’s arm a gentle nudge as a white-haired old man passed close by them with an old woman at his side, with short white hair exactly like him. “The man who invented spectrum analysis﻿—and that’s his wife; they’re both great fishermen.” Miriam gazed. *There*, was the splendid thing.﻿ ﻿… In her mind blazed the coloured bars of the spectrum. In the room was the light of the beauty, the startling *life* these two old people shed from every part of their persons. The room blazed in the light they shed. She stood staring, moving to watch their gentle living movements. They moved as though the air through which they moved was a living medium﻿—as though everything were alive all round them﻿—in a sort of hushed vitality. They were young. She felt she had never seen anyone so young. She longed to confront them just once, to stand for a moment the tide in which they lived.
“*Ah* Meesturra Hancock﻿—you *are* a faceful votary.”
That’s a German, thought Miriam, as the flattering deep caressing gutturals rebounded dreadfully from her startled consciousness. What a determined intrusion. How did he come to know such a person? Glancing she met a pair of swiftly calculating eyes fixed full on her face.

===Chinese===

这些人正是那样的人，就他们的心智类型而言——唯一的、不变的；永远以温和的智慧或犀利的智慧朝某样东西望着……这是阿尔玛的世界……这倒是可以同阿尔玛谈的一件事。有一样东西他们不是。他们不……快活。他们快活不了。他们永远不会停止“注视”。教养与文雅；可那上面又有一样东西，使他们同世俗的人们截然两样，一种生涩，一种不自觉的、生硬的书卷气，它自己意识不到自己，也活泼不起来。他们的肩膀和后脑勺永远活泼不起来。这给他们一种死气，一种同世俗人们的死气全然不同的死气，远没有那么可怕——倒有些好笑，有些讨人喜欢。可以想象他们全体在盥洗，非常仔细地、心不在焉地洗着，一边还在注视、在思索，心里永远装着科学的进步；因为科学的种种奇迹，对身后周围的一切永远浑然不觉。看见这些人，科学本身也变了一点点。他们几乎是一种震人的东西；但差一点。
---
“那是老哈金斯，”汉考克先生低声说，轻轻碰了碰米里亚姆的胳膊，一位白发的老人正偕一位老妇人从他们近旁走过，那老妇人短短的白发同他的一模一样。“发明光谱分析的人——那是他的太太；他们俩都是了不起的钓鱼客。”米里亚姆凝望。那，就是那件辉煌的东西。……她心里烧灼着光谱的彩色条纹。屋子里是那两位老人从周身每一处散出的美之光、那叫人一惊的生命。整间屋子在他们散发的光里燃烧。她站着凝望，挪动着，好看清他们温文的、活生生的动作。他们移动着，仿佛穿行的空气是一种活的媒质——仿佛四下里的一切都活着——带着一种屏息的活力。他们年轻。她觉得自己从没见过这样年轻的人。她渴望同他们正面相对一回，在他们生活的潮水里站上一刻。
“啊（Ah）——汉考克先生（Meesturra Hancock）——您真是个满面虔诚的信徒（a faceful votary）。”
这是个德国人，米里亚姆想，那谄媚的、深沉的、爱抚般的喉音，从她受惊的意识上可怕地弹开。好一记执意的闯入。他怎么会认识这样一个人物？斜眼一瞥，她撞上一对眼睛，飞快地估量着，直钉在她的脸上。

===Original===

There was fuzzy black hair lifted back from an anxious, yellowish, preoccupied little face. Under the face came the high collar-band of a tightly-fitting dark claret-coloured ribbed silk bodice, fastened from the neck to the end of the pointed peak by a row of small round German buttons, closely decorated with a gilded pattern. Mr. Hancock was smiling an indulgent, deprecating smile. He made an introduction and Miriam felt her hand tightly clasped and held by a small compelling hand, while she sought for an answer to a challenge as to her interest in science. “I don’t really know anything about it,” she said vaguely, strongly urged to display her knowledge of German. The eyes were removed from her face and the little lady boldly planted and gazing about her made announcements to Mr. Hancock﻿—about the fascinating subject of the lecture and her hopes of a large and appreciative audience.
What did she want? She could not possibly fail to see that Mr. Hancock was telling her that he could see through her social insincerities. It was dreadful to find that even here there were social insincerities. She was like a busy ambassador for things that belonged somewhere else and that he was laughing at in an indulgent, deprecating way that must make her blaze with an anger that she did not show. Looking at her as her eyes and mouth made and fired their busy sentences, Miriam suddenly felt that it would be easy to deal with her, take her into a corner and talk about German things, food and love affairs and poetry and music. But she would always be breaking away to make a determined intrusion on somebody she knew. She could not really know any English person. What was she doing, bearing herself so easily in the inner circle of English science? Treating people as if she knew all about them and they were all alike. How surprised she must often be, and puzzled.
---
“That was Miss Teresa Szigmondy,” said Mr. Hancock, reproducing his amused smile as they took their seats in the dark theatre.
“Is she German?”
“Well﻿ ﻿… I think, as a matter of fact, she’s part Austro-Hungarian and part﻿—well, *Hebrew*.” A Jewess﻿ ﻿… Miriam left her surroundings, pondering over a sudden little thread of memory. An eager, very bright-eyed, curiously dimpling schoolgirl face peering into hers, and a whispering voice﻿—“D’you know why we don’t go down to prayers?

一头蓬松的黑发从一张焦虑的、微黄的、心事重重的小脸向后拢起。脸底下是一圈高高的领座，连着一件紧身的深酒红棱纹绸上衣，从脖颈直到尖角下摆，钉着一排小小的德式圆纽扣，密密地压着镀金的花样。汉考克先生微笑着，一副宽容的、抱歉的笑。他作了引见，米里亚姆觉出自己的手被一只小小的、不由分说的手紧紧握住不放，同时她在搜罗一句话，回答那句对她科学兴趣的挑衅。“我对它实在一无所知，”她含含糊糊地说，心里强烈地鼓动着要露一露她的德语。那双眼睛从她脸上撤走了，小小的夫人大胆地站定，四下打量，向汉考克先生发布起通告来——关于演讲题目的迷人，关于她盼望一个人数众多、善于领会的听众。
她想要什么？她不可能看不出，汉考克先生正告诉她，他看穿了她的社交虚套。竟发现连这里也有社交虚套，可怕。她像个忙碌的大使，替一些属于别处的东西奔走，而他正宽容地、抱歉地笑着那些东西——那笑法准会叫她烧起一团火，一团她不显示出来的火。望着她的眼睛和嘴忙忙碌碌地造出、射出一句句话，米里亚姆忽然觉得，对付她很容易，把她带到角落里，同她谈谈德国的事情、吃食、风流韵事、诗和音乐。可她总会不断挣脱开去，执意闯到某个她认得的人跟前。她不可能真认识任何一个英国人。她在做什么，在英国科学的内圈里这样如鱼得水地拿架子？把人们当作她对他们了如指掌、当作他们全是一路货。她一定常常吃惊，常常犯糊涂。

“那位是特蕾莎·西格蒙迪小姐，”汉考克先生说，两人在黑黢黢的讲演厅落座时，又摆出他那副觉得好玩的笑。
“她是德国人？”
“呃……我想，其实，她一半是奥匈人，一半是——嗯，希伯来。”
一个犹太女人……米里亚姆离开了她的四周，对记忆里突然冒出的一小缕线头思量起来。一张热切的、眼睛极亮的、有个奇怪酒窝的女生脸凑近她的脸，一个耳语的声音——“你知道我们为什么不下楼去祷告吗？”

’Cos we’re *Jews*”﻿—they had always been late; fresh faced and shiny haired and untidy and late and clever in a strange brisk way and talkative and easy and popular with the teachers.﻿ ﻿… Their guttural voices ringing out about the stairs and passages, deep and loud and stronger than any of the voices of the other girls. The Hyamson girls﻿—they had been foreigners, like the Siggs and the de Bevers, but different﻿ ﻿… what was the difference in a Jew? Mr. Hancock seemed to think it was a sort of disgraceful joke﻿ ﻿… what was it? Max Sonnenheim had been a Jew, of course, the same voice. Banbury Park “full of Jews”﻿ ﻿… the Brooms said that in patient contemptuous voices. But what *was* it? What did everybody mean about them?
“Is she scientific?”
“She seems to be interested in science,” smiled Mr. Hancock.
“How funny of her to ask me to go to tea with her just because you told her I knew German.”
“Well, you go; if you’re interested in seeing notabilities you’ll meet all kinds of wonderful people at her house. She knows everybody. She’s the niece of a great Hungarian poet. I believe he’s to be seen there sometimes. They’re all coming in now.” Mr. Hancock named the great names of science one by one as the shyly gentle and the pompously gentle little old men ambled and marched into the well of the theatre and took their seats in a circle round the central green table.

“*There’s* a pretty lady,” said Mr. Hancock, conversationally, just as the light was lowered. Miriam glanced across the half circle of faintly shining faces and saw an effect, a smoothly coiffured head and smooth neck and shoulders draped by a low deep circular flounce of lace rising from the gloom of a dark dress, sweep in through a side door bending and swaying﻿—“or a pretty dress at any rate”﻿—and sat through the first minutes of the lecture, recalling the bearing and manner of the figure, with sad fierce bitterness. Mr. Hancock admired “feminine” women﻿ ﻿… or at any rate he was bored by her own heavy silence and driven into random speech by the sudden dip and sweep of the lace appearing in the light of the doorway. He was surprised himself by his sudden speech and half corrected it﻿ ﻿… “or a pretty dress.”﻿ ﻿… But anyhow he, even he, was one of those men who do not know that an effect like that was just an effect, a deliberate “charming” feminine effect.

“因为我们是犹太人（Jews）”——她们总是迟到；脸蛋新鲜，头发亮泽，衣衫不整，迟到，用一种古怪的麻利劲儿聪明着，多嘴，随和，得老师们的欢心。……她们的喉音在楼梯和过道里回响，深沉、响亮，比别的女孩的嗓音都强。海厄姆森家的女孩们——她们一直是外国人，同西格斯家、德贝弗斯家一样，可又不同……犹太人的不同究竟是什么？汉考克先生似乎觉得那是个丢人的笑话……那到底是什么？马克斯·索嫩海姆当然是个犹太人，同一副嗓音。班伯里公园“满是犹太人”……布鲁姆一家（the Brooms）用耐心的、轻蔑的声音这样说。可那究竟是什么？关于他们，大家说的到底是什么意思？
“她懂科学吗？”
“她似乎对科学有兴趣，”汉考克先生微笑道。
“只因为你告诉她我懂德语，她就请我去喝茶，多奇怪。”
“去嘛；你若有意看看名流，她家里什么了不得的人物都会碰上。她谁都认得。她是一位伟大的匈牙利诗人的侄女。我相信有时在那儿还见得着他。他们都进来了。”汉考克先生把科学的伟名一个个报出来，羞怯地温文的和神气地温文的小老头们蹒跚着、迈步走进讲演厅的洼地，围着中央那张绿桌成圈落座。

“那边有位漂亮的太太，”汉考克先生闲谈似的说，灯光恰好调低。米里亚姆扫过那半圈微微发亮的脸，看见一个效果，一个梳得溜光的头、光洁的颈和肩，罩着一圈从黑裙的幽暗里升起的又低又深的环形蕾丝边，从边门里一俯一摆地飘进来——“至少是条漂亮的裙子”——讲座开头的几分钟她坐在那里，怀着悲哀的、凶狠的苦味回想那身段的做派和风度。汉考克先生欣赏“有女人味的”女人……或者至少，他被她自己沉重的沉默闷坏了，被门口灯光里骤然一沉一漾的蕾丝逼出了这句信口的话。他自己也吃了一惊，改了半句……“至少是条漂亮的裙子。”……可是不管怎样，他，连他，也属于那一类男人——不知道那样的一个效果就只是一个效果，一个处心积虑的“迷人”的女人味的效果。
