“格罗夫先生求见，小姐。”
“格罗夫*先生*？”
“是，小姐；一位黑黑的先生。”
米丽亚姆从椅子里站起来。詹姆斯同情地候了片刻，已回地下楼梯吃她的饭去了。米丽亚姆觉得自己又高又瘦——被隔开、被环拱着；从一切奋战与劳苦里痊愈了。她快步穿过门厅，什么也不想；自己，正走在哈丽特家的花园小径上。在候诊室门口她迟疑了。格罗夫先生在门那一侧，等着她进去。她豁地推开门，伸直了手臂迎上去。还来不及用一声快活的、业务腔的“看牙？”把过去炸个粉碎——话一出口往昔就散了——她先看见了他脸上痛楚的焦灼，和他深色皮肤下升起的红晕。
“不，”他说，飞快地从那软沓沓的握手中缩回，猛地坐回他刚从那儿站起来的椅子上。

===Original===

Miriam watched him go helplessly on to say in stiff resentfulness what he had come to say while she stood apologetically at his chair side.
“I meant to write to you﻿—two or three times.”
“Oh why didn’t you?” she responded emphatically.﻿ ﻿… Why can’t I be quiet and hear what he has to say? He must have wanted to see me dreadfully to come here like this.
His eyes were fixed blindly upon the far-off window.
“Yes. I wanted to very much. How do you like your life here?” He was flushing again. His skin still had that shiny film over it, so unlike the clear snaky brilliance of the eyes. They were dreadful and all the rest flappy and floppy and somehow feverish.
“Oh﻿—I like it immensely.”
“That is a very good thing.”
“Do you like your life?”
He drew in his lower lip on an indrawn breath and held it with his teeth. His eyes were thinking busily under a slight frown.
“That is one of the things I wished to discuss with you.”
“Oh *do* discuss it with me,” cried Miriam.
“I am very glad you are getting on here so well,” he murmured thoughtfully, gazing through the window, to and fro as if scanning the opposite house-fronts.
“Oh, I like it immensely,” said Miriam after a silence. Her head was beginning to ache. He sat quite still, scanning to and fro, his lip recaptured under his teeth.
“They are such nice people. I like it for so many things.”
He looked absently round at her.
“M-yes. On several occasions I thought of writing to you.”
“Yes,” said Miriam sitting down opposite to him.
He shifted a little in his chair to keep his way clear to the window.
For a few moments they sat silent; then he suddenly took out his watch and stood up.
Miriam rose. “Have you seen the Ducaynes lately?” she asked hurriedly, moving nervously towards the door. Murmuring an indistinct response he led the way to the door and held it open for her.
James was coming forward with a patient. They stood aside for the patient to pass in, James waiting to escort Mr. Grove to the front door. They shook hands limply and silently. Miriam stood watching his narrow loosely knit clerical back as he plunged along through the hall and out. She turned as James turned from the door.﻿ ﻿… What it must have cost him to break in here and ask for me﻿ ﻿… how silly and how rude I was.﻿ ﻿… I *can’t* believe he’s been; it’s like a dream. He’s seen me in the new life changed﻿ ﻿… and I’m not really changed.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆无助地看着他僵硬而含怨地继续说他来要说的话，她抱歉地立在他椅边。
“我本想写信给你——写过两三回。”
“哦，怎么没写呢？”她加重语气应道。……我怎么就不能安安静静听他要说什么？他一定是想我想得厉害，才这样跑到这儿来。
他的眼睛茫茫地盯着远处的窗。
“是。我很想写。你在这儿的生活喜欢吗？”他又红了脸。他的皮肤上还蒙着那层亮亮的薄膜，同眼睛里清亮的、蛇一样的光辉那么不同。那双眼睛可怕，其余的一切都软塌塌、松沓沓、有几分热病似的。
“哦——我非常喜欢。”
“那就很好。”
“你喜欢你的生活吗？”
他吸一口气，把下唇抿进嘴里，用牙咬住。眼睛在微微的皱眉下忙碌地思索。
“那正是我想同你讨论的几件事之一。”
“哦*一定*同我讨论，”米丽亚姆喊道。
“你在这里这样顺利，我很高兴，”他若有所思地低声说，望着窗外，目光来回扫，仿佛在检视对面的屋脸。
“哦，我非常喜欢，”隔了一阵沉默，米丽亚姆说。她的头开始疼了。他一动不动坐着，来回扫视，嘴唇又咬在齿间。
“他们都是很好的人。我喜欢它的地方多得很。”
他心不在焉地掉头看她一眼。
“嗯——是的。有几次我想过写信给你。”
“嗯，”米丽亚姆说着在他对面坐下。
他在椅子里挪了挪，让开自己通向窗户的路。
两人默默坐了几息；随后他忽然掣出怀表，站了起来。
米丽亚姆起身。“最近见到杜肯一家了吗？”她慌忙问，不安地朝门口挪。他含糊应了一声，在前头引路到门口，替她拉住门。
詹姆斯正陪一位病人进来。他们侧身让病人过去，詹姆斯等着送格罗夫先生出前门。两人软沓沓、无声地握了手。米丽亚姆站着，看他那瘦窄、松散的教士背影一头扎过门厅、出了门。詹姆斯从门口转回身时她也转过身。……他闯进这里来找我，得付出怎样的代价……我方才多傻、多无礼。……我*不能*相信他来过；像一场梦。他看见的是新生活里变了的我……而我并没有真的变。

## VII / 七
===Original===

Why must I always think of her in this place.﻿ ﻿… It is always worst just along here.﻿ ﻿… Why do I always forget there’s this piece﻿ ﻿… always be hurrying along seeing nothing and then suddenly Teetgen’s Teas and this row of shops. I can’t bear it. I don’t know what it is. It’s always the same. I always feel the same. It is sending me mad. One day it will be worse. If it gets any worse I shall be mad. Just here. Certainly. Something is wearing out in me. I am meant to go mad. If not I should not always be coming along this piece without knowing it, whichever street I take. Other people would know the streets apart. I don’t know where this bit is or how I get to it. I come every day because I am meant to go mad here. Something that knows brings me here and is making me go mad because I am myself and nothing changes me.

===Chinese===

为什么我总在这个地方想起她。……总是一到这一段就最难受。……为什么我总是忘记有这一段……总是勿勿赶着路什么也没看见，然后突然就是蒂特根茶叶店和这一排铺子。我受不了。我不知道这是什么。永远一样。我的感觉永远一样。这要把我逼疯了。有一天会更糟。再糟下去我就疯了。就在这儿。一定的。我身上有什么东西在磨损。我是命中要疯的。不然的话，我不会总是不知不觉走到这一段，不管我走哪条街。别人能把一条条街分得清清楚楚。我不知道这一小段在哪儿、我怎么走到它跟前的。我天天来，因为我命中要在这里疯掉。有一样知道的东西把我带到这里，正在叫我发疯，因为我是我自己，什么也改变不了我。

## VIII / 八
===Original===

The morning went on. It seemed as though there was to be no opportunity of telling Mr. Hancock until lunch had changed the feeling of the day. He knew there was something. Turning to select an instrument from a drawer she was at work upon he had caught sight of her mirth and smiled his amusement and anticipation into the drawer before turning gravely back to the chair. Perhaps that was enough, the best, like a moment of amusement you share with a stranger and never forget. Perhaps by the time she was able to tell him he would be disappointed. No. It was too perfect. Just the sort of thing that amused him.
He had one long sitting after another, the time given to one patient overlapping the appointment with the next so that her clearings and cleansings were done with a patient in the chair, noiselessly and slowly, keeping her in the room, making today seem like a continuation of yesterday afternoon. Yesterday shed its radiance. The shared mirth made a glowing background to her toil. The duties accumulating downstairs made her continued presence in the surgery a sort of truancy. She felt more strongly than ever the sense of her usefulness to him. She had never so far helped him so deftly and easily, being everywhere and nowhere, foreseeing his needs without impeding his movements, doing everything without reminding the patient that there was a third person in the room. She followed sympathetically the long slow processes of excavation and root treatment, the delicate shaping and undercutting of the walls of cavities, the adjustment and retention of the many appliances for the exclusion of moisture, the insertions of the amalgams and pastes whose pounding and mixing made a recurrent crisis in her morning. She wished again and again that the dentally ignorant dentally ironic world could see the operator at his best; in his moments of quiet intense concentration on giving his best to his patients.
---
The patients suffering the four long sittings were all of the best group, leisurely and untroubled as to the mounting up of guineas, and three of them intelligently appreciative of what was being done. *They* knew all about the “status” of modern dentistry and the importance of teeth. They were all clear serene tranquil cheerful people who probably hardly ever went to a doctor.

===Chinese===

上午过去了。似乎要一直等吃过午饭、白天的心情换了调，才有机会告诉汉考克先生。他知道有什么事。有一次他转身从她正整理的抽屉里挑器械，瞥见了她那团欢喜，先对着抽屉把他的有趣和期待笑了一笑，才庄重地转回椅子那边。也许那样就够了，最好不过了，像同一个陌生人分享的一刻欢喜，永不忘记。也许等她能告诉他的时候，他倒要失望了。不。这事太完美了。正是逗他发笑的那一类事。

他一个长诊接一个长诊，一个病人的时间漫过下一个的预约，于是她的清理和擦洗都是在椅子上躺着病人时做的，无声地、慢慢地做，使她留在诊室里，使今天像是昨天下午的延续。昨天洒下它的光辉。共享的那团欢喜，给她的劳作铺了一层发亮的底子。楼下越积越多的杂务，使她继续待在诊室里成了一种小小的逃学。她比任何时候都强烈地感到自己对他的用处。她还从来没有这样利落、这样轻巧地帮过他，无处不在又无处所在，预见他的需要而不碍他的动作，做一切事情而不让病人想起屋里还有第三个人。她满怀同情地跟着那些漫长缓慢的工序走——挖凿和根管治疗，洞壁的精细塑形和倒凹，一件件隔湿器械的安放和固定，还有汞合金和糊剂的充填——它们的捣捣和调拌，是她上午一次又一次的小小危机。她一遍遍希望，这个对牙科无知、对牙科冷嘲的世界，能看看这位医者最出色的时候；看看他安静地、全神贯注地把自己最好的献给病人的那些时刻。
---
挨那四个长诊的病人，全属于最好的一类，几尼一张张往上摆，他们从容不迫、心平气和，其中三位对正在接受的处置心明眼亮地领情。他们全知道现代牙科的“地位”和牙齿的重要。他们都是清爽、安详、宁静、快活的人，多半几乎从不看内科。

===Original===

They would rate oculists and dentists on a level with doctors and two of them at least would rate Mr. Hancock on a level with anybody.﻿ ﻿… Tomorrow would be quite different, a rush of gas cases, that man who was sick if an instrument touched the back of his tongue; Mrs. Wolff, disputing fees, the deaf-mute, the grubby little man on a newspaper﻿ ﻿… he ought to have no patients but these intelligent ones and really nervous and delicate people and children.
---
“I sometimes wish I’d stuck to medicine.”
“Why?”
“Well﻿—I don’t know. You know they get a good deal more all round out of their profession than a dentist does. It absorbs them more.﻿ ﻿… I don’t say it ought not to be the same with dentistry. But it isn’t. I don’t know a dentist who wants to go on talking shop until the small hours. I’m quite sure *I* don’t. Now look at Randle. He was dining here last night. So was Bentley. We separated at about midnight; and Randle told me this morning that he and Bentley walked up and down Harley Street telling each other stories, until two o’clock.”
“That simply means they talk about their patients.”
“Well﻿—yes. They discuss their cases from every point of view. They get more human interest out of their work.”
“Of course everybody knows that medical students and doctors are famous for stories. But it doesn’t really mean they know anything about *people*. I don’t believe they do. I think the dentist has quite as much opportunity of studying human nature. Going through dentistry is like dying. You must know almost everything about a patient who has had much done, or even a little﻿—”
“The fact of the matter is their profession is a hobby to them as well as a profession. That’s the truth of the matter. Now I think a man who can make a hobby of his profession is a very fortunate man.”
---
How surprised the four friendly wealthy patients, especially the white-haired old aristocrat who was always pressing invitations upon him would have been, ignoring or treating her with the kindly consideration due to people of her station, if they could have seen inside his house yesterday and beheld her ensconced in the most comfortable chair in his drawing-room﻿ ﻿… talking to Miss Szigmondy.
---
Each time she came downstairs she sat urgently down to the most pressing of her clerical duties and presently found her mind ranging amongst thoughts whose beginnings she could not remember.

===Chinese===

他们会把眼科医生和牙医同内科医生等量齐观，其中至少两位会把汉考克先生同任何人等量齐观。……明天可就全然不同了，一阵笑气病例的忙乱，那个器械一碰舌根就要呕的男的；沃尔夫太太，为诊费争执；聋哑人；报纸上那个邋遢的小个子……他本该只收这些明白人，外加真正神经脆弱的娇气人和孩子。
---
“我有时想，当初坚持学医就好了。”
“为什么？”
“呢——说不清。你知道，从他们的行业里，方方面面得到的都比牙医多。它更把他们整个人吸进去。……我不是说牙科不该也一样。可它不是。我没见过哪个牙医想谈本行谈到深更半夜。我敢说我自个儿就不想。瞧瞧兰德尔。他昨晚在这儿吃饭。本特利也在。我们差不多半夜散的；兰德尔今早告诉我，他跟本特利在哈利街上来回踱，互相讲故事，一直讲到两点。”
“那只说明他们谈的是病人。”
“呢——对。他们从各个角度讨论病例。他们的行当里人身趣更多。”
“当然，谁都知道医科学生和医生以故事出名。可这并不真说明他们懂人。我不信他们懂。我看牙医研究人性的机会一样多。看完牙就像死过一回。凡是治过些牙的病人，哪怕只治过一点，你差不多就知道了他的一切——”
“事实是，他们的行当于他们既是行当又是癖好。事情的真相就是这个。依我看，能把行当变成癖好的男人，是个非常有福的男人。”
---
那四位和气的阔病人——尤其那位再三向他发出邀请的白发老贵胄——若能看见昨天他家的内部，看见她安坐在他客厅里最舒服的那把椅子里……同齐格蒙迪小姐（Miss Szigmondy）谈话，该多么吃惊；他们对她，是不予理会，或按她的身份施与和气的体恤。
---
每次下楼，她都急急坐下做最急的文牍，不多久便发觉心思荡开了，荡进一些想不起开头来的念头里。

===Original===

She felt equal to anything. Every prospect was open to her. Simple solutions to problems that commonly went unanswered round and round in her head presented themselves in flashes. At intervals she worked with a swiftness and ease that astonished her, making no mistakes, devising small changes and adjustments that would make for the smoother working of the practice, dashing off notes to friends in easy expressive phrases that came without thought.
---
Rushing up towards lunch time in answer to the bell she found Mr. Hancock alone. He turned from the washstand and stood carefully drying his hands. “Are they showing up?” he murmured and seeing her, smiled his sense of her eagerness to communicate and approached a few steps waiting and smiling with the whole of his face exactly as he would smile when the communication was made. There was really no need to tell. Miriam glanced back for an incoming patient. “Miss Szig*mon*dy,” she began in a voice deep with laughter.
He laughed at once, with a little backward throw of his head just as the patient came in. Miriam glided swiftly into her corner.
---
At teatime she found herself happily exhausted, sitting alone in the den waiting for the sound of footsteps. For the first time the gas-stove was unlit. The rows of asbestos balls stood white and bare. But a flood of sunlight came through the western panes of the newly washed skylight. The little low tea-table with its fresh uncrumpled low hanging white cover and compact cluster of delicate china stood in full sunshine amidst the comfortable winter shabbiness. The decorative confusion on the walls shone richly out in the new bright light. It needed only to have all the skylights open, the blue of the sky visible, the thin spring air coming in, the fire alight making a summery glow, to be perfect; like spring teatime in a newly visited house. The Wilsons’ sitting-room would be in an open blaze of shallow spring sunshine. She saw it going on day by day towards the rich light of summer﻿ ﻿… jealously. One ought to be there every day. So much life would have passed through the room. Every day last week had been full of it, everything changed by it, and now, since yesterday it seemed months ago. It seemed too late to begin going down again. One thing blots out another. You cannot have more than one thing intensely.

她觉得自己样样都应付得来。每一条路都向她敞着。那些寻常绕来绕去没有答案的问题，此刻简单的解答一闪一闪自己送上门来。她间或以一种叫她自己吃惊的敏捷和轻快做工，不出一点错，设计些小小的改动和调整，让诊所运转得更顺，又挥笔给朋友们写便条，信手就是现成的达意句子，不假思索。
---
临近午饭时分，铃声一响她奔上楼去，见汉考克先生独自一人。他从盥洗架前转过身，站着仔细擦干手。“病人来了没有？”他低声问，一看见她，便笑出他感到她急于倾诉的样子，上前几步，等着，用他的整张脸微笑着，同消息揭晓时将要有的笑一模一样。其实根本不用说了。米里亚姆回头望了一眼进门的病人。“齐格蒙迪小姐，”她开口，嗓音里沉着一汪笑。

他立刻笑了，头微微向后一扬，恰好病人进来。米里亚姆轻捷地滑回她的角落。
---
到了喝茶的时辰，她发觉自己快活地乏透了，独自坐在小书房里，等脚步声。煤气炉头一回没点。一排排石棉球白裸裸地立着。可新洗过的大天窗的西面格子里，涌进一片阳光。小小的矮茶桌，罩着平整无痕、低垂的白布，一簇精巧的瓷器，整个沐在阳光里，四周是一屋子舒服的冬日的寒酸。墙上那琳琅的杂陈，在新的亮光里焕然放彩。只消把天窗全推开，望得见蓝天，细细的春天的空气进来，炉火点着，烘出一团夏意，就十全十美了；像新到一户人家里的春天茶时。威尔逊家的起居室此刻准浸在一大片浅浅的春阳里。她看见它一天一天朝着夏天浓艳的光走过去……带着醋意。人应该天天在那里。多少生命将从那间屋子里过过去。上星期每一天都盛着它，一切都被它改了样，而如今，自昨天以来，它仿佛已是几个月前的事。再开始下去，似乎太迟了。一件事抹掉另一件事。人不能同时强烈地拥有两件事。

Quite soon it would be as if she had never been down; except in moments now and again, when something recalled the challenge of their point of view. But other people had already begun to identify her with them. That came of talking. If she had said nothing, nothing would have been changed; either at Wimpole Street or with the girls. Did they really like reading *The Evolution of the Idea of God* or were they only pretending? Sewing all the time, busily, like wives, instead of smoking and listening and thinking.

Which was the stronger? The interest of getting the whole picture there, and struggling with Mr. Wilson’s deductions or the interest of getting the girls to grasp and admire his conclusions even while she herself refused them.﻿ ﻿…
“Why can’t I keep quiet about the things that happen? It’s all me, my conceit and my way of rushing into things.”﻿ ﻿… But other people were the same in a way. Only there was something real in their way. They believed in the things they rushed into. “Miss Henderson knows the great critic, intimately.” He had thought that would impress Miss Szigmondy. It did. For a moment she had stopped talking and looked surprised. There was time to disclaim, to tell them they were being impressed in the wrong way; to tell them something, to explain in some way. The moment had passed, full of terrible far-off trouble, “decisive.”

There is always a fraction of a second when you know what you are doing. Miss Szigmondy would have gone on talking about bicycling until Mr. Hancock came back. If, then, one had suddenly said “I am dying to learn bicycling” or *anything* they would have known something of the truth about Mr. Wilson. It was the worrying thought of him, still there, that made one say, without thinking, “I am dying to learn.” It was too late. It linked up with the silence about Mr. Wilson and left one being a person who knew and altogether approved of Mr.

“为什么我对发生的事就不能闭嘴？全是我，我的虚荣，我那股子往事情里冲的劲。…”可旁人某种意义上也一样。只是他们的方式里有点真东西。他们信他们冲进去的那些事。“亨德森小姐同那位大批评家很熟，很熟。”他以为这能镇住齐格蒙迪小姐。确实镇住了。她停下了话头，一脸惊讶。那会儿来得及撇清，来得及告诉他们，他们镇错了方向；告诉他们一点什么，以某种方式解释一下。那一刻过去了，盛满遥远的可怕的烦恼，“决定性的”。…
总有一秒钟的几分之一，你知道自己在做什么。齐格蒙迪小姐本会一直谈骑车，谈到汉考克先生回来。根本用不着想也不想地突然冒一句“我巴不得学呢”。真的，那句突兀的话，是先前大家谈威尔逊先生时没能开口的后果。倘若那时突然说一句“我巴不得学骑车”，或者说任何一句，他们就会知道一点关于威尔逊先生的真相。正是对他放不下的那点烦心——他还在那儿——叫人不加思索地说出“我巴不得学”。太迟了。它同关于威尔逊先生的沉默接上了头，把人留成了一个知情并且全然赞许威尔逊先

Wilson and wanted to learn bicycling. Altogether wrong. “You know﻿—I don’t approve of Mr. Wilson; and you might not if you heard him talk, and﻿ ﻿… his marriage﻿ ﻿… you know.﻿ ﻿…”
… If I had done that, I should have been easy and strong and could have “made conversation” when she began talking about bicycling. “*No* one can pgonounce my name. You had better call me Thégèse my dear girl. Yes, do; I want you to.” She had said that with a worried face, a sudden manner of unsmiling intimacy. She certainly had some plan. Standing there with her broken hearted voice and her anxious face she seemed to be separate from the room, even from her own clothes. Yet something within her was moving so quickly that it made one breathless. She was so intent that she was unconscious of the appealing little figure she made huddled in her English clothes. She stood dressed and determined and prosperous, her smart little toque held closely against her dark hair and sallow face with the kind of chenille-spotted veil that was a rampart against *everything* in the world to an Englishwoman. But it did not touch her or do anything for her. It gave an effect of prison bars behind which she was hanging her head and weeping and appealing. One could have laughed and gathered her up. Why was she forlorn? Why did she imagine that one was also forlorn? The sight of her made all the forlornness one had ever seen or read about seem peopled with knowledge and sympathy and warm thoughts that flew crowding along one’s brain as close and bright as the texture of everybody’s everyday. But the eyes were anxious and preoccupied, blinking now and then in her long unswerving appealing gaze, shutting swiftly for lightning calculations between her rapid appealing statements. What was she trying to do?
---
She tried to stand in front of everything, to put everything aside as if it were part of something she knew. Laughing over it with Mr. Hancock would not dispose of that. After the fun of telling him, she would still be there, with the two bicycle lessons that were going begging.

===Chinese===

生、又想学骑车的人。全错了。“您知道——我并不赞许威尔逊先生；您若听他讲话，兴许也不会赞许，还有……他的婚事……您知道。…”
……倘若我那样做了，我便轻松而有力，等她谈起步骑车来也能“敷衍应酬”了。我像舞会上那个小伙子，因为想不出话同姑娘说，便向她求了婚。他想不出话说，是因为心里搁着事。…
那么，齐格蒙迪小姐今天上午也就不会来了。
---
“没有人念得对我的名字（No one can pgonounce my name）。你还是叫我泰盖丝（Thégèse）吧，好姑娘。对，就这么办；我要你这样叫。”她说这话时一脸愁容，一副突然的、不笑的亲昵。她一定有个盘算。站在那儿，带着她那副心碎的嗓音和焦虑的脸，她仿佛同这间屋子分开了，甚至同她自己的衣裳也分开了。可她体内的什么东西动得那样快，叫人喘不上气。她那样专注，全然不觉自己裹在英国衣裳里缩着的那一小团身影多么惹人怜。她站在那里，穿戴齐整，决心坚定，气派富足，那顶时新的小帽紧贴着她的黑发和黄脸，配着那种满是小点儿的雪绒纱——对英国女人而言是一堵挡住世上一切的壁垒。可它碰不着她，也帮不了她什么。它造成一种效果：监牢的铁栅，她在栅后垂着头、哭着、哀告着。人真可以笑一笑，把她一把拢起来。她为什么凄惶？她凭什么想象人也凄惶？看见她，人平生见过读过的一切凄惶，都仿佛住满了知识和同情，一阵阵温暖明亮的念头紧紧地、亮亮地沿着脑子飞涌而过，稠密得同每个人每天的日常一样。可那双眼睛焦虑而恍惚，在她那久久不肯移开、一味哀告的凝视里，时时眨一眨，在她一条条急促的哀告之间，飞快地合上，打着闪电般的算盘。她想干什么？
---
她想把什么都挡在身前，想把什么都拨到一边，仿佛它们全是她某项已知的一部分。同汉考克先生笑谈一场，了不掉这个。把讲给他听的乐趣享完，她还在那儿，还有那两堂没人领受的自行车课。

He knew already that she had been and would assume that she had suggested things and that one was not going to do them. She does not want anybody. She does not know the difference between one person and another.﻿ ﻿… He knows only her social manner. She has never been alone with him and come close and shown him her determination and her sorrow﻿ ﻿… sorrow﻿ ﻿… sorrow.﻿ ﻿…
He could never see that it was impossible without forcibly crushing her, to get out of doing some part of what she desired.﻿ ﻿…
If one were drawn in and did things, let oneself want to do things for anyone else, there would be a change in the atmosphere at Wimpole Street. That never occurred to him. But he would feel it if it happened. If there were someone near who made distractions there would be a difference, something that was not given to him. He was so unaware of this. He was absolutely ignorant of what it was that kept things going as they were.

他已经知道她来过，并会假定是她提了些什么，而人不打算照办。人若把课程的事告诉他，他会说那太好了，而且是真心的。他一向把“好意”之举真当好意……可她来，不是因为她要我。她谁也不要。她分不清这个人和那个人。……他只认得她的社交做派。她从没单独同他在一起、凑近他、让他看看她的决心和她的悲哀……悲哀……悲哀。……
他永远不会明白：不硬生生碾碎她，就不可能一点不做她所愿的事，脱得了身。……
人若被拽进去、做了事，纵容自己想为别人做事，温波尔街的空气就要变了。这一点他从来没想过。可它一旦发生，他会觉出来的。倘若近旁有一个制造分心的人，就会有一样不同，一样不是给他的东西。他对此毫无知觉。到底是什么东西使一切照旧运转下去，他全然无知。

## IX / 九
===Original===

The cycling school was out of sight and done with and Miriam hurried down the Chalk Farm Road. If only she could see an omnibus and be in it going anywhere down away from the north. Miss Szigmondy had brought shame and misery upon her, in Chalk Farm. There was nothing there to keep off the pain. Once back she would never think of Chalk Farm again. How could anyone think it was a place, like other places? It was torture even to be in it, going through it.﻿ ﻿… Of course the man had thought I should take on a course of lessons and pay for them. I have to learn everything meanly and shamefully. He thinks I’m getting all I can for nothing. The people in the bus will see me pay my fare and I shall be all right again, going down there. What an *awful* road, going on and on with nothing in it. I am shamed and helpless; *helpless*. It’s no use to try and do anything. It always exposes me and brings this maddening shame and pain. It’s over again this time and I shall soon forget it altogether. I might just as well begin to stop thinking about it now. It’s this part of London. It’s like Banbury Park. The people are absolutely awful. They take cycling lessons quite coolly. They are not afraid of anybody. To them this part is the best bit of North London. They are that sort of people. They are all alike. All of them would dislike me. I should die of being with them.
Why is it that no one seems to know what north London is? They say it is healthy and open. Perhaps I shall meet someone who feels like I do about it and would get ill and die there. It is not imagination. It is a real feeling that comes upon me.﻿ ﻿…
The north London omnibus reached the tide of the Euston Road and pulled up at Portland Road station. Miriam got out weak and ill. The first breath of the central air revived her. Standing there, the omnibus looked like any other omnibus. She crossed the road, averting her eyes from the north-going roads on either side of the church and got into the inmost corner of another bus. She wanted to ride about, getting from bus to bus, inside London until her misery had passed. Opposite her was a stout woman in a rusty bonnet and shawl and dust-defaced black skirt, looking about with eyes that did not see what they looked at, all the London consciousness in her. Miriam sat gazing at her. The woman’s eyes crossed her and passed unperturbed.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

骑车学校已经望不见了、结束了，米里亚姆顺着查克农场（Chalk Farm）路匆勿往下走。但愿能看见一辆公共马车，坐上去随便往哪儿，往南，离开北边。齐格蒙迪小姐在查克农场把羞耻和悲惨加在了她身上。那里没有任何东西挡得住疼痛。一旦回去，她再也不会想查克农场。怎么会有人把它当成一个地方，同别处一样的地方？哪怕只是身处其中、穿行而过，也是刑罚。……当然，那男人以为我会订一整套课、付一整套的钱。我什么都得学得这样卑贱、这样丢脸。他一定当我是在能白拿多少拿多少。车上的人会看见我付车钱，我就没事了，往南去了。多可怕的一条路，没完没了地走，里面什么也没有。我又羞又无助；无助。试也无益。一试就把我暴露出来，招来这叫人发疯的羞和疼。这回也过去了，我很快就会忘个精光。不妨现在就开始不去想它。是伦敦的这一片。像班伯里公园。这里的人坏透了。他们学骑车学得若无其事。他们谁也不怕。对他们，这一片是北伦敦最好的地段。他们是那种人。全一个样。他们个个都会不喜欢我。同他们待在一起我会死掉。

为什么似乎没有人知道北伦敦是什么？他们说它有益健康、开阔。也许我会遇上一个同我感受一样的人，在那里病倒、死掉。这不是想象。这是来到我身上的一种真实的感觉。…

北伦敦的公共马车抵达尤斯顿路的车流大潮，在波特兰路车站停住。米里亚姆下车，虚弱，想吐。中心区的空气第一口就使她复活了。站在那里望，那辆马车同别的马车没有任何两样。她穿过马路，避开眼睛不去看教堂两侧通往北边的一条条马路，上了另一辆车，坐进最里面的角落。她想就这么乘来乘去，一辆换一辆，在伦敦里面乘，直到她的悲惨过去。对面坐着一个教实的女人，戴一顶褪色的软帽，披披肩，黑裙子上蒙满尘痕，四下张望着，一双眼望着却不见所望之物，满身都是伦敦的意识。米里亚姆坐着端详她。那女人的目光扫过她，平静无事地过去了。…

===Original===

The lane of little shops flowed away, their huddled detail crushing together, wide shop windows glittered steadily by and narrowed away. When the bus stopped at Gower Street the spire of St. Pancras church came into sight spindling majestically up, screened by trees.
The trees in Endsleigh Gardens came along gently waving their budding branches in bright sunshine. The colour of the gardens was so intense that the sun must just be going to set behind Euston Station. The large houses moved steadily behind the gardens in blocks, bright white, with large quiet streets opening their vistas in between the blocks, leading to green freshness and then safely on down into Soho. The long square came to an end. The shrub-trimmed base of St. Pancras church came heavily nearer and stopped. As Miriam got out of the bus she watched its great body rise in clear sharp outline against the blue. Its clock was booming the hour out across the gardens through the houses and down into the squares. On this side its sound was broken up by the narrow roar of the Euston Road and the clamour coming right and left from the two great stations.
Her feet tramped happily across the square of polished roadway patterned with shadows and along the quiet clean sunlit pavement behind the gardens. It was always bright and clean and quiet and happy there, like the pavement of a road behind a seafront. The sound of a mail van rattling heavily along Woburn Place changed to a soft rumble as she turned in between the great houses of Tansley Street and walked along its silent corridor of afternoon light. Sparrows were cheeping in the stillness. To be able to go down the quiet street and on into the squares﻿—on a bicycle.﻿ ﻿… I must learn somehow to get my balance. To go along, like in that moment when he took his hands off the handlebars, in knickers and a short skirt and all the summer to come.﻿ ﻿… Everything shone with a greater intensity. Friends and thought and work were nothing compared to being able to ride alone, balanced, going along through the air.
On the hall table was a postcard. “Come round on Sunday if you’re in town﻿—*Irlandisches Ragout*. Mag.” Her heart stirred; that settled it﻿—the girls wanted her; Mag wanted her. She took Alma’s crumpled letter from her pocket and glanced through it once more﻿ ﻿… “such a dull Sunday and all your fault. Why did you not come?

===Chinese===

小巷般的一排小铺子流走了，挤作一团的细节挤成一团，宽宽的橱窗稳稳地闪着光过去，又缩窄远去。车到高尔街，圣潘克拉斯教堂的尖塔进入视野，细高庄严地拔起，掩在树后。

恩德斯利花园的树轻轻过来，把含苞的枝条在明亮的阳光里摇着。花园的颜色那样浓，太阳一定正要落到尤斯顿车站背后去。大房子一栋一栋稳稳地随在花园后面，亮白，之间敞开一条条安静的宽街，街的远景通向清新的绿，再稳稳地一路向下通进苏荷。长长的广场到了头。圣潘克拉斯教堂修剪过的灌木底座沉沉地逼近、停住。米里亚姆下车时，望着它巨大的身躯以清晰锐利的轮廓升起，衬着蓝天。它的钟正把钟点轰出花园、穿过一排排房子、送进一个个广场。这一侧，钟声被尤斯顿路窄窄的轰鸣和左右两大火车站的人声鼎沸搅碎了。

她的双脚欢欢喜喜地踏过那方磨亮的、印满树影的路面，沿花园背后那条安静、干净、洒满阳光的人行道走去。那里永远明亮、干净、安静、快活，像海滨大道背后那条街的人行道。一辆邮车沿沃本坊（Woburn Place）隆隆驶过的声响，在她拐进坦斯利街（Tansley Street）的大宅之间、走在那条午后之光的寂静长廊里时，化成了柔软的轰鸣。静寂里麻雀啁啾。能顺着这条安静的街一路走下去、走进一个个广场——骑一辆自行车。…我总得想法子学会保持平衡。就这样走，像他双手离开车把的那一瞬那样，穿灯笼裤、短裙，整个夏天在前面。…一切都在更大的强度里发亮。同能独自骑车、保持平衡、穿过空气前行相比，朋友、思想、工作都不算什么。

门厅桌上有一张明信片。“星期天若在城里就来——爱尔兰式炖肉（Irlandisches Ragout）。玛格（Mag）”她的心一动；这就定了——姑娘们要她来；玛格要她来。她从口袋里掏出阿尔玛皱巴巴的信，又草草看了一遍…“这么乏味的一个星期天，全怪你。你干吗不来？星期六随时来，若周末挪不开就星期天早晨来？”幸亏没写信答应去。她将在伦敦，星期天安然待在肯尼特街（Kennett Street）。玛格说得全对；出门一趟叫你一星期定不下神，星期天也捞不着。她看看表，五点半；半个钟头后姑娘们多半已在斯莱特了；伦敦的周末这一分钟就可以开始；那些半盼着她的人——布鲁姆一家、珀恩家、萨拉和哈丽特、威尔逊家——此刻都在遥远的各自的家里；她安然待在布卢姆斯伯里（Bloomsbury），在大宅子里，在宽厚的大街上，在肯尼特街；他们谁也不知道的地方；安然度过整个周末。星期六原来看着那样堵——骑车课、访齐格蒙迪小姐，还有余下时间的种种去处的搾择。…“哦，我星期六的约会有五十个呢”，如今星期六空了，她觉得这个周末什么都应付得来。多大的发现啊——躲在伦敦的宅子里，把一切一撂，下去，把抛下的约请、把那些殷勤护佑的朋友们统统搁置一边；独自轻轻爽爽穿过闪着微光的绿广场，一路到斯特兰德街（the Strand）尽头，找到斯莱特。…我再也不出伦敦一步了。姑娘们说得对。不值得。

===Original===

Come on Saturday *any* time or Sunday morning if you can’t manage the weekend?” What a good thing she had not written promising to go. She would be in London, safe in Kennett Street for Sunday. Mag was quite right; going away unsettled you for the week and you did not *get* Sunday. She looked at her watch, five-thirty; in half an hour the girls would probably be at Slater’s; the London weekend could begin this minute; all the people who half-expected her, the Brooms, the Pernes, Sarah and Harriett, the Wilsons, would be in their homes far away; she safe in Bloomsbury, in the big house, the big kind streets, Kennett Street; places they none of them knew; safe for the whole length of the weekend. Saturday had looked so obstructed, with the cycling lesson, and the visit to Miss Szigmondy and the many alternatives for the rest of the time.﻿ ﻿… “Oh I’ve got about *fifty* engagements for Saturday” and now Saturday was clear and she felt equal to anything for the weekend. What a discovery, standing hidden, there in the London house, to drop everything and go down, with all the discarded engagements, all the solicitous protecting friends put aside; easy and alone through the glimmering green squares to the end of the Strand and find Slater’s.﻿ ﻿… I’ll never stir out of London again. The girls are right. It isn’t worth it.
---
She saw the girls seated at a table at the far end of the big restaurant and shyly advanced.
“Hulloh child!”
“What you having?” she asked sitting down opposite to them. The empty white tablecloth shone under a brilliant incandescent light; far away down the vista the door opened on the daylit street.
“Isn’t it a glorious Spring evening?” Spring? It was, of course. Everyone had been saying the spring would never come, but today it was very warm. Spring was here of course. Perspiring in a dusty cycling school and sitting in a hot restaurant was not spring. Spring was somewhere far away. Going to stay and talk in people’s houses did not bring Spring﻿—landscapes belonging to people were *painted*; you must be alone﻿ ﻿… or perhaps at the Brooms. Perhaps next weekend at the Brooms would be in time for the spring; in their back garden, the watered green lawn and the sweetbriar and the distant trees in the large garden beyond the fence. In London it was better not to think about the times of year.
But Mag seemed to find Spring in London.

===Chinese===

她远远看见姑娘们坐在大餐馆尽头的一张桌旁，怯生生地走过去。

“喂，孩子！”

“你们吃什么？”她问，在她们对面坐下。空白桌布在雪亮的白炽灯下发光；长长视线的尽头，门开向亮着天光的街。

“多好的春夜，是不是？”春天？是啊，当然。人人都在说春天永远不会来了，可今天真暖。春天当然在这里。在尘土飞扬的骑车学校里出汗、坐在闷热的餐馆里，不是春天。春天在远处什么地方。去别人家里坐着谈天，带不来春天——属于人的风景是画出来的；你必须独自一人…也许在布鲁姆家可以。也许下个周末在布鲁姆家正赶上春天；在他们家的后花园，浇过水的绿草坪，甜蔷薇，篱笆外大花园里的远树。在伦敦，最好不去想时令。

可玛格似乎在伦敦也找得到春天。

===Original===

Her face was all glowing with the sense of it.
“What you having?”
“Have you observed with what a remarkable brilliance the tender green shines out against the soot-black branches?” Yes, that was wonderful but what was the joke?
“Every spring I have spent in Lonndonn I have heard that remark at least fifty times.”
Miriam laughed politely. “Jan, *what* have you ordered?”
“We’ve ordered beef my child, cold beefs and salads.”
“Do you think I should like salad?”
“If you *had* a brother would he like salad?”
“Do they put dressing on it? If I could have just plain lettuce.”
“Ask for it my child, ask and it shall be given unto thee.”
A waitress brought the beef and salad, two glasses with an inch of whisky in each, and a large syphon.
Miriam ordered beef and potatoes.
“I suppose the steak and onion days are over.”
“I shan’t have another steak and onions, please God, until next November.”
Miriam laughed delightedly.
“Why haven’t you gone away for the weekend, child?”
“I told you she wouldn’t.”
“I don’t know. I wanted to come down here.”
“Is that a compliment to us?”
“I say, I’ve had a bicycle lesson.”
Both faces came up eagerly.
“You remember; that extraordinary woman I met at the Royal Institution.”
The faces looked at each other.
“Oh you know; I *told* you about it﻿—the two lessons she didn’t want.”
“Go on my child; we remember; go on.”
Miriam sat eating her beef.
“Go on Miriam. You’ve really had a lesson. I’m delighted my child. Tell us all about it.”
“D’you remember the extraordinary moment when you felt the machine going along; even with the man holding the handlebars?”
“You wait until there’s nobody to hold the handlebars.”
“Have you been out alone yet?”
The two faces looked at each other.
“Shall we tell her?”
“You *must* tell me; *es ist bestimmt in Gottes Rath*.” They leaned across the table and spoke low one after the other. “We went out﻿—last night﻿—after dark﻿—and rode﻿—round Russell Square﻿—twice﻿—in our knickers﻿—”
“*No.* Did you really? How simply heavenly.”
“It *was*. We came home nearly crying with rage at not being able to go about, permanently, in nothing but knickers. It would make life an *absolutely* different thing.”
“The freedom of movement.”
“Exactly. You feel like a sprite you are so light.”
“And like a poet though you don’t know it.”

===Chinese===

她的脸因为这个感觉而整个放着光。

“你们吃什么？”

“你可曾注意到，那娇嫩的绿衬着煤烟色的黑枝子，闪耀出怎样一种非凡的光彩？”是啊，那确实妙，可笑点在哪儿？

“我在伦——敦（Lonndonn）过的每个春天，这话我至少听过五十遍。”

米里亚姆礼貌地笑了。“简，你们到底点了什么？”

“我们点了牛肉，孩子，冷牛肉和沙拉。”

“你们觉得我吃得来沙拉吗？”

“你若有个兄弟，他吃得来沙拉吗？”

“他们往上浇调味汁吗？要是能给我光生菜就好了。”

“要呀，孩子，求，就给你们。”

女侍端来牛肉和沙拉，两只杯子各盛一英寸威士忌，还有一只大苏打水瓶。

米里亚姆点了牛肉和土豆。

“我看牛排洋葱的日子结束了。”

“上帝保佑，到十一月前我不再吃牛排洋葱了。”

米里亚姆开心地笑。

“周末你怎么没出城，孩子？”

“我说过她不会去的。”

“不知道。我想下来这儿。”

“这是恭维我们吗？”

“喂，我上了一堂骑车课。”

两张脸急切地抬起来。

“你们记得吧；我在皇家研究院碰到的那个怪女人。”

两张脸对望。

“哦你们知道的；我同你们讲过——她不要的那两堂课。”

“讲下去，孩子；我们记得；讲下去。”

米里亚姆坐着吃她的牛肉。

“讲呀米里亚姆。你真上了一课。我真高兴，孩子。一五一十讲给我们听。”

“你们记不记得那奇妙的时刻，你觉出机器自己在往前走；哪怕那男人还扶着车把？”

“等到没人扶车把的时候你再看吧。”

“你们单独出去过没有？”

两张脸对望。

“告诉她吗？”

“你们必须告诉我；此乃上帝旨意所定（es ist bestimmt in Gottes Rath）。”她们隔着桌子俯过来，低声一句接一句。“我们出去了——昨晚——天黑以后——骑着——绕罗素广场——两圈——穿着灯笼裤——”

“不会吧。真骑了？简直如登天堂。”

“真的。我们回到家，气得差点哭——气不能就这么永远穿着灯笼裤到处走。那会把生活变成全然不同的东西。”

“行动的自由。”

“正是。你轻得像个小精灵。”

“又像一位诗人，虽然你自己不知道。”

===Original===

“You feel like a sprite you are so light, and you feel so strong and capable and so broadshouldered you could knock down a policeman. Jan and I knocked down several last night.”
“Yes; and it is not only that; think of never having to brush your skirt.”
“I know. It would be bliss.”
“I spend half my life brushing my skirt. If I miss a day I notice it﻿—if I miss two days the office notices it. If I miss three days the public notices it.”
“*La vie est dure; pour les femmes.*”
“You don’t want to be a man Jan.”
“Oh I do, sometimes. They have the best of everything all round.”
“*I* don’t. I wouldn’t be a man for anything. I wouldn’t have a man’s﻿—*consciousness*, for anything.”
“Why not asthore?”
“They’re too absolutely pigheaded and silly.﻿ ﻿…”
“*Isn’t* she intolerant?”
Miriam sat flaring. That was not the right answer. There was something; and they must know it; but they would not admit it.
“Then you can both really ride?”
“We do nothing else; we’ve given up walking; we no longer walk up and downstairs; we ride.”
Miriam laughed her delight. “I can quite understand; it alters everything. I realised that this afternoon at the school. To be able to bicycle would make life utterly different; on a bicycle you feel a different person; nothing can come near you, you forget who you are. Aren’t you glad you are alive today, when all these things are happening?”
“What things little one?”
“Well cycling and things. You know, girls, when I’m thirty I’m going to cut my hair short and wear divided skirts.”
Both faces came up.
“Why on earth?”
“I can’t face doing my hair and brushing skirts and keeping more or less in the fashion, that means about two years behind because I never realise fashions till they’re just going, even if I could afford to﻿—all my life.”
“Then why not do it now?”
“Because all my friends and relatives would object. It would worry them too﻿—they would feel quite sure then I should never marry﻿—and they still entertain hopes, secretly.”
“Don’t you want to marry﻿—ever; ever?”
“Well﻿—it would mean giving up this life.”
“Yes, I know. I agree there. That can’t be faced.”
“I should think *not*. Aren’t you going to have any pudding?”
“But why thirty? Why not thirty-one?”
“Because nobody cares what you do when you’re thirty; they’ve all given up hope by that time. Aren’t you two going to have any pudding?”
“No. But that is no reason why you should not.”

===Chinese===

“你轻得像个小精灵，又觉得自己那样强壮、那样能干、那样肩膀宽阔，一拳能撂倒个警察。简和我昨晚撂倒了几个。”

“可不是；还有呢——想想再也不用刷裙子。”

“可不是。那才叫福气。”

“我半辈子都花在刷裙子上。一天不刷我自己知道——两天不刷办公室知道。三天不刷公众就知道。”

“生活是艰难的；对于女人（La vie est dure; pour les femmes）。”

“你不想当男人吧，简。”

“哦想，有时候。样样好事都是他们的。”

“我就不想。拿什么都不换我都不当男人。拿什么都不换我都不要男人的——意识（consciousness）。”

“为什么不想呢，宝贝儿（asthore）？”

“他们蠢得那样绝对、那样死脑筋。…”

“她这不是太不能容人了吗？”

米里亚姆坐在那里冒火。那不是对的回答。有个东西在；她们一定知道；可她们不肯认。

“那你们俩是真会骑了？”

“我们别的什么也不干了；走路都戒了；上下楼也不走了；都骑。”

米里亚姆笑出她的欢喜。“我完全明白；它改变一切。今天下午在学校我就明白了。会骑车会把生活整个变样；骑在车上你觉得自己是另一个人；什么也挨不着你，你忘了自己是谁。活在今天这一切正在发生的年代，你们不觉得幸运吗？”

“什么事呀，小家伙？”

“骑车这一类的事呗。你们知道吗，姑娘们，我到三十岁要把头发剪短，穿分裙裤。”

两张脸抬起来。

“到底为什么？”

“梳头、刷裙子、多少跟一跟时兴——跟也总落在后头两年，因为时兴总要等快过去了我才发觉——这些事我做不来，哪怕我付得起——做不了一辈子。”

“那为什么不现在就做？”

“因为我所有的朋友亲戚都要反对。他们也要操心——那样他们就彻底确信我永远嫁不出去了——而他们心里还偷偷抱着指望呢。”

“你不想结婚吗——永远不？”

“呢——那意味着放弃这种生活。”

“是，这个我懂。这一点没法面对。”

“可不是嘛。你们不要布丁了吗？”

“可为什么是三十岁？为什么不是三十一？”

“因为人到了三十，你做什么都没人管了；那时候他们全都死心了。你们俩真不要布丁了？”

“不要。可这不等于你也不要。”

===Original===

“What a good idea﻿—to have just one dish and coffee.”
“That’s what we think; and it’s cheap.”
“Well, I couldn’t have had any dinner at all, only I’m cadging dinner with you tomorrow.”
“What would you have done?”
“An egg, at an A.B.C.”
“How fond you are of A.B.C.s.”
“I love them.”
“What is it that you love about them?”
“Chiefly I think their dowdiness. The food is honest; not showy, and they are so blissfully dowdy.”
Both girls laughed.
“It’s no good. I have come to the conclusion I like dowdiness. I’m not smart. You are.”
“This is the first we have heard of it.”
“Well you know you are. You keep in the fashion. It may be quite right, perhaps you are more sociable than I am.”
“One is so conspicuous if one is not dressed more or less like other people.”
“That’s what I hate; dressing like other people. If I could afford it I should be stylish﻿—not smart. Perfect coats and skirts and a few good evening dresses. But you must be awfully well off for that. If I can’t be stylish I’d rather be dowdy and in a way I like dowdiness even better than stylishness.”
The girls laughed.
“But aren’t clothes awful, anyhow? I’ve spent four and eleven on my knickers and I can’t possibly get a skirt till next year if then, or afford to hire a machine.”
“Why don’t you ask them to raise your salary?”
“After four months? Besides any fool could do the work.”
“If I were you I should tell them. I should say ‘Gentlemen﻿—I wish for a skirt and a bicycle.’ ”
“Mag, don’t be so silly.”
“I *can’t* see it. *They* would benefit by your improved health and spirits. Jan and I are new women since we have learned riding. *I* am thinking of telling the governor I must have a rise to meet the increased demands of my appetite. Our housekeeping expenses I shall say are doubled. What *will* you? *Que faire?*”
“You see the work I’m doing is not worth more than a pound a week﻿—my languages are no good there. I suppose I ought to learn typing and shorthand; but where could I find the money for the training?”
“Will you teach her shorthand if I teach her typing?”
“Certainly, if the child wants to learn. I don’t advise her.”
“Why not, Jan. *You* did. How long would it take me in evenings?”
“A year at least, to be marketable. It’s a vile thing to learn, unless you are thoroughly stupid.”
“That’s true. Jan was a perfect fool. The more intelligent you are the longer you take.”
“You see it isn’t a language.

===Chinese===

“好主意——只要一道菜加咖啡。”

“我们就是这么想的；还便宜。”

“唉，我本来连饭都吃不上，还不是明天要蹭你们一顿晚饭。”

“不然你怎么办？”

“在A.B.C.吃个鸡蛋。”

“你多喜欢A.B.C.呀。”

“我爱它们。”

“你爱它们哪一点？”

“主要是我看是它们那股寒磣劲儿。东西实在；不摆谱，而且它们寒磣得叫人幸福。”

两个姑娘都笑了。

“没法子。我得出的结论是：我喜欢寒磣。我不时新。你们时新。”

“这倒头一回听说。”

“你们知道的呀。你们跟着时兴走。也许挺对，兴许你们比我更合群。”

“穿得同旁人差不多，人就不显眼；不然就太显眼。”

“我恨的就是这个；同旁人穿得一样。我若付得起，我要讲究——不是时新。十全十美的大衣和裙子，几身好的晚装。可那得非常有钱。讲究不成，我宁可寒磣，而且某种意义上，寒磣我比讲究还更喜欢些。”

姑娘们笑了。

“可衣裳这东西怎么说都可怕吧？我这条灯笼裤花了四先令十一便士，裙子明年也未必买得上，车也租不起。”

“为什么不叫他们给你加薪水？”

“才干了四个月？再说这活儿随便哪个傻瓜都会干。”

“我要是你我就去说。我就说：‘诸位先生——我想要一条裙子和一辆自行车。’”

“玛格，别犯傻。”

“我就是不明白。你身子骨、精气神儿好了，受益的是他们。简和我自打学会骑车，简直是新女性。我正琢磨着告诉老板，我的胃口见涨，必须加薪。我就说，我们的伙食开支翻了一番。你说怎么办？怎么办呢（Que faire）？”

“你们看，我做的活儿一星期就值一镑——我的语言在那里派不上用场。我想我该去学打字和速记；可培训的钱从哪儿来？”

“我教她打字，你教她速记，怎么样？”

“当然行，只要孩子想学。不过我不劝她。”

“为什么不劝，简。你自己就学过。每晚学，我得学多久？”

“至少一年，才能上市。那是样下贱东西，除非你彻底蠢。”

“这话不假。简从前是个十足的傻瓜。人越聪明，学得越久。”

“你瞧，它不是一门语言。”

===Original===

It is an arbitrary system of signs.”
“With your intelligence you’d probably grow grey at the school. Wouldn’t she, Jan?”
“Probably.”
“Besides I can’t imagine Mistress Miriam in an office.”
“Nobody would have me. I’m not businesslike enough. I am learning bookkeeping at their expense. And don’t forget they give me lunch and tea. I say, are we going to read *The Evolution of the Idea of God* tonight?”
“Yes. Let’s get back and get our clothes off. If I don’t have a cigarette within half an hour I shall die.”
“Oh, so shall I. I had forgotten the existence of cigarettes.”
Out in the street Miriam felt embarrassed. The sunset glow broke through wherever there was a gap towards the northwest, and flooded a strip of the street and struck a building. The presence of the girls added a sharpness to its beauty, especially the presence of Mag who felt the spring even in London. But both of them seemed entirely oblivious. They marched along at a great rate, very upright and swift﻿—like grenadiers﻿—why grenadiers? Like grenadiers, making her hurry in a way that increased the discomfort of her hard cheap down-at-heel shoes. Their high-heeled shoes were in perfect condition and they went on and on laughing and jesting as if there were no spring evening all round them. She wanted to stroll, and stop at every turn of the road. She grew to dislike them both long before Kennett Street was reached, their brisk gait as they walked together in step, leaving her to manoeuvre the passing of pedestrians on the narrow pavements of the side streets, the self-confident set of their this-season’s clothes, “line” clothes, like everyone else was wearing, everyone this side of the west-end; Oxford Street clothes﻿ ﻿… and to long to be wandering home alone through the leafy squares. Were people who lived together always like this, always brisk and joking and keeping it up? They got on so well together﻿ ﻿… and she got on so well too with them. “No one ever feels a third,” Mag had said. I am tired, too tired. They are stronger than I am. I feel dead; and they are perfectly fresh.
“D’you know I believe I feel too played out to read,” she said at their door.
“Then come in and smoke,” said Mag taking her arm. “The night is yet young.”

===Chinese===

“它是一套专断的符号。”

“以你的聪明，在学校里怕要学到头发白。是不是，简？”

“多半是。”

“再说我想象不出米里亚姆女师傅坐办公室的样子。”

“也没人要我。我够不上生意人。我在他们的开销上学着记账呢。别忘了他们还供我午饭和茶。我说，今晚我们读《上帝观念的演化》（The Evolution of the Idea of God）吗？”

“读。回去把外衣脱了。半个钟头内抽不上一支烟，我就要死了。”

“哦，我也是。我把香烟的存在都忘了。”

到了街上，米里亚姆觉得不自在。落日的霞光从朝西北的每一道缝隙里挤出来，淹了半条街，照亮一栋楼。姑娘们的在场给这份美添了一分锐利，尤其是玛格的在场——她在伦敦也感觉得到春天。可她们俩似乎全然不觉。她们大步流星地走着，笔直、飞快——像掷弹兵——为什么像掷弹兵？就是像掷弹兵，叫她一路赶，赶得她那双又硬又贱、后跟磨塌的鞋愈发受罪。她们的高跟鞋完好无损，她们走下去、走下去，一路说笑，仿佛四周根本没有春夜。她想闲步走，每个拐弯都想停一停。还没到肯尼特街，她就已经开始不喜欢她们俩了——她们齐步走时那股轻快，在小街窄窄的人行道上把躲让行人的活儿全留给她，她们这一季的衣裳那副自信的样子，“线条”衣裳，人人都穿的那种，西区这一头人人如此；牛津街的衣裳……她一心想独自一人穿过一一方方绿荫广场溜达回家。住在一起的人永远这样吗，永远这样轻快、说笑、撑个没完？她们俩处得那样好……她同她们也处得好。“第三个人永远没人觉出来，”玛格说过。我累了，太累了。她们比我强。我觉得死了；她们却精神抖撇。

“你们知道吗，我怕是乏得读不动了，”到了她们门口她说。

“那就进来抽烟，”玛格挽住她的胳膊说。“夜还年轻呢。”

## X / 十
===Original===

Miriam swung her legs from the table and brought her tilted chair to the ground. The leads sloped down as she got to her feet and the strip of sky disappeared. The sunlight made a broad strip of gold along the parapet and a dazzling plaque upon the slope of the leads. She lounged into the shadowy middle of the room and stood feeling tall and steady and easy and agile in the freedom of knickers. The clothes lying on the bed were transformed. “I say,” she murmured, her cigarette end wobbling encouragingly from the corner of her lips as she spoke, “they’re not bad.” She strolled about the room glancing at them from different points of view. They really made quite a good whole. It was the lilac that made them a good whole, the fresh heavy blunt cones of pure colour. In the distance the bunched ribbon looked almost all green. She drew the hat nearer to the light and the ribbon became mauve with green shadows and green with mauve shadows as it moved. The girl had been right about bunching the ribbon a little way up the sugar-loaf and over the wide brim. It broke the papery stiffness of the lilac and the harshness of the black straw. The straw looked very harsh and black in the clearer light. Out of doors it would look almost as if it had been done with that awful shiny hat polish. If the straw had been dull and silky and some shaded tone of mauve and green it would have been one of those hats that give you a sort of madness, taking your eyes in and in, with the effect of a misty distant woodland brought near and moving, depths of interwoven colour under your eyes. But it would not have gone with the black and white check. The black part of the hat was right for the tiny check. That is the idea of some smart woman.﻿ ﻿… I did not think of it in the shop, but I got it right somehow, I can see now. It’s right. Those might be someone else’s things.﻿ ﻿… The sight of the black suède gloves and the lace-edged handkerchief and the powder box laid out on the chest of drawers made her eager to begin. This was dressing. The way to feel you were dressing was to put everything out first and come back as another person and make a grand toilet. It makes you feel free and leisurely. There had been the long strange morning. In half an hour the adventure would begin and go on and be over. The room would not be in it. Something nice or horrible would come back.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆把腿从桌上放下来，翘着的椅子落回地面。她一站起来，铅皮屋顶斜下去，那一条天空不见了。阳光在女儿墙上铺出一条宽阔的金带，在屋面的坡上烙出一块晃眼的亮斑。她懒懒踱进屋子中央的暗处站着，穿着灯笼裤的自在里，觉得自己又高、又稳、又轻松、又矫捷。摊在床上的衣裳变了样。“我说，”她喃喃道，说话时烟卷头从她嘴角一翘一翘地鼓着劲，“还不赖嘛。”她在屋里踱来踱去，从各个角度看它们。它们当真凑成了一个不坏的整体。是那丁香花使它们成了个整体，那新鲜、沉甸、钝圆的一簇簇纯色。远看，那绾起的缎带几乎全绿。她把帽子拉近光处，缎带一转，紫中透出绿的影，绿中透出紫的影。那店员说得对，缎带在糖锥形帽身上挽高一截，再翻过宽帽檐。它破了丁香花纸一样的硬挺，也破了黑草帽的生硬。光更亮处看，草帽显得非常生硬、非常黑。到了户外，看着会几乎像抹过那种吓人的亮亮帽油。倘若草是暗的、丝一样柔的，带一点紫与绿的晕色，那就是那种叫人发疯的帽子了，把你的目光往里又往里地带，像一片迷蒙的远树林移到了眼前、还在动，交相叠映的颜色深藏在你的眼下。可它配不上黑白格呢。帽子的黑，配这细小的格子正对。这是哪位时新女人的主意。…在铺子里我可没想到，可不知怎么我拿对了，现在看得出来了。对了。这些也许是别人的东西。…黑麂皮手套、镶花边的手绢、粉盒在五斗柜上摆开的那一景，使她急着想开始。这才叫打扮。要觉出你在打扮，就得先把一切摆出来，然后换一个人似的回来，作一场盛妆。这使你觉得自由、从容。刚才那漫长古怪的上午已经过去了。半个钟头后，历险就要开始，进行，然后结束。房间不会参与其中。会有什么好东西或者可怕的东西回来。

===Original===

But the room would not be changed.
---
She found the dark green Atlas bus standing ready by the curb and waited until it was just about to start, looking impatiently up and down the long vistas of the empty Sunday street, and then jumped hurriedly in with the polite half-irritated resignation of the man about town who finds himself stranded in a godforsaken part of London, and steered herself carefully against the swaying of the vehicle along between the rows of seated forms, keeping her eyes carefully averted and fixed upon distant splendours. Securing an empty corner she sat down provisionally, on the edge of the seat, occupying the least possible space, clear of her neighbour, her eyes turned inwards on splendours still raking the street, her person ready to leap up at the sight of a crawling hansom﻿—telling herself in a drawl that she felt must somehow be audible to an observant listener how damnable it was that there were not hansoms in these remarkable backwoods﻿—so damned inconvenient when your own barrow is laid up at Windover’s. But a hansom might possibly appear.﻿ ﻿… She turned to the little corner window at her side and gazed with fierce abstraction down the oncoming street. Presently she would really be in a hansom. Miss Szigmondy had mentioned hansoms﻿ ﻿… supposing she should have to pay her share? Her heart beat rapidly and her face flushed as she thought of the fourpence in her purse. She would not be able even to offer. But if Miss Szigmondy were alone she would take cabs. There would be no need to mention it. The ambling trit-trot of the vehicle gradually prevailed over the mood in which she had dressed. She was becoming aware of her companions. Presently she would be taking them all in and getting into a world that had nothing to do with her afternoon. Turning aside so that her face could not be seen and her own vision might be restricted to the roadway rolling slowly upon her through the little end window she dreamed of contriving somehow or other to save money for hansoms. Hansoms were a necessary part of the worldly life. Floating about in a hansom in the west-end, in the season, was like nothing else in the world. It changed you, your feelings, manner, bearing, everything. It made you part of a wonderful exclusive difficult triumphant life, a streak of it, going in and out.

===Chinese===

可房间不会变。
---
她看见那辆深绿的阿特拉斯公共马车停在路沿候着，便等到它正要开动才上车，一面不耐烦地顺着星期天空街长长的视线上下游移，然后急急跳上去，带着城里人被困在伦敦鸟不生蛋地段时那副客气一半、恼火一半的听天由命，在左右摇晃的车厢里，顺着两排坐人中间小心地稳住自己，眼睛小心地避开，钉在远处的光华上。占住一个空角落后，她暂且坐下，坐在座位边上，占尽可能小的地方，不沾着邻座，眼睛向内，望着还在扫过街道的光华，整个人随时准备一看见一辆姗姗的双轮马车就弹起来——一面用一种她觉得心细的听者一定听得见的拖腔对自己念叨：这些了不得的穷乡僻壤竟没有双轮马车，真是可恨——自己的车又送进温多弗行里躺着，真真不便。可双轮马车兴许会出现。…她转向身边那个小小的角窗，狂然而出神地望着迎面而来的街。不多时她就要真的坐上双轮马车了。齐格蒙迪小姐提起过双轮马车…倘若要她摊份子呢？她想起钱包里那四便士，心跳快起来，脸也红了。她连请客的份都拿不出。可齐格蒙迪小姐若是独自出门，就会坐出租马车。到时候不提就是了。马车嘚嘚的缓步渐渐压过了她打扮时的心境。她开始觉出她的同伴们来了。不多时她就要把它们一并收进眼里，走进一个同她这个下午毫无关系的世界。她侧过身去，叫人看不见她的脸，叫自己的视野只限于那方小端窗里缓缓滚来的路面，一面梦想着如何想个法子攒出双轮马车的钱。双轮马车是世俗生活的必要部分。时令季节里，在西区坐着双轮马车漂来漂去，世上再没有同它一样的东西。它改变你，你的感情、举止、仪态，一切。它使你成为那种奇妙的、排外的、艰难的、凯旋的生活的一部分，它的一道纹路，出出入入。

===Original===

It cut you off from all personal difficulties, made you drop your personality and lifted you right out into the freedom of a throng of happy people, a great sunlit tide singing, all the same laughing song, wave after wave, advancing, in open sunlight. It took you on to a great stage, lit and decked, where you were lost, everything was lost and forgotten in the masque. Nothing personal could matter so long as you were there and kept there, day and night. Everyone was invisible and visionless, united in the spectacle, gilding and hiding the underworld in a brilliant embroidery﻿ ﻿… continuously.
As they rumbled up Baker Street, she wondered impatiently why Miss Szigmondy had not appointed a meeting place in the West end. Baker Street began all right; one felt safe going up Orchard Street, past the beautiful china shop and the Romish richness of Burns and Oates, seeing the sequestered worldliness of Granville Place and rolling through Portman Square with its enormous grey houses masking hidden wealth; but after that it became a dismal corridor retreating towards the full chill of the north. If they had met in Piccadilly they could have driven straight down through heaven into Chelsea. Perhaps it would not be heaven with Miss Szigmondy. She would not know the difference in the feeling of the different parts of London. She would drive along like a foreigner﻿—or a member of a provincial antiquarian society, “intelligently” noticing things, knowing about the buildings and the statues. Londoners were always twitted with not knowing about London﻿ ﻿… the reason why they jested about it, half proudly, was their consciousness of being Londoners, living in London, going about happy, the minute they were outside their houses, looking at nothing and feeling everything, like people wandering happily from room to room in a well known house at some time when everybody’s attention was turned away by a festival or a catastrophe.﻿ ﻿… London was like a prairie. In a hansom it would be heaven, with anybody. A hansom saved you from your companion more than any other vehicle. You were as much outside it in London as you were inside with your companion, if you were anywhere south of Marylebone﻿ ﻿… the way the open hood framed the vista.﻿ ﻿…
---
There was a hansom waiting outside Miss Szigmondy’s garden gate. The afternoon would begin at once with a swift drive back into the world.

===Chinese===

它把你从一切个人的难处里割出来，叫你卸下你的人身，把你整个提起来，放进一群快活人流的自由里，一片阳光的大潮，唱着同一支笑歌，一浪接一浪，在开阔的日光里前进。它把你带上一座点灯挂彩的大舞台，在那里你被淹没了，一切都被淹没、被忘却在假面剧里。只要你身在彼处、留在彼处，昼夜不息，就没有一件私人的事要紧。人人都是看不见的、不看的，在盛景中结成一体，用一片灿烂的绣幕给地下世界镀金、遮羞…连绵不断。

马车轰隆隆驶上贝克街，她不耐烦地纳闷，齐格蒙迪小姐为什么不把会面地点约在西区。贝克街开头还好；沿奥查德街上行，过那家漂亮的瓷器店和伯恩斯与奥茨那股罗马式的富丽，望一眼格兰维尔坊幽静的世俗气，再碾过波特曼广场一栋栋巨大的灰房子——掩着藏而不露的财富——这些都还叫人安稳；可再往前，它就成了一条阴惨的走廊，退向北边彻骨的寒。倘若约在皮卡迪利，她们本可以一路直下天堂，进切尔西南。同齐格蒙迪小姐一道，也许算不得天堂。她不会懂得伦敦各个地方的不同感觉。她会像个外国人一样一路坐过去——或者像个外省文物学会的会员，“聪明地”注意各样东西，知道建筑和雕像的掌故。伦敦人总被人笑说不认得伦敦…他们拿这个打趣、一半带着骄傲，缘由正是那份身为伦敦人的意识：住在伦敦，一出自己的家门就快活地走动，什么也不看而什么都感到，像人家在某个节日或灾变把所有人的注意都引开时，在一所熟稔的宅子里快活地从一个房间荡到另一个房间的人们。…伦敦像一片大草原。坐在双轮马车里，同谁一起都是天堂。双轮马车替你挡同伴，比任何别的车都挡得好。只要你身在马里波恩以南的任何地方，你在车里同同伴在一起的分量，就同你在车外的伦敦里一样重…那敞开的车罩取景一样框出街景的方式。…
---
齐格蒙迪小姐家的院门外正候着一辆双轮马车。这下午立刻就要以一趟返回尘世的疾驰开始。

===Original===

Miss Szigmondy met her in the dark hall, with an outbreak of bright guttural talk, talking as she collected her things, breaking in with shouted instructions to an invisible servant. Her voice sounded very foreign in the excited upper notes, but it rang, a thin wiry ring, not shrieking and breaking like the voices of excited Englishwomen, perhaps that was “voice production.”
In the cab she sat sorting her cards, reading out names. Miriam thrilled as she heard them. Miss Szigmondy’s attention was no longer on her. Her mind slipped easily back; the intervening time fell away. She was going with her sisters along past the Burlington Arcade, she saw the pillar box, the old man selling papers, the old woman with the crooked black sailor hat and the fringed shawl, sitting on a box behind her huge basket of tulips and daffodils﻿ ﻿… the great grimed stone pillars, the courtyard beyond them blazing with sunshine, the wide stone steps at the far end of the courtyard leading up into cool shadow, the turnstile and great hall, an archway, and the sudden fresh blaze of colours.﻿ ﻿…
---
But the hansom had turned into the main road and was going *north*. They were going even further north than Miss Szigmondy’s﻿ ﻿… up a straight empty Sunday suburban road between rows of suburban houses with gardens that tried to look pretty﻿ ﻿… an open silly prettiness like suburban ladies coming up to town for matinées﻿ ﻿… if there were artists living up here it would not be worth while to go and see them.﻿ ﻿…
---
As the afternoon wore on it dawned upon Miriam that if Miss Szigmondy were to be at the poet’s house in evening dress by half past six, they had seen nearly all they were going to see. There could be no thought of Chelsea. But she answered with a swift negative when Miss Szigmondy enquired as they were shown into their hansom outside their eighth large Hampstead house whether she were tired. Her unsatisfied consciousness ran ahead, waiting; just beyond, round the next corner was something that would relieve the oppression. “I just want to rgun in and see that poor boy Gilbert Haze.” Then it was over and she must go on enduring whilst Miss Szigmondy paid a call; unable to get free because she was being paid for and could not afford to go back alone.

===Chinese===

齐格蒙迪小姐在幽暗的门厅里迎她，迸出一串亮亮的喉音，一面收拾她的物件一面说，不时扬声对看不见的仆人喊一句吩咐。她兴奋时拔高的调门听着非常外国，可它发亮，一种细而韧的金属亮，不像英国女人兴奋时那样尖利破裂——兴许那就是“嗓音的训练”。

马车里她坐着理她的名片，一个个念出名字。米丽亚姆听着，一阵阵战栗。齐格蒙迪小姐的注意已不在她身上。她的心思轻轻滑了回去；中间的时日纷纷坠落。她正同姐妹们走过伯灵顿拱廊，她看见邮筒，卖报的老人，戴歪歪的黑水手帽、披流苏披肩的老妇，坐在她那一大篮郁金香和黄水仙后面的一只箱子上…巨大的、熏黑的石柱，柱后阳光烧亮的庭院，庭院尽头宽宽的石阶通向清凉的暗影，回转栏和大厅，一道拱门，随即猛然又一片色彩的烧灼。…
---
可马车拐上了大路，正往北走。他们要去的地方比齐格蒙迪小姐家还往北…沿一条笔直的、空荡荡的星期天郊外马路，两边一排排带花园的郊外住宅，那些花园拼命想显得俏丽…一种敞开的、傻气的俏丽，像进城里看日场的郊外太太们…倘若真有艺术家住在这里头，也不值得专程来看他们。…
---
下午过半，米丽亚姆才恍然：齐格蒙迪小姐若要六点半穿晚礼服赶到诗人府上，那她们要看的东西差不多已看完了。切尔西南想都别想。可当她们被让进第八幢汉普斯特德大宅门外的双轮马车、齐格蒙迪小姐问她累不累时，她飞快地答了个不字。她那不馁足的意识还在往前奔，等着；就在前头，下一个拐角，有一样东西会解开这股郁闷。“我只想跑进去看看那个可怜的男孩吉尔伯特·黑兹。”然后一切就完了，她还只得熬下去，趁齐格蒙迪小姐作一次拜访；脱不了身，因为她这一路是人家付的钱，又付不起独自回去的车钱。

===Original===

They drove for some distance, the large houses disappeared, they were in amongst little drab roadways like those round about Mornington Road. Perhaps if she improvised an engagement she could find her way to Regent’s Park and get back. But they had come so far. They must be on the outskirts of N.W., perhaps even in N. They pulled up before a small drab villa. The sun had gone behind the clouds, the short street was desolate. No touch of life or colour anywhere, hardly a sign of spring in the small parched shrub-filled front gardens, uniformly enclosed by dusty railings. She dreaded her wait alone in the cab with her finery and her empty afternoon while Miss Szigmondy visited her sick friend.
“Come along,” said Miss Szigmondy from the little garden path, “poor cgeature you *do* look tired.” Miriam got angrily out of the cab. Whose fault was it that she was tired? Why did Miss Szigmondy go to these things? She had not cared and was not disappointed at not caring. She was just the same as when she had started out.
“I will wait in the garden,” she said hurriedly as the door opened on the house of sickness. A short young man with untidy dark hair and a shabby suit stood in the doorway. His brilliant dark eyes smiled sharply at Miss Szigmondy and shot beyond her towards Miriam as he stood aside holding the door wide. “Come along,” shouted Miss Szigmondy disappearing. Miriam came reluctantly forward and got herself through the door, reaping the second curious sharp smile as she passed. The young man had an extraordinary face, cheerful and grimy, like a street arab; he was rather like a street arab. Miss Szigmondy was talking loudly from a little room to the right of the door. Miriam’s embarrassment in the impossibility of explaining her own superfluous presence was not relieved when she entered the room. The young man was clearly not prepared. It was a most unwarrantable intrusion. She stood at a loss behind Miss Szigmondy who was planted, still eagerly talking, on the small clear space of bare boards﻿—cracked and dusty, like a warehouse﻿—in the middle of the room and tried not to see anything in particular; but her eyes already had the sense that there was nothing to sit upon, no corner to retire into, nothing but an extraordinary confusion of shabby dust-covered things laid bare by the sunlight that poured through the uncurtained window.

===Chinese===

马车走了一程，大房子没有了，她们进了一片灰扑扑的小街小巷，同莫宁顿路四周的那些一个样。也许她可以现编一个约会，设法到摄政公园再回去。可她们已经走得这样远。这准是西北区的边上了，兴许都到了北区。马车在一栋灰扑扑的小别墅前停住。太阳缩进云后，短短的街一片荒凉。哪里都没有一丝生趣、一点颜色，小小的前院干巴巴地种满灌木，几乎不见春意，一律围着积尘的铁栏杆。她怕独自在马车里守着她那身盛装和她那空荡荡的下午，等齐格蒙迪小姐探望她生病的朋友。

“来吧，”齐格蒙迪小姐在小径上说，“可怜的人儿，你看着真是累了。”米丽亚姆憋着火下了车。她累，是谁的错？齐格蒙迪小姐干吗要来这些地方？她本来就不上心，没上心也就谈不上失望。她还同出发时一模一样。

“我在花园里等，”门向病宅打开时她急急说。一个矮个子青年站在门口，深色头发乱蓬蓬，一套寒酸的西服。他那双明亮的黑眼睛朝齐格蒙迪小姐锐利地一笑，又越过她射向米丽亚姆，一面侧身把门敞开。“来吧，”齐格蒙迪小姐喊了一声，人不见了。米丽亚姆不情愿地上前，把自己送过门去，收获了他扫来的第二记好奇的锐笑。这青年有一张奇特的脸，快活而脏污，像个街头野孩子；他简直就是个街头野孩子。齐格蒙迪小姐在门右手的小房间里大声说着话。米丽亚姆走进房间，那股没法解释自己这个多余存在的窘迫，也没有减轻。这青年显然没有准备。这是一次全然无端的闯入。她不知所措地立在齐格蒙迪小姐身后，那位正站在房间中央一小块空着的裸地板上——开裂、积尘，像座货栈——仍旧热烈地说着话；她努力什么都不细看；可她的眼睛已经觉出这里没有东西可坐，没有角落可退，只有一片寒酸的、蒙尘之物的惊人大杂烩，被无帘的窗口倾泻进来的阳光照得纤毫毕露。

===Original===

Her eyes took refuge in the face of the young man confronting Miss Szigmondy, making replies to her volley of questions. He had no front teeth, nothing but blackened stumps; dreadful, one ought not to look, unless he were going to be helped. Perhaps Miss Szigmondy was going to help him. But he did not look ill. His bright glancing eyes shot about as if looking at something that was not there and he answered Miss Szigmondy’s sallies with a sort of cheerful convulsion of his whole frame. He seemed to be “on wires”; but not weak; strong and cheerful; happy; a kind of cheerfulness and happiness she had never met before. It was quiet. It came from him soundlessly making within his pleasant voice a gay noise that conquered the strange embarrassing room. Presently in answer to a demand from Miss Szigmondy he opened folding doors and ushered them into an adjoining room.
---
Miriam stood holding the little group in her hands longing for words. She could only smile and smile. The young man stood by looking at it and smiling, too, giving his attention to Miss Szigmondy’s questions about some larger white things standing in the bare room. When he moved away towards these and she could leave off wondering whether it would do to say “and is this really going to the Academy next week” instead of again repeating “how beautiful,” and her eye could run undisturbed over and over the outlines of the two horses, impressions crowded upon her. The thing moved and changed as she looked at it; it seemed as if it must break away, burst out of her hands into the surrounding atmosphere. Everything about took on a happy familiarity, as if she had long been in the bright bare plaster-filled little room. From the edges of the small white group a radiance spread freshening the air, flowing out into the happy world, flowing back over the afternoon, bringing parts of it to stand out like great fresh bright Academy pictures. The great studios opening out within the large garden-draped Hampstead houses rich and bright with colour in a golden light, their fur rugs and tea services on silver trays, and velvet coated men, wives with trailing dresses and the people standing about, at once conspicuous and lost, were like Academy pictures.

===Chinese===

她的眼睛只好退避到那青年的脸上，他正迎着齐格蒙迪小姐，回答她连珠炮的问话。他没有门牙，只剩焦黑的残根；可怕，人不该看，除非他要得到救助。也许齐格蒙迪小姐就要救助他。可他看着不像有病。他那双亮闪闪的眼睛四下扫来扫去，像在看一样不在那儿的东西，回答齐格蒙迪小姐的一句句逗话时，整个身架来一阵快活的抽动。他像“吊着线”；可不弱；结实而快活；幸福；一种她从未见识过的快活和幸福。它是安静的。它从他身上无声地发出，在他那悦耳的嗓音里造出一种欢快的响，盖过了这间古怪尴尬的屋子。不久，应齐格蒙迪小姐一句要求，他推开折叠门，引她们进隔壁一间屋。
---
米丽亚姆站着，那小小的雕塑群像捧在手里，渴望着言语。她只能笑了又笑。青年站在一旁看着它，也笑着，注意听着齐格蒙迪小姐关于裸屋里立着的几件大些的白色物的问话。待他朝那些走开、她可以不必再搾磨该不该说一句“这当真下周就要送学院画展么”来代替又一迭声的“多美啊”、她的眼睛可以不受打扰地一遍遍描摹那两匹马的轮廓时，种种印象涌上了她。那东西在她注视下移动、变化；仿佛它就要挣脱，从她手里迸进四周的空气里去。周遭的一切都披上一层快活的熟稔，仿佛她早已在这间明亮、空落、摆满石膏的小屋里待了很久。那一小群白色雕塑的边缘漾出一圈光辉，把空气刷新，流进快活的世界，又流回来漫过这个下午，使它的一些部分凸现出来，像一幅幅巨大、新鲜、明亮的学院画展画。汉普斯特德那些大宅——花园掩映中的大画室一间间敞开，金色光线里色彩富丽明亮，银盘上的茶具和毛皮地毯，丝绒上衣的男人们、长裙曳地的太太们，还有那些站立的人们，一下子显眼又湮没——都像学院画展的画。

It was all real now, the pictures on the great easels, scraps of the Academy blaze; the studio with the bright light, and marble, and bright clear tiger skins on the floor, the big clean fresh tiger almost filling the canvas﻿ ﻿… the dark studio with antique furniture and pictures of people standing about in historical clothes.﻿ ﻿…

“Goodness gracious, *isn’t* she a swell!”
“Are they all right?”
“Are you a millionaire my dear? Have they raised your salary?”
“Do you really like them?”
“Yes. I’ve never seen you look so nice. You ought always to go about in a large black hat trimmed with lilac.”
“Didn’t one of the artists want to paint your portrait.”
“They all did. I’ve promised at least twenty sittings.”
“Come nearer to the lamp fair child that I may be even more dazzled by thy splendour.”
“I’m awfully glad you like them﻿—they’ll have to go on forever.”
“Where on earth did you find the money child?”
“Borrowed it from Harry. It was her idea. You see I shall get four pounds for my four weeks’ holiday; and if I go to stay with them it won’t cost me anything; so she advanced me two pounds.”
“And you got all this for two pounds?”
“Practically; the hat was ten and six and the other things twenty seven and six and the gloves half a crown.”
“Where did you get them?”
“Edgware Road.”
“And just put them on?”
“It is really remarkable. Do you realise how lucky you are in being a stock size?”
“I suppose I am. But you know the awful thing about it is that they will never come in for Wimpole Street.”
“Why on earth not? What could be more ladylike, more﻿—simple, more altogether suitable?”
“You see I have to wear black there.”
“What an extraordinary idea. *Why?*”
“Well they asked me to. I don’t know. I believe it’s the fault of my predecessor. They told me she *rustled* and wore all kinds of dresses﻿—”
“I see﻿—a series of explosions.”
“On silk foundations.”
“But why should they assume that you would do the same?”
“I don’t know. It’s an awful nuisance. You can’t get black blouses that will wash; it will be awful in the summer; besides it’s so unbecoming.”
“There I can’t agree. It would be for me. It makes me look dingy; but it suits you, throws up your rose-leaf complexion and your golden hair. But I call it jolly hard lines. I’d like to see the governor dictating to me what I should wear.”
“It’s so expensive if one can’t wear out one’s best things.”
“It’s intolerable.

===Chinese===

如今这一切都是真的了，大画架上那些画，学院画展那片光焰的碎片；那间亮堂的画室，大理石，地上明亮干净的虎皮，那只几乎占满画布的又大又清新的老虎…那间暗的画室，古董家具，画上的人穿着古装站在那儿。…
---
“天哪，她可真阔气！”

“还合适吗？”

“你成百万富翁了吗，亲爱的？他们给你加薪了？”

“你们真喜欢？”

“真喜欢。从没见你这么好看过。你就该永远戴着一顶缀丁香的大黑帽到处走。”

“不是有个画家想给你画像吗。”

“他们都想。我答应了至少二十次坐堂。”

“往灯这儿来点儿，漂亮孩子，好叫我被你的光辉更晃一晃。”

“你们喜欢，我太高兴了——这下它们得永远穿下去了。”

“你到底哪儿弄来的钱，孩子？”

“跟哈丽借的。是她的主意。你们看，我四个星期假期能拿四镑；去住在她们那儿又一分不用花；所以她先垫了我两镑。”

“这些全是两镑置办的？”

“差不多；帽子十先令六便士，别的东西二十七先令六便士，手套半克朗。”

“哪儿买的？”

“埃奇韦尔路。”

“买来就上身？”

“真了不起。你意识到你运气多好吗——标准尺码。”

“大概是的。可你知道糟糕的是，这些在温波尔街永远穿不着。”

“到底为什么不能？还有什么比这更淑女、更——素净、更完全合式的？”

“你们看，我在那儿得穿黑。”

“多古怪的念头。为什么？”

“呢，他们要求的。我不知道。我想是我前任惹的祸。他们告诉我，她浑身窸窣作响，什么样的裙子都穿——”

“我明白了——一连串的爆炸。”

“丝绸底子上。”

“可他们凭什么认定你也会那样？”

“不知道。烦透了。买不到禁得住洗的黑罩衫；到夏天可要命；再说也不衬人。”

“这一点我不能同意。对我来说是不衬。它叫我显得晦气；可它衬你，衬出你的玫瑰瓣肤色和金头发。可我说这真是委屈死了。倒要叫老板来管管我该穿什么试试。”

“自己最好的衣裳穿不旧，多破费。”

“不能容忍。

===Original===

Why do you stand it?”
“What can I do?”
“Tell them you must either wear *scarlet* at the office or have a higher *screw*.”
“It isn’t an office you see. I have to be so much in the surgeries and interviewing people in the waiting-room, you know.”
“Yes﻿—from dukes to dustmen. But would either the dukes or the dustmen disapprove of scarlet?”
“One has to be a discreet nobody. It’s the professional world; you don’t understand; you are equals, you two, superiors, pampered countesses in your offices.”
“Well I think it’s a beastly shame. I should brandish a pair of forceps at Mr. Hancock and say ‘scarlet﻿—or I leave.’ ”
“Where should I go? I have no qualifications.”
“You wouldn’t leave. They would say ‘Miss Henderson, wear purple and yellow, only stay.’ I think it’s a reflection on her taste, don’t you Jan?”
“Certainly it is. It is fiendish. But employers *are* fiends﻿—to women.”
“I haven’t found that soh.”
“Ah you keep yours in order, you rule them with a rod of iron.”
“I do. I believe in it.”
“I envy you your late hours in the morning.”
“Ah-ha﻿—she’s had a row about that.”
“*Have* you Mag?”
“Not a row; simply a discussion.”
“What happened?”
“Simply this. The governor begged me﻿—almost in tears﻿—to come down earlier﻿—for the sake of the discipline of the office.”
“What did you say?”
“I said Herr *Epstein*; what can I do? How do you suppose I can get up, have breakfast and be down here before eleven?”
“What did he say?”
“He protested and implored and offered to pay cabs for me.”
“Good Lord, Mag, you are extraordinary.”
“I am not extraordinary and it is no concern of the Deity’s. I fail to see why I should get to the office earlier than I do. I don’t get my letters before half-past eleven. I am fresh and gay and rested, I get through my work before closing-time. I work like anything whilst I am there.”
“And you still go down at eleven?”
“I still go down at eleven.”
“I *do* envy you. You see my people always want me most first thing in the morning. It’s awful, if one has been up very late.”
“And what is our life worth without late hours? The evening is the only life we have.”
“Exactly. And they are the same really. They do their work to be free of it and live.”
“Precisely; but they are waited on. They have their houses and baths and servants and meals and comforts. We get up in cold rooms untended and tired. *They* ought to be first at the office and wait upon us.”

“你干吗忍着？”

“我能怎么办？”

“我说这真是奇耻大辱。我就要朝汉考克先生挥一副止血钳，说‘猠红——不然我走人。’”

“我倒不觉得。”

“啊，你把你那位治得服服帖帖，你拿铁腕统治他们。”

“可不。我信这个。”

“我羡慕你早上可以迟到。”

“啊哈——她为这个吵过一架。”

“不算吵；不过一场讨论。”

“结果呢？”

“不过这样。老板求我——几乎带着眼泪——早点下来——为了办公室的纪律。”

“你怎么说？”

“他抗议，恳求，还提出替我付出租马车钱。”

“老天，玛格，你真是个人物。”

“我不是人物，这也不干老天爷的事。我不明白，我为什么该比现在更早到办公室。我的信十一点半前到不了。我新鲜、快活、歇足了，收工前把活儿全干完。人在那儿的时候拼命干。”

“那你还照旧十一点到？”

“照旧十一点到。”

“我真羡慕你。你知道，我那几位一大早最缺不得我。要是前一晚熬得晚，真要命。”

“不能熬夜，咱们这条命还值个什么？晚上才是咱们唯一的生活。”

“正是。他们其实也一样。他们做工是为了脱开工夫去活。”

“一点不错；可他们有人伺候。他们有自己的宅子、浴缸、仆人、饭食、种种舒服。我们在冷屋子里没人管、带着倦意起床。他们才该头一个到办公室，来伺候我们。”

“She is a queen in her office; waited upon hand and foot.”
“Well﻿—why not? I do them the honour of bringing my bright petunia clad feminine presence into their dingy warehouse; I expect some acknowledgment of the honour.”
“You don’t allow them either to spit or swear.”
“I do not; and they appreciate it.”
“Mine are beasts. I defy anyone to do anything with them. I *loathe* the city man.”
Miriam sighed. In neither of these offices, she felt sure, could she hold her own﻿—and yet compared to her own long day﻿—what freedom the girls had﻿—ten to five and eleven to six and any clothes they found it convenient to wear. But city men﻿ ﻿… no restrictions were too high a price to pay for the privileges of her environment; the association with gentlemen, her quiet room, the house, the perpetual interest of the patients, the curious exciting streaks of social life, linking up with the past and carrying the past forward on a more generous level. The girls had broken with the past and were fighting in the world. She was somehow between two worlds, neither quite sheltered, nor quite free﻿ ﻿… not free as long as she wanted, in spite of her reason, to stay on at Wimpole Street and please the people there. Why did she want to stay? What future would it bring? Less than ever was there any chance of saving for old age. She could not forever go on being secretary to a dentist.﻿ ﻿… She drove these thoughts away; they were only one side of the matter; there were other things; things she could not make clear to the girls; nor to anyone who could not see and feel the whole thing from inside, as she saw and felt it. And even if it were not so, if the environment of her poorly paid activities had been trying and unsympathetic, at least it gave seclusion, her own room to work in, her free garret and her evening and weekend freedom. But what was she going to do with it?
“Tell us about the *show*, Miriam. Cease to gaze at Jan’s relations; sit down, light a cigarette.”
“These German women fascinate me,” said Miriam swinging round from the mantelshelf; “they are so like Jan and so utterly different.”
“Yes; Jan is Jan and they are Minna and Erica.”
Taking a cigarette from Mag’s case Miriam lit it at the lamp.

米丽亚姆叹了口气。这两个办公室，她拿得稳，哪一个她也站不住脚——可比起她自己那漫长的一天——姑娘们多么自由——十点到五点、十一点到六点，爱穿什么穿什么。可城里男人们…为了她那环境的种种特权，多高的限制也不算贵：同绅士们的来往，她安静的房间，那幢房子，病人们绵绵不断的兴味，社交生活那一道道奇异撩人的条纹，接上过去、又把过去载到更宽绰的水面上。姑娘们同过去断了，正在世界上打拼。她不知怎么悬在两个世界之间，既不十分有荫庇，也不十分自由…只要她——违着她的理智——还想留在温波尔街、讨那边的人喜欢，就不自由。她为什么要留下？那会带来什么前程？为养老攒钱，比以往更没有指望了。她不能永远当牙医的秘书。…她把这些念头赶开；那只是事情的一面；还有别的；那些她对姑娘们说不清的；对任何不能像她这样从里面看到、感到全局的人，都说不清。退一步说，即便不是这样，即便她这份薄酬差事的环境是难堪的、冷硬的，它至少给她幽静，给她一间自己的工作室，她那自由的阁楼，她晚间和周末的自由。可她拿它做什么呢？

Before her eyes the summer unrolled﻿—concerts with Miss Szigmondy, going in the cooling day in her new clothes, with a thin blouse, from daylight into electric light and music, taking off the zouave inside and feeling cool at once, the electric light mixing with the daylight, the cool darkness to walk home in alone, full of music that would last on into the next day; Miss Szigmondy’s musical at homes, evenings at Wimpole Street, weekends in the flowery suburbs, windows and doors open, cool rooms, gardens in the morning and evening, weekends in the country, each journey like the beginning of the summer holiday, weekends in town, Sunday afternoons at Mr. Hancock’s and Miss Szigmondy’s﻿—all taking her away from Kennett Street. All these things yielded their best reality in this room. Glowing brightly in the distance they made this room like the centre of a song. But a weekend taken up was a weekend missed at Kennett Street. It meant missing Slater’s on Saturday night, the week end stretching out ahead immensely long, the long evening with the girls, its lateness protected by the coming Sunday, waking lazily fresh and happy and easy-minded on Sunday morning, late breakfast, the cigarette in the sunlit window space, its wooden sides echoing with the clamour of St. Pancras bells, the three voices in the little rooms, *irlandisches ragout*, the hours of smoking and talking out and out on to strange promontories where everything was real all the time, the faint gradual coming of the twilight, the evening untouched by the presence of Monday, no hurry ahead, no social performances, no leave-taking, no railway journey.
“Yes; Jan is Londonised; she looks German; her voice suggests the whole of Germany; these girls are Germany untouched, strong, cheerful, musical, tree-filled Germany, without any doubts. They’ve got Jan’s sense of humour without her cynicism.”
“Is that so, Jan?”
“Yes, I think perhaps it is. They are sweet simple children.” Yes, sweet﻿—but maddening too. German women were so sure and unsuspicious and practical about life. Jan had some of that left. But she was English too, more transparent and thoughtful.
“The show! The show!”
She told them the story of the afternoon in a glowing précis, calling up the splendours upon which she felt their imaginations at work, describing it as they saw it and as with them, in retrospect, she saw it herself.

在她眼前，夏天展开了——同齐格蒙迪小姐去听音乐会，趁着傍晚转凉穿新衣裳去，一件薄罩衫，从天光走进灯光和音乐，进屋脱下左夫短外衣立刻一身凉快，灯光混着天光，回家时一路凉幽幽的暗，满心音乐，能一直响到第二天；齐格蒙迪小姐家的音乐茶会，温波尔街的夜晚，繁花郊外的周末，门窗大开，凉爽的房间，早晚的花园，乡间的周末，每趟出门都像暑假的开头，城里的周末，在汉考克先生家和齐格蒙迪小姐家过的星期天下午——这一切把她带离肯尼特街。这一切东西，在这间屋里交出它们最好的真实。它们在远处灼灼发亮，把这间屋子衬成一支歌的中心。可被人占去的周末，就是肯尼特街错过的周末。那意味着错过星期六晚上的斯莱特，意味着前方铺开的周末长得望不到头，同姑娘们一起的长夜，它的晚有即将到来的星期天护着，星期天早晨懒懒地醒来，新鲜、快活、无忧无虑，迟开的早饭，太阳地里窗间小木屋中那支烟，圣潘克拉斯的钟声在木壁上回响，小小房间里三个人的嗓音，爱尔兰式炖肉，抽烟长谈的几个钟头，一路谈出去、谈出去，登上一个个奇怪的岬角，那里一切始终是真的，暮色渐渐淡淡地来，那个夜晚不沾星期一的影子，前头没有催促，没有社交的表演，没有道别，没有火车。

“是啊；简伦敦化了；她长着德国人的模样；她的嗓音透出整个德国；这两个姑娘是没动过的德国，强壮、快活、音乐、满是树林的德国，没有任何怀疑。她们有简的幽默感，却没有她的讥诮。”

“是这样吗，简？”

“是，我想大概是。她们是可爱的、单纯的孩子。”是啊，可爱——也叫人发狂。德国女人对生活那样有把握、不疑、实际。简身上还剩着几分。可她也是英国人，更透明，更多思。

“画展！画展！”

她把下午的事用一篇灼灼的梗概讲给她们，把那一重重光华唤到她们想象里，她觉出那想象正在动工——按她们看到的样子描述它，也同她们一道、在回望里，照她自己再看到的样子。

Her descriptions drew Mag’s face towards her, glowing, wrapt and reverent. Jan sat sewing with inturned eyes and half open, half-smiling appreciative face. They both fastened upon the great gold-framed pictures, asking for details. Presently they were making plans to visit the Academy and foretelling her joy in seeing them again and identifying them. She had not thought of that; certainly, it would be delightful; and perhaps seeing the pictures in freedom and alone she might find them wonderful.
“Why do you say their wives were all like cats?”
“They were.” She called up the unhatted figures moving about among the guests in trailing gowns﻿—keeping something up, pretending to be interested, being cattishly nice to the visitors, and thinking about other things all the time.﻿ ﻿… I can’t *stand* them, oh, I can’t stand them.﻿ ﻿… But the girls would not have seen them in that way; they would have been interested in them and their dresses, they would have admired the prettiness of some of them and found several of them “charming”﻿ ﻿… if Mag were an artist’s wife she would behave in the way those women behaved.﻿ ﻿…
“Were they all *alike*?” that was half sarcastic.﻿ ﻿…
“Absolutely. They were all *cats*, simply.”
“Isn’t she *extraordinary*?”
“It’s the cats who are extraordinary. Why do they do it, girls! *Why* do they do it?” She flushed, feeling insincere. At this moment she felt that she knew that Mag, in social life, would conform and be a cat. She had never thought of her in social life; here in poverty and freedom she was herself.
“Do *phwatt* me dear?”
“Oh let them go. It makes me tired, even to think of them. The thought of the sound of their voices absolutely wears me out.”
“I’m not laaazy﻿—I’m tie‑erd﻿—I was *born* tie‑erd.”
“I say girls, I want to ask you something.”
“Well?”
“Why don’t you two write?”
“*Write?*”
“Write what?”
“Us?”
“Just as we are, without one”﻿—
“Flea﻿—I know. No. *Don’t* be silly. I’m perfectly serious. I mean it. Why don’t you write things﻿—both of you. I thought of it this morning.”
Both girls sat thoughtful. It was evident that the idea was not altogether unfamiliar to them.
“Someone kept telling me the other day I ought to write and it suddenly struck me that if anyone ought it’s you two. Why don’t you Mag?”
“Why should I? Have I not already enough on my fair young shoulders?”
“*Jan*, why don’t *you*?”
“I, my dear?

她的描述把玛格的脸整个引了过来，发着光，出着神，一脸虔敬。简坐着做针线，眼神内敛，半开的脸上挂着欣赏的浅笑。两人都钉住那几幅金框大画，追问细节。不多时她们已在计划去学院画展，预言她再见那些画、认出那些画时的欢喜。她没想到这一层；自然，那会很有趣；而且独自一人、无拘无束地看画，她说不定会觉得它们妙极了。

“你为什么说他们的太太个个像猫？”

“就是像。”她唤起那些不戴帽的身影，曳地长裙在宾客间穿来穿去——撑着一份什么，装着兴致，对客人猫一样地和气，心里却一直想着别的。…我受不了她们，哦，我受不了她们。…可姑娘们不会那样看她们；她们会对她们、对她们的衣裳感兴趣，会称赞其中几个的俏丽，会觉得好几个“真迷人”…玛格若做了画家的太太，行事就会同那些女人一模一样。…

“她们全都一个样吗？”那话带着一半讥诮。…

“绝对。她们全是猫，干脆说吧。”

“她这个人是不是怪极了？”

“我为什么该写？我这双年轻漂亮的肩膀上压的还不够多吗？”

“简，你为什么不写？”

“我吗，亲爱的？

===Original===

For a most excellent reason.”
“What reason?” demanded Miriam in a shaking voice. Her heart was beating; she felt that a personal decision was going to be affected by Jan’s reason, if she could be got to express it. Jan did not reply instantly and she found herself hoping that nothing more would be said about writing, that she might be free to go on cherishing the idea, alone and unbiased.
“I do not write,” said Jan slowly, “because I am perfectly convinced that anything I might write would be mediocre.”
Miriam’s heart sank. If Jan, with all her German knowledge and her wit and experience of two countries felt this, it was probably much truer of herself. To think about it, to dwell upon the things Mr. Wilson had said was simply vanity. He had said *anyone* could learn to write. But he was clever and ready to believe her clever in the same way, and ready to take ideas from him. It was true she had material, “stuff” as he called it, but she would not have known it, if she had not been told. She could see it now, as he saw it, but if she wrote at his suggestion, a borrowed suggestion, there would be something false in it, clever and false.
“Yes﻿—I think Jan’s right,” said Mag cheerfully. “That is an excellent reason and the true one.”
It was true. But how could they speak so lightly and cheerfully about writing﻿ ﻿… the thing one had always wanted to do, that everyone probably secretly wanted to do, and the girls could give up the idea without a sigh. They were right. It would be wrong to write mediocre stuff. Why was she feeling so miserable? Of course because neither of them had suggested that she should write. They knew her better than Mr. Wilson and it never occurred to them that she should write. That settled it. But something moved despairingly in the void.
“Do you think it would be *wrong* to write mediocre stuff?” she asked huskily.
“It would be worse than *wrong* child﻿—it would be foolish; it wouldn’t sell.”

===Chinese===

“我有一个再好不过的理由。”

“什么理由？”米丽亚姆声音发颤地质问。她心在跳；她觉得一个私人的决定就要被简的理由左右了——倘若能叫她说出来的话。简没有立刻回答，她发觉自己竟盼着别再谈写作了，好让她独自一人、不受左右地继续珍藏这个念头。

“我不写，”简慢慢地说，“因为我完全相信，我写出的任何东西都会是平庸的。”

米丽亚姆的心沉了下去。倘若简——凭她全部的德国学识、她的机智、两个国度的阅历——都这样想，那这话用在她自己身上多半更真。去想它、去反复咀嚼威尔逊先生说过的那些话，简直就是虚荣。他说过谁都能学会写作。可他聪明，也就乐于相信她同样聪明、乐于从他那里接受点子。不错，她有素材，他所谓的“料”，可若没人告诉她，她自己并不知道。她现在看得出它了，照他看它的样子；可她若是照他的提议去写——一个借来的提议——那里面就会有一点假，聪明而假。

“是啊——我想简说得对，”玛格痛快地说。“这是个好理由，也是真的理由。”

是真的。可她们怎么能这样轻飘飘、这样欢欢喜喜地谈写作…这件人一直想做的事，多半人人心里都偷偷想做的事，姑娘们却连一声叹息都不叹就放下了。她们是对的。写平庸的东西是错的。那她为什么这样难受？当然是因为她们俩谁也没有提议她来写。她们比威尔逊先生更了解她，压根没想到她会写。这就定了。可虚空里有什么东西绝望地动了一下。

“你们觉得写平庸的东西是错的吗？”她哑着嗓子问。

“那比错还糟，孩子——那是蠢；卖不掉。”

## XI / 十一
===Original===

Everything was ready for the two o’clock patient. There was no excuse for lingering any longer. Half past one. Why did they not come up? On her way to the door she opened the corner cupboard and stood near the open door hungry, listening for footsteps on the basement stairs, dusting and ranging the neat rows of bottles. At the end of five minutes she went guiltily down. If he had finished his lunch they would wonder why she had lingered so long. If she had hurried down as soon as she could no one would have known that she hoped to have lunch alone. Now because she had waited deliberately someone would read her guilt. She wished she were one of those people who never tried to avoid anything. The lunchroom door opened and closed as she reached the basement stairs. James’s cheerful footsteps clacked along﻿—neat high-heeled shoes﻿—towards the kitchen. She had taken something in. They were still at lunch, unconsciously, just in the same way. No. She was glad she was not one of those people who just went on﻿—not avoiding things.﻿ ﻿…
Mr. Hancock was only just beginning his second course. He must have lingered in the workshop.﻿ ﻿… He was helping himself to condiments; Mr. Orly proffered the wooden pepper mill; “oh﻿—thank you;” he screwed it with an air of embarrassed appreciativeness. There was a curious fresh lively air of embarrassment in the room making a stirring warmth in its cellar-like coolness. Miriam slipped quietly into her place hoping she was not an interloper.
