At any rate everyone was too much engrossed to ponder over her lateness. Mr. Orly was sitting with his elbows on the table and his serviette crumpled in his hands, ready to rise from the table, beaming mildness and waiting. Mrs. Orly sat waiting and smiling with her elbows on the table.
“Ah,” said Mr. Orly gently as Miriam sat down, “here comes the clerical staff.”
Miriam beamed and began her soup. It was James waiting today too, with her singing manner; a happy day.
Mrs. Orly asked a question in her happiest voice. They were fixing a date.﻿ ﻿… They were going﻿ ﻿… to a *theatre*﻿ ﻿… together. Her astonished mind tried to make them coalesce﻿ ﻿… she saw them sitting in a row, two different worlds confronted by one spectacle﻿ ﻿… there was not a scrap of any kind of performance that would strike them both in the same way.
“Got anything on on Friday Miss Henderson?”
The sudden question startled her.

===Chinese===

两点钟病人的一切都备好了。再赖着就没有借口了。一点半。他们怎么还不上来？去门口的路上她开了墙角柜，挨着敞开的柜门站着，饿着，听地下楼梯上的脚步声，掸着、排着一排排齐整的瓶子。五分钟后她负疚地下了楼。倘若他已用完午餐，他们要奇怪她怎么赖了这么久。倘若她一腾出手就赶紧下来，谁也不会知道她指望独自用午餐。如今她有意等了，就会有人读出她的亏心。她但愿自己是那种从不想躲避什么的人。她刚到地下楼梯，午餐室的门开了又关。詹姆斯轻快的脚步嗒嗒地过去——小巧的高跟鞋——往厨房去。她端了东西进去。他们还在用午餐，浑然不觉，还是老样子。不。她庆幸自己不是那种只管往前走的人——什么都不躲避。……
汉考克先生才刚开始他的第二道菜。他准是在工场耽搁了。……他正在自己加调料；奥利先生递过木胡椒磨；“哦——谢谢；”他带着窘迫的赏识拧着它。屋里有一阵新鲜活泼的窘迫的空气，在地窖似的清凉里携起一阵暖。米丽亚姆悄悄溜进自己的位子，但愿自己不算个闯入者。无论如何人人都太专注，没工夫琢磨她的迟到。奥利先生坐着，胳膊肘支在桌上，手里揉着餐巾，随时要从桌边站起来，一脸温和的光，等着。奥利太太也支着胳膊肘坐着等，笑着。
“啊，”米丽亚姆坐下时奥利先生轻轻说，“文书班子来了。”
米丽亚姆绽开笑，喝起她的汤。今天也是詹姆斯伺候，带着她唱歌似的样子；一个快活的日子。
奥利太太用她最快活的嗓音问了一句什么。他们在定日子。……他们要……一道去*戏院*……她惊愕的心思试着把他们捏到一处……她看见他们并排坐着，两个不同的世界面对同一场演出……世上没有一星半点的演出能同样地打中他们俩。
“星期五你有事吗，亨德森小姐？”
这突如其来的问题叫她一惊。

===Original===

Had it been asked twice? She answered, stammering, in amazed consciousness of what was to follow and accepted the invitation in a flood of embarrassment. Her delight and horror and astonishment seemed to flow all over the table. Desperately she tried to gather in all her emotions behind an easy appreciative smile. She felt astonishment and dismay coming out of her hair, swelling her hands, making her clumsy with her knife and fork. Far away, beyond her grasp was the sense she felt she ought to have, the sense of belonging; socially. It was being offered. But something or someone was fighting it. Always, everywhere someone or something was fighting it.
Mr. Orly had given a ghostly little chuckle. “Like dining at restaurants?” he asked kindly and swiftly.
“I don’t think I ever have.”
“Then we shall have the pleasure of initiating you. Like caviar?”
“I don’t even know what it is,” said Miriam trying to bring gladness into her voice.
“Oh﻿—this is great. Caviar to the million eh?﻿—oh, I ought not to have put it like that, things one would rather have said otherwise﻿—no offence intended﻿—none taken I hope﻿—don’t yeh know really?﻿—Sturgeon’s roe, y’know.”
“Oh, I know I don’t like *roe*,” said Miriam gravely.
“Chalk it up. Miss Henderson doesn’t like roe.”
Miriam flushed. Pressing back through her anger to what had preceded she found inspiration.
“My education has been neglected.”
“Quite so, but now’s your chance. Seize your opportunity; carpe diem. See?”
“I thought it was caviar, not carp,” said Mr. Hancock quietly.
Was it a rescue, or a sacrifice to the embarrassing occasion? She had never heard him jest with the Orlys. Mrs. Orly chuckled gleefully, flashing out the smile that Miriam loved. It took every line from her care-fashioned face and lit it with a most extraordinary radiance. She had smiled like that as a girl in response to the jests of her many brothers﻿ ﻿… her eyes were sweet; there was a perfect sweetness in her somewhere.
“Bravo Hancock, that’s a good one.﻿ ﻿… Ye gods and fishes large and small listen to *that*,” he murmured half turning towards the door.
The clattering of boots on the stone stairs was followed by the rattling of the loose door knob and the splitting open of the door. Mr. Leyton shot into the room searching the party with a swift glance and taking his place in the circle in a state of headlong silent volubility.

===Chinese===

这话问过两遍了吗？她结结巴巴地答了，惊觉到接下来将是什么，在一阵窘态的洪流里接受了邀请。她的欢喜、惊骇与惊讶仿佛漫过了整张桌子。她拼命想把全部情绪收拢到一声从容赏识的微笑后面。她觉出惊讶和沮丧从头发里冒出来，把手胀大，叫她的刀叉都笨拙了。远远的、她够不着的地方，是她觉得自己该有的那种感觉：归属感；社交上的。它正在被递过来。可是有什么或什么人在同它作战。永远，到处，有什么人或什么东西在同它作战。
奥利先生发出一声幽灵般的小笑。“喜欢在馆子里吃饭吗？”他亲切而利落地问。
“我想我从没吃过。”
“那我们有幸领您入门了。喜欢鱼子酱吗？”
“我连它是什么都不知道，”米丽亚姆说，努力往嗓音里放进欢喜。
“哦——这可了不得。鱼子酱给大众，嗯？——哦，这话不该这么说，有些话宁可换个说法——无意冒犯——但愿没有见怪——您当真不知道？——鲟鱼子，您知道。”
“哦，那我知道我不喜欢*鱼子*，”米丽亚姆一本正经地说。
“记到查克的账上。亨德森小姐不吃鱼子。”
米丽亚姆红了脸。从愤怒往回压、压到方才之前的地方，她得了灵感。
“我的教育被荒疏了。”
“正是，可如今机会来了。抓住机会；carpe diem（及时行乐）。明白吗？”
“我记得那叫鱼子酱（caviar），不叫鲤鱼（carp），”汉考克先生轻轻说。
这是搭救，还是给这个尴尬场合的一件祭品？她从没听他对奥利一家说过一句玩笑。奥利太太快活地咯咯笑，闪出米丽亚姆喜爱的那个笑。它把她那张操劳成形的脸上的每一道线都抹去，点亮一片非凡的光彩。她做姑娘时回应众多兄弟的玩笑就是这样笑的……她的眼睛甜；她身上某处有一种完完全全的甜。
“好样的汉考克，这句妙。……诸位神明和大大小小的鱼儿，都来*听听*这个，”他半转向门口喃喃道。
石楼梯上靴声橐橐，接着是松脱的门把手哗啦啦一阵响，门豁然洞开。莱顿先生射进屋来，飞快一瞥扫过众人，进了圈子里坐下，一副一往无前、默不作声的滔滔不绝的样子。

===Original===

By the way he attacked his lunch it was clear he had a patient waiting or imminent. It occurred to Miriam to wonder why he did not always arrange his appointments round about lunchtime﻿ ﻿… but any such manoeuvre would be discovered and things would be worse than ever. Mr. Orly watched quietly while he refused Mrs. Orly’s offer to ring for soup, devouring bread and butter until she should have carved for him﻿—and then extended his invitation to his son.
“Oh, is this the annual?” asked Mr. Leyton gruffly. “What’s the show?”
“My dear will you be so good as to inform Mr. Leyton of﻿—”
“Don’t be silly Ro,” said Mrs. Orly trying to laugh “we’re going to Hamlet Ley.”
“We have the honour of begging Mr. Leyton’s company on the occasion of our visit, dinner included, to﻿—”
“What’s the date?” rapped Mr. Leyton with his tumbler to his lips.
“The date, ascertained as suited to all present with the exception of your lordship﻿—oh my God, Ley,” sighed Mr. Orly hiding his face in his serviette, his huge shoulders shaking.
“What have I done now?” asked Mr. Leyton, gasping after his long drink.
“Don’t be so silly Ley. You haven’t answd fathez queshun.”
“How can I answer till I’m told the *date*?”
“Don’t be silly, you can come any evening.”
“*Friday*,” whispered Miriam.
“*What?*” said Mr. Orly softly, emerging from his serviette, “a traitor in the camp?”
“Friday is it? Well, then it’s pretty certain I *can’t* come.”
“Don’t be silly Ley﻿—you haven’t any engagements.”
“*Haven’t I?* There’s a singsong at Headquarters Friday.”
“Enough, my dear, enough, press him no more,” said Mr. Orly rising. “Far be it from us to compete. Going to sing Ley or to song, eh? Never mind boy, sorry you can’t come,” he added, sighing gustily as he left the room.
“You’ll be able to come Ley won’t you?” whispered Mrs. Orly impatiently lingering.
“If you’d only let me know the date beforehand instead of springing it on me.”
“Don’t be si’y Ley it vexes Father so. You needn’t go to the si’y singsong.”
“I don’t see how I can get out of it. It’s rather a big function; as an officer I ought to be there.”
“Oh never mind; you’d better come.”
Mr. Orly called from the stairs.
“All right darling,” she said in anxious cheerful level tones hurrying to the door. “You *must* come Ley, you can manage somehow.”
Miriam sat feeling wretchedly about in her mind. Mr. Leyton was busily finishing his lunch. In a moment Mr.

===Chinese===

看他扑向午餐的那架势，分明有个病人正等着或马上到。米丽亚姆忽然纳闷，他为什么不总把预约安排在午饭前后……可任何这样的花招都会被发现，事情会比从前更糟。奥利先生静静看着他谢绝奥利太太替他叫汤的提议、狼吞虎咽地吃黄油面包等她给他切肉——随后向儿子发出邀请。
“哦，是每年一度的吗？”莱顿先生粗声问。“什么戏？”
“亲爱的，可否请您告知莱顿先生——”
“别犯傻了，罗，”奥利太太强笑着说，“我们去看《哈姆雷特》，莱。”
“我们有幸恭请莱顿先生同行，包括晚宴，前往——”
“哪天？”莱顿先生脆声问，杯子举在唇边。
“日子，经查明适合在座各位、唯独不合尊驾——哦我的天，莱，”奥利先生叹道，把脸埋进餐巾，宽宽的肩膀直抖。
“我又怎么了？”莱顿先生长饮一口后喘着问。
“别这么傻，莱。你还没回答爸爸的问题。”
“连*日子*都不告诉我，我怎么回答？”
“别傻了，哪天晚上来都行。”
“*星期五*，”米丽亚姆悄声道。
“*什么？*”奥利先生轻声说，从餐巾里探出头来，“营中有叛徒？”
“星期五是吧？那八成我*来不了*。”
“别犯傻，莱——你没有什么约会。”
“*没有吗？*星期五总部有个歌咏会。”
“够了，亲爱的，够了，别再逼他，”奥利先生起身说。“我们岂敢竞争。是去唱呢还是去听，嗯？不打紧，孩子，你来不了真遗憾，”他叹着补了一句，一阵风出了屋。
“你能来的，莱，能吧？”奥利太太不耐烦地流连着，悄声问。
“要是你们事先告诉我日子，别这么突然袭击。”
“别犯傻（si’y），莱，爸爸要恼了。你不用去那个傻（si’y）歌咏会。”
“我看躲不掉。场面不小；作为军官我该到场。”
“哦算了；你还是来吧。”
奥利先生从楼梯上喊她。
“来了，亲爱的，”她用焦急而快活的平声应着，赶到门口。“你*一定*要来，莱，你总有办法的。”
米丽亚姆坐着，心里凄凄惶惶地摸索。莱顿先生忙着收尾他的午餐。转眼汉

===Original===

Hancock would reassert himself by some irrelevant insincerity. She found courage to plunge into speech, on the subject of her two lessons at the school. Her story strove strangely against the echoes and fell, impeded. It was an attempt to create a quiet diversion.﻿ ﻿… It should have been done violently﻿ ﻿… how many times had she seen it done, the speaker violently pushing off what had gone before and protruding his diversion, in brisk animated deliberately detached tones. But it was never really any good. There was always a break and a wound, something left unhealed, something standing unlearned﻿ ﻿… something that can only grow clear in silence.﻿ ﻿…
“You’ll never learn cycling like *that*,” said Mr. Leyton with the superior chuckle of the owner of a secret, as he snatched up a biscuit and made off. She clung fearfully to his cheerful harassed departing form. There was nothing left now in the room but the echoes. Mr. Hancock sat munching his biscuits and cheese with a look of determined steely preoccupation in his eyes that were not raised above the level of the spread of disarray along the table; but she could hear the busy circulation of his thoughts. If now she could endure for a moment. But her mind flung hither and thither seeking with a loathed servility some alien neutral topic. She knew anything she might say with the consciousness of his thoughts in her mind would be resented and slain. To get up and go quietly away with some murmured remark about her work would be to leave him with his judgment upon him. What he wanted was to give her an instruction about something in a detached professional voice and get rid of her, believing that she had gone unknowing, and remaining in his circle of reasonable thoughts. She hit out with all her force, coming against the buttress of silent angry forehead with random speech.
“I can’t believe that it’s less than two months to the longest day.”
“Time flies,” responded Mr. Hancock grimly. She recoiled exhausted by her effort and quailed under the pang in the midday gaslit room of realisation of the meaning of her words. Her eye swept over the grey-clad form and the blunted features seeking some power that would stay the inexorable consumption of the bright passing days.
“ ‘*Tempus fugit*’ I suppose one ought to say,” he said with a little laugh getting up.

===Chinese===

考克先生就要用一句不相干的敷衍重新立起自己。她鼓起勇气一头扎进话里，讲起她在学校的那两堂课。她的故事奇怪地同回声搏斗着，跌落，受挫。这是一次制造安静岔道的尝试。……本该做得凶猛些……她见过多少回，说话的人凶猛地推开先前的话头，把自己的岔路支棱出来，用干脆、生动、刻意超脱的声调。可它从来不管用。总有一道裂口一处伤，有一样东西没愈合，有一样东西立着没学成……那东西只能在静默里长清楚。……
“照*这么*学，你一辈子学不会骑车，”莱顿先生说，带着秘密持有者的那种居高临下的一笑，抓起一块饼干走了。她心惊地攀住他那快活的、苦恼的、离去的身影。此刻屋里什么都不剩，只剩回声。汉考克先生坐着嚼他的饼干和干酪，眼里一副坚决的、钢铁般的专注，眼睛不抬到桌上一片狼藉的水平线以上；可她听得见他思绪的奔流。此刻她只要能忍耐片刻。可她的心思抛来抛去，带着可憎的奴态寻找某个外来的中性话题。她知道，只要带着对他思绪的知觉开口，无论说什么都会被恼恨、被扼杀。起身、咕哝一句工作、悄悄走开，就是把他连同他的裁判一起留下。他要的是用公事公办的超脱声调向她交代一件什么事，然后打发她走，相信她一无所知地去了，自己留在他的道理的圈子里。她拼尽全力挥出一拳，用胡乱的话撞上那沉默而恼怒的额头的支垛。
“我没法相信，离白昼最长的一天不到两个月了。”
“光阴似箭，”汉考克先生冷冷地应。她被这一击耗尽，缩了回来，在正午煤气灯的房间里，在她话中意思的 realization 这一刺痛下瑟缩。她的目光扫过那灰衣的身影和钝钝的眉目，寻找一种能把明亮流走的日子那无情的销蚀止住的力量。
“‘*Tempus fugit*（光阴飞逝）’，我想该这么说，”他说，轻轻一笑站起来。

===Original===

“*Oui*,” said Miriam angrily, “*le temps s’envole*; *die Zeit vergeht*, in other words.”

===Chinese===

“*Oui*（是），”米丽亚姆没好气地说，“*le temps s’envole*（光阴飞走）；*die Zeit vergeht*（时间流逝），换句话说。”

## XII / 十二
===Original===

Running upstairs to Mr. Hancock’s room a quarter of an hour before his arrival in the morning Miriam found herself wishing that she lived altogether at Wimpole Street. They were all so kind. Life would be simplified if she could throw in her lot with them. Coming in to breakfast after the lesson had been a sort of homecoming. There were pleasant noises about the house; the family shouted carelessly to each other on the stairs, the schoolboy slid down the banisters; the usual subdued manner of the servants was modified by an air of being a possession of the house and liking it. They rushed quietly and happily about. The very aroma of the coffee seemed tranquilly to feed one. At breakfast everyone was cheerful and kind. It was home. They were so sympathetic and amused over the adventure. The meeting in the freshness of the morning made everything easier to handle. It gave the morning a beginning and shed its brightness over the professional hush that fell upon the house at nine o’clock. It would make lunchtime more easy; and at the end of the day, if asked, she would join the family party again.
While Mr. Hancock was looking through his letters she elaborately suppressed a yawn.
“How did you get on?” he asked, with prompt amusement, his eyes on a letter.
“Well, I couldn’t get off; that was just it,” murmured Miriam quietly, enjoying her jest; how strong she felt after her good breakfast.﻿ ﻿…
He turned an amused enquiring face and they both laughed.
Everything in the room was ready for the day’s work. She polished the already bright set of forceps with a luxurious sense of leisure.
“It was perfectly awful. When we got to the Inner Circle Mr. Leyton simply put me on the bicycle and sent me off. *He* rode round the other way and I had to go on and on. He scorched about and kept passing me.”
Mr. Hancock waited smiling for the more that stood in her struggling excited voice.
“There were people going round on horseback and a few other people on bicycles.”
“I expect they all gave you a pretty wide berth.”
“They *did*; except one awful man, an old gentleman sailing along looking at nothing.”
“What happened?” laughed Mr. Hancock delightedly.
“It was awful, I was most fearfully rude﻿—I shouted ‘*Get* out of the way’ and I was on the wrong side of the road; but miles off, only I *knew* I couldn’t get back I had forgotten how to steer.”
“What did he do?”

===Chinese===

早晨，比汉考克先生到早一刻钟，米丽亚姆跑上楼去他的房间，发觉自己巴不得干脆整个人住在温波尔街。他们全这么和气。倘若能把自己的命运同他们拢在一起，生活就简单了。上完课进来吃早饭，有一种回家的滋味。宅子里处处是快活的声响；一家人在楼梯上彼此随意地喊话，上学的小儿子从扶手栏上滑下来；仆役们一贯的收敛里，带着一点“自己是这宅子的一份子、且乐得如此”的神气。他们安安静静、欢欢喜喜地奔忙。连咖啡的香气都仿佛安安静静地滋养人。早饭桌上人人快活、和气。这就是家。他们对这场冒险那样富于同情又觉得好玩。在清晨的新鲜里碰头，一切都好对付了。它给早晨一个开头，把它的亮光洒在九点钟降临全宅的行医的肃静上。它会叫午饭时分也好过些；一天终了，若蒙邀请，她就再入这家的圈子。
汉考克先生翻检信件时，她煞费苦心地压下一个哈欠。
“您进行得怎么样？”他即刻带着趣味问，眼睛还盯着信。
“嗯，我下不了车；症结就在这儿，”米丽亚姆轻轻咕哝，玩味自己的玩笑；一顿好早饭之后她觉得自己多有力气。……
他转过一张觉得有趣、探问的脸，两人都笑了。
屋里样样已为一天的活计备齐。她带着奢侈的闲暇感，把那本来已亮的一组拔牙钳又擦了一遍。
“简直糟透了。到了内环道，莱顿先生干脆把我扶上车就放开了。*他*从另一头绕，我只得一直骑一直骑。他飞来飞去，不停地赶过我。”
汉考克先生含笑等着她那吃力兴奋的嗓音里还憋着的下文。
“有人骑马绕圈，还有几个骑车的。”
“我料想他们全躲您躲得远远的。”
“可不；除了一个可怕的男人，一位老先生，一路漂过去，什么都不看。”
“怎么啦？”汉考克先生开心地笑。
“糟透了，我凶得吓人——我喊‘*让*开’，而我自己走错了道；不过隔得老远，只是我*知道*拐不回来了，我忘了怎么掌把了。”
“他怎么样？”

===Original===

“He swept round me looking very frightened and disturbed.”
“Hadn’t you a bell?”
“Yes, but it meant sliding my hand along. I daren’t do that; nobody seemed to want it, they all glided about; they were really awfully nice. I *had* to go on because I couldn’t get off. I can wobble along, but I can’t mount or dismount. I was never so frightened in my life.”
“I’m afraid you’ve had a very drastic time.”
“I fell off in the end I was so dead beat.”
“But this is altogether too drastic. Where was Leyton?”
“Rushing round and round meeting me and then overtaking me, startling me out of my wits by ringing behind for me to get to the side. Nobody else did that. It was awfully kind. I went tacking about from side to side.”
“I’m afraid you’ve had a very drastic time. I think you’d better come up this evening and learn getting on and off on the lawn; that’s the way to do it.”
“Oh,” said Miriam gratefully; “but I have no machine. Mrs. Orly lent me hers.”
“I daresay we can hire a machine.”

===Chinese===

“他绕着我掠过去，一脸的惊惶和不安。”
“您没按铃吗？”
“有铃，可那得把手滑过去够。我不敢；也没人需要铃，人人都滑来滑去；他们真是好得不得了。我*只得*一直骑，因为下不来。我能摇摇晃晃地前行，可上不去也下不来。我这辈子没这么害怕过。”
“恐怕您受了场太严酷的考验。”
“最后我摔下来了，实在累瘫了。”
“可这就太过严酷了。莱顿呢？”
“一圈一圈地飞，迎着我，又赶过我，在后面按铃叫我靠边，吓得我魂飞魄散。别人都不这样。真是好得不得了。我就这么左一拐右一拐地抢风。”
“恐怕您受了场太严酷的考验。我看您今晚最好到我们草地来学上下车；就得这么学。”
“哦，”米丽亚姆感激地说；“可我没有车。是奥利太太借我的。”
“我想我们可以租一辆。”

## XIII / 十三
===Original===

Miriam found it difficult to believe that the girl was a dental secretary. She swept about among Miss Szigmondy’s guests in a long Liberty dress, her hands holding her long scarf about her person as if she were waiting for a clear space to leap or run, staying nowhere, talking here and there with the assurance of a successful society woman, laughing and jesting, swiftly talking down the group she was with and passing on with a shouted remark about herself as she had done in the library on the night of Lord Kelvin’s lecture.﻿ ﻿… “I’m tired of being good; I’m going to try being naughty for a change.” Mr. Hancock had stood planted before her in laughing admiration, waiting for the next thing that she might say. How could he of all men in the world be taken out of himself by an effective trick? He had laughed more spontaneously than Miriam had ever seen him do. What *was* this effective thing? An appearance of animation. That it seemed, could make any man, even Mr. Hancock, if it were free from any suggestion of loudness or vulgarity, stand gaping and disarmed. Why had he volunteered the information that she was eighteen and secretary to his friend in Harley Street. “You don’t seem very *keen*;” that was her voice from the other end of the room; using the new smart word with a delicate emphasis, pretending interest in something, meaning nothing at all. She was a middle-aged woman, she would never be older than she was now. She saw nothing and no one, nor ever would. In all her life she would never be arrested by anything. Nice kind people would call her “a charming girl.”﻿ ﻿… “Charming girls” were taught to behave effectively and lived in a brilliant death, dealing death all round them. Nothing could live in their presence. No natural beauty, no spectacle of art, no thought, no music. They were uneasy in the presence of these things, because their presence meant cessation of “charming” behaviour﻿—except at such moments as they could use the occasion to decorate themselves. *They had no souls.* Yet in social life nothing seemed to possess any power but their surface animation.
There was real power in that other woman. Her strong young comeliness was good, known to be good. It was strange that a student of music should be known for her work among the poor.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆很难相信那姑娘是个牙医诊所秘书。她穿着一条长长的利伯提面料长裙（Liberty dress）在齐格蒙迪小姐（Miss Szigmondy）的宾客间扫来扫去，双手把长围巾拢在身上，仿佛在等一块空地好腾身跃起或奔跑，哪儿也不停留，东一处西一处地谈笑，带着社交名媛的笃定，笑着、说着俏皮话，利利索索把身边一群人说到无话，再高声抛下一句关于自己的话扬长而去，一如开尔文勋爵演讲那晚她在图书馆里的做派。……“我做好姑娘做腻了；换个花样，我要试试做坏姑娘。”汉考克先生曾稳稳站在她面前，笑着赞叹，等她的下一句话。天下男人里怎么会偏偏是他，被一个奏效的把戏带出了自我？他笑得比米丽亚姆见过的任何时候都自然。这奏效的东西到底是什么？一种生动的外表。这东西仿佛能让任何男人、连汉考克先生也包括在内——只要它不带一点聒噪或俗气的意味——张着嘴、缴了械地呆立。他为什么会主动报出她十八岁、是他哈利街那位朋友的秘书。“您看上去不太*热切*嘛；”那是她从房间另一头传来的嗓音；把那句新式的时髦字眼用精微的重音说出来，对什么装出兴致，实则毫无所指。她是个中年女人了，却永远不会比现在更老。她什么都看不见、谁也看不见，永远如此。她一生中不会被任何东西攔住。好心的人们会叫她“迷人的姑娘”。……“迷人的姑娘”们被教养成行事奏效的人，活在一片辉煌的死亡里，向四周散发死亡。在她们面前什么都活不成。没有自然的美，没有艺术的壮观，没有思想，没有音乐。她们在这些东西面前浑身不自在，因为这些东西的在场意味着“迷人”举止的终止——除非在她们能借这场合装点自己的时刻。*她们没有灵魂。*可在社交生活里，仿佛除了她们表面的生动，什么也不具备任何力量。
那另一个女人身上有真正的力量。她那年轻而健美的身段安详的大轮廓在屋里放着光；她白皙的前额上那道光——在刻意和善的脸庞上方浑然不觉——是满眼所见最可爱的东西；那刻意和善的脸精蹋了它，不久还要改了它；除非有什么宏大的异象降临，它将在“住房问题”上沟壑纵横，那张脸会干涸，它的白色的生命从源头被切断；此刻她正在源头；什么都可以对她说。汉考克先生认得她的好，说起她来带着钦佩与敬意。她在这里干什么，在这些俗世的音乐家中间？*她*永远成不了音乐家，成不了一流的音乐家。那么她有野心。她穷。有什么人在资助她……齐格蒙迪小姐！为什么？她一定知道自己永远成不了音乐家。米丽亚姆蜷在她角落里。这位好女人竟要当着这些名流唱歌。多好的、洪大的、自由的嗓音。……“*何时*我们相会——自由而*高洁*，在荒原的石楠丛——”倘若汉考克先生能听见她唱那支歌，他的心一定早就飞向她了吧？她知道，刻骨铭心地知道那意味着什么。她*渴望*洗净的火焰，连她，连她那发光的额头；她的灵魂顺着绵延的长音飞向它的异象，她唱时黑眼睛定定地望着它，她清亮的音一声声呼唤她灵魂的伴侣，要他里面最好的东西。她是真实的灵魂，不计代价。她会抵达她的异象，尽管她渴望的尘世伴侣或许同她交臂而过。荒原纯粹的美将为她留存，将永远沿着她生命的两岸铺展。……

===Original===

The serene large outlines of her form gave out light in the room; and the light on her white brow unconscious above her deliberately kind face was the loveliest thing to be seen; the deliberately kind face spoiled it, and would presently change it; unless some great vision came to her it would grow furrowed over “the housing problem” and the face would dry up, its white life cut off at a source; at present she was at the source; one could tell her anything. Mr. Hancock recognised her goodness, spoke of her with admiration and respect. What was she doing here, among all these worldly musicians? *She* would never be a musician, never a first-class musician. Then she had ambition. She was poor. Someone was helping her﻿ ﻿… Miss Szigmondy! Why? She must know she would never make a musician. Miriam cowered in her corner. The good woman was actually going to sing before all these celebrities. What a fine great free voice.﻿ ﻿… “*When* shall we meet﻿—*refined* and free, amongst the moorland *brack‑en*﻿ ﻿…” if Mr. Hancock could have heard her sing that, surely his heart must have gone out to her? She knew, to her inmost being, what that meant. She *longed* for cleansing fires, even she with her radiant forehead; her soul flew out along the sustained notes towards its vision, her dark eyes were set upon it as she sang, the clear tones of her voice called to the companion of her soul for the best that was in him. She was the soul of truth, counting no cost. She would attain her vision, though the earthly companion she longed for might pass her by. The pure beauty of the moorland would remain for her, would set itself along the shores of her life forever.﻿ ﻿…
But she could not sing. It was the worst kind of English singing, all volume and emphasis and pressure. Was there that in her goodness too﻿ ﻿… deliberate kindness to everybody. Was that a method﻿—just a social method? She was one of those people about whom it would be said that she never spoke ill of anyone. But was not indiscriminate deliberate conscious goodness to everybody an insult to humanity? People who were like that never knew the difference between one person and another. “Philanthropic” people were never sympathetic. They pitied. Pity was not sympathy. It was a denial of something. It assumed that life was pitiful. Yet her clear eyes would see through anything, any evil thing to the human being behind.

===Chinese===

可她不会唱。这是最坏的一种英国式唱法，全靠音量、重音和挤压。她的好里头是否也有这个……对人人刻意地和善。那是一种方法吗——只是一种社交的方法？她是那种人们会说她从不讲任何人坏话的人。可是，不分彼此、刻意自觉的对人人都好，难道不是对人类的侮辱？那样的人从来分不清张三和李四。“慈善”的人们从不富有同情。他们怜悯。怜悯不是同情。它是对某样东西的否认。它假定生命是可怜的。然而她那双清亮的眼睛看穿一切，看穿任何邪恶的东西，直看到背后的人。

===Original===

But she knew it, and practised it like a doctor. She had never been amazed by the fact that there were any human beings at all﻿ ﻿… and with all her goodness she had plans and ambitions. She wanted to be a singer﻿—and she was thinking about somebody. Men were dazzled by the worldly little secretary and they reverenced the singer and her kind. Irreligious men would respect religion for her sake﻿—and would wish, thinking of her, to live in a particular kind of way; but she would never lead a man to religion because she had no thoughts and no ideas.
The surprise of finding these two women here and the pain of observing them was a just reward for having come to Miss Szigmondy’s At Home without a real impulse﻿—just to see the musicians and to be in the same room with them. All that remained was to write to someone about them by name. There was nothing to do but mention their names. There was no wonder about them. They were all *fat*. Not one of them was an artist and they all hated each other. It was like a ballad concert. They all sang in the English way. They were not in the least like the instrumentalists; or St. James’s Hall Saturday afternoon audiences, not that kind of “queer soft lot”; not shadowy grey or dead white or with that curious transparent look; they all looked ruddy or pink, and sleek; they had the same sort of kindly common sense as Harriett’s Lord and Lady Bollingdon﻿ ﻿… perhaps to keep a voice going it was necessary to be fat.

===Chinese===

可她认得它，并且像医生一样施行它。她从不曾为人间竟有人存在这件事惊愕过……而怀着她的全部好处，她有计划有野心。她想当歌唱家——而她心里想着某个男人。男人们被那个俗世的小秘书晃得眼花，却对歌唱家和她的同类心怀敬意。不信教的男人会为她看重宗教——会想着她、希望自己活得端正；可她永远带不来一个男人走向宗教，因为她没有思想、没有见解。
在这里遇见这两个女人，这场惊讶与观察她们的痛苦，是对自己毫无真实冲动地来赴齐格蒙迪小姐的家庭招待会（At Home）——只为看看音乐家们、同他们共处一室——的公正报偿。剩下的只有写信给什么人，指名道姓地写她们。除了提一提她们的名字，无事可做。她们身上没有奇迹。她们全*胖*。没有一个算艺术家，而她们个个彼此相恨。这像一场民谣音乐会。她们全用英国路数唱。她们一点也不像器乐家；也不像圣詹姆斯厅星期六下午的听众，不是那种“古怪的软心肠”；不阴灰、不惨白、不带那种奇异的透明相；她们个个红润或粉红，油光水滑；她们有哈丽特家博林顿勋爵和夫人那种和气的常识……也许要把嗓子维持下去，就得胖。

## XIV / 十四
===Original===

“It was simply heavenly going off﻿—all standing in the hall in evening dress while the servants blew for hansoms. I wore my bridesmaid’s dress with a piece of tulle arranged round the top of the bodice. It was wrong at the back so I had to sit very carefully the whole evening to prevent it going up like a muffler, but never mind; it was heavenly I tell you. We bowled off down through the west-end in three hansoms one behind the other, in the dark. You know the gleam and shine inside a hansom sprinting along a dark empty street where the lamps are few and dim; (see *The Organist’s Daughter*) and then came the bright streets all alight and full of dinner and theatre people in evening dress in hansoms and you kept getting wedged in between other hansoms with people talking and laughing all round you; and it took about ten minutes to get from the end of Regent Street across to the other side of Piccadilly where we dined in wicked Rupert Street. Just as the caviar was brought in we heard that the Prince of Wales had won the Derby. Shakespeare is extraordinary. I had no idea Hamlet was so full of quotations.”
Miriam flushed heavily as the last words ran automatically from her pen. The sense of the richly moving picture that had filled her all the morning and now kept her sitting happily under the hot roof at her small dusty table in the full breadth of Saturday afternoon would be gone if she left that sentence. She felt a curious painful shock at the tips of her fingers as she reread it; a current singing within her was driven back by it.﻿ ﻿… Mrs. Orly’s face had been all alive and alight when she had leant forward across Mr. Hancock and said the words that had seemed so meaningless and irritating. Perhaps she too had felt something she wanted to express and had lost it at that moment. Certainly both she and Mr. Orly would feel the beauty of Shakespeare. But the words had shattered the spell of Shakespeare and writing them down like that was spoiling the description of the evening, though Harriett would not think so.
But anyhow the letter would not do for Harriett﻿—even if words could be found to express “Shakespeare.” That would not interest Harriett. She would think the effort funny and Miriamish but it would not mean anything to her. She had been to Shakespeare because she adored Ellen Terry and put up with Irving for her sake.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“动身的时候简直像上了天——一大家子穿晚礼服站在门厅里，仆人们吹哨叫双轮马车。我穿着伴娘裙，上衣领口围了一圈薄纱。后腰处出了岔子，只好整晚小心翼翼地坐着，免得它像围脖一样蹿上来，不过不要紧；我告诉你，那真是天堂。三辆双轮马车一前一后，在黑暗里一路滚下西区。你知道双轮马车沿着灯稀光暗的空街疾驰时，车厢里那种微光和闪耀；（见《管风琴师的女儿》）然后是亮堂堂的街，灯火通明，满是赴宴看戏的晚礼服人潮坐在一辆辆双轮马车里，你不断被挤进别的马车中间，四下里全是人说人笑；从摄政街这头横过皮卡迪利到我们吃晚饭的那条邪恶的鲁珀特街，足足走了十分钟。鱼子酱刚端上来，我们听说威尔士亲王赢了德比。莎士比亚真是了不起。我原来一点不知道《哈姆雷特》里有这么多名言。”

最后几个字从笔尖自动淌出来时，米丽亚姆重重地红了脸。那幅丰盈流动的画面充实了她一上午、此刻还使她快活地坐在灼热的屋顶下、坐在积尘的小桌前、坐在星期六下午满满的日光里——留下这句话，那画面就要走了。她重读这一句，指尖感到一阵古怪的、发疼的震颤；体内一道歌唱的电流被它逼了回去。…奥利太太当时俯身越过汉考克先生说出这句话时，满脸活跃放光，那几个字听着却那样无谓、那样叫人恼。也许她也感到了一点想表达的东西，就在那一刻失去了。她同奥利先生俩当然感受得到莎士比亚的美。可那几个字击碎了莎士比亚的咒，这样写下来，也就毁了那个夜晚的描写——虽然哈丽特不会这么看。

可无论如何这封信给哈丽特不合适——哪怕能找到字眼表达“莎士比亚”。那引不起哈丽特的兴趣。她只会觉得这份苦劲好笑、够米丽亚姆风格，可对她没有任何意义。她去听莎士比亚，是因为她崇拜埃伦·特里，为了特里才将就欧文。…

===Original===

People in London seemed to think that Irving was just as great as Ellen Terry.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps now Irving would seem different. Perhaps Irving was great.﻿ ﻿… I will go and hear Irving in Shakespeare﻿ ﻿… no money and no theatres except with other people.﻿ ﻿… The rest of the letter would simply hurt Harriett, because it would seem like a reflection on theatres with her. Theatres with her had had a magic that last night could not touch﻿ ﻿… sitting in the front row of the pit, safely in after the long wait, the walls of the theatre going up, softly lit buff and gold, fluted and decorated and bulging with red curtained boxes, the clear view across the empty stalls of the dim height of fringed curtain hanging in long straight folds, the certainty that Harriett shared the sense of the theatre, that for her too when the orchestra began the great motionless curtain shut them in in a life where everything else in the world faded away and was forgotten, the sight of the perfection of happiness on Harriett’s little buff-shadowed face, the sudden running ripple, from side to side, of the igniting footlights﻿ ﻿… the smoothly clicking rustle of the withdrawing curtain﻿ ﻿… the magic square of the lit scene﻿ ﻿… the daily growth of the charm of these things during that week when they had gone to a theatre every night, so that on looking back the being in the theatre with the certainty of the moving changing scenes ahead was clearer than either of the plays they had seen.﻿ ﻿… She sat staring through the open lattice.﻿ ﻿… The sound of the violin from the house down the street that had been a half-heard obbligato to her vision of last night came in drearily, filling the space whence the vision had departed, with uneasy questions. She turned to her letter to recapture the impulse with which she had sat down.﻿ ﻿… If she turned it into a letter to Eve, all the description of the evening would have to be changed; Eve knew all about grandeurs, with the Greens’ large country house and their shooting-boxes and visits to London hotels; the bright glories must go﻿—overwhelming and unexpressed. Why did that make one so sad? Was it because it suggested that one cared more for the gay circumstances than for the thing seen? What was it they had seen? Why had they gone? What *was* Shakespeare? Her vision returned to her as she brooded on this fresh problem.

===Chinese===

伦敦的人好像都觉得欧文同埃伦·特里一样伟大。…也许如今再看欧文会不一样了。也许欧文是伟大的。…我要去听欧文演的莎士比亚…没有钱，也没有戏院，除非同人同去。…信余下的部分只会伤哈丽特，因为那看着像影射同她一起看的戏。同她一起的戏院自有一种昨夜碰不着的魔力…坐在正厅后排的第一排，长长的等候之后安然入场，剧场的四壁升上去，柔柔地亮着浅黄和金，起棱带饰，一层层红帘包厢鼓出来，越过空着的正厅前座，望得清高远处垂着流苏的大幕，笔直的长褶，确知哈丽特分享着剧场的感觉，确知对她也一样——乐队一响，那幅巍然不动的大幕便把她们关进一种生活，世上其余的一切都褪去、被忘却，看得见哈丽特那张小小的、罩着浅黄光影的脸上圆满的幸福，脚灯点燃时那一阵突然的、从一头燃到另一头的细浪…大幕退开时那声顺滑的咔嗒轻响…亮起来的舞台那一方魔镜…那一星期她们每晚上一家戏院，这些事物的魅力一天天长，以致回望起来，身在那剧场里、确知前头有流动变幻的场景在等着，竟比她们看过的两出戏都更清晰。…她坐在那里，望着敞开的窗格。…街那头人家的小提琴声，原是她昨夜幻象的半伴奏，此刻凄凄地进来，填满幻象离去后留下的空当，带着不安的问题。她转回信上，想追回落座时的那股冲动。…倘若把它改写成给伊芙的信，整晚的描写就都得改；伊芙见识惯了阔气——格林家的大乡宅、猎屋、住伦敦旅馆；那些明亮的光华只好去掉——压倒一切而无从表达。为什么这叫人这样难过？是因为它暗示人更看重热闹的场面，而不是看到的那样东西吗？她们看到的究竟是什么？她们为什么去？莎士比亚究竟是什么？她对着这道新问题出神，幻象又回到了她身上。

===Original===

The whole scene of the theatre was round her once more; she was sitting in the half darkness gazing at the stage. What had it been for her? What was it that came from the stage? Something﻿—*real*﻿ ﻿… to say that drove it away. She looked again and it clustered once more, alive. The gay flood of the streets, the social excitements and embarrassments of the evening were a conflagration; circling about the clear bright kernel of moving lights and figures on the stage. She gazed at the bright stage. Moments came sharply up, grouped figures, spoken words. She held them, her contemplation aglow with the certainty that something was there that set her alight with love, making her whole in the midst of her uncertainty and ignorance. Words and phrases came, a sentence here and there that had suddenly shaped and deepened a scene. Perhaps it was only in seeing Shakespeare acted that one could appreciate him? But it was *not* the acting. No one could act. They all just missed it. It was all very well for Mag to laugh. They *did* just miss it.﻿ ﻿… “Why, my child? In what way?” “They act at the audience, they take their cues too quickly and have their emotions too abruptly; and from outside not inside.” “But if they felt at all, all the time, they would go mad or die.” “No, they would not. But even if they did not feel it, if they looked, it would be enough. They don’t *look* at the thing they are doing.” It was not the acting. Nor the play. The characters of the story were always tiresome. The ideas, the wonderful quotations if you looked closely at them were everyone’s ideas; things that everybody knew. To read Shakespeare carefully all through would only be to find all the general things somewhere or other. But that did not matter. Being ignorant of him and of history did not matter, as long as you heard him. Poetry! The poetry of Shakespeare﻿ ﻿… ? Primers of literature told one that. It did not explain the charm. Just the sound. Music. Like Beethoven. Bad acting cannot spoil Shakespeare. Bad playing cannot destroy Beethoven. It was the *sound* of Shakespeare that made the scenes real﻿—that made Winter’s Tale, so long ago and so bewildering, remain in beauty.﻿ ﻿… “Dear Eve, Shakespeare is a sound﻿ ﻿…” She tore up the letter. The next time she wrote to Eve she must remember to say that. The garret was stifling.

===Chinese===

剧场的整个场景又围住了她；她坐在半暗里凝望舞台。那于她曾是什么？从台上下来的是什么？一样东西——真的…一说出口它就跑了。她再看，它又聚拢来，活着。街上快活的人潮，晚间的社交兴奋与窘迫，是一场大火；环绕着舞台上一团移动的光与人的、清亮的核。她凝望那明亮的舞台。一些时刻锐利地浮上来，成组的人影，说出的话。她攔住它们，她的凝望灼灼放光，确知那里有一样东西把她点燃成爱，在她的不定和无知中使她完整。字句来了，这里一句那里一句，曾骤然使一个场景成形、加深。也许只有看莎士比亚的演出，人才欣赏得了他？可那不是演技。没有人演得出。他们全都差那么一点。玛格笑就随她笑。他们就是差那么一点。…“为什么呢，孩子？差在哪儿？”“他们对着观众演，接台词接得太快，情绪起得太陡；而且从外面不从里面。”“可他们若真一直感受着，会发疯，会死。”“不会。可哪怕他们感受不到，只要看，就够了。他们不看自己正做着的那件事。”不是演技。也不是剧本。故事里的人物永远烦人。那些思想，那些妙极了的名句，凑近了一看，人人的思想；人人都知道的东西。把莎士比亚从头到尾细读一遍，无非是在这里那里撞见所有的泛泛之理。可那不要紧。不懂他、不懂历史都不要紧，只要听他。诗！莎士比亚的诗…？文学入门书会告诉你。它解释不了那魅力。就是那声音。音乐。像贝多芬（Beethoven）。糟糕的演出毁不了莎士比亚。糟糕的演奏毁不了贝多芬。是莎士比亚的声音使那些场景成真——是它使《冬天的故事》，那么久远、那么令人迷惑的《冬天的故事》，留在美里。…“亲爱的伊芙，莎士比亚是一种声音…”她把信撕了。下次给伊芙写信，一定记得说这句。阁楼闷死了。

===Original===

Away from the brilliant window the room was just as hot; the close thick smell of dust sickened her. She came back to the table, sitting as near as possible to the open. The afternoon had been wasted trying to express her evening and nothing had been expressed. The thought of last night was painful now. She had spoiled it in some way. Her heart beat heavily in the stifling room. Her head ached and her eyes were tired. She was too tired to walk; and there was no money; barely enough for next week’s A.B.C. suppers. There was no comfort. It was May﻿ ﻿… in a stuffy dusty room. May. Her face quivered and her head sank upon the hot table.

===Chinese===

离了那扇明亮的窗，屋里一样热；密闭的、厚厚的尘味叫她恶心。她回到桌前，尽可能挨近敞开的窗坐。这个下午浪费了——想表达她的夜晚，什么也没表达出来。此刻想起昨夜就叫她疼。她在什么地方把它弄坏了。闷热的屋里她的心沉沉地跳。头也疼，眼睛也乏。乏得走不动路；又没有钱；下星期的A.B.C.晚饭都刚够。没有一点慰藉。已经是五月了…在一间闷热积尘的屋子里。五月。她的脸抽搐了一下，头垂落在滚烫的桌上。

## XV / 十五
===Original===

Nearly all the roses were half-opened buds; firm and stiff. Larger ones put in here and there gave the effect of mass. Closest contemplation enhanced the beauty of the whole. Each rose was perfect. The radiant mass was lovely throughout. The body of the basket curved firmly away to its slender hidden base; the smooth sweep of the rim and the delicate high arch of the handle held the roses perfectly framed. It was a perfect gift.﻿ ﻿… It had been quite enough to have the opportunity of doing little things for Mrs. Berwick﻿ ﻿… the surprise of the roses. The *surprise* of them. Roses, roses, roses﻿ ﻿… all the morning they had stood, making the morning’s work happy; visible all over the room. Everyone in the house had had the beautiful shock of them. And they were still as they had been when they had been gathered in the dew. If they were in water by the end of the afternoon the buds would revive and expand﻿ ﻿… even after the hours in the Lyceum. If they were thrown now into the wastepaper basket it would not matter. They would go on being perfect﻿—to the end of life. “And as long; as my heart is beat‑ing; as long; as my eyes; have; tears.”
---
Winthrop came up punctually at one o’clock as he had promised. “It would save you comin’ down if I was to ph‑come up.” It would go on then. He had thought about it and meant to do it. She opened the cash box quickly and deftly in her gratitude and handed him his four sovereigns and the money for the second mechanic and the apprentices. He waited gently while she counted it out. Next Saturday she would have it ready for him. “Thank you Miss﻿—; ph‑ph *Good* afternoon,” he said cheerfully. “Good afternoon Mr. Winthrop,” she responded busily with all her heart and listened as he clattered away downstairs. A load was lifted from Saturday mornings, for good. No more going down to run the gauntlet of the row of eyes and get herself along the bench, depositing the various sums. Nothing in future but the letters, the overhauling of Mr. Hancock’s empty surgery, the easy lunch with Mr. Leyton, and the weekend. She entered the sums in the petty cash book. There was that. They would always be that week after week. But today the worrying challenge of it disappeared in the joy of the last entry. “Self,” she wrote, the light across the outspread prospect of her life steadying and deepening as she wrote, “one pound, five.”

===Chinese===

玫瑰几乎全是半开的骨朵；结实而挺括。这里那里插几朵大的，攒出团团的效果。凑到最近处端详，反增整体之美。每一朵玫瑰都完美。这灿然的一团处处可爱。篮身结实地弯下去，收进纤细的暗底；篮口平滑的一道弧，篮柄纤纤高拱，把玫瑰框得完完美美。这是一件完美的礼物。……能为贝里克太太做点小事就已经很够了……还有这玫瑰的惊奇。它们带来的*惊奇*。玫瑰，玫瑰，玫瑰……整个上午它们立在那里，叫上午的活计都欢喜；满屋都望得见。宅子里人人都受过它们美丽的一惊。而它们还同带露摘下时一样。倘若到下午过去之前浸进水里，骨朵会还阳、舒展……哪怕在兰心剧院待过几个钟头之后。倘若这会儿把它们扔进字纸篓，也不要紧。它们会继续完美下去——直到生命尽头。“只要我的心还跳着；只要我的眼还有泪。”
---
温思罗普如约一点整上了楼。“我要是上——上来，就省得您下去了。”这么说这事就成了。他想过，并且打定了主意。她感激之下，快而利落地开了钱箱，递给他那四枚金镑，还有第二个技工和学徒们的钱。他温和地等她点清。下星期六她会备好。“谢谢小——；再见，下午好，”他喜洋洋地说。“再见，温思罗普先生，”她全心全意地忙着应道，听他嗒嗒地下楼去。星期六早晨的一副担子，从此永远卸下了。再也不用下去闯那一排眼睛的夹道、把自己挪过长工作台、把一笔笔钱各归各位。往后只剩下信，汉考克先生空诊室的检视，同莱顿先生轻省的午饭，和周末。她把这几笔登进零用现金簿。有这么一样。它们将一星期又一星期总是这样。可今天，它那恼人的挑战消失在最后一笔的欢喜里。“自己，”她写道，落笔时光在铺展开的生平前景上稳住、加深，“一镑，五先令。”

===Original===

The five, written down, sent a thrill from the contemplated page. Taking the customary sovereign from the cashbox she placed it carefully in the middle pocket of her purse and closed the clip. The five shillings she distributed about the side-pockets; half a crown, a shilling, two sixpenny bits and six coppers. The purse was full of money. By September she would have about four pounds five in hand and two pounds ten of her month’s holiday money still unspent; six pounds fifteen; she could go to a matinée every week and still have about half the four pounds five; about four pounds fifteen altogether; enough to hire a bicycle for the month and buy some summer blouses for the holiday.﻿ ﻿… She pocketed the heavy purse. Why was there always a feeling of guilt about a salary? It was the same every week. The life at Wimpole Street was so full and so interesting; she was learning so much and seeing so much. Salary was out of place﻿—a payment for leading a glorious life, half of which was entirely her own. The extra five shillings was a present from the Orlys and Mr. Hancock. She could manage on the pound. The new sum was wealth, superfluity. They would expect more of her in future. Surely it would be possible to give more; with so much money; to find the spirit to come punctually at nine; always to have everything in complete readiness in all three surgeries; to keep all the books up to date.﻿ ﻿… But they would not have given her the rise at the end of five months if they had not felt she was worth it.﻿ ﻿… It would make all the difference to the summer. Hopefully she took a loose sheet of paper and made two lists of the four pages of the week’s entries﻿—dissecting them under the heads of workshop and surgery. About fifteen pounds had been spent. Again and again with heating head she added her pages of small sums, getting each time slightly different results, until at last they balanced with the dissected lists﻿—twice in succession. The hall clock struck one and Mr. Leyton came downstairs rattling and rattled into her room. “How d’you like this get up?” The general effect of the blue grey uniform and brown leather belt and bandolier was pleasing. “Oh, *jolly*,” she said abstractedly to his waiting figure. He clattered downstairs to lunch. *Everybody* had outside interests. Mr. Hancock would be on the Broads by now.

===Chinese===

写下的那“五先令”从端详着的页面上送来一阵颤栗。她从钱箱里取出惯常的那枚金镑，小心地放进钱夹正中的口袋，扣上夹子。五先令她分派到各个侧袋；半克朗一枚，先令一枚，两个六便士，六个铜子。钱夹满了钱。到九月她手头会有大约四镑五，还有两个月假期的钱两镑十没花；六镑十五；她可以每星期去看一场日戏，四镑五还能剩下约一半；一共约四镑十五；够租一个月自行车、再给假期买几件夏衫。……她把沉沉的钱夹装进口袋。为什么一提薪水总有亏心之感？每星期都一样。温波尔街的生活这么充实、这么有趣；她学这么多、见这么多。薪水是错位的——等于为一段辉煌人生领酬，而那人生有一半全然是她自己的。多出来的五先令是奥利一家和汉考克先生的礼物。一镑她过得去。新的数目是财富，是余裕。往后他们对她的指望会更多。多半真能多给些；有这么多钱；打得起九点整到的精神；三间诊室永远样样齐备；所有账簿永远赶在前头。……可他们若不是觉得她值，就不会在五个月头上给她加薪。……这将叫这个夏天全然不同。她满怀希望地抽出一页散纸，把本周四页流水分列两张单子——按工场和诊室两项解剖。花掉了大约十五镑。她一遍又一遍、头都发烫地加她那一页页小账，每回结果都略有出入，直到终于同解剖开的单子对平了——连着两回。门厅的钟敲一点，莱顿先生一路哗啦下楼、哗啦进她屋。“这一身穿起来怎么样？”蓝灰军服加棕色皮带和弹药带，总的效果悦目。“哦，*帅*，”她心不在焉地对他候着的身影说。他嗒嗒嗒下楼吃午饭去了。*人人*都有户外的事。汉考克先生这会儿该到布罗兹湖区了。

===Original===

Her afternoon beckoned, easy with the superfluity of money. Anxiously she counted over the balance in the cash box. It was two and ninepence short. Damnation. Damnation. “Put it down to stamps﻿—or miscellanea; not accounted for.” She looked back through her entries. Stamps, one pound, at the beginning of the week. Stamps, ten shillings yesterday. It could not be that. It was some carelessness﻿—something not entered﻿—or a miscalculation. Something she had paid out to the workshop in the middle of a rush and forgotten to put down. She went back through her entries one by one with flaring cheeks; recovering the history of the week and recalling incidents. Nothing came that would account for the discrepancy. It was simply a mistake. Something had been put down wrong. The money had been spent. But was it a workshop or a surgery expense that had gone wrong? “Postage etc.: two and nine,” would make it all right﻿—but the account would not be right. Either the workshop or the surgery account must suffer. It would be another of those little inaccurate spots that came every few weeks; that she would always have to remember﻿ ﻿… her mind toiled, goaded and hot.﻿ ﻿… Mr. Orly had borrowed five pounds to buy tools at Buck and Hickman’s and come back with the money spent and some of the tools to be handed to the practice. Perhaps it was in balancing that up that the mistake had occurred﻿ ﻿… or the electric lamp account; some for the house, some for the practice and some for the workshop. Thoroughly miserable she made a provisional entry of the sum against surgery in pencil and left the account unbalanced. Perhaps on Monday it would come right. When the ledgers were all in place and the safe and drawers locked she stretched her limbs and forced away her misery. The roses reproached her, but only for a moment. They understood, in detail, as clearly as she did, all the difficulties. They took her part. Standing there waiting, they too felt that there was nothing now but lunch and Irving.
---
With the basket of roses over her arm she walked as rapidly as possible down to Oxford Circus taking the first turning out of Wimpole Street to hurry the more secretly and conveniently. A bus took her to Charing Cross where she jumped off as soon as it began pulling up and ran down the Strand.

===Chinese===

她的下午在招手，宽裕得有钱上的余裕。她不安地清点钱箱里的结余。短了两先令九便士。该死。该死。“记到邮票——或杂项；未说明。”她回头翻检流水。邮票，一镑，在本周初。邮票，十先令，昨天。不会是那处。是哪一疏忽——一笔没登——或一处错算。忙乱里付给工场、忘了记下的什么。她两颊发烧，一笔一笔往回捋；把这一星期复了一遍，回想种种小事。什么也对不上这个差额。就是一笔错。什么东西记错了。钱是花掉了。可错的究竟是工场的账还是诊室的账？“邮资等：两先令九，”就能摆平——可账就不真了。工场或诊室的账必得有一头吃亏。这将是那种每隔几星期就来的小小的不准的疮点之一；她将永远得记着它……她的脑子苦苦地转，被鞭策着，发热。……奥利先生借了五镑去巴克-希克曼买工具，回来钱花光了、有几件工具归了诊所。也许错就出在平那笔账上……或是电灯的账；一部分归宅子，一部分归诊所，一部分归工场。她惨兮兮地用铅笔给诊室暂记上这笔，账悬着不平。也许星期一会对上的。等账簿各归各位、保险柜和抽屉都锁好，她伸展四肢，强把愁苦驱开。玫瑰责备她，可只有一刻。它们懂得，清清楚楚同她一样，懂得这一切难处。它们站在她一边。立在那儿等着，它们也觉出此刻除了午饭和欧文，再没有别的。
---
她挎着那篮玫瑰，尽快走到牛津广场，出温波尔街就拐头一个弯，好走得又隐蔽又顺当。一辆公共汽车把她载到查令十字，车一靠站她就跳下来，奔下斯特兰德街。

===Original===

As soon as she felt herself flying towards her bourne the fears that last week’s magic would have disappeared left her altogether. Last week had been wonderful, an adventure her first deliberate piece of daring in London. Inside the theatre the scruples and the daring had been forgotten. Today again everything would be forgotten, everything; today’s happiness was more secure; it would not mean going almost foodless over the weekend and without an egg for supper all next week; there was no anticipation of disapproving eyes in the theatre this week; the sense of the impropriety of going alone had gone; it would never return; the feeling of selfishness in spending money on a theatre alone was still there, but a voice within answered that﻿—saying that there was no one at hand to go and no one she knew who would find at the Lyceum performance just what she found, no one to whom it would mean much more than a theatre; like any other theatre and a play, amongst other plays, with a celebrated actor taking the chief part﻿ ﻿… except Mag. Mag had been with her as she gazed. Mag was with her now. Mag, fulfilling one or other of her exciting Saturday afternoon engagements would sit at her side.
Easy and happy she fled along﻿ ﻿… her heart greeting each passenger in the scattered throng she threaded, her eyes upon the traffic in the roadway. A horseless brougham went by, moving smoothly and silently amongst the noisy traffic﻿—the driver looked as though he were fastened to the front of the vehicle, a little tin driver on a clockwork toy; there was nothing between him and the road but the platform of the little tank on which his feet were set. He looked as if he were falling off. If anything ran into him there was nothing to protect him. It left an uncomfortable memory﻿ ﻿… it would only be for carriages; the well-loved horse omnibuses would go on﻿ ﻿… it must be somewhere near here﻿ ﻿… “Lyceum Pit,” there it was, just ahead, easily discernible. Last week when she had had to ask, she had not noticed the words printed on the side of the passage that showed as you came down the Strand. The pavement was clear for a moment and she rounded the near angle and ran home down the passage without slackening her pace, her half-crown ready in her hand, a Lyceum pittite.

===Chinese===

一经觉出自己正飞向目的地，上周的魔法会已消失的恐惧便全然离她而去。上星期妙极了，一场冒险，她在伦敦头一回蓄意的胆大。进了剧院，顾虑和胆大便都忘了。今天一切又将被忘掉，一切；今天的幸福更有把握；它不再意味着周末几乎断粮、下星期整周晚饭没有一只蛋；这星期不用再预想剧院里不以为然的眼睛；独自来看戏不妥的感觉没有了；再也不会回来了；独自花钱看戏自私的感觉还在，可心里有个声音应答它——说身边没有人可同去，她认识的人里没有一个会在兰心的演出里看出她看出的东西，没有一个人，对谁它不过是一家剧院；同别的剧院、一出戏同别的戏一样，一位名角儿演主角……除了玛格。她凝望时玛格同她在一起。玛格此刻也同她在一起。玛格，正赴她这一个或那一个兴奋的星期六午后的约会，会坐在她身边。
轻松而快活，她一路飞奔……心里向她穿插其间的稀疏人流里的每一位乘客致意，眼睛望着路上的车马。一辆无马的四轮轿车驶过，在喧闹的车流里顺顺滑滑、悄然无声——车夫看着像拴在车头，一座发条玩具上的小小的铁皮司机；横在他和路面之间的，只有他双脚所踏的那只小罐子的平台。他看着像随时要栽下去。要有什么撞上他，没有什么护着他。这留下一段不舒服的记忆……那只会是私家车的；深爱的公共马车会一直走下去……该在这附近了……“兰心后座”，就在那儿，就在前头，一眼可辨。上星期她不得不开口问人，没留意从斯特兰德街下来时通道侧面印着的这几个字。人行道恰好空了一瞬，她拐过近处的墙角，脚步不停，一路奔回通道深处，半克朗攥在手里，一个兰心后座客。

===Original===

---
The dark pit seemed very full as she entered the door at the left hand corner; dim forms standing at the back told her there were no seats left; but she made her way across to the right and down the incline hoping for a neglected place somewhere on the extreme right. Her vain search brought her down to the barrier and the end of her inspection of the serried ranks of seated forms to her left swept her eyes forward. She was just under the overhanging balcony of the dress-circle; the well of the theatre opened clear before her as she stood against the barrier, the stalls half full and filling with dim forms gliding in right and left, the upward sweep of the theatre walls covered with boxes from which white faces shone in the gloom, a soft pervading saffron light, bright light heavily screened. There was space all round her, the empty gangway behind, the gangway behind the stalls just in front of the barrier, the view clear away to the stage over the heads of the people sitting in the stalls.﻿ ﻿… Why not stay here? If people stood at the back of the pit they might stand in front. She retreated into the angle made by the out-curving wall of the pit and the pit barrier. Putting down the basket of roses on the floor at her side she leaned against the barrier with her elbows on its rim.
---
He was there before he appeared﻿ ﻿… in the orchestra, in the audience, all over the house. Presently, in a few moments he was going to appear, moving and speaking on the stage. Someone might come forward and announce that he was ill or dead. He would die; perhaps only years hence; but long before one was old﻿ ﻿… death of Henry Irving. No more thoughts of that; he is there﻿—perhaps for twenty years; coming and going, having seasons at the Lyceum. He knew he must die; he did not think about it. He could turn with a smile and go straight up, in a rosy chariot﻿ ﻿… well done thou good and faithful and happy servant. He would go, closing his eyes upon the vision that was always in them, something they saw, something they gave out every moment. Whom the gods love die young﻿ ﻿… not always young in years, but young always; trailing clouds of glory. It is always the unexpected that happens. Things you dread never happen. That is Weber﻿—or Meyerbeer. Who chooses the music? Perhaps he does.
---
The orchestration brought back last week’s performance.

===Chinese===

---
进了左手角上的门，幽暗的后座仿佛满满的；后头站着的朦胧身影告诉她没座了；可她还是往右挤过去、顺坡往下走，指望右端极远处有个被忽略的位子。徒劳的搜寻把她带到围栏，向左扫视一排排紧坐的人影到尽头时，目光被向前掠去。她正站在花楼挑出的包厢底下；背倚围栏，剧场的井在她面前豁然敞开，正厅前座半满，昏暗的身影从左右滑进来，剧场四壁向上的一路尽是包厢，白脸在幽暗里放光，一片弥漫的柔黄的光，重帘密掩的亮光。她四周全是空当，身后空空的走道，围栏前正厅后座后的走道，越过正厅前座一排排头顶，视野直通舞台。……何不就待在这儿？人们既能在后座后头站，也可以在前头站。她退进后座弧出的墙与围栏夹成的角落。把玫瑰篮放在脚边，胳膊肘支上围栏的横档，倚着。
---
他出场之前就在那里……在乐队里，在观众里，满剧场都是。转眼，再过几分钟他就要出场，在台上走动、说话了。说不定会有人走上前宣布他病了或死了。他会死的；也许要到许多年后；可早在人老去之前……亨利·欧文之死。不想这个了；他在那儿——也许还要二十年；来了又去，在兰心一季一季地演。他知道必有一死；他不去想它。他能含笑转身，径直上升，乘一辆玫红的马车……好，你这又良善又忠心又快乐的仆人。他会去的，闽上双眼，眼底永远装着那个幻象，他们看见的某样东西，他们每一刻放射出的某样东西。神所爱的人死得早……不一定年轻在年头，却永远年轻；拖着荣耀的云朵。发生的永远是意外。你怕的事从不发生。那是韦伯——或梅耶贝尔。谁选的曲子？也许是他。
---
乐队的演奏带回了上星期的演出。

===Original===

It was all there; behind the curtain. Shylock, swinging across the stage with his halting dragging stride; halting, standing with bent head; shut-in, lonely sweetness. She looked boldly now, untrammelled in her dark corner at the pictures which had formed part of her distant view all last week in the faraway life at Wimpole Street; the great scenes﻿ ﻿… beautifully staged; “Irving always *stages* everything perfectly”﻿—and battled no longer against her sympathy for Shylock. It no longer shocked her to find herself sharing something of his longing for the blood of the Christians. It was wrong; but were not they too wrong? They must be; there must be some reason for this certainty of sympathy with Shylock and aversion from Bassanio. It might be a wrong reason, but it was there in her. Mag said “that’s his genius; he makes you sympathise even with Shylock.﻿ ﻿…” He shows you that you *do* sympathise with Shylock; Mag thinks that is something to admit shamefacedly. Because those other people were to her just “people.” Bassanio﻿—was it not just as wrong to get into debt and raise money from the Jews as to let money out on usury? But it was his friend. He was innocent. Never mind. They were all, all, smug and complacent in their sunshine. Polished lustful man, with his coarse lustful men friends. Portia and Nerissa were companions in affliction. Beautiful first of all; as lovely and wandering and full of visions as Shylock until their lovers came. Hearn was right. English lovers would shock any Japanese. Not that the Japanese were prudish. According to him they were anything but﻿ ﻿… they would not talk as Englishmen did among themselves and in mixed society in a sort of code; thinking themselves so clever; anyone could talk a code who chose to descend to a mechanical trick.
How much more real was the relation between Portia and Nerissa than between either of the sadly jesting women and their complacently jesting lovers. Did a man *ever* speak in a natural voice﻿—neither blustering, nor displaying his cleverness, nor being simply a lustful slave? Women always despise men under the influence of passion or fatigue. What horrible old men those two would be﻿—still speaking in put on voices to hide their shame, pompous and philosophising.﻿ ﻿… “Man’s love is of man’s life a thing apart﻿ ﻿…” so much the worse for man; there must be something very wrong with his life.

===Chinese===

一切都在那儿；在幕后。夏洛克，拖着迟滞的步子横过舞台；一顺一顿，低头站住；封闭的、孤独的甜美。这会儿她大胆地、无拘无束地在她的暗角里看这些画面——上星期在温波尔街遥远的生活里，它们是她远望的一部分；那几场大戏……排得美极了；“欧文*场面*上从来十全十美”——也不再同她对夏洛克的同情搏斗了。发现自己竟分享着他一点对基督徒见血的渴望，也不再叫她震惊。这是错的；可他们不也错吗？一定错；这份对夏洛克的确凿的同情、对巴萨尼奥的确凿的疏远，必有缘故。这缘故也许是错的，可它在她身上。玛格说“这就是他的天才；他让你连夏洛克也同情。…”他是让你看见你*确实*同情夏洛克；玛格以为那是件该红着脸承认的事。因为那些别的人在她眼里只是“人们”。巴萨尼奥——欠下债、向犹太人借钱，同放债收利难道不是一样错？可那是为朋友。他是无辜的。不要紧。他们全都、全都，在他们阳光里自得而满足。光溜溜的好色之徒，带着他那群粗俗好色的男友。鲍西娅和尼莉莎是苦难中的同伴。先有美；同夏洛克一样可爱、迷惘、满心幻象，直到她们的情人来了。小泉八云说得对。英国的情人会吓坏任何日本人。倒不是日本人假正经。照他说，他们绝非如此……只是他们不会像英国男人那样、在男人堆里和男女混杂的场合用一套暗语说话；还自以为聪明；谁肯降格到机械的把戏，谁都会说暗语。
鲍西娅同尼莉莎之间，比她们哪一位同那两个强颜说笑、自得打趣的情人之间，真实得多。男人可曾*曾经*用天然的嗓音说话——不虚张声势，不卖弄聪明，也不干脆做个情欲的奴隶？女人在情欲或疲倦的影响下总是看不起男人。那两个人老了会多可怕——仍旧用拿腔的嗓子说话遮他们的丑，摆架子、发议论。……“男人的爱情是男人生命中的一段插曲……”那男人就更糟了；他的生命一定出了很大的毛病。

===Original===

But it would go on until men saw and admitted this.﻿ ﻿… Portia was right when she preached her sermon﻿—it made everyone feel sorry for all harshness﻿—then one ought not to be harsh to the blindness of men﻿ ﻿… somebody had said men would lose all their charm if they lost their vanity and childish cocksureness about their superiority﻿—to force and browbeat them into seeing themselves would not help﻿—but that is what I want to do. I am like a man in that, overbearing, bullying, blustering. I am something between a man and a woman; looking both ways. But to pretend one did not see through a man’s voice would be treachery. Nearly all men will hate me﻿—because I can’t play up for long. Harshness must go; perhaps that was what Christ meant. But Portia only wanted to save Bassanio’s life; and did it by a trick. It was not a Daniel come to judgment; it showed the folly of law; pettifogging; the abuse of the letter of the law. She was harsh to Shylock. Which is most cruel, to take life or to torture the living? The Christians were so self-satisfied; going off to their lovemaking; that spoiled the play, their future was much more dark and miserable than the struggle between the sensual Englishman and the wily Jew. The play ought to have ended there, with the woman in the cap and gown pleading, showing something that could not be denied﻿—ye are all together in one condemnation. In that moment Portia was great, her red robe shone and lit the world. She ought to have left them all and gone through all the law courts of the world; showing up the law. Wit. Woman’s wit. Men at least bowed down to that; though they did not know what it was. “Wit” used to mean knowledge﻿—“in-wit,” conscience. The knowledge of woman is larger, bigger, deeper, less wordy and clever than that of men. Certainly. But why do not men acknowledge this? They talk about mother-love and mother-wit and instinct, as if they were mysterious tricks. They have no real knowledge, but of things; a sort of superiority they get by being free to be out in the world amongst things; they do not understand people. If a woman is good it is all right; if she is bad it is all wrong. Cherchez la femme. Then everything in life depends upon women? “A civilisation can never rise above the level of its women.” Perhaps if women became lawyers they would change things. Women do not respect law.

===Chinese===

可这要一直继续下去，直到男人们看见它、承认它。……鲍西娅布她的道是对的——它叫人人痛惜一切苛刻——那么对男人的盲目也不该苛刻……有人说过，男人若失去他们的虚荣和对自身优越的孩子气的自信，便失去全部魅力——强逼他们、喍令他们看清自己，无济于事——可那正是我要做的。在那一点上我像男人，专横，欺压，咋呼。我是介于男人和女人之间的什么；两头看。可是假装看不穿一个男人的嗓音，会是背叛。几乎所有的男人都会恨我——因为我装不了多久。苛刻必须除去；也许基督指的就是这个。可鲍西娅只想救巴萨尼奥的命；而且靠一个把戏救的。那不是但以理来断案；它显示的是法律的荒唐；讼棍的路数；滥用法条的字面。她对夏洛克是苛刻的。夺走生命和折磨活人，哪个更狠？基督徒们那样心安理得；转身去调情；这毁掉了那出戏，他们的前途远比那个肉感的英国人和那个狡黠的犹太人之间的争斗更黑暗、更悲惨。戏本该就在那儿收场，那戴帽穿袍的女人哀恳着，显出一样无法否认的东西——你们全都在同一定罪之中。那一刻鲍西娅是伟大的，她的红袍照耀，照亮了世界。她本该离开他们所有人，走遍世上所有的法庭；把法律亮出来。机智。女人的机智。男人至少向它低头；尽管他们不知道那是什么。“Wit（机智）”从前指知识——“in-wit”，良心。女人的知识比男人的更大、更宽、更深，更少掉书袋和小聪明。一定的。可男人为什么不承认这个？他们侈谈母爱、母性的机智和本能，仿佛那是些神秘的把戏。他们没有真正的知识，除了对物；一种靠自由在外面的世界里混迹于事物之间挣来的优越；他们不懂人。女人若是好的，一切就好；她若是坏的，一切就糟。Cherchez la femme（去找那个女人）。那么生活里一切都系于女人？“一种文明永远超不过其女性的水准。”也许女人做了律师就会改变事情。女人不敬重法律。

===Original===

No wonder, since it is folly, an endless play on words. Portia? She had been quite complacent about being unkind to the Jew. She had been invented by a man. There was no reality in any of Shakespeare’s women. They please men because they show women as men see them. All the other things are invisible; nothing but their thoughts and feelings about men and bothers. Shakespeare did not know the meaning of the words and actions of Nerissa and Portia when they were alone together, the beauty they knew and felt and saw, holy beauty everywhere. Shakespeare’s plays are “universal” because they are about the things that everybody knows and hands about, and they do not trouble anybody. They make everyone feel wise. It isn’t what he says it’s the way he says all these things that don’t matter and leave everything out. It’s all a sublime fuss.
Italians! Of course. Well﻿—Europeans. It is the difference between the Europeans and the Japanese that Hearn had meant.
---
Then there *is* tragedy! Things are not simple right and wrong. There are a million sides to every question; as many sides as there are people to see and feel them and in all big national struggles two clear sides, both right and both wrong. The man who wrote *The Struggle Against Absolute Monarchy* was a Roundhead; and he made me a Roundhead; Green’s History is Roundhead. I never saw Charles’ point of view or thought about it; but only of the unjust levies and the dissolution of Parliament and the dissoluteness of the Court. If I had seen Irving then it would have made a difference. He could never have been Cromwell. He is Charles. Things happen. People tell him things and he cannot understand. He believes in divine right﻿ ﻿… sweet and gentle, with perfect manners for all﻿ ﻿… perfect in private life﻿ ﻿… the first gentleman in the land, the only person free to have perfect manners; the representative of God on earth. “Decaying feudalism.” But they ought not to have killed him. He cannot *understand*. He is the scapegoat. Freedom looks so fine in your mind. Parliaments and Trial by Jury and the abolition of the Star Chamber and the triumph of Cromwell’s visionaries. But it means this gentle velvet-coated figure with its delicate ruffled hands, its sweetness and courtesy, going with bandaged eyes﻿—to death. Was there no way out? Must one either be a Royalist or a Roundhead. Must monarchies decay?

===Chinese===

难怪，既然它是愚蠢，一场无穷无尽的文字游戏。鲍西娅？她对自己对那犹太人的不厚道相当心安理得。她是男人造出来的。莎士比亚所有的女人身上都没有真实。她们讨男人喜欢，因为她们照男人看女人的样子展示女人。其余的一切都看不见了；只剩下她们对男人和烦恼的心思与情感。尼莉莎和鲍西娅单独相处时的话与行是什么意思，她们知道、感到、看见的美，处处皆在的神圣之美，莎士比亚并不懂。莎士比亚的戏“放之四海”，因为它们写的是人人知道、人人传递的东西，不打扰任何人。它们叫人人觉得自己有智慧。要紧的不是他说什么，而是他说这一切无关紧要、漏掉一切的方式。全是一场崇高的忙乱。
意大利人！当然。唔——是欧洲人。小泉八云指的正是欧洲人和日本人之间的分别。
---
这么说*果真*有悲剧！事情不是简单的对与错。每个问题有一百万面；有多少人去看、去感，就有多少面；而在一切重大的国民争斗里，两面清楚，都对，也都不对。写《反对绝对君主制的斗争》的那个人是个圆颅党；他把我造成了圆颅党；格林的《历史》是圆颅党的。我从没看见查理的观点、没想过它；只想着横征暴敛、解散国会、宫廷的荒淫。我那时若看了欧文，就会不同。他永远做不成克伦威尔。他就是查理。事情发生了。人们告诉他事情，他不明白。他信君权神授……甜美而温文，对人人礼貌周全……私生活无懈可击……国内的第一君子，惟一有自由做到礼貌周全的人；上帝在尘世的代表。“衰朽的封建主义。”可他们不该杀他。他*不能理解*。他是替罪羊。自由在人的心里看上去多美。国会、陪审制、星室法院的废除、克伦威尔那些空想家的胜利。可它意味着这个穿丝绒外套的身影，那双精致的镶花边的手，那份甜美与谦和，蒙着双眼走向——死亡。没有出路吗？人非做保王党即做圆颅党吗？君主制必得衰朽吗？

===Original===

Then why did the Restoration come? What do English people want? “A limited Monarchy”; a King controlled by Parliament. As well not have a King at all. Who would not rather live with Charles than with Cromwell? Charles would have entertained a beggar royally. Cromwell was too busy with “affairs of State” to entertain beggars. Charles dying for his faith was more beautiful than Cromwell fighting for his reason. Yet the people must be free; there must be justice. Kings ought to be taught differently. He did not understand. No one believing in divine right can understand. Was the idea of divine right a mistake? Can no one be trusted? Cromwell’s son was a weak fool. How can a country be ruled? People will never agree. What ought one to be if one can neither be quite a Roundhead nor quite a Cavalier? They worshipped two gods. Are there two Gods?﻿ ﻿… Irving﻿ ﻿… walking gently about inside Charles, feeling as he felt, the beauty of the sunlit garden, the delicate clothes, the refinement of fine living, the charm of perfect association, the rich beauty of each day as it passed.﻿ ﻿… Charles died with all that in his eyes, *knowing* it *good*. Cromwell was a farmer. Christ was a carpenter. Christ did not bother about kings. “Render unto Caesar.”

===Chinese===

那王政复辟为什么还会来？英国人要什么？“有限的君主制”；受国会节制的国王。倒不如根本不要国王。谁不愿意同查理而不是克伦威尔一起生活？查理会拿君王的排场款待一个乞丐。克伦威尔忙着“国务”，无暇款待乞丐。查理为他的信仰而死，比克伦威尔为他的理性而战更美。然而人民必须自由；必须有正义。国王们该受另一种教养。他不明白。信君权神授的人没有谁能明白。君权神授这个观念是一个错误吗？就没有一个人信得过？克伦威尔的儿子是个软弱的蠢人。一个国家怎么能统治？人们永远不会一致。既做不成十足的圆颅党、又做不成十足的骑士党，人该做什么？他们敬拜两个神。有两个上帝吗？……欧文……在查理体内轻轻地走动，感他所感，日光明媚的花园之美，精致的衣裳，精致生活的雅致，完美情谊的迷人之境，流走的每一天丰饶的美。……查理死去时这一切都在他眼里，*知道*它是*好的*。克伦威尔是个农夫。基督是个木匠。基督不操心国王。“恺撒的物当归给恺撒。”

## XVI / 十六
===Original===

They had walked swiftly and silently along through the bright evening daylight of the Finchley Road. Miriam held her knowledge suspended, looking forward to the enclosure at the end of the few minutes’ walk. But the conservatoire was not enclosed. The clear bright light flooding the rows and rows of seated summer clad Hampstead people and lighting up every corner of the level square hall was like the outside evening daylight. The air seemed as pure as the outside air. She followed Mr. Hancock to their seats at the gangway end of the fourth row passing between the sounds echoing thinly from the platform and the wave of attention sweeping towards the platform from the massed rows of intelligent faces. As they sat down the chairman’s voice ceased and the lights were lowered; but so slightly that the hall was still perfectly exposed and clear. The people still looked as though they were out of doors or in their large houses. This was modern improvement﻿—hard clear light. Their minds and their thoughts and their lives and their clothes were always in it. She stared at the screen. A large slide was showing, lit from behind. It made a sort of stage scenery for the rest of the scene, all in one light. She fixed her attention. An enormous vessel with its side stove in, yes, “stove in”; in a dock. They got *information* at any rate and then perhaps got free and thought their own thoughts. No. They would follow and think and talk intelligently about the information. Rattling their cultured voices. Mad with pretences.﻿ ﻿… In *dry* dock, going to be repaired. Gazing sternly at the short man with the long pointer talking in an anxious high thin voice, his head with its upstanding crest of hair half-turned towards the audience, she suppressed a giggle. Folding her hands she gazed, shaking in every limb, not daring to follow what he said for fear of laughing aloud. Shreds of his first long sentence caught in her thoughts and gave her his meaning, shaking her into giggles. Her features quivered under her skin as she held them in forcing her eyes towards the distances of sky beyond the ship. Her customary expletives shot through her mind in rapid succession, with each one the scarves and silk and velvet of the audience grew brighter about the edge of her circle of vision.
---
She was an upstart and an alien and here she was.

===Chinese===

他们一路疾行，默不作声，穿过芬奇利路明亮的傍晚天光。米丽亚姆悬着她知道的那件事，盼着几步路尽头的那座围场。可这座音乐堂没有围场。澄澈明亮的光漫过一排排盛夏装束、坐着不动的汉普斯特德人，照亮这方平平整整的方形大厅的每个角落，同外面的傍晚天光一样。空气也似乎同外面一样纯净。她跟着汉考克先生走向第四排过道尽头的座位，从平台方向淡淡回响的种种声音，同那一排排聪慧的脸汇成的、扫向平台的注意的浪，中间穿行而过。他们一落座，主席的声音停了，灯光调低；可只低了一点点，大厅依然纤毫毕现。人们看上去仍像身在户外，或身在各自的大宅子里。这就是现代的改良——又硬又亮的光。他们的头脑、思想、生活、衣裳，永远泡在这种光里。她盯着幕布。一幅大幻灯片正放映着，从后面照亮。它为整个场面充当一种舞台布景，通体一种光。她定住注意。一艘巨轮，船身撞瘪了一大块，是的，“撞瘪了”；停在船坞里。他们至少得到了信息，随后或许得到自由，想自己的念头。不。他们会跟着想、跟着谈，把信息谈得头头是道。把他们那有教养的嗓门拨得铮铮响。被种种体面逼疯。…在干船坞里，等着修。她板着脸瞪着那个矮个子、长指点棒的男人——他用一种焦虑的、又高又细的嗓音讲着，头发直立的冠半转向听众——忍住一声咯咯。她交叠双手，凝望，四肢发着抖，不敢细听他讲，怕笑出声。他那第一个长句的碎片挂进她的思绪，把意思递给她，抖得她直咯咯。她把五官按在皮下绷住，逼着眼睛望向轮船以外远处的天。她惯用的那几句咒骂在心里接连炸响，每响一句，观众席上的丝巾、绸缎和丝绒便在她视野的边缘亮一分。
---
她是个暴发户，一个外来者，而她就在这里。

===Original===

It was more extraordinary in this Hampstead clarity than at a theatre or concert in town. It was a part of his world﻿ ﻿… and theirs; one might get the manner and still keep alive.﻿ ﻿… Was he out of humour because he had realised what he had done or because she had been late for dinner? Was he thinking what his behaviour amounted to in the eyes of his aunt and cousins; even supposing they did not know that the invitation to dinner and the lecture had been given only this afternoon? He must have known it was necessary to go home and tidy up. When he said the conservatoire was so near that there would be plenty of time was not that as good as saying she might be a little late? Why had he not said they were staying with him? Next week was full of appointments for their teeth. So he knew they were coming﻿ ﻿… and then to go marching in to the midst of them three quarters of an hour late and to be so dumbfounded as to be unable to apologise﻿ ﻿… my dear I shall *never* forget the faces of those women. I could not imagine at first what was wrong. He was looking so strange. The women barely noticed me﻿—barely noticed me. “I’m afraid dinner will be spoiled,” he said, in his way. “They had all been sitting round the fire three mortal quarters of an hour waiting for *me*!” How they would talk. Their thoughts and feelings about employees could be seen at a glance. It was bad enough for them to have a secretary appearing at dinner the first evening of their great visit. And now they were sitting alone round the fire and she was at the lecture alone, unchaperoned, with him, “she had the effrontery to come to dinner three quarters of an hour late﻿ ﻿…” feathery hair and periwinkle eyes and white noses; gentle die-away voices. Perhaps the thought of his favourite cousins coming next week buoyed him up. No wonder he wanted to get away to the lecture. He had come, reasonably; not seeing why he should not; just as he would have gone if they had not been there. Now he saw it as they saw it. There he sat. She gazed at the shifting scenes﻿ ﻿… ports and strange islands in distant seas, sunlit coloured mountains tops peaking up from forests. The lecturing voice was far away, irrelevant and unintelligible. Peace flooded her.

===Chinese===

在这份汉普斯特德的澄明里，这比城里的戏院或音乐会还要离奇。这是他的世界的一部分…也是他们的；人尽可以学得那套做派，还照样活。…他不痛快，是因为明白了自己干了什么，还是因为她晚饭迟到了？他是不是在想，他那做派落在他姨妈和表亲们眼里算个什么；哪怕她们不知道，晚饭和演讲的邀请今天下午才发出？他一定知道，总得先回家、梳洗一番。他说音乐堂这么近、时间尽够的时候，那不就等于说她可以晚到一点点吗？他为什么不说她们正住在他家？下星期排满了她们看牙的预约。这么说他知道她们要来…却又迟到三刻钟，大步走进她们中间，愕得连道歉都道不出…亲爱的，我永远忘不了那几个女人的脸。起初我想不出哪里不对。他那样子怪极了。那几个女人几乎没理我——几乎没理我。“恐怕晚饭要糟了，”他用他那副腔调说。“她们围炉坐了足足三刻钟，就等我一个人！”她们会有得讲了。她们对雇员的想法和感觉，一眼就看得出。她们这次大驾光临的头一晚，就冒出一个秘书来上桌，已经够呛了。此刻她们正独自围炉坐着，而她独自在这里听演讲，无人监护，同他一起，“她脸皮可真厚，晚饭迟到三刻钟还敢来…”羽毛似的头发，长春花色的眼睛，白鼻子；娇柔的、弱不胜衣的嗓音。也许一想到他宠爱的表亲们下星期要来，他就有了撑劲。怪不得他想到讲座这儿来躲一躲。他来了，事出有因；看不出为什么不能来；就像她们若不在，他也会照来。如今他看这件事，用的是她们的眼睛。他就坐在那儿。她望着变换的布景…远海的港口和陌生的岛屿，阳光里彩色的山顶从森林中探出。讲课的声音远远的，不相干，听不懂。安宁淹没了她。

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

The patient sat up with a groan of relief. His dark strong positive liverish profile turned away towards the spittoon. There was a clean broad gap of neck between the strong inturned ending of his hair and the narrow strip of firm heavily glazed blue white collar fitting perfectly into the collar of the well-cut grey coat clothing the firm bulk of his body. “To my mind there’s no reason why they shouldn’t do thoroughly well,” he said into the spittoon. “All the hospitals would employ ’em in the end. They’re more natty and conscientious than men and there’s nothing in the work they can’t manage.”
“No, I think that’s so.”
Miriam cleared her throat emphatically. They had no right to talk in that calm disposing way in the presence of a woman. Mr. Hancock felt that too. That kind of man was always nice to women. Strong and cheerful and helping them; but with his mind full of quotations and generalisations. He would bring them out anywhere. It would never occur to him that the statement of them could be offensive. His newspaper office would be full of little girls. “It’s those little ph’girls.” But the Amalgam Company probably had quite uneducated girls. Nobody ought to be asked to spend their lives calculating decimal quantities. The men who lived on these things had their drudgery done for them. They did it themselves first. Yes, but then it meant their future. A woman clerk never becomes a partner. There was no hope for women in business. That man’s wife would be wealthy and screened and looked after all her days; he working. He would live as long as she﻿—a little old slender nut-brown man.
“What was the employment Mr. Dolland was speaking of?”
“Dispensing. I think he’s quite right. And it’s not at all badly paid.”
“It ought not to be. Think of the responsibility and anxiety.”
“It’s a jolly stiff exam too.”
“I like the calm way he talks, as if it were his business to decide what is suitable.”
Mr. Hancock laughed. “He’s a very influential man, you know,” he said going to the tube. “Yes?﻿—Oh, show them up.”
Miriam detected the note that meant a trial ahead and went about her clearing with quiet swift busy sympathy. But Mr. Dolland had been a good introduction to the trying hour. Her thoughts followed his unconsciousness down to his cab.

===Chinese===

病人一声宽解的呻吟，坐了起来。他那深色的、强壮的、武断的、肝火旺的侧脸转向痰盂。头发强劲内卷的收梢同那条浆得又硬又亮的蓝白领条之间，露出一段干净宽阔的后颈，领条严丝合缝地嵌在剪裁考究的灰呢上装的领子里，上装裹着他结实的身躯。“依我看，她们没有理由干不出个名堂，”他对着痰盂说。“到头来所有医院都会用她们。她们比男人利落、比男人尽责，这行里没有什么她们拿不下来的。”

“是，我也这么看。”

米丽亚姆重重地清了清嗓子。当着一位女人的面，他们无权用那种平静的、一锤定音的口气谈话。汉考克先生也觉出来了。那种男人对女人永远和气。强壮、快活、帮她们的忙；可满脑子语录和泛论。走到哪儿带到哪儿。他绝不会想到那些话本身可能得罪人。他的报馆里一定满是年轻姑娘。“就是那些年轻姑娘。”可联合公司的姑娘们多半全然没受过教育。谁也不该被人请求拿一辈子去计算小数。靠这些数字过活的男人，苦工都是别人代做。他们自己是先做过的。是，可那还关系到她们的前程。女职员永远成不了合伙人。女人在商界没有指望。那人的太太会一生富有、被遮护、被照料；他在外做工。他会同她一样长寿——一个瘦小的、坚栗色的老头。

“多兰先生方才说的那个差事是什么？”

“配药。我想他说得很对。工钱也一点不坏。”

“本不该坏。想想那份责任和操心。”

“考试也硬得很。”

“我喜欢他讲话那副安稳劲儿，好像该不该、合不合适，全由他来裁定似的。”

汉考克先生笑了。“他是个很有势力的人，你知道，”他边说边走向传声管。“是？——哦，请她们上来。”

米丽亚姆听出了那个音——意味着一场考验在前头——便带着安静的、利落的、忙而不乱的同感去做她的清理。可多兰先生已为这难熬的钟点作了不错的开场。她的思绪随着他的浑然不觉一路送他下到他的马车。

===Original===

She saw the spatted boot on the footplate, the neat strong swing of the body, the dip of the hansom, the darkling face sitting inside under the shiny hat﻿ ﻿… the room had become dreadful; empty and silent; pressed full with a dreadful atmosphere; those women from Rochester﻿—but they always sat still. These people were making little faint fussings of movement, like the creakings of clothes in church and the same silent hostile feeling; people being obliged to be with people. There were two or three besides the figure in the chair. Mr. Hancock had got to work with silent assiduity. His face when he turned to the cabinet was disordered, separate from the room and from his work; a most curious expression. He turned again, busily. It was something in the mouth, resentful, and a bad-tempered look in the eyes; a look of discomposed youth. Of course. The aunt and cousins. Had she cut them, standing with her back to the room, or they her? She moved sideways with her bundle of cleaned instruments to the cabinet putting them all on the flap and beginning to open drawers, standing at his elbow as he stood turned away from the chair mixing a paste.
“You might leave those there for the present,” he murmured. She turned and went down the room between the unoccupied seated figures, keeping herself alert to respond to a greeting. They sat vacant and still. Ladies in church. Acrimonious. Querulously dressed in pretty materials and colours that would only keep fresh in the country. She went to the door lingeringly. It was so familiar. There had been all that at Babington. It was that that was in these figures straggling home from school, in pretty successful clothes, walking along the middle of the sunlit road﻿ ﻿… *May‑bell* deah﻿ ﻿… not balancing along the row of drain pipes nor pulling streaks of Berkshire goody through their lips. This was their next stage. When she reached the stairs she felt herself wrapped in their scorn. It was true; there was something impregnable about them. They sat inside a little fortress, letting in only certain people. But they did not know she could see everything inside the fortress, hear all their thoughts much more clearly than the things they said. To them she was a closed book. They did not want to open it. But if they had wanted to they could not have read.
---
The *insolence* of it.

===Chinese===

她看见那双套着鞋罩的靴子踏上踏板，那利落有力的身一摆，双轮马车一沉，帽檐发亮的帽底下那张沉暗的脸坐着……这房间变得可怕了；空而静；塞满一种可怕的气氛；罗切斯特来的那几个女人——可她们从来安坐不动。这几位却在弄出一点一点细细的窸窣，像教堂里衣裳的吱呀，还有同样无声的敌意；人们被迫与人们共处。椅上那个身影之外，还有两三个。汉考克先生已带着无声的勤勉上了手。他转向器械柜时那张脸是乱了的，同这房间、同他的活计分了家；一副再古怪不过的表情。他又转回来，忙碌着。是嘴上的什么东西，一股怨气，眼睛里一股坏脾气的神色；一副狼狈的少年相。当然。姨妈和表亲们。是她冷落了她们——背对着房间站着——还是她们冷落了她？她抱着那捆洗净的器械侧身挪向器械柜，把它们全数搁在翻板上，开始拉开一个个抽屉，站在他肘边——他正背对牙科椅调着糊剂。

“这些你暂且摆在这儿吧，”他低声说。她转身顺着那排空坐着的身影往房间下首走，保持着随时应酬一声招呼的警觉。她们呆坐着，一动不动。教堂里的太太们。尖刻。娇嗔地穿着一身娇俏料子和颜色，只有在乡下才保持得住新鲜。她迟迟地走向门口。这一切多么熟悉。巴宾顿那儿也全有过。正是那东西，在这几个放学回家的身影里——穿着相当像样的衣裳，走在洒满阳光的路中央……梅-贝尔，亲-爱的……既不沿着那排水管管顶走平衡，也不把伯克郡的甘草根抿在嘴唇间拉成一条条。这是她们的下一站。走到楼梯口时，她觉得自己裹进了她们的轻蔑。是真的；她们身上有一种攻不破的东西。她们坐在一座小堡垒里面，只放某些人进去。可她们不知道，堡垒里面的一切她都看得见，她们的心思她听得比她们嘴上的话还清楚。在她们眼里她是一本合着的书。她们不想打开。可她们就算想，也读不懂。
---
那份放肆。

===Original===

Her social position had been identical with theirs and his. Her early circumstances a good deal more ambitious and generous.﻿ ﻿… “A moment of my consciousness is wider than any of theirs will be in the whole of their lives.”﻿ ﻿… If she could have stayed in all that, she would have been as far as possible just the same, sometimes﻿ ﻿… for certain purposes. A little close group, loyal and quarrelsome for ends that any woman could see through. Fawning and flattering and affectionate to each other and getting half-maddened by the one necessity. The girls would repeat the history of their mother, and get her sour faced pretty delicate refinement. They were so exquisite, now, to look at﻿—the flower-like edges of their faces, unchanging from morning to night; warmth and care and cleanliness and rich clean food; no fatigue or worry or embarrassment, once they had learned how to sit and move and eat. To many men they would appear angels. They would not meet many in the Berkshire valley. But their mother would manoeuvre engagements for them and their men would see them as angels fresh from their mother’s hands; miracles of beauty and purity.﻿ ﻿…
Refined shrews, turning in circles, like moths on pins; brainless, mindless, heartless, the prey of the professions; priests, doctors and lawyers. These two groups kept each other going. There was something hidden in the fact that these women’s men always entered professions.
---
Large portions of the mornings and afternoons of that week were free from visits to the upstairs surgery. From Tuesday morning she kept it well filled with supplies; guessing that she was to be saved further contact with the aunt and cousins; and drew from the stimulus of their comings and goings, the sound of their voices in the hall and on the stairs a fund of energy that filled her unexpected stretches of leisure with unceasing methodical labour. Uninterrupted work on the ledgers awakened her interest in them, the sense that the books were nearly all up to date, the possibility of catching up altogether before the end of the week brought a relief and a sense of mastery that made the June sunshine gay morning after morning as she tramped through it along the Euston Road. Every hour was full of a strange excitement. Wide vistas shone ahead. On the first of September shone a blinding radiance.

===Chinese===

她的社会地位曾同她们、同他的一模一样。她早年的境况，还要更阔绰、更气派得多。…“我一刻的意识，比她们一辈子的都宽。”…倘若她能留在那一切里面，她也会——有时，为某些目的——尽可能同她们一个样。一个紧巴巴的小圈子，忠诚而好吵，为一些任何一个女人都一眼看穿的目的。彼此奉承、讨好、亲热，又被那唯一的必要逼得半疯。姑娘们将重演她们母亲的历史，得来她那副板起的、娇俏的、精致的、挑剔的脸。她们此刻多么耐看——花儿一样的脸缘，从早到晚一成不变；温暖、照料、清洁、干净的富足食物；一旦学会了怎样坐、怎样走、怎样吃，便再没有疲惫、烦愁和窘迫。在许多男人眼里，她们会是天使。在伯克郡的河谷里她们遇不上几个男人。可她们的母亲会替她们运筹婚约，她们的男人会把她们看成刚从母亲手里交出来的天使；美丽与纯洁的奇迹。…

精致的悍妇，团团地转，像钉在针上的蛾子；无脑、无心、无情，是各行专业的猎物；教士、医生和律师。这两伙人互相供养。这些女人的男人总是进专业行当——这事实里藏着一点什么。
---
那一周里，上午和下午的大段时光都不必上楼进诊室。从星期二早晨起，她把它用补给药填得满满的；猜着姨妈和表亲们的事不用她再沾手；又从她们进进出出、她们在门厅和楼梯上的嗓音里汲取刺激，换来一股精力，把她意外多出来的闲暇填满不停的、有条理的劳作。不间断地整理账簿，唤醒了她对账簿的兴趣，眼见账目即将全部追平、有望赶在周末之前彻底赶齐，带来一种宽解、一种驾御之感，使六月的阳光一连好几个早晨都欢畅着，随她踏过它走在尤斯顿路上。每个钟头都盛着一种奇异的兴奋。宽宽的远景在前头发亮。九月一日上，闪着一片晃眼的辉光。

===Original===

She would get up that morning in her dusty garret in the heat and dust of London with nothing to do for a month; and ride away, somewhere, ride away through the streets, free, out to the suburbs, like a Sunday morning ride, and then into the country. She had weathered the winter and the strange beginnings and would go away to come back; the rest of the summer till then would go dancing, like a dream. There was all that coming; making her heart leap when she thought of it, unknown Wiltshire﻿—with Leader landscapes for a week and then something else. And meanwhile Wimpole Street. She went about her work borne along unwearied upon a tide that flowed out in glistening sunlit waves over the sunlit shore of the world. The doors and windows of her cool shaded room opened upon a life that spread out before her fanwise towards endless brilliant distances. Moments of fatigue, little obstinate knots and tangles of urgent practical affairs did their utmost to convince her that life was a perpetual conflict, nothing certain and secure but the thwarting and discrediting of the dream-vision; every contact seemed to end in an assurance of her unarmed resourceless state. Pausing now and again to balance her account, to try to find a sanction for her joy, she watched and felt the little stabs of the actual facts as they would be summarised by some disinterested observer, and again and again saw them foiled. Things danced, comically powerless against some unheard piping; motes, funny and beloved, in the sunbeam of her life.﻿ ﻿… Next week and the coming of the favourite cousins made a bright barrier across the future and a little fence round her labours. Everything must be ordered and straight before then. She must be free and reproachless for the wonders and terrors of their visit.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps there might be only the one meeting; the evening already arranged might be all the week’s visit would bring. The week would pass unseen by her and everything would be as before. As before; was not that enough, and more than enough?
---
Her rare visits to the surgery were festivals.

===Chinese===

那天早晨她将在伦敦的炎热和尘土里，从积尘的阁楼里起来，一整个月无事可做；然后骑上自行车走，随便哪里，穿过一条条街骑走，自由地，骑到郊外去，像星期天早晨的一趟骑行，然后进乡下。她熬过了冬天和种种古怪的开头，要出走一趟再回来；到那之前的整个夏天会像梦一样舞过去。那一切都在前头；一想起来心就跳，陌生的威尔特郡——一个星期的利德式风景，然后是别的什么。而眼下是温波尔街。她四下做工，被一股潮水托着不知疲倦，那潮水泛着日照的粼粼波浪，涌向世界的日照的岸。她那间清凉有荫的房间的门窗，敞向一扇面展开在她面前、铺向无尽辉煌的远方的生活。疲惫的时刻，急务打上的一个个顽固的死结和乱麻，竭力叫她相信生活是一场没完的争斗，除了挫败梦幻、败坏梦幻之景，再没有什么是确凿可靠的；每一次接触似乎都以一声担保告终——担保她手无寸铁、一无依傍。她不时停下来对一对账，想给她的欢喜找一个批准，看着、也感着实际事实的那些小针刺——按某位无利害的旁观者的汇总——而它们一次又一次被挫败。万物都舞着，对一支听不见的笛音可笑地无能为力；一粒粒微尘，又好笑又可爱，在她生命的日光里。…下星期和宠爱的表亲们的驾临，在前面横出一道明亮的屏障，也在她的劳作四围圈了一道小栅。到那时一切都得安置停当、理得笔直。为了她们来访的种种奇观与惊怖，她必须自由、无懈可击。…也许只有那一面；已排定的那个晚上，也许就是整个一周的来访的全部。这一周会从她眼前溜过去，一切照旧。照旧；这难道还不够，还远远不够吗？
---
她难得的几次上诊室，都是节日。

===Original===

Free from the usual daily fatigue of constant standing for reiterated clearances and cleansings of small sets of instruments, she swept full of cheerful strength, her mind free for method, her hands steady and deft, upon the accumulations left by long sittings, rapping out her commentary upon his prolonged endurance by emphatic bumpings of basins and utensils; making it unnecessary for him to voice the controlled exasperation that spoke for her from every movement and tone. Once or twice she felt it wavering towards speech and whisked about and bumped things down with extra violence. Once or twice he smiled into her angry face and she feared he was going to speak of them.

===Chinese===

不必再忍受日常的、整日站着为一套套小器械反复清理擦洗的劳顿，她满载着一身轻快的力气扫荡上去，头脑闲出来搞章法，双手稳而巧，对付那些长诊留下的堆积，用盆和器械一声声结实的磕碰，敲出她对那场漫长忍耐的评论；也就用不着他把那股压着的恼怒说出口——她的每一个动作、每一种声调都替他说了。有一两回她觉出那恼怒摇摇欲坠要开口，便一个旋身，把东西砸得格外响。有一两回他朝她那愠怒的脸笑了笑，她怕他要谈起她们了。

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

It was a sort of formality. They all three seemed to be waiting for something to begin. They were not at ease. Perhaps they had come to the end of everything they had to say to each other and had only the memory of their common youth to bind them to each other. Members of the same family never seemed to be quite at ease sitting together doing nothing. These three met so seldom that they were obliged when they met to appear to be giving their whole attention to each other, sitting confronted and trying to keep talk going all the time.
