That made everyone speak and smile and look self-consciously. Perhaps they reminded each other by their mutual presence that the dreams of their youth had not been fulfilled. And the cousins were formal. Like the other cousins they belonged to the prosperous provincial middle class that always tries to get its sons into professions. Without the volume of Sophocles one would have known he was part of a school and she would have been nothing but the wife or daughter or sister of an English professional man. It was always the same world; once the only world that was worthy of one’s envious admiration and respect; changed now﻿ ﻿… “hardworked little text book people and here and there an enlightened thwarted man.”﻿ ﻿… Was Mr. Canfield thwarted? There was a curious look of lonely enlightenment about his head. At the University, and now and again with a head master or a fellow assistant-master he had had moments of exchange and been happy for a moment and seen the world alight. But his happiest times had been in loneliness, with thoughts coming to him out of books. They had been his solace and his refuge since he was fifteen; and in spite of the hair greying his temples he was still fifteen; within him were all the dreams and all the dreadful crudities of boyhood﻿ ﻿… he had never grown to man’s estate.﻿ ﻿… He had understood at once. “It always seems unnecessary to explain things to people; you feel while you are explaining that they will meet the same thing themselves, perhaps in some different form; but certainly, because things are all the same.” “Oh yes; that’s certainly so.” He had looked pleased and lightened. Darkness and cold had come in an instant with Mrs. Canfield’s unexpected reverent voice. “I don’t quite understand what that means; tell me.”

===Chinese===

这像是一种客套。他们三个仿佛都在等什么开始。他们并不自在。也许他们彼此要说的话已尽数说完，只剩共同青春的记忆把彼此维系。一家人坐在一起无所事事，似乎从来难得自在。这三个义难得碰一回头，碰上了便不得不装出把全副心思放在彼此身上，对坐枯守，勉力让谈话一直不断。这叫人人开口都带笑，都带着自觉。也许彼此的在场就互相提醒着：青春的梦没有兑现。表亲们也是拘谨的。同别的表亲一样，他们属于那种总想把儿子送进专门职业的股实的外省中产阶层。若没有那部索福克勒斯，人也会知道他是学校里的一部分，而她则不过是一个英国职业人士的太太、女儿或姊妹。总是同一个世界；从前是惟一配得上看重与艳羡和尊敬的世界；如今变了……“劳碌的小小教科书人物，间或有一个开明而受挫的男人。”……坎菲尔德先生受挫了吗？他的头上有一种孤独的启蒙的神情。在大学里，如今偶尔同一位校长或同事，他有过交谈的时刻，快活片刻，看见世界亮起来。可他最快乐的时光是在孤独里，思想从书里向他走来。它们是他十五岁以来的慰藉与避难；尽管鬓发已经灰了，他还是十五岁；他心里装着少年时代全部的梦和全部骇人的生涩……他从不曾长到成人的地步。……他一下子就懂了。“向人解释事情似乎总是多余的；你一面解释一面就觉得，他们自己也会碰到同样的事，也许换一种形式；但一定会，因为事情都是一样的。”“哦是的；一定是这样。”他显得欢喜而轻快。黑暗和寒冷随着坎菲尔德太太出人意料的虔诚嗓音霎时降临。“我不大明白这话的意思；给我讲讲。”

===Original===

She had put down her fancy work and lifted her flower-like face, not suspiciously as the other cousins would have done, but with their type of gentle formal refinement and something of their look. She could be sour and acid if she chose. She could curl her lips and snub people. What was the secret of the everlasting same awfulness of even the nicest of refined sheltered middle-class Englishwoman? He had stumbled and wandered through a vague statement. He knew that all the long loneliness of his mind lay revealed before one﻿—and yet she had been the dream and wonder and magic of his youth and still was his dear companion. The “lady” was the wife for the professional Englishman﻿—simple sheltered domesticated, trained in principles she did not think about and living by them; revering professional and professionally successful men; never seeing the fifth-form schoolboys they all were. No woman who saw them as they were with their mental pride and vanity and fixity, would stay with them; no woman who saw their veiled appetites.﻿ ﻿… But where could all these wives go?
---
Throughout the evening she was kept quiet and dull and felt presently very weary. Her helpless stocktaking made it difficult to face the strangers, lest painful illumination and pity and annoyance should stream from her too visibly.﻿ ﻿… Perhaps they too took stock and pitied; but they were interested, a little eager in response and though too well bred for questions, obviously full of unanswered surmises, which perhaps presently they would communicate to each other. There were people who would say she was too egoistic to be interested in them, a selfish, unsocial, unpleasant person and they were kind charming people, interested in everybody. That might be true.﻿ ﻿… But it was also true that they were eager and interested because their lives were empty of everything but principles and a certain fixed way of looking at things; and one could be fond of their niceness and respectful to their goodness but never interested because one knew everything about them, even their hidden thoughts and the side of them that was not nice or good without having any communication with them.﻿ ﻿… He had another side; but there was no place in his life which would allow it expression.

===Chinese===

她放下了针线活，抬起那张花似的脸，不像别的表亲们那样带着猜疑，却带着她们那一种温文拘谨的教养，和几分她们的神情。她愿意的话也可以刻薄。她能撒撒嘴角，悉落人。连英国有教养的、受庇护的中产阶级淑女里最好的那些，也永远同样可怕，其秘密何在？他磕磕绊绊，含舍糊糊地说了一遍。他知道他心灵全部的漫长孤独都摊开在一个人面前——而她曾是、也仍旧是他青春的梦与奇妙与魔法，是他亲爱的伴侣。“淑女”是英国职业人士的妻——单纯、受庇护、居家，受训于一套她从不思考的原则并靠它们活着；敬重职业人士和职业上成功的人士；从看不见他们人人都是五年级的男生。哪个女人若看见他们的真面目——他们心智上的骄傲与虚荣与固执——都不会留在他们身边；哪个女人若看见他们遮掩的胃口。……可这些太太们又能到哪里去呢？
---
整个晚上她被安置在安静和沉闷里，不久便觉出深深的疲倦。她无助的盘点叫她难以面对这两个生人，生怕痛楚的洞察、怜惘和恼怒从她身上流泻得太显眼。……也许他们也在盘点、也在怜惘；可他们有兴趣，回应里带着一点热切，而且虽然教养太好不肯发问，显然满肚子没问出口的猜测，回头多半会彼此交流。会有人说她太自我，对他们不感兴趣，一个自私、不喜交际、不讨人喜欢的人，而他们是和善可爱的人，对人人都有兴趣。这也许是真的。……可同样真的是：他们热切、有兴趣，是因为他们的生活里除了原则和一套固定的看事物的方式，空无一物；人可以喜爱他们的和气、敬重他们的良善，却永远不会对他们有兴趣，因为人凭不与他们交往就知道关于他们的一切，连他们藏着的念头、连他们身上不和不善的那一面都知道。……他还有另一面；可他的生活里没有一处容它表达。

===Original===

It could only live in the lives of people met in books; in sympathies here and there for a moment; in people who passed “like ships in the night”; in moments at the beginning and end of holidays when things would seem real, and as if henceforth they were going to be real every day. If it found expression in his life, it would break up that life. Anyone who tried to make it find personal expression would be cruel; unless it were to turn him into a reformer or the follower of a reformer. That could happen to him. He was secretly interested in adventurers and adventuresses.

===Chinese===

它只能活在书本里遇见的人们的生命里；活在四下里片刻的会心里；活在那些“如夜航之船”交错而过的人们身上；活在假期开头和末尾的那些时刻——那时万物显得真，仿佛从今往后它们天天都要真下去。它若在他的生活里找到表达，就会把那生活打散。谁想让它找到个人的表达，谁就是残忍；除非那把他变成一个改革家，或改革家的追随者。这倒可能落在他头上。他暗暗对男冒险家和女冒险家感兴趣。

## XIX / 十九
===Original===

It had evidently been a great festival. One of the events of Mr. Hancock’s summer; designed by him for the happiness and enjoyment of his friends and enjoyed by him in labouring to those ends. It was *beautiful* to look back upon; in every part; the easy journey, the approach to the cottage along the mile of green-feathered river, the well-ordered feast in the large clean cottage; the well-thought out comfort of the cottage bedrooms, the sight of the orchard lit by Chinese lanterns, the lantern-lit boats, the drifting down the river in the soft moonlit air; the candle lit supper table, morning through the cottage windows, upstairs and down, far away from the world, people meeting at breakfast like travellers in a far-off country, pleased to see well known faces﻿ ﻿… the morning on the green river﻿ ﻿… the gentleness and kindliness and quiet dignity of everybody, the kindly difficult gently jesting discussion of small personal incidents; the gentle amiable strains; the mild restrained self-effacing watchfulness of the women; the uncompeting mutual admiration of the men; the general gratitude of the group when one or other of the men filled up a space of time with a piece of modestly narrated personal reminiscence.﻿ ﻿…
---
Never, never could she belong to that world. It was a perfect little world; enclosed; something one would need to be born and trained into; the experience of it as an outsider was pure pain and misery; admiration, irritation and resentment running abreast in a fever. Welcome and kindliness could do nothing; one’s own straining towards it, nothing; a night of sleepless battering at its closed doors, nothing. There was a secret in it, in spite of its simple seeming exterior; an undesired secret. Something to which one could not give oneself up. Its terms were terms on which one could not live. That girl could live on them, in spite of her strenuous different life in the east-end settlement﻿ ﻿… in spite of her plain dull dress and red hands. She knew the code; her cheap straw hat waved graciously, her hair ruffled about her head in soft clouds. Why had he never spoken of her uncle’s cottage so near his own? She must be always there. When she appeared in the surgery she seemed to come straight out of the east-end﻿ ﻿… his respect for workers amongst the poor﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

那显然是一场盛大的节日。汉考克先生夏天的一桩盛事；由他一手设计，为的是朋友们的幸福和欢愉，而他呢，在为这些目标的操劳里享受它。回望起来，它美极了；每一个部分都美；那趟轻松的旅程，沿着一英里绿羽般的河走近村舍，宽敞洁净的村舍里秩序井然的盛宴；村舍卧室里想得妥帖周到的舒适，灯笼照亮果园的那一景，挂灯的小船，在柔漫的月夜里顺流而下；烛光里的晚餐桌，清晨透过村舍的窗子，楼上楼下，远离尘世，人们在早餐桌上相聚，像远方的国度里的旅人，见了熟识的面孔欢喜……绿河上的早晨……每个人的温文、和善与安详的尊严；对一桩桩私人小事的那种和气的、艰难的、轻轻打趣的议论；温和的、亲切的种种张力；女人们那种收敛的、自我消隐的、柔柔的看顾；男人们那种不相争竞的彼此欣赏；每当这个或那个男人用一段谦逊铺叙的个人往事填起一段时光，全伙人便一致地感激。…
---
她永远、永远也不可能属于那个世界。那是一个完美的小世界；封闭的；人须得生在其中、又受训于其中；作为外来者经历它，纯粹的只有疼痛和悲惨；倾慕、恼怒和怨恨并辔奔跑，烧成一场热病。欢迎与和善无能为力；自己朝它的苦挣，无能为力；一夜无眠地捶它闭着的门，也无能为力。它里面有一个秘密，尽管它外表朴素；一个不受欢迎的秘密。一样人无法把自己交付过去的东西。它的条款，是人活不下去的条款。那个姑娘却能在那些条款上活，尽管她在东区贫民服务所过着艰苦的不同生活…尽管她一身素净的暗裙、一双通红的手。她懂得那套法典；她那顶便宜的草帽优雅地摆动，她的头发在头周蓬成柔云。他为什么从不提起，她叔叔的村舍离他自己的这么近？她一定常在那儿。她在诊室里露面的时候，像是径直从东区走出来的……他对贫民工作者那份敬意……

===Original===

his general mild revulsion from philanthropists; but down here she was not a philanthropist﻿ ﻿… outwardly a girl with blowy hair and a wavy hat, smiling in boats, understanding botany and fishing﻿ ﻿… inwardly a designing female, her mind lit by her cold intellectual “ethical”﻿—hooooo﻿—the very *sound* of the word﻿—“ethical Pantheism”; cool and secret and hateful. “Rather a nice little thing”; “pretty green dress”; *nice*!

===Chinese===

……他对慈善家那种普遍的、温和的反感；可在这儿，她不是慈善家…外表上，是个头发吹乱、帽子摇摆的姑娘，在船里笑着，懂植物学、会钓鱼…内里，是一个工于算计的雌儿，头脑被她那冷冰冰的智识的“伦理的”——嚯——这个词的发音本身——“伦理的泛神论”照亮；凉凉的，秘密的，可憎。“怪不错的小东西”；“漂亮的绿裙子”；不错！

## XX / 二十
===Original===

Miriam turned swiftly in her chair and looked up. But Mr. Hancock was already at the door. There was only a glimpse of his unknown figure arrested for a moment with its back to her as he pulled the door wide enough to pass through. The door closed crisply behind him and his crisp unhastening footsteps went away out of hearing along the thickly carpeted hall.
“*Dear* me!” she breathed through firmly held lips, standing up. Her blood was aflame. The thudding of her heart shook the words upon her breath. She was fighting against something more than amazement. She knew that only part of her refused to believe. In a part of her brain illumination leaving the shock already far away in the past, was at work undisturbed, flowing rapidly down into thoughts set neatly in the language of the world. She held them back, occupying herself irrelevantly about the room, catching back desperately at the familiar trains of revery suggested by its objects; cancelling the incident and summoning it again and again without prejudice or afterthought. Each time the shock recurred unchanged, firmly registered, its quality indubitable. She sat down at last to examine it and find her thoughts. Taking a pencil in a trembling hand she began carefully adding a long column of figures. A system of adding that had been recommended to her by the family mathematician now suggested itself for the first time in connection with her own efforts.﻿ ﻿…
How *dare* he?
It was deliberate. A brusque casual tone, deliberately put on; a tone he sometimes used to the boys downstairs, or to cabmen. How did he dare to use it to her? It must cease instantly. It was not to be suffered for a moment. Not for a moment could she hold a position which would entitle anyone, particularly any man to speak to her in that﻿—outrageous﻿—*official* tone. Why not? It was the way of business people and officials all the world over.﻿ ﻿… Then he should have begun as he meant to go on.﻿ ﻿… I won’t endure it now. No one has ever spoken to me in that way﻿—and no one shall, with impunity. I have been fortunate. They have spoiled me.﻿ ﻿… I should never have come if I had found they had that sort of tone. It was his difference that made me come.
---
Those two had talked to him and made him think. The aunt and cousins had prepared the way. But their hostility had been harmless. These two had approved. That was clear at the weekend.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆在椅子里霍地转身，抬头。可汉考克先生已到了门口。只来得及瞥见他那不为人知的身影停了一瞬，背对着她，把门拉到刚够自己过去。门在他身后干脆地合拢，他那干脆的、不紧不慢的脚步声顺着厚地毯铺的门厅走远，直到听不见。
“*天*哪！”她抿紧的唇缝里迸出这一声，站了起来。她的血在燒。心的擂动把她送出的字都震得发颤。她在同不止是惊愕的什么东西搏斗。她知道，拒不相信的只是她的一部分。在她脑子的一处，那照亮一切的光正在工作，不受打扰，把震动早已远远抛回过去，迅速流进用世上通行的语言整整齐齐排好的思绪。她把它们扣住，在屋里没相干地忙这忙那，拼命抓回屋里各件东西勾起的熟悉的遐想；把这件事取消，又一一遍无成见、无后思地重新召来。每一次震动都原样重现，凿实登记，性质无可置疑。她终于坐下来检视它，寻找自己的思绪。她用发抖的手拿起铅笔，开始小心地加一长列数字。家里那位数学家曾推荐给她的一套加法，此刻头一回同她自己的事联系了起来。……
他怎么*敢*？
这是故意的。一种粗率的、随口的腔调，故意装出来的；他有时对楼下的学徒们用，或对马车夫用。他怎么敢对她用？必须立刻叫它停住。一刻也不能容忍。她一刻也不能占着一个任凭任何人、尤其是任何男人用那种——蛮横的——*公务*腔调对她说话的位置。为什么不能？生意人和官老爷全世界都这么说话。……那他就该一开始就照他打算继续的祥子开始。……我现在就不容忍。从没有人这样对我说过话——也不会有人这样说了不付代价。我一直幸运。他们把我宠坏了。……我若发现他们有那种腔调，我就根本不会来。是他的那份不同叫我来的。
---
那两个同他谈过，叫他想了。姑妈和表亲们铺了路。可她们的敌意是无害的。那两个是赞许的。周末那次已经清楚了。

===Original===

They must have chaffed him and given him their blessing. Then, for the first time, he had thought, sitting alone and pondering reasonably. It was he himself who had drawn back. He was quite right. He belonged to that side of society and must keep with them and go their way. Very wise and right﻿ ﻿… but damn his insolent complacency.﻿ ﻿…
---
“Everything a professional man does, must stabilise his position.” Perhaps that is true. But then his business relationships must be business relationships from the first﻿ ﻿… that was expected. The wonder of the Wimpole Street life was that it had not been so. Instead of an employer there had been a sensitive isolated man; prosperous and strong outwardly and as suffering and perplexed in mind as anyone could be. He had not hesitated to seek sympathy.
---
*Any* fair-minded onlooker would condemn him. Anyone who could have seen the way he broke through resistance to social intercourse outside the practice. He may have thought he was being kind to a resourceless girl. It was *not* to resourcelessness that he had appealed. It was not that. That was not the truth.
---
He would have cynical thoughts. The truth was that something came in and happened of itself before one knew. A woman always knows first. It was not clear until Babington. But there was a sharp glimpse then. He must have known how amazed they would be at his cycling over after he had neglected them for years, on that one Sunday. They had concealed their amazement from him. But it was they who had revealed things. There was nothing imaginary after that in taking one wild glance and leaving things to go their way. Nothing. No one was to blame. And now he knew and had considered and had made an absurd reasonable decision and taken ridiculous prompt action.
---
A business relationship﻿ ﻿… by *all* means. But he shall acknowledge and apologise. He shall explain his insulting admission of fear. He shall admit in plain speech what has accounted for his change of manner.
---
Then that little horror is also condemned. *She* is not a wealthy efficient woman of the world.
---
Men are simply paltry and silly﻿—all of them.
---
In pain and fear she wandered about her room, listening for her bell. It had gone; the meaning of their days had gone; trust and confidence could never come back. A door was closed. His life was closed on her forever.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

他们一定打趣了他，又给了他祝福。随后，头一回，他想了，独自坐着，条理分明地思量。是他自己退了回去。他完全对。他属于社会的那一侧，必须同他们待在一起、走他们的路。很明智、很对……可是去他的无礼的自得。……
---
“职业人士做的每一件事，都必须稳固他的地位。”也许这是真的。可那样的话，他的业务关系就谈从第一天起是业务关系……这本是意料中的。温波尔街生活的奇妙就在于它偏不是这样。那里没有雇主，只有一个敏感的、孤独的男人；外表兴旺强健，内心却同任何人一样受苦、一样惶惑。他从不曾迟疑于寻求同情。
---
*任何*公正的旁观者都会指责他。任何人只要见过他怎样突破隔阂、闯进诊所之外的社交，都会指责。他也许自以为是在善待一个无依无靠的姑娘。他诉求的*不是*无依无靠。不是那个。那不是真相。
---
他会有愤世的念头。真相是，有些东西进来、自己发生了，在人明白之前。女人总是先知道。到巴宾顿之前还不清楚。可那时有一瞥清亮的闪光。他多年怠慢了他们、却在那个星期天骑车过去，他一定知道他们会多么惊讶。他们对他掩住了惊讶。可正是他们泄露了事情。此后再取一个狂野的眼色、再放任各走各路，就没有一点想象的成分了。没有。谁也没有错。而如今他知道了、掂量过了、做出了一个荒唐的理性的决定、采取了可笑的迅速行动。
---
业务关系……悉听*尊*便。但他必须承认、必须道歉。他必须解释他那侮辱人的对恐惧的招认。他必须用明白话承认，是什么解释了他态度的改变。
---
那么那个小恐怖也一同被定了罪。*她*可不是什么有钱能干的社会名媛。
---
男人们简直琐碎而愚蠢——他们全体。
---
她在疼痛与恐惧里绕着房间打转，听着她的铃。它去了；他们那些日子的意义去了；信任与信心再也回不来。一扇门关上了。他的生命对她永远关上了。……

===Original===

---
The bell rang softly in its usual way. The incident had been an accident; an illusion. Even so; she had been prepared for it, without knowing she was prepared, otherwise she would not have understood so fully and instantly. If she had only imagined it, it had changed everything, her interpretation of it was prophetic; just as before he had not known where they were so now the rupture was imminent whether he knew it or no. She found herself going upstairs breathing air thick with pain. This was dreadful.﻿ ﻿… She could not bear much of this.﻿ ﻿… The patient had gone. He would be alone. They would be alone. To be in his presence would be a relief﻿ ﻿… this was appalling. This pain could not be endured. The sight of the room holding the six months would be intolerable. She drew her face together, but her heart was beating noisily. The knob of the door handle rattled in her trembling hand﻿ ﻿… large flat brass knob with a row of grooves to help the grasp﻿ ﻿… she had never observed that before. The door opened before her. She flung it wider than usual and pushed her way, leaving it open﻿ ﻿… he was standing impermanently with a sham air of engrossment at his writing table and would turn on his heel and go the moment she was fairly across the room. Buoyant with pain she flitted through the empty air towards the distant bracket-table. Each object upon it stood marvellously clear. She reached it and got her hands upon the familiar instruments﻿ ﻿… no sound; he had not moved. The flame of the little spirit-lamp burned unwavering in the complete stillness﻿ ﻿… now was the moment to drop thoughts and anger. Up here was something that had been made up here, real and changeless and independent. The least vestige of tumult would destroy it. It was something that no one could touch; neither his friends nor he nor she. They had not made it and they could not touch it. Nothing had happened to it; and he had stood quietly there long enough for it to reassert itself. Steadily with her hands full of instruments she turned towards the sterilising tray. The room was empty. Pain ran glowing up her arms from her burden of nauseating relics of the needs of some complacent patient﻿ ﻿… the room was stripped, a west-end surgery, among scores of other west-end surgeries, a prison claiming her by the bonds of the loathsome duties she had learned.

===Chinese===

---
铃照常轻轻地响了。那件事是个意外；一场错觉。即便如此；她也是早有准备的，自己不知道自己有准备，否则她不会理解得这样充分、这样即刻。哪怕只是她的想象，它也改变了一切，她对它的解读是先知的；正如从前他不知道他们在哪里，如今决裂也已迫在眉睫，无论他知不知道。她发觉自己上着楼，呼吸的空气里痛得稠稠的。这太可怕了。……这她受不了多久。……病人走了。他会是一个人。他们会是两个人。到他面前去会是一种解脱……这太骇人了。这疼痛无法忍受。一看见盛着那六个月的房间会受不了。她绷紧了脸，心却咚咚地响。门把手的旋钮在她发抖的手里哗啦作响……又大又平的黄铜旋钮，带一遭抓握用的凹槽……这个她从没留意过。门在她面前开了。她把门推得比平时更豁，挤身进去，任它敞着……他正装出一副专注的假象立在写字台前，立得随时要走，等她一过房间中线就会旋踵而去。她带着疼痛的浮力穿过空空的空气，朝远处那张边架台翩然掠去。台上每一件东西都清清楚楚得奇妙。她到了，双手搭上那些熟悉的器械……没有声音；他没有动。酒精灯的小火苗在一片彻底的寂静里纹丝不动地燒着……此刻正是放下念头和愤怒的时刻。在这上头，有一样在这上头自己长成的东西，真实、不变、独立。最微小的骚动也会毁了它。它是谁也碰不得的东西；他的朋友们碰不得，他碰不得，她也碰不得。不是他们造的，他们就碰不得。它毫发无损；而他静静地立了够久，久到它重新立定了自己。她双手满捧器械，稳稳转向消毒盘。房间是空的。痛楚顺着双臂从她捧着的那些叫人作呕的遗物——某个自得病人的需要留下的——向上燒……房间被剥光了，一间西区诊室，同几十间别的西区诊室一起，一座监牢，用她学下的那些可憎的职分捆住她。

## XXI / 二十一
===Original===

Today the familiar handwriting brought no relief. This letter must be the final explanation. She opened it, standing by the hall table. “Dear Miss Henderson﻿—you are very persistent.” She folded the letter up and walked rapidly out into the sunshine. The way down to the Euston Road was very long and sunlit. It was radiant with all the months and weeks and days. She thought of going on with the unread letter and carrying it into the surgery, tearing it up into the waste paper basket and saying I have not read this. It is all right. We will not talk any more. One thing would have gone. But there would be a tremendous cheerfulness and independence and the memory of the things in the other letters. The letter once read two things would have gone, everything. She paused at the corner of the gardens looking down at the pavement. There was in some way that would not come quite clear so much more at stake than personal feelings about the insulting moment. It was something that stuck into everything, made everything intolerable until it was admitted and cancelled. As long as he went on hedging and pretending it was not there there could be no truth anywhere. It was something that must go out of the world, no matter what it cost. It would be smiling and cattish and behaving to drop it. Explained, it would be wiped away, and everything else with it. To accept his assertions would be to admit lack of insight. That would be treachery. The continued spontaneity of manner which it would ensure would be the false spontaneity that sat everywhere﻿ ﻿… all over that woman getting into the bus; brisk cheerful falsity. She glanced through to the end of the letter﻿ ﻿… “foolish gossip which might end by making your position untenable.” *Idiot.* Charming chivalrous gentleman.
---
I want to have it both ways. To keep the consideration and flout the necessity for it. No one shall dare to protect me from gossip. To prove myself independent and truth-demanding I would break up anything. That’s damned folly. Never mind. Why didn’t he admit it at once? He hated being questioned and challenged. He may have thought that manner was “the kindest way.” It is not for him to choose ways of treating me. This cancels the past. But it admits it. Not to admit the past would be to go on forever in a false position. He still hides. But he knows that I know he is hiding.

===Chinese===

今天，熟悉的笔迹没有带来宽解。这封信必须是最后的解释。她站在门厅桌边拆开它。“亲爱的亨德森小姐——您真是锲而不舍。”她把信折起，快步走进阳光里。通往尤斯顿路的那段下坡路又长又亮。它被这几个月、几个星期、几天照得光辉灿烂。她想着就这样不读下去，把信带进诊室，撕碎扔进字纸篓，说：这信我没有读。没有事了。我们不再谈了。那样，一样东西就去了。可那将换来天大的快活、独立，加上那几封信里种种事物的记忆。信一旦读了，两样东西就都去了，一切。她在花园的拐角停住，低头望着人行道。不知怎么，这里赌着的，远不止对那受辱一刻的个人感受——虽然那层道理总归说不清。它是一样扎进一切的东西，叫一切都难以为继，直到它被承认、被勾销。只要他继续闪避、继续装作它不在，天下就没有一处有真实。它是一样必须从世界上出去的东西，不管付出什么。把它一笑置之、猫一样地做作，是不行的。解释了，它就会被抹掉，其余一切也一并抹掉。接受他的那套断言，就等于承认自己没有眼光。那是背叛。那会换来的做派上绵绵不绝的自然，将是那种坐在一切地方的假自然…坐在那正在上公共马车的女人浑身的那种；轻快快活的虚假。她把信草草扫到结尾…“愚蠢的闲话，到头来也许弄得您的地位难以维持。”白痴。侠骨柔肠的可爱绅士。
---
我想要两头占。保住那份体恤，又嘲弄对体恤的需要。谁也别想保护我、不叫我挨闲话。为证明我独立、我求真，我什么都肯砸。这是该死的蠢。不管它。他为什么不立刻承认？他恨被质问、被挑战。他也许以为那样做派是“最仁慈的方式”。用什么方式待我，不由他挑。这一下勾销了过去。可它也承认了过去。不承认过去，就永远在一个虚假的位置上走下去。他还在躲。可他知道我知道他在躲。

===Original===

Where we have been we have been. It may have been through a false estimate of me to begin with. That does not matter. Where we have been we have been. That is not imagination. One day he will know it is not imagination. There is something that is making me very glad. A painful relief. Something forcing me back upon something. There is something that I have smashed, for some reason I do not know. It’s something in my temper, that flares out about things. Life allows no chance of getting at the bottom of things.﻿ ﻿…
---
I have nothing now but my pained self again, having violently rushed at things and torn them to bits. It’s all my fault from the very beginning. But I stand for something. I would dash my head against a wall rather than deny it. I make people hate me by *knowing* them and dashing my head against the wall of their behaviour. I should never make a good chess-player. Is God a chess-player? I shan’t leave until I have proved that no one can put me in a false position. There is something that is untouched by positions.﻿ ﻿…
---
I did not know what I had.﻿ ﻿… Friendship is fine fine porcelain. I have sent a crack right through it.﻿ ﻿…
Mrs. Bailey﻿ ﻿… numbers of people I never think of would like to have me always there.﻿ ﻿…
The sky fitting down on the irregular brown vista bore an untouched life.﻿ ﻿… There were always mornings; at work. I am free to work zealously and generously with and for him.
At *least* I have broken up his confounded complacency.
He will be embarrassed. *I* shan’t.

===Chinese===

我们到过的地方，就是到过了。开头也许出于对我的一个错误估量。那不要紧。到过的地方，就是到过了。那不是想象。总有一天他会知道那不是想象。有一样东西正使我很觉快意。一种疼的宽解。一样逼我退回到某样东西上的东西。有一样东西叫我砸碎了，为了一个我自己也不知道的缘故。是我脾气里的什么东西，为一点事就冒出来。生活不给机会让人把事情看到底。…
---
如今我什么也没有了，只剩这个疼着的自己，刚任着性子朝东西扑上去、把它们撕成了碎片。从头到尾全是我的错。可我代表一样东西。我宁可拿脑袋撞墙也不否认它。我因为“识得”人、因为朝他们行为的墙上撞，招得人人恨我。我永远成不了好棋手。上帝是棋手吗？我不走，直到证明了谁也无法把我放进一个虚假的位置。有一样东西，是一切位置碰不着的。…
---
我不知道我曾拥有什么。…友谊是细细的细瓷。我在它上面裂开了一道通缝。…贝利太太…许多我从不想起的人，倒愿意我永远在那儿。…天扣在参差的棕色远景上，载着一段没人动过的生命。…早晨总会有；上工的时候。我可以自由地热忱地、慷慨地同他一起、为他做工了。

至少，我捅破了他那讨厌的自满。

他会尴尬。我不会。

## XXII / 二十二
===Original===

… “And at fifty, when a woman is beginning to sit down intelligently to life﻿—behold, it is beginning to be time to take leave.﻿ ﻿…”
That woman was an elderly woman of the world; but a dear. She understood. She had spent her life in amongst people, having a life of her own going on all the time; looking out at something through the bars, whenever she was alone and sometimes in the midst of conversations; but no one would see it, but people who *knew*. And now she was free to step out and there was hardly any time left. But there was a little time. Women who *know* are quite brisk at fifty. “A man must never be silent with a woman unless he wishes for the quiet development of a relationship from which there is no withdrawing﻿ ﻿… if ordinary social intercourse cannot be kept up he must fly﻿ ﻿… in silence a man is an open book and unarmed. In speech with a man a woman is at a disadvantage﻿—because they speak different languages. She may understand his. Hers he will never speak nor understand. In pity, or from other motives she must therefore, stammeringly, speak his. He listens and is flattered and thinks he has her mental measure when he has not touched even the fringe of her consciousness.﻿ ﻿… Outside the life relationship men and women can have only conversational, and again conversational, interchange.”﻿ ﻿… That’s the truth about life. Men and women never meet. Inside the life relationship you can see them being strangers and hostile; one or the other or both, completely alone. That was the world. Social life. In social life no one was alive but the lonely women keeping up half-admiring half-pitying endless conversations with men, with one little ironic part of themselves﻿ ﻿… until they were fifty and had done their share of social life. But outside the world﻿—one could be alive always. Fifty. Thirty more years.﻿ ﻿…
---
When I woke in the night I felt nothing but tiredness and regret for having promised to go. Now, I never felt so strong and happy. This is how Mag is feeling. Their kettle is bumping on their spirit lamp too.

===Chinese===

……“而到了五十岁，当一个女人正开始安安稳稳坐下来过生活的时候——看哪，也差不多该告辞了。…”

那女人是位上了年纪的世故妇人；可是个可人。她懂。她一辈子活在人们中间，自己的日子始终在一旁进行着；每逢独处、有时甚至在谈笑中间，便隔着栏杆朝某样东西望出去；可除了“识得”的人，谁也看不见。如今她自由了，可以走出去了，却几乎不剩时间了。可还剩一点。懂的女人们到了五十，脚步相当轻快。“男人同女人在一起，万万不可沉默，除非他有意让一段退不出去的关系悄悄滋长…倘若寻常的社交往来维持不下去，他就得逃…沉默中，男人是一本摊开的书，而且手无寸铁。同男人说话，女人居于劣势——因为他们说的是两种语言。她可以懂他的。她的，他永远不会说，也永远不懂。出于怜悯，或别的动机，她便只好结结巴巴，说他的语言。他听着，受用，以为已经捯出了她的头脑的分量，其实连她意识的边都没碰着。…在生命的关系之外，男人和女人之间只能有谈天的、再谈天的往来。”…这就是生活的真相。男人和女人永不相逢。在生命的关系里面，你可以看见他们彼此陌生、彼此敌对；这一方，或那一方，或双方，彻底孤单。那个世界就是这样。社交生活。社交生活里没有一个人活着，除了那些孤独的女人——用她们自己那一小块反讽的部分，同男人维持着半倾慕半怜惜的无穷谈话…直到五十岁，尽完了她们社交生活的份额。可是在世界之外——人可以永远活着。五十岁。还有三十年。…
---
夜里醒来时，我只觉出疲倦，后悔答应了来。此刻呢，我从未这样强壮、这样快活。玛格此刻就是这感觉。她们的壶也在她们酒精炉上颠着呢。

===Original===

She loves the sound just in this way, the Sunday morning sound of the kettle with the air full of coming bells and the doors opening﻿—I’m half-dressed, without any effort﻿—and shutting up and down the streets is *perfect*, again, and again; at seven o’clock in the silence, with the air coming in from the Squares smelling like the country, is bliss. “You know, little child, you have an extraordinary capacity for happiness.” I suppose I have. Well; I can’t help it.﻿ ﻿… I *am* frantically frantically happy. I’m up here alone, frantically happy. Even Mag has to talk to Jan about the happy things. Then they go, a little. The only thing to do is either to be silent or make cheerful noises. Bellow. If you do that too much people don’t like it. You can only keep on making cheerful noises if you are quite alone. Perhaps that is why people in life are always grumbling at “annoyances” and things; to hide how happy they are﻿ ﻿… “there is a dead level of happiness all over the world”﻿—hidden. People go on about things because they are always trying to remember how happy they are. The worse things are the more despairing they get, because they are so happy. You know what I mean. It’s there﻿—there’s nothing else there.﻿ ﻿… But some people *know* more about it than others. Intelligent people. I suppose I am intelligent. I can’t *help* it. I don’t want to be different. Yes I do﻿—oh Lord yes I *do*. Mag knows. But she goes in amongst people and the complaints and the fuss and takes sides. But they both come out again; to be by themselves and talk about it all﻿ ﻿… they sit down intelligently to life.﻿ ﻿… They do things that have nothing to do with their circumstances. They were always doing things like this all the year round. Spring and Summer and Autumn and Winter things. They had done, for years. The kind of things that made independent elderly women, widows and spinsters who were free to go about, have that look of intense appreciation﻿ ﻿… “a heart at leisure from itself to soothe and sympathise”; no, that type was always inclined to revel in other people’s troubles. It was something more than that. Never mind. Come on. Hurry up. *Oh*﻿—for a man, oh for a man *oh* for a man﻿—*sion* in the skies.﻿ ﻿… Wot a big voice I’ve got, Mother.
---
“Cooooooo﻿—ooo﻿—er﻿—Bill.” The sudden familiar sound came just above her head. Where was she? *What* a pity. The boys had wakened her.

===Chinese===

她爱的正是这个声响，这样爱——星期天早晨的壶声，空气里满是将至的钟声和一扇扇正开的门——我衣裳穿了一半，毫不费力——沿街上上下下关着的门，是完满的，一次，又一次；七点钟的寂静里，从广场那边来的空气带着乡下的气味，是天福。“你要知道，小孩子，你有一种了不起的幸福的能力。”大概我有。唉；我拿它没办法。…我简直是、简直是疯狂地快活。我一个人在楼上，疯狂地快活。连玛格也得把快活的事说给简听。随后它们淡一点，走一点。唯一的办法，要么沉默，要么弄出快活的声响。吼。吼得太多人家就不喜欢了。只有彻底独处，才能一直弄出快活的声响。也许正因为这样，生活里的人才永远抱怨“烦心事”、抱怨这样那样；好藏起他们有多快活…“全世界铺着一层平匀的幸福”——藏着的。人们絮叨种种事情，因为他们总在努力记起自己有多快活。事情越糟，他们越绝望，因为他们那样快活。你懂我的意思。它在那儿——别的什么也不在那儿。…可有些人对它知道得比旁人多。聪明人。我想我算聪明人。我拿它没办法。我不想变。不，我想——主啊，不，我想变。玛格知道。可她走进人们中间，走进抱怨和忙乱里，站边。可她们俩总会再退出来；独处，把这一切谈个够…安安稳稳坐下来过生活。…她们做的事，同她们的环境毫无关系。她们一年到头总在做这样的事。春事、夏事、秋事、冬事。做了好多年了。就是那些事，造就了独立的上了年纪的女人——那些随便走动的寡妇和单身女人——脸上那种深深的领受…“一颗闲适的心，去抚慰、去同情”；不，那一类人总是惯于把别人的烦恼当享受。是比那更多的一点什么。不管它。来吧。抓紧。哦——要一个男人，哦要一个男人，哦要个男——人（mansion 在天上）。…我嗓门多大呀，妈妈。
---
“咕——————比——尔。”那声熟悉的呼唤突然来到她头顶上。她在哪儿？真可惜。男孩们把她吵醒了。

===Original===

Then she had been asleep! It was perfect. The footsteps belonging to the voice had passed along just above her head; nice boys, they could not help chi-iking when they saw the sleeping figures, but they did not mean to disturb. They had wakened her from her first daytime sleep. Asleep! She had slept in broad sunlight at the foot of the little cliff. Waking in the day time is *perfect* happiness. To wake suddenly and fully, nowhere; in paradise; and then to see sharply with large clear strong eyes the things you were looking at when you fell asleep. She lay perfectly still. Perhaps the girls were asleep. Presently they would all be sitting up again and she would have to begin once more the tiring effort to be as clever as they were. But it would be a little different now that they had all lain stretched out at the foot of the cliffs asleep. She was changed. Something had happened since she had fallen asleep disappointed in the east-coast sea and the little low cliff, wondering why she could not see and feel them like the seas and cliffs of her childhood. She could see and feel them now, as long as no one spoke and the first part of the morning remained far away. She closed her eyes and drifted drowsily back to the moment of being awakened by the sudden cry. In the instant before her mind had slid back and she had listened to the muffled footsteps thudding along the turf of the low cliff above her head, waiting angrily and anxiously for further disturbance, she had been perfectly alive, seeing; perfect things all round her, no beginning or ending﻿ ﻿… there had been moments like that, years ago, in gardens, by seas and cliffs. Her mind wandered back amongst these; calling up each one with perfect freshness. They were all the same. In each one she had felt exactly the same; outside life, untouched by anything, free. She had thought they belonged to the past, to childhood and youth. In childhood she had thought each time that the world had just begun and would always be like that; later on, she now remembered, she had always thought when such a moment came that it would be the last and clung to it with wide desperate staring eyes until tears came and she had turned away from some great open scene with a strong conscious body flooded suddenly by a strong warm tide to the sad dark world to live for the rest of her time upon a memory.

===Chinese===

那么她方才是睡着了！再完满不过。那嗓音主人的脚步刚从她头顶上过去；好孩子，见了睡着的人影忍不住学两声怪叫，可无意搅扰。他们把她从她头一场白日觉里吵醒了。睡着了！她在大天白日的太阳底下、在小峭壁脚下睡着了。白天醒来是完满的幸福。骤然间、彻彻底底地醒来，哪儿也不是；在乐园里；然后用又大又清又亮的眼睛，锐利地看见你入睡时望着的东西。她一动不动地躺着。姑娘们也许也睡着了。不多时她们就都要再坐起来，她就得重新开始那桩累人的功课——装得同她们一样伶俐。可如今多少有点不同了，既然她们都曾在峭壁脚下伸直了身子睡着。她变了。自从她带着对东海岸的海和那道低低的小峭壁的失望睡去、纳闷为什么不能再像童年时代的海和峭壁那样看它感它，有些事情发生了。她此刻能看它、能感它了，只要没有人说话，只要早晨的头一部分还离得远远的。她闭上眼睛，昏昏沉沉漂回被那声突来的呼喊惊醒的那一刻。在她的心思滑回去之前的那一瞬，她听着头顶低峭壁草皮上闷闷的脚步声，又气又急地等着下一场搅扰——那一瞬她彻底活着，看着；完满的事物在她四周，没有开头也没有结尾…那样的时刻从前也有过，多年前，在花园里，在海边和峭壁旁。她的心思在这些里面游回去；每一个都以其完满的新鲜被唤起来。它们全是一个。每一个里她的感觉分毫不差；在生活之外，不受任何东西触碰，自由。她原以为它们属于过去，属于童年和少年。童年时她每一回都以为世界刚刚开始，以为世界永远会是这样；再后来，她如今记起来，每逢这样的时刻来到，她总以为这是最后一回，睁着一双绝望的大眼睛死死抓住它，直到眼泪涌上来，然后从某一幅开阔的大景象前转过身，强壮的、有知觉的身体里骤然涨过一股强壮的暖潮，回到悲哀的黑暗世界，往后靠一段记忆过活。

===Original===

But the moment she had just lived was the same, it was exactly the *same* as the first one she could remember, the moment of standing, alone, in bright sunlight on a narrow gravel path in the garden at Babington between two banks of flowers, the flowers level with her face and large bees swinging slowly to and fro before her face from bank to bank, many sweet smells coming from the flowers and amongst them a strange pleasant smell like burnt paper.﻿ ﻿… It was the same moment. She saw it now in just the same way; not remembering going into the garden or any end to being in the bright sun between the blazing flowers, the two banks linked by the slowly swinging bees, nothing else in the world, no house behind the little path, no garden beyond it. Yet she must somehow have got out of the house and through the shrubbery and along the plain path between the lawns.
---
All the six years at Babington were the blazing alley of flowers without beginning or end, no winters, no times of day or changes to be seen. There were other memories, quarrelling with Harriett in the nursery, making paper pills, listening to the bells on Sunday afternoon, a bell and a pomegranate, a bell or a pomegranate round about the hem of Aaron’s robe, the squirting of water into one’s aching ear, the taste of an egg after scarlet fever, the witch in the chimney, cowslip balls, a lobster walking upstairs on its tail, dancing in a ring with grownups, the smell of steam and soap the warm smell of the bath towel, Martha’s fingers warming one’s feet, her lips kissing one’s back, something going to happen tomorrow, crackling green paper clear like glass and a gold paper fringe in your hand before the cracker went off; an eye blazing out of the wall at night “Thou God seest me,” apple pasties in the garden; coming up from the mud pies round the summer house to bed, being hit on the nose by a swing and going indoors screaming at the large blots of blood on the white pinafore, climbing up the cucumber frame and falling through the glass at the top, blowing bubbles in the hayloft and singing Rosalie the Prairie Flower, and whole pieces of life indoors and out coming up bit by bit as one thought, but all mixed with sadness and pain and bothers with people. They did not come first or without thought. The blazing alley came first without thought or effort of memory.

===Chinese===

可她方才活过的那一刻是一样，同她记得起的头一个一模一样——那一回，她独自站在巴宾顿花园里一条窄窄的砾石小径上，明亮的阳光里，两畦花之间，花同她的脸一般高，大蜜蜂在她脸前从这畦到那畦慢慢地荡来荡去，花里升起种种甜香，其中混着一股像烧焦的纸的奇异好闻的气味。…是同一个时刻。她此刻看它，还是那样看法；记不起走进花园，也记不起待在烈焰繁花之间的亮阳光里有什么尽头，两畦花被慢慢荡着的蜜蜂连在一起，世上别无一物，小径背后没有房子，房子那边没有花园。可她总归是不知怎么从宅子里出来、穿过灌木丛、沿草坪间那条素净的小径走过来的。
---
巴宾顿那六年，全是那条烈焰的花巷，没有开头没有结尾，没有冬天，没有一天里的时辰，没有看得出的变化。也有别的记忆，在育儿室里同哈丽特吵架，搓纸球，星期天下午听钟声，一口钟和一枚石榴，一口钟或一枚石榴缀在亚伦的袍边，往疼的耳朵里灌水，猩红热之后鸡蛋的味道，烟囱里的巫婆，黄花九轮草团，一只龙虾尾巴朝前上楼梯，同大人们围圈跳舞，蒸汽和肥皂的气味，浴巾暖烘烘的气味，玛莎的手指烜脚，她的嘴唇吻背，明天要发生一件事了，手里哗啦响的绿纸，透明如玻璃，还有一圈金纸须，等着爆竹炸开；夜里墙上烧出来的一只眼睛“你看不见上帝，上帝看得见你”，花园里的苹果酥饼，从夏屋四周的泥巴饼堆上楼睡觉，叫秋千撞了鼻子，尖叫着跑进屋，白罩衣上一大团一大团的血，爬黄瓜架，从顶上的玻璃里跌下去，在干草棚里吹泡泡，唱《草原上的罗莎莉》，室内室外整段整段的日子随着想一件浮起一件——可全都掺着悲伤、疼痛和同人们的麻烦。它们不是头一个来的，也不是不招自来的。烈焰的花巷头一个来，不假思索，不费记忆。

===Original===

The flowers all shining separate and distinct and all together, indistinct in a blaze. She gazed at them﻿ ﻿… sweet Williams of many hues, everlasting flowers gold and yellow and brown and brownish purple, pinks and petunias and garden daisies white and deep crimson﻿ ﻿… then *memory* was happiness, one happiness linked to the next.﻿ ﻿… It was the same already with Germany﻿ ﻿… the sunny happy beautiful things came first﻿ ﻿… in a single glance the whole of the time in Germany was beautiful, golden happy light, and people happy in the golden light, garlands of music, and the happy ringing certainty of voices, no matter what they said, the way the whole of life throbbed with beauty when the hush of prayers was on the roomful of girls﻿ ﻿… the wonderful house, great dark high wooden doors in the distance thrown silently open, great silent space of sunlight between them, high windows, alight against the shadows of rooms; the happy confidence of the open scene.﻿ ﻿… Germany was a party, a visit, a gift. It *had* been, in spite of everything in the difficult life, what she had dreamed it when she went off; all woods and forests and music﻿ ﻿… happy Hermann and Dorothea happiness in the summer twilight of German villages. It had become that now. The heart of a German town was that, making one a little homesick for it.﻿ ﻿… The impulse to go and the going had been right. It was part of something﻿ ﻿… with a meaning; perhaps there is happiness only in the things one does deliberately, without a visible reason; drifting off to Germany, because it called; coming here today﻿ ﻿… in freedom. If you are free you are alive﻿ ﻿… nothing that happens in the part of your life that is not free, the part you do and are paid for, is alive. Today, because I am free I am the same person as I was when I was there, but much stronger and happier because I know it. As long as I can sometimes feel like this nothing has mattered. Life is a chain of happy moments that cannot die.
“Damn those boys﻿—they woke me up.”
“Did they Mag; so they did me; did you dream?”
Perhaps Mag would say something﻿ ﻿… but people never seemed to think anything of “dropping off to sleep.”
“I drempt that I dwelt in Marble Halls; you awake von Bohlen?”
“I don’t quite know.”
“But speaking *tent*atively.﻿ ﻿…”
“A long lean mizzerable tentative﻿—”
“I perceive that you are still asleep.

===Chinese===

那些花全都在发亮，一朵朵分得清清楚楚，又汇作一片，融成一团烈焰。她凝望它们…各色的美洲石竹，干花的金、黄、棕、褐紫，石竹、牵牛、白的和深绯的雏菊…于是记忆便是幸福，一份幸福连着下一份。…德国也已然是这样了…那些晴朗的、快活的、美的东西头一个来…只消一眼，德国的全部时光都是美的，金色的快乐的光，人们在金光里快活，音乐的种种花环，嗓音里快活的、铮铮的笃定，无论说的是什么，还有当祷告的静默罩满一屋子姑娘时，整个生命同美一起搏动的那个样子…那幢奇妙的宅子，远处一道道高大幽暗的木门无声地敞开，门与门之间一片浩大的、寂静的阳光，高窗迎着一间间屋子的暗影发亮；那敞开的场面的快活的自信。…德国是一场聚会，一次做客，一件礼物。它确实曾经是——尽管艰难生活里种种不如意——她动身去时梦想的那个样子；全是树林、森林和音乐…德意志村落的夏暮里，快活的《赫尔曼与窦绿苔》式的快活。如今它变成了那个样子。一座德国小城的心就是那个，叫人为它起一点乡愁。…去的冲动和去本身，都是对的。它是某样东西的一部分…带着一个意义；也许幸福只在你为某种看不见的理由、故意去做的事情里：漂去德国，因为它在召唤；今天到这里来…在自由里。你若自由，你就活着…你生命里不自由的那一部分——你做着、领着钱的那一部分——无论发生什么，都不是活的。今天，因为我自由，我是同我在那儿时同一个人，只是更强壮、更快活，因为我知道了它。只要我还能时时有这种感觉，一切都没有关系。生命是一串不会死的幸福时刻。

“该死的男孩——把我吵醒了。”

“是吗玛格；也把我吵醒了；你做梦了吗？”

也许玛格会说点什么…可人们对“打了个盹儿”好像从来不当回事。

“我梦见（我梦呀——见）我住在大理石厅堂；你醒着吗，冯·博伦（von Bohlen）？”

“我说不准。”

“不过，就着帐——篷（tent-atively）说说…”

“一句又长又瘦、惨兮兮的帐——篷话——”

“我看你还睡着呢。

===Original===

Shall I sing it﻿—‘I *durr‑e‑*empt I da‑*we*‑elt in ma‑ha‑har‑ble halls.’ ”
“Cooooo﻿—oooo﻿—er Bill.” The response sounded faintly from far away on the cliffs.
“Cooooo﻿—ooo﻿—er Micky,” warbled Miriam. “I like that noise. When they are further off I shall try doing it very loud to get the proper crack.”
“I think we’d better leave her here, don’t you von Bohlen?”
Was it nearly teatime? Would either of them soon mention tea? The beauty of the rocks had faded. Yet, if they ceased being clever and spoke of the beauty, it would not come back. The weariness of keeping things up went on. When the gingernuts and lemonade were at last set out upon the sand, they shamed Miriam with the sense of her long preoccupation with them. The girls had not thought of them. They never seemed to flag in their way of talking. Perhaps it was partly their regular meals. It was dreadful always to be the first one to want food.﻿ ﻿…
But she was happier down here with them than she would have been alone.
---
Going alone for a moment in the twilight across the little scrub, as soon as she had laughed enough over leaving the room in the shelter of a gorse bush, she recovered the afternoon’s happiness. There was a little fence, bricks were lying scattered about and half-finished houses stood along the edge of the scrub. But a soft land-breeze was coming across the common carrying the scent of gorse; the silence of the sea reminded her of its presence beyond the cliffs; her own gorse-scented breeze, and silent sea and sunlit cliffs.

===Chinese===

“我唱给你听好不好——‘我——梦呀——见我住——在——大大理石厅堂。’”

“咕———比——尔。”回声从峭壁上远处淡淡地传来。

“咕———米基，”米丽亚姆颤着嗓音应道。“我喜欢这声响。等他们走远一点，我要试试放足了嗓门，喊出那声脆响来。”

“我看咱们最好把她留在这儿，你说呢，冯·博伦？”

快到喝茶的时候了吗？她们俩谁会先提起茶？礁石的美已经褪了。可是，倘若她们不再抖机灵、说起点美来，它也不会回来。撑住种种事物的倦意继续着。当姜汁饼和柠檬水终于摆在沙地上时，米丽亚姆羞愧了——她惦记它们竟这样久。姑娘们没想到过它们。她们那套谈话好像永不衰减。兴许也仗着她们按时吃饭。永远是头一个想吃东西的人，真可怕。…

可同她们一起在下面这儿，她比独自一人要快活。
---
暮色里她独自穿过那小片灌木地走一小段，笑够了躲在荆豆丛后头“离席”的事之后，她把这个下午的幸福拾了回来。有一道小栅栏，砖头散四处，半成的房子沿灌木地边上立着。可一股柔软的陆风正掠过公地，载着荆豆的香气；海的寂静提醒她它在峭壁之外的存在；她自己这份荆豆香的风，和无声的海、日照的峭壁。

## XXIII / 二十三
===Original===

Cool with sound short sleep she rose early, the memory of yesterday giving a Sunday leisure to the usual anxious hurry of breakfast. She was strong with her own possessions. Wimpole Street held nothing but her contract of duties to fulfil. These she could see in a clear vexatious tangle, against the exciting oncoming of everybody’s summer; an excitement that was enough in itself. Patients were pouring out of town﻿—in a fortnight the Orlys would be gone; all Mr. Orly’s accounts must be out by then. In a month Mr. Hancock would go. For a month before her own holiday there would be almost nothing to do. If everyone’s accounts were examined before then, she could get them off at leisure during that month﻿ ﻿… then for this month there was nothing to do but the lessening daily duties and to get everyone to examine accounts; then the house to herself, with only Mr. Leyton there; the cool ease of summer in her room, and her own month ahead.
---
The little lavatory with its long high window sending in the light from across the two sets of back to back tree-shaded Bloomsbury gardens, its little shabby open sink cupboard facing her with its dim unpolished taps and the battered enamel cans on its cracked and blistered wooden top became this morning one of her own rooms, a happy little corner in the growing life that separated her from Wimpole Street. There were no corners such as this in the beautiful clever Hampstead house; no remote shabby happy corners at all; nothing brown and old and at peace. Between him and his house were his housekeeper and servants; between him and his life was his profession﻿ ﻿… and the complex group of people with whom he must perpetually deal, with whom he dealt in alternations of intimacy and formality. He was still at his best in his practice. That was still his life. There was nothing more real as yet in his life than certain times and moments in his room at Wimpole Street.﻿ ﻿… Life had answered no other questions for him.﻿ ﻿… His thought-life and his personal life were troubled and dark and cold﻿ ﻿… in spite of his attachment to some of his family group﻿ ﻿… he could buy beautiful things, and travel freely in his leisure﻿ ﻿… perhaps that, those two glorious things, were sufficient compensation. But there was something wrong about them; they gave a false sense of power﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

短短一场酣睡过后清清爽爽，她早早起了身，昨日的记忆给往常焦灼勿促的早饭添了星期天般的从容。她靠自己的所有而强壮。温波尔街只拴着她一份待尽的职分。她能把这些职分看成一团清清楚楚的乱麻，衬着人人夏天即将到来的兴奋；那兴奋本身就尽够了。病人正涌出城去——两星期内奥利一家就要走了；到时候奥利先生所有的账都必须发出去。一个月后汉考克先生也要走。她自己的假期之前的那一个月，将几乎无事可做。若是那之前把所有人的账都核完，她那一个月里就能从从容容把它们发出去……那么这一个月里，除了日日递减的日常事务和催人人核账，便无事可做；然后宅子归她一个人，只留着莱顿先生；她房间里夏日的清凉安闲，和她自己的一个月在前头。
---
那间小盥洗室，高高的长窗透进两排背靠背的、绿树荫覆的布卢姆斯伯里花园那头的光，那口寒碜的敞式脸盆柜迎着她，龙头晦暗无光，开裂起泡的木柜面上磕瘪的搪瓷水罐——今晨成了她自己的房间之一，在那把她同温波尔街分开的、日渐生长的生命里一个快活的小角落。汉普斯特德那座美丽聪明的宅子里没有这样的角落；根本没有偏远、寒碜而快活的角落；没有一样棕色的、老旧的、安宁的东西。隔在他和他的宅子之间的是他的管家和仆人；隔在他和他的生活之间的是他的职业……和他必须永无休止地打交道的那一群人，亲疏交替地打着交道。他仍在他的诊所里处于全盛。那仍旧是他的生活。他的生活里，迄今没有比温波尔街他房间里的一些时刻更真实的东西。……生活没有回答过他别的问题。……他的思想生活和个人生活烦恼、幽暗而寒冷……尽管他对他那一族里的几个人有情分……他能买美丽的东西，闲暇时随意旅行……也许那两样堂皇的东西，就是足够的补偿了。可它们有点不对；它们给人权力的错觉……

===Original===

the way all those people smiled at each other when they went about and bought things, picked up a fine thing at a bargain, or gave a price whose size they were proud of﻿ ﻿… thinking other people’s thoughts﻿ ﻿… apart from this worldly side of his life, he was entirely at Wimpole Street; the whole of him; an open book; there was nothing else in his life, yet﻿ ﻿… his holiday with those two men﻿—even the soft-voiced sensuous one who would quote poetry and talk romantically and cynically about women in the evenings﻿—would bring nothing else. Yet he was counting upon it so much that he could not help unbending about his boat and his boots and his filters﻿ ﻿… perhaps all that was the best of the holiday﻿—men were never tired of talking about the way they did this and that﻿ ﻿… clever difficult things that made all the difference; but they missed all the rest. Even when they sat about smoking their minds were fussing. The women in their parties dressed, and smiled and appreciated. There would be no real happiness in such a party﻿ ﻿… except when the women were alone, doing the things with no show about them. Supposing I were able to go anywhere on this page﻿ ﻿… Ippington﻿ ﻿… 295 m.; pop. 760﻿ ﻿… trains to Tudworth and thence two or three times daily﻿ ﻿… Spray Bay Hotel.﻿ ﻿… A sparrow cheeped on the window sill and fluttered away. The breath of happiness poured in at the high window; all the places in the railway guide told over their charms; mountains and lakes and rivers, innumerable strips of coast, village streets to walk along for the first time, leading out﻿ ﻿… going, somewhere, in a train. Standing on tiptoe she gazed her thoughts across the two garden spaces towards the grimed backs of the large brown houses. Why was one allowed to be so utterly happy? There it was﻿ ﻿… happily here and happily going away﻿ ﻿… away.

===Chinese===

像那些人出去买东西时彼此微笑的那样，检一件便宜的好货，或出一个以数目自豪的价……想着别人的心思……除开他生活的这一俗世侧面，他整个儿在温波尔街；全部的他；一本摊开的书；他的生活里还没有别的，眼下……他同那两个男人的度假——连那个嗓音柔和、感性的、晚上会引诗、会又浪漫又刻薄地谈论女人的——也带不来别的。可他指望它指望得那么厉害，忍不住一提起他的船、他的靴子、他的滤水器就松了口……也许度假里最好的就是这一口——男人们谈他们这样那样做事的路数永不疲倦……聪明而费难的事，一做就全然不同；可他们错过其余的一切。哪怕坐着抽烟，他们的脑子也在忙叨。他们一行里的女人穿戴、微笑、赏识。这样的一伙里不会有真正的幸福……除非女人们自己待着，做那些不带表演的事。倘若我此刻能去这一页上的任何地方……伊平顿……295英里；人口760……有车通塔德沃斯，每日再转两三次……斯普雷湾旅馆。……一只麻雀在窗台上啁啾一声，扑翅飞走了。幸福从高窗里灌进来；火车时刻指南上所有的地方逐一数说着各自的妙处；山、湖、河，数不清的一绺绺海岸，头一回可以漫步的村街，一路引出去……乘着火车，去，去什么地方。她踮起脚尖，把她的思绪望过两片园地，投向那些棕色大宅熏黑的后背。为什么容许人这样彻底地幸福？它就在这儿……快活地在这里，快活地要走……走了。

## XXIV / 二十四
===Original===

“There; how d’ye like that, eh? A liberal education in twelve volumes with an index. Read them when ye want to. See?”﻿ ﻿…
They looked less set up like that in a row than when they had lain about on the floor of the den﻿ ﻿… taking up Dante and Beethoven at tea time.
---
“Books posted? I wonder I’m not more rushed. I say﻿—v’you greased all Hancock’s and the Pater’s instruments?”
He knows I’m slacking﻿ ﻿… he’ll tell the others when they come back.﻿ ﻿…
Mr. Leyton’s door shut with a bang. He would be sitting reading the newspaper until the next patient came. The eternal sounds of laughter and dancing came up from the kitchen. The rest of the house was perfectly still. Her miserable hand reopened the last page of the Index. There were five or six more entries under “Woman.”
---
If one could only burn all the volumes; stop the publication of them. But it was all books, all the literature in the world, right back to Juvenal﻿ ﻿… whatever happened, if it could all be avenged by somebody in some way, there was all that﻿ ﻿… the classics, the finest literature﻿—“unsurpassed.” Education would always mean coming in contact with all that. Schoolboys got their first ideas.﻿ ﻿… *How* could Newnham and Girton women endure it? How could they go on living and laughing and talking?
---
And the modern men were the worst﻿ ﻿… “we can now, with all the facts in our hands sit down and examine her at our leisure.” There was no getting away from the scientific facts﻿ ﻿… *inferior;* mentally, morally, intellectually and physically﻿ ﻿… her development arrested in the interest of her special functions﻿ ﻿… reverting later towards the male type﻿ ﻿… old women with deep voices and hair on their faces﻿ ﻿… leaving off where boys of eighteen began. If that is true everything is as clear as daylight. “Woman is not undeveloped man but diverse” falls to pieces. Woman is undeveloped man﻿ ﻿… if one could die of the loathsome visions﻿ ﻿… I *must* die. I can’t go on living in it﻿ ﻿… the whole world full of *creatures*; half-human. And I am one of the half-human ones or shall be if I don’t stop now.
---
Boys and girls were much the same﻿ ﻿… women stopped being people and went off into hideous processes. What for? What was it all for? Development. The wonders of science.

===Chinese===

“喏；这个您喜欢吗，嗯？十二卷的通才教育，还带索引。想读的时候就读。明白？”……
这样一排立着，倒不如先前摊在“窝”的地板上那么叫人得意……喝茶时随手拈起但丁和贝多芬。
---
“书都寄了？奇怪我竟然没更忙。我说——汉考克的和老爸的器械你都上油了吗？”
他知道我在偷懒……他们回来他会告诉其他人。……
莱顿先生的门砰地关上。他会坐着看报，直到下一个病人来。厨房里传来永恒的笑声和跳舞声。宅子的其余部分寂静无声。她那只悲苦的手重新翻开索引的最后一页。“女人”词条下还有五六条。
---
但愿能把所有的卷册烧掉；叫它们绝版。可它是所有的书，世上全部的文学，一直上溯到尤维纳利斯……无论发生什么，倘若这一切能由什么人以什么方式雪耻，那还有那么一大堆……古典作品，最好的文学——“无可超越”。教育将永远意味着同这一切接触。男学生们从这里得到最初的观念。……纽纳姆和格顿的女人们怎么忍得下去？她们怎么能继续活着、笑着、谈着？
---
而现代的男人最坏……“如今我们手里握着全部事实，可以坐下来从容地审问她了。”科学的 facts 是逃不掉的……*低劣；*心智上、道德上、智力上、体格上……她的发育为着她的特殊机能而停住了……日后又向男性型回返……嗓音低沉、脸上生毛的老妇……停在最十八岁男孩子起步的地方。这若是真的，一切就亮如白昼。“女人不是未发育的男人，而是别样”就塌了。女人就是未发育的男人……但愿能死在这些可憎的幻象里……我*必须*死。我不能在里面活下去……全世界满是这样的*生物*；半人。而我就是半人中的一个，再不止住现在，就会是。
---
男孩女孩原本差不多……女人不再是人，掉进种种可憎的过程。为的什么？这一切为的什么？发育。科学的奇迹。

===Original===

The wonders of science for women are nothing but gynæcology﻿—all those frightful operations in the *British Medical Journal* and those jokes﻿—the hundred golden rules.﻿ ﻿… Sacred functions﻿ ﻿… highest possibilities﻿ ﻿… sacred for what? The hand that rocks the cradle rules the world? The Future of the Race? What world? What race? Men.﻿ ﻿… Nothing but men; forever.
---
If by one thought all the men in the world could be stopped, shaken and slapped. There *must*, somewhere be some power that could avenge it all﻿ ﻿… but if these men were right there was not. Nothing but Nature and her decrees. Why was nature there? Who started it? If nature “took good care” this and that﻿ ﻿… there must be somebody. If there was a trick there must be a trickster. If there is a god who arranged how things should be between men and women and just let it go and go on I have no respect for him. I should like to give him a piece of my mind.﻿ ﻿…
---
It will all go on as long as women are stupid enough to go on bringing men into the world﻿ ﻿… even if civilised women stop the colonials and primitive races would go on. It is a nightmare.
---
They invent a legend to put the blame for the existence of humanity on woman and if she wants to stop it they talk about the wonders of civilisation and the sacred responsibilities of motherhood. They can’t have it both ways. They also say women are not logical.
---
They despise women and they want to go on living﻿—to reproduce﻿—themselves. None of their achievements, no “civilisation,” no art, no science can redeem that. There is no pardon possible for man. The only answer to them is suicide; all women ought to agree to commit suicide.
---
The torment grew as the August weeks passed. There were strange interesting things unexpectedly everywhere. Streets of great shuttered houses, their window boxes flowerless, all grey cool and quiet and untroubled on a day of cool rain; the restaurants were no longer crowded; torturing thought ranged there unsupported, goaded to madness, just a mad feverish swirling in the head, ranging out, driven back by the vacant eyes of little groups of people from the country. Unfamiliar people appeared in the parks and streets talking and staring eagerly about, women in felt boat-shaped hats trimmed with plaid ribbons﻿—Americans. They looked clever﻿—and ignorant of worrying thoughts. Men carried their parcels.

===Chinese===

科学的奇迹于女人不过是妇科——那些《英国医学杂志》上可怕的手术，还有那些笑话——金科玉律一百条。……神圣的机能……最高的可能性……神圣在哪里？摇摇篮的手统治世界？种族的未来？什么世界？什么种族？男人。……除了男人什么都没有；永远。
---
但愿一个念头能把世上所有的男人定住、摇撼、掴打。一定在什么地方有一种力量，能给这一切雪耻……可这些男人若是对的，那就没有。只有自然和它的律令。自然为什么在那里？谁起头的？自然若“照顾好”这桩那桩……就一定有个谁。有机关就一定有设机关的。倘若有个神，安排了男女之间该如何，然后就撒手、任它继续，我尊敬不起他。我倒想教训他几句。……
---
只要女人蠢到继续把男人生到世上，这一切就会继续……文明的女子哪怕停了，殖民地的人和原始种族也会继续。这是一场噩梦。
---
他们编一个传说，把人类存在的罪责推给女人；她若想叫它停止，他们又大谈文明的奇迹、母性的神圣责任。两头的便宜不能都占。他们还说女人不讲逻辑。
---
他们鄙夷女人，又想继续活下去——繁殖——他们自己。他们的一切成就，什么“文明”、什么艺术、什么科学，都赎不了这个。男人没有可得赦免的。对他们惟一的回答是自尽；所有的女人都该约定一同自尽。
---
折磨随着八月的几个星期加深。处处意外地冒出奇怪而有趣的东西。整街上了门板的大宅，窗箱里没了花，一个冷雨天里满目灰色，清凉、安静、无忧；餐馆不再拥挤；受刑的念头在那里游荡，无所依托，被鞭向疯狂，只是头脑里一阵狂热的打旋，荡出去，又被一小群一小群乡下来的人空洞的眼睛逼回来。公园和街上出现眼生的人，谈着话，急切地四下张望，女人戴毡制的船形帽，缀格子缎带——美国人。她们看着聪明——又不知愁。男人们自己提着包裹。

===Original===

But it was just the same. It was impossible to imagine these dried, yellow-faced women with babies. But if they liked all the fuss and noise and talk as much as they seemed to do.﻿ ﻿… If they did *not*, what were they doing? What was everybody *doing*? So busily.
---
Sleeplessness and every day a worse feeling of illness. Every day the new torture. Every night the dreaming and tossing in the fierce, stifling, dusty heat, the awful waking, to know that presently the unbearable human sounds would begin again; the torment of walking through the streets the solitary torment of leisure to read again in the stillness of the office; the moments of hope of finding a fresh meaning; hope of having misread.
---
There was nothing to turn to. Books were poisoned. Art. All the achievements of men were poisoned at the root. The beauty of nature was tricky femininity. The animal world was cruelty. Humanity was based on cruelty. Jests and amusements were tragic distractions from tragedy. Religion was the only hope. But even there there was no hope for women. No future life could heal the degradation of having been a woman. Religion in the world had nothing but insults for women. Christ was a man. If it was true that he was God taking on humanity﻿—he took on *male* humanity﻿ ﻿… and the people who explained him, St. Paul and the priests, the Anglicans and the nonconformists it was the same story everywhere. Even if religion could answer science and prove it wrong there was no hope, for women. And no intelligent person can prove science wrong. Life is poisoned, for women, at the very source. Science is true and will find out more and more and things will grow more and more horrible. Space is full of dead worlds. The world is cooling and dying. Then why not stop *now*?
---
“Nature’s great Salic Law will never be repealed.” “Women can never reach the highest places in civilisation.” Thomas Henry Huxley. With side-whiskers. A bouncing complacent walk. Thomas Henry Huxley. (*Thom*as *Ba*bington Ma*cau*lay.) The same sort of walk. Eminent men. Revelling in their cleverness. “The Lord has delivered him into my hand.” He did not believe in any future for anybody. But he built his life up complacently on home and family life while saying all those things about women, lived on them and their pain, ate their food, enjoyed the comforts they made﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

可还是一样。没法想象这些干瘦的、黄脸的女人会生孩子。可她们若真像看上去那样喜欢这一切喧哗、热闹和谈话。……她们若*不*喜欢，她们在干什么？人人都在*干什么*？这么忙。
---
夜夜失眠，一天比一天病得重。每天是新的拷问。每夜在那灼热、窒息、多尘的暑气里做梦、翻腾，醒来可怕的清醒，知道那些不堪忍受的人声转眼又要开始；穿过街道的折磨；办公室寂静里重新去读的孤独的闲暇折磨；寻找新解的希望的时刻；希望是自己读错了。
---
没有可以转向的地方。书下了毒。艺术。男人的一切成都在根上下毒。自然的美是刁滑的女性。动物界是残忍。人类奠基于残忍。笑话和消遣是悲剧的悲剧性岔道。宗教是惟一的希望。可在宗教里女人也没有希望。任何来世都治不好“曾经是个女人”的耻辱。世间的宗教给女人的只有侮辱。基督是个男人。倘若他真是取了人性的神——他取的是*男性*的人性……而解释他的那些人，圣保罗和众祭司，国教的和非国教的，处处是同一个故事。宗教哪怕能驳倒科学、证明它错了，女人也没有希望。而任何有智力的人都驳不倒科学。生命在源头上就为女人下了毒。科学是真的，还会发现得越来越多，事情会越来越可怕。空间里满是死去的星球。世界在冷却、在死去。那何不*现在*就停？
---
“自然伟大的萨利克法永远废除不了。”“女人永远达不到文明的最高位置。”托马斯·亨利·赫胥黎。留着连鬓胡。一副弹跳的自得的步态。托马斯·亨利·赫胥黎。（托马斯·巴宾顿·麦考莱。）同一种步态。显赫的人物。陶醉于自己的聪明。“耶和华已将他交在我手里。”他不信任何人有来世。却一面说着那些关于女人的话，一面把自己的生活自得地筑在家宅和家庭生活上，靠她们和她们的痛苦活着，吃她们做的饭，享受她们造就的安适……

===Original===

and wrote conceited letters to his friends about his achievements and his stomach and his feelings.
---
What is it in me that stands back? Why can’t it explain? My head will burst if it can’t explain. If I die now in wild anger it only makes the thing more laughable on the whole.﻿ ﻿… That old man lives quite alone in a little gaslit lodging. When he comes out he is quite alone. There is nothing touching him anywhere. He will go quietly on like that till he dies. But he is me. I saw myself in his eyes that day. But he must have money. He can live like that with nothing to do but read and think and roam about because he has money. It isn’t fair. Some woman cleans his room and does his laundry. His thoughts about women are awful. It’s the best way﻿ ﻿… but I’ve made all sorts of plans for the holidays. After that I will save and never see anybody and never stir out of Bloomsbury. The woman in black works. It’s only in the evenings she can roam about seeing nothing. But the people she works for know nothing about her. She knows. She is sweeter than he. She is sweet. I like her. But he is more me.

===Chinese===

还给朋友们写自命不凡的信，谈他的成就、他的肠胃和他的心情。
---
是我身上的什么在退后？它为什么不能解释？它再不能解释我的头就要裂了。我此刻若在狂怒中死去，只会让整件事更可笑。……那位老人独自住在一间点煤气灯的小寓所里。出门时孤零零一个人。没有任何地方触得着他。他会这样安安静静过下去直到死去。可他就是我。那天我在他眼睛里看见了自己。可他一定有钱。他能那样活着，除了读书、思想、游荡无事可做，因为他有钱。这不公平。某个女人替他打扫房间、洗衣服。他对女人的念头是可憎的。这是最好的活法……可假期我已做了种种计划。之后我要攒钱，谁也不见，再不出布卢姆斯伯里。那个黑衣女人做工。只有晚上她才能游荡，什么也不看。可她做工的人家对她一无所知。她知道。她比他甜。她是甜的。我喜欢她。可他更是我。

## XXV / 二十五
===Original===

The room still had the same radiant air. Nothing looked worn. There was not a spot anywhere. Bowls of flowers stood about. The Coalport tea service was set out on the little black table. The drawn-thread work table cover.﻿ ﻿… She had arranged the flowers. That was probably all she did; going in and out of the garden, in the sun, picking flowers. *The Artist’s Model* and *The Geisha* and the *Strand Musicals* still lay about; the curious new smell came from the inside of the piano. But there was this dreadful tiredness. It was dreadful that the tiredness should come nearer than the thought of Harriett. A pallid worried disordered face looked back from the strip of glass in the overmantel. No need to have looked. Always now, away from London, there was this dreadful realisation of fatigue, dreadful empty sense of worry and hurry﻿ ﻿… feeling so *strong* riding down through London, everything dropping away, nothing to think of; off and free, the holiday ahead, nothing but lovely, lonely freedom all round one.
---
Perhaps Harriett would be nervous and irritable. She had much more reason to be. But even if she were it would be no good. It would be impossible to conceal this frightful fatigue and nervousness. Harriett *must* understand at once how battered and abject one was. And it was a misrepresentation. Harriett knew nothing of all one had come from; all one was going to in the distance. Maddening.﻿ ﻿… Lovely; how rich and good they looked, more honest than those in the London shops. Harriett or Mrs. Thimm or Emma had ordered them from some confectioner in Chiswick. Fancy being able to buy anything like that without thinking. How well they went with the black piano and the Coalport tea-service and the garden light coming in. Gerald did not think that women were inferior or that Harriett was a dependent.﻿ ﻿… But Gerald did not think at all. He knew nothing was too good for Harriett. Oo, *I* dunno, she would say with a laugh. She thought all men were duffers. Perhaps that was the best way. Selfish babies. But Gerald was not selfish. He would never let Harriett wash up if he were there. He would never pretend to be ignorant of “mysteries” to get out of doing things. I get out of doing things﻿—in houses. But women won’t let me do things. They all know I want to be mooning about. How do they know it? What is it? But they like me to be there.

===Chinese===

房间还是那副容光焕发的样子。样样看不出磨损。处处没有一点污渍。花瓶四下摆着。考尔波特瓷茶具摆在小黑桌上。抽丝细工的桌布。……花是她插的。她大概就做这个；在太阳底下进进出出花园，剪花。《艺术家的模特》和《艺伎》（The Geisha）和《斯特兰德音乐剧集》还摊在四处；钢琴里透出那股古怪的新味儿。可这可憎的疲倦又来了。疲倦竟然比想到哈丽特还近，这真可怕。炉架镜那窄条玻璃里回望过来一张苍白、烦恼、散乱的脸。本来不必照的。如今只要一离开伦敦，就有这种对疲劳的可憎的真切体认，可憎的空洞的焦虑与匆促……骑车子穿伦敦城下去时那么*有力*，一切都往下掉，什么都不用想；脱身了，自由了，假期在前头，四周只有可爱的、孤独的自由。
---
哈丽特没准会紧张、会烦躁。她的理由多得多。可就算她这样，也无济于事。这份可怕的疲劳与紧张是藏不住的。哈丽特*必须*立刻明白人是被磋磨成什么样、多么低声下气。而这又是一种歪曲。哈丽特全然不知人从何处来；也不知人在远处将去往何处。真叫人发疯。……可爱；它们看着多丰厚多好，比伦敦铺子里的更实在。哈丽特或西姆太太或艾玛从奇西克哪家点心店订的。想想能想都不想就买下这样的东西。它们同黑钢琴和考尔波特茶具、同进来的园光多相配。杰拉尔德不认为女人低劣，也不认为哈丽特是依附的。……可杰拉尔德根本不思想。他只知道哈丽特配得上天下任何好东西。唔，*我*说不好，她会笑着这样说。她认为男人全是笨蛋。也许那才是最好的办法。自私的婴孩。可杰拉尔德不自私。他在家就绝不会让哈丽特洗碗。他绝不会假装不懂那些“神秘”来逃脱干活。我倒是逃脱干活——在人家家里。可女人们不让我干活。她们全知道我想到处闲晃。她们怎么知道的？那是什么？可她们喜欢我在场。

===Original===

And now in houses there’s always this fearful worry and tiredness. What is the meaning of it?
Heavy footsteps came slowly downstairs.
“I put tea indoors. I thought Miss Miriam’d be warm after her ride.”
A large undulating voice with a shrewd consoling glance in it. She must have come to the kitchen door to meet Harriett in the hall.
“Yes, I’ke spect she will.” It was the same voice she had had in the nursery, resonant with practice in speaking to new people. Miriam felt tears coming.
“Hullo, you porking? Isn’t it porking?”
“Simply porked to death my dear. Porked to *Death*,” bawled Miriam softly, refreshed and delighted. Harriett was still far off, but she felt as if she had touched her. Even the end of the awful nine months was not changing her. Her freshly shampooed hair had a leisurely glint. There was colour in her cheeks. She surreptitiously rubbed her own hot face. Her appearance would improve now with every hour. By the evening she would be her old self. After tea she would play *The Artist’s Model* and *The Geisha*.
“Let’s have tea. I was asleep. I didn’t hear you come.” She sank into one of the large chairs, her thin accordion pleated black silk tea-gown billowing out round her squared little body. Even her shoulders looked broader and squarer. From the little pleated white chiffon chemisette her radiant firm little head rose up, her hair glinting under the light of the window behind her. She looked so fine﻿—such a “fine spectacle”﻿—and seemed so strong. How did she feel? Mrs. Thimm brought the teapot. The moment she had gone Harriett handed the rich cakes. Mrs. Thimm *beaming*, shedding strong beams of happiness and approval.﻿ ﻿…
“Come on,” said Harriett. “Let’s tuck in. There’s some thin bread and butter somewhere but I can’t eat anything but these things.”
“Can’t you?”
“The last time I went up to town Mrs. Bollingdon and I had six between us at Slater’s and when we got back we had another tea.”
“Fancy *you*!”
“I know. I can’t ’*elp* it.”
“I can’t ’elp it, Micky. *Love*lay b-hird.”
The fourth cup of creamy tea; Harriett’s firm ringed hand; the gleaming serene world; the sunlit flower-filled garden shaded at the far end by the large tree the other side of the fence coming in, one with the room; the sun going to set and bring the evening freshness and rise tomorrow.

===Chinese===

而如今一进人家家里就有这份可怕的焦虑和疲倦。这是什么意思？
沉重的脚步慢慢下了楼。
“我把茶摆在屋里了。我想米丽亚姆小姐骑了一路该热了。”
一副宽宽厚厚的嗓音，里头带着精明的宽慰的一瞥。她准是到厨房门口来门厅里迎哈丽特。
“是，我——猜也是。”还是她育儿室里那副嗓音，同新来的人说惯了话的洪亮。米丽亚姆觉得眼泪要上来。
“喂，你在猪吗？是不是在猪？”
“简直猪死了，亲爱的。猪*死*了，”米丽亚姆轻轻嚷道，精神一爽，满心欢喜。哈丽特还离得远，可她觉得仿佛已经碰到她了。那可怕的九个月的尾声也没有改变她。她刚洗过的头发闲闲地闪着一亮。两颊有颜色。她偷偷摩挲自己发烫的脸。她的模样从今每小时都会好起来。到晚上她就是老样子了。喝过茶她要弹《艺术家的模特》和《艺伎》。
“喝茶吧。我睡着了。没听见你来。”她陷进一张大椅子，那件密密打裾的黑绸茶会袍在方方的小小身架四周鼓开来。连她的肩看着都更宽更方。小小的打裙白雪纺胸衣上头，她那容光焕发、结实的小头颅昂起来，头发在她背后窗光里闪亮。她看着这么好——这么一副“好景象”——又显得这么有力。她感觉如何？西姆太太端进茶壶。她一出去哈丽特就递过丰盛的蛋糕。西姆太太*满面红光*，撒出阵阵幸福与赞许的强光。……
“来，”哈丽特说。“開吃吧。薄黄油面包是有一点，可我除了这些什么都吃不下。”
“吃不下？”
“上回我进城，我和博林顿太太两个人在斯莱特吃了六份，回到家又吃了一顿茶。”
“想不到是*你*！”
“我知道。我忍不*住*嘛。”
“我忍不住嘛，米基。漂—亮的小—鸟儿。”
第四杯奶油浓茶；哈丽特那戴着戒指的结实的手；这明澈安详的世界；满园阳光的花园，远端隔着栅栏那棵大树的荫翳漫进来，同房间连成一体；太阳将落，带来傍晚的清新，明天还会升起。

===Original===

Twenty-eight leisurely teas, twenty-eight long days; a feeling of strength and drowsiness. Nothing to do but clean the bicycle and pump up the tyres on the lawn, tomorrow. Nothing﻿—after carrying the bicycle from the coal cellar up the area steps and through the house into the Tansley Street back yard. Nothing more but setting out after two nights of sleep in a cool room.
---
“That your machine in the yard, Mirry?”
“Yes; I’ve hired it, thirty bob for the whole month.”
“Well, if you’re going a sixty mile ride on it I advise you to tighten up the nuts a bit.”
“I will if you’ll show me where they are. I’ve got a lovely spanner. Did you look in the wallet?”
“I’ll have a look at it all over if you like.”
“Oh Gerald you saint.﻿ ﻿…”
“Now he’s happy,” said Harriett as Gerald’s white flannelled figure flashed into the sunlight and disappeared through the yard gate.
“Ph﻿—how hot it is; it’s this summerhouse.”
“Let’s go outside if you like,” said Miriam lazily, “it seems to me simply perfect in here.”
“It’s all right﻿—ph﻿—it’s hot everywhere,” said Harriett languidly. She mopped her face. Her face emerged from her handkerchief fever-flushed, the eyes large and dark and brilliant; her lips full and drawn in and down at the corners with a look of hopeless anxiety.
Anger flushed through Miriam. Harriett at nineteen, in the brilliant beauty of the summer afternoon facing hopeless fear.
“That’s an awfully pretty dress,” she faltered nervously.
Harriett set her lips and stretched both arms along the elbows of her basket chair.
“You could have it made into an evening gown.”
“I loathe the very sight of it. I shall burn it the minute I’ve done with it.”
It was awful that anything that looked so charming could seem like that.
“D’you feel bad? Is it so awful?”
“I’m all right, but I feel as if I were bursting. I wish it would just hurry up and be over.”
“I think you’re simply splendid.”
“I simply don’t think about it. You don’t think about it except now and again when you realise you’ve got to go through it and then you go hot all over.”
“The head’s a bit wobbly,” said Gerald riding round the lawn.
“Does that matter?”
“Well, it doesn’t make it any easier to ride, especially with this great bundle on the handlebars. You want a luggage-carrier.”
“I daresay. I say Gerald, show me the nuts tomorrow, not now.”

===Chinese===

二十八顿从容的茶，二十八个长长的日子；一种又有力又困倦的感觉。除了明天在草地上把车擦净、把胎打足，无事可做。无事——自从把自行车从煤窖搬上地下采光井的台阶、穿过全宅搬进坦斯利街的后院。再无别事，只等在凉快的屋里睡上两夜之后上路。
---
“院里那车是你的，米丽？”
“是；我租的，整个月三十先令。”
“唔，你要骑着它走六十英里，我劝你把螺母紧一紧。”
“你指给我它们在哪儿我就紧。我有一把可爱的扳手。看过工具袋没有？”
“你乐意的话我给它整个检查一遍。”
“哦杰拉尔德，你真是圣人。…”
“这会儿他舒坦了，”哈丽特说，杰拉尔德穿白法兰绒的身影闪进阳光里，穿出后院的门不见了。
“呒——真热；都怪这凉亭。”
“你乐意咱们就出去，”米丽亚姆懒懒地说，“要我说，这里面简直十全十美。”
“没关系——呒——哪儿都热，”哈丽特恹恹地说。她抹了抹脸。脸从手绢后头露出来，烧得通红，眼睛又大又黑又亮；嘴唇饱满，嘴角往下撇着，带着一种绝望的忧虑。
一阵怒气涌上米丽亚姆。哈丽特才十九岁，在夏日午后的灿烂美景里面对绝望的恐惧。
“这条裙子真好看，”她紧张地磕磕绊绊说。
哈丽特绷紧嘴唇，两条胳膊沿藤椅的扶手直直伸开。
“你可以把它改成一件晚礼服。”
“我看见它就恶心。一用完我马上就烧了它。”
一样东西看着这么讨喜，在人心里却可以是这个样子，真可怕。
“你难受吗？有那么糟？”
“我还好，就是觉得要撑破了。但愿它快点、快点完事。”
“我觉得你简直了不起。”
“我就是不去想它。平常你不想它，只是偶尔意识到自己非经历它不可，那会儿你浑身发烫。”
“车把有点晃，”杰拉尔德绕着草坪骑车说。
“要紧吗？”
“嗯，骑着不更省力，尤其车把上还驮着这大包。你需要一个行李架。”
“也许吧。我说杰拉尔德，明天再指给我看螺母，现在别弄了。”

===Original===

The machine was lying upside down on the lawn with its back wheel revolving slowly in the air.
“The front wheel’s out of the true.”
“What do you think of the saddle?”
“The saddle’s all right enough.”
“It’s a Brooks’s, B. 40; about the best you can have. It’s my own and so’s the Lucas’s Baby bell.”
“By Jove, she’s got an adjustable spanner.”
“That’s not mine nor the repair outfit; Mr. Leyton lent me those.”
“And vaseline on the bearings.”
“Of course.”
“I don’t think much of your gear-case, my dear.”
“Gerald, do you think it’s all right on the whole?”
“Well, it’s sound enough as far as I can see; bit squiffy and wobbly. I don’t advise you to ride it in traffic or with this bundle.”
“I *must* have the bundle. I came down through Tottenham Court Road and Oxford Street and Bond Street and Piccadilly all right.”
“Well, there’s no accounting for tastes. Got any oil?”
“There’s a little oil can in the wallet wrapped up in the rag. It’s lovely; perfectly new.”

===Chinese===

自行车倒扣在草地上，后轮在空中慢慢转着。
“前轮失圆了。”
“你觉得车座怎么样？”
“车座够好的。”
“这是布鲁克斯的，B.40；差不多是能买到的最好的。是我自己的，还有那卢卡斯‘宝宝’铃也是。”
“天哪，她还有一把活动扳手。”
“那不是我的，修理包也不是；是莱顿先生借我的。”
“轴承还上了凡士林。”
“当然。”
“你那链罩我可不敢恭维，亲爱的。”
“杰拉尔德，你说总体上它行吗？”
“嗯，就我看得见的来说还结实；就是有点歪、有点晃。我不劝你骑着它上热闹道，也别驮着这大包。”
“我*非*驮包不可。我一路从托特纳姆考特路和牛津街和邦德街和皮卡迪利骑下来，好好的。”
“嗯，口味没法说理。有油吗？”
“工具袋里有只小油壶，裹在布子里。可爱得很；全新。”

## XXVI / 二十六
===Original===

There was a strong soft grey light standing at the side of the blind﻿ ﻿… smiling and touching her as it had promised. She leaped to the floor and stood looking at it swaying with sleep. Ships sailing along with masts growing on them, poplars streaming up from the ships, all in a steam of gold.﻿ ﻿… Last night’s soapy water poured away and the fresh poured out ready standing there all night, everything ready.﻿ ﻿… I must not forget the extra piece of string.﻿ ﻿… Je‑ru‑sa‑*lem* the Gol‑den, with-milk-and-hun‑ney﻿—blest.﻿ ﻿… Sh, not so much noise﻿ ﻿… beneath thy con, tem, pla, tion, sink, heart, and, voice, o, ppressed.

> I *know* not, oh, I, *know*, not.

Sh﻿—Sh﻿ ﻿… hark hark my soul angelic songs are swelling O’er earth’s green fields, and ocean’s wave-beat shore﻿ ﻿… damn﻿—blast where are my bally knickers? sing us sweet fragments of the songs above.
---
The green world everywhere, inside and out﻿ ﻿… all along the dim staircase, waiting in the dim cold kitchen.
---
No blind, brighter. Cool grey light, a misty windless morning. Shut the door.

> *They*
 **stand**
 *those*
 **halls**
 *of*
 **zi‑on**

**All jubilant** *with* **song**.

===Chinese===

百叶窗边立着一股又强又软的灰光……它照着说过的那样微笑着、抚着她。她跳到地板上，站着，摇摇地带着睡意看它。船一路驶着，桅杆在船上长出来，杨树从船上涌起，全在一派金色的蒸汽里。…昨夜的肥皂水倒掉了，新水倒满了，整整一夜站在那儿，一切都停当。…别忘了那截备用的绳。…耶——路——撒——冷黄金的，流——奶——与——蜜——有——福。…嘘，别这么响…在你，的，静，观，之，下，沉，落，心，与，声，被，压。

> 我不知，哦，我，不，知。

嘘——嘘…听啊我的灵魂，天使之歌正涨起，漫过大地绿野和海洋拍岸的水滨…见鬼——该死，我的灯笼裤死哪儿去了？给我们唱唱上面那些歌的甜美片段。
---
到处都是绿色的世界，里里外外…沿昏暗的楼梯一路，在昏暗冰冷的厨房里等着。
---
没有百叶窗，更亮。凉凉的灰光，一个雾蒙蒙的无风的早晨。关上门。

> 它们立着，那些锡安的殿堂，全都欢腾着歌声。

===Original===

---
As she neared Colnbrook the road grew heavier and a closer mist lay over the fields. It was too soon for fatigue but her knees already seemed heavy with effort. Getting off at the level crossing she found that her skirt was sodden and her zouave spangled all over with beads of moisture. She walked shivering across the rails and remounted rapidly, hoisting into the saddle a draggled person that was not her own and riding doggedly on beating back all thoughts but the thought of sunrise.
---
“Is this Reading?”
The cyclist smiled as he shouted back. He knew she knew. But he liked shouting too. If she had yelled Have you got a *soul*, it would have been just the same. If everyone were on bicycles all the time you could talk to everybody, all the time, about anything﻿ ﻿… sailing so steadily along with two free legs﻿ ﻿… how much easier it must be with your knees going so slowly up and down﻿ ﻿… how *funny* I must look with my knees racing up and down in lumps of skirt. But I’m here, at the midday rest. It must be nearly twelve.
Drawing into the curb near a confectioner’s she thought of buying two bars of plain chocolate. There *was* some sort of truth in the Swiss Family Robinson. If you went on, it was all right. There was only death. People frightened you about things that were not there. I will never listen to anybody again; or be frightened. That cyclist knew, as long as he was on his bicycle. Perhaps he has people who make him not himself. He can always get away again. Men can always get away. I am going to lead a man’s life always getting away.﻿ ﻿…
Wheeling her machine back to the open road she sat down on a bank and ate the cold sausage and bread and half of the chocolate and lay down to rest on a level stretch of grass in front of a gate. Light throbbed round the edges of the little high white fleecy clouds. She swung triumphantly up. The earth throbbed beneath her with the throbbing of her heart﻿ ﻿… the sky steadied and stood further off, clear peaceful blue with light neat soft bunches of cloud drifting slowly across it. She closed her eyes upon the dazzling growing distances of blue and white and felt the horizon folding down in a firm clear sweep round her green cradle.

===Chinese===

---
走近科尔布鲁克，路面沉了，雾更紧地伏在田野上。说累还太早，可她的膝头已似灌了铅。在平交道口下车，她发觉裙子湿透了，左夫外衣上缀满细小的水珠。她打着战走过铁轨，迅速重新上车，把一个湿淋淋的、不是她自己的身子扛上鞍座，倔头倔脑地骑下去，把一切念头都打回去，只留着日出的念头。
---
“这儿是雷丁吗？”

骑车人一面回喊一面笑。他知道她知道。可他也爱喊。倘若她喊的是你有灵魂吗，结果也一模一样。倘若人人永远骑着车，你就可以同人人交谈，时时刻刻，谈任何事…用两条自由自在的腿这样稳稳地驶着…膝盖这样慢慢地一起一落，该省力多少啊…我的膝盖裹在一条条裙子里飞快起落，样子一定多滑稽。可我在这里了，在正午的歇脚处。快十二点了。

在一间糖果点心铺子附近挨上路沿，她想起买两条纯巧克力。《瑞士家庭鲁滨逊》里毕竟有点真的东西。只要你走下去，就没事。只有死亡。人们拿并不存在的东西吓你。我再也不听任何人的了；再也不害怕了。那位骑车人懂得，只要他在车上。也许他也有叫他做不成他自己的人。他总能再走开。男人总能走开。我要过一种男人的生活，永远走开。…

她把车推回大路，在一道土坡上坐下，吃了冷香肠、面包和半块巧克力，然后在一扇栅门前平平的草地上躺下休息。光在高高的白色小云团的边上搏动。她凯旋地一跃而起。大地随她的心跳在她脚下搏动…天空稳住，站得更远了，清明安详的蓝，一簇簇又亮又软的云缓缓漂过。她对着蓝与白那炫目生长的远方合上眼，觉出地平线以一道坚稳的弧线，围着她的绿色摇篮折下来。

===Original===

Within her eyelids fields swung past green, cornfields gold and black, fields with coned clumps of harvested corn, dusty gold, and black, on either side of the bone-white grass trimmed road. The road ran on and on lined by low hedges and the strange everlasting back-flowing fields. Thrilling hedges and outstretched fields of distant light, coming on mile after mile, winding off, left behind﻿ ﻿… “it’s the Bath Road I shall be riding on; I’m going down to Chiswick to see which way the wind is on the Bath Road.﻿ ﻿…” Trees appeared, golden and green and shadowy with warm cool strong shaded trunks coming nearer and larger. They swept by, their shadowy heads sweeping the lower sky. Poplars shot up drawing her eyes to run up their feathered slimness and sweep to the top of the pointed plumes piercing the sky. Trees clumped in masses round houses leading to villages that shut her into little corridors of hard hot light﻿ ﻿… the little bright sienna form of the hen she had nearly run over; the land stretching serenely out again, rolling along, rolling along in the hot sunshine with the morning and evening freshness at either end﻿ ﻿… sweeping it slowly in and out of the deeps of the country night﻿ ﻿… eyelids were transparent. It was *light* coming through one’s eyelids that made that clear soft buff; soft buff light filtering through one’s body﻿ ﻿… little sounds, insects creeping and humming in the hedge, sounds from the grass. Sudden single quiet sounds going up from distant fields and farms, lost in the sky.
---
I’ve got my sea-legs﻿ ﻿… this is *riding*﻿—not just straining along trying to forget the wobbly bicycle, but feeling it wobble and being able to control it﻿ ﻿… being able to look about easily﻿ ﻿… there will be a harvest moon this month, rolling up huge and hot, suddenly over the edge of a field; the last moon. I shall see that anyhow whatever the holiday is like. It will be cold again in the winter. Perhaps I shan’t feel so cold this winter.
---
She recognised the figure the instant she saw it. It was as if she had been riding the whole day to meet it. Completely forgotten it had been all the time at the edge of the zest of her ride. It had been everywhere all the time and there it was at last dim and distant and unmistakable﻿ ﻿… coming horribly along, a murk in the long empty road. She slowed up looking furtively about. The road had been empty for so long.

===Chinese===

眼脸里面，田野一片片荡过去，绿的，麦田金黑相间，田野上一丛丛收割下的庄稼堆成锥，尘金和黑，分列在骨头白、草修剪的路两旁。路延伸又延伸，两行低矮的树篱，还有那永远向后倒流的、古怪的田野。一阵阵战栗的树篱和远光的、张开的田野，一英里又一英里地迎上来，蜿蜒开去，落在身后…“这是巴斯大道，我就要走在这条道上了；我要下奇西克去看看巴斯大道上风向哪边。”…树出现了，金的绿的，荫出温凉强健的树干，近来、变大。它们掠过去，荫影的树冠扫着低空。杨树窜上去，引着她的眼睛顺着它们羽毛似的细瘦一路跑到顶尖那束刺破天空的尖羽。树一团团簇拥在宅子四周，通往一个个村子，把她关进一条条又硬又热的光的窄廊…那只差一点被她轧着的母鸡，亮亮的一小团赭色；大地又安详地铺展开去，滚滚向前，滚滚向前，在热的阳光里，两端各缀着清晨和黄昏的新鲜…把它缓缓地扫进、扫出乡野之夜的深处…眼脸是透明的。正是透过眼脸的光，造出那种又清又软的浅黄；软黄的光滤过人的身体…细碎的声响，昆虫在树篱里爬、在哼，草里的声响。一声一声孤零零的轻响从远处的田野和农舍升上去，没进天里。
---
我有了我的航海腿…这才叫骑——不只是拼命往前挣、想法忘掉那摇晃的车，而是觉出它摇、又能驾驭它…能轻轻松松四下看了…这个月会有一轮收获月的，滚起来又大又热，突然从一片田野的边上冒出来；最后一轮月了。不管这个假期怎样，那轮月我横竖看得到。冬天又要冷了。兴许这个冬天我不会觉得那样冷。
---
那身影她一眼就认了出来。仿佛她这一整天的骑行，就是为了去会它。它被忘得干干净净，却始终待在她这趟车兴头的边上。它一直无处不在，而此刻它终于在那里了，昏暗、遥远、错不了…一路可怕地走过来，是空荡荡的长路上一团暗影。她放慢，偷偷四顾。这条路已经空了这样久。

===Original===

It stretched invisibly away behind, empty. There was no sound of anything coming along; nothing but the squeak squeak of her gear-case; bitter empty fields on either side, greying away to the twilight, the hedges sharp and dark, enemies; nothing ahead but the bare road, carrying the murky figure; there all the time; and bound to come. She rode on at her usual pace struggling for an absorption so complete as to make her invisible, but was held back by her hatred of herself for having wondered whether he had seen her. The figure was growing more distinct. Murky. Murk from head to foot. Wearing openly like a coat the expression that could be seen hidden inside everybody. She had made an enemy of him. It was too late. The voice in her declaring sympathy, claiming kinship faded faint and far away within her﻿ ﻿… hullo old boy, isn’t it a bloody world﻿ ﻿… he would know it had come too late. He came walking along, slowly walking like someone in a procession or a quickly moving funeral; like someone in a procession, who must go on. He was surrounded by people, pressed in and down by them, wanting to kill everyone with a look and run, madly, to root up trees and tear down the landscape and get outside﻿ ﻿… he is myself.﻿ ﻿… He stood still. Her staring eyes made him so clear that she saw his arrested face just before he threw out an arm and came on, stumbling. Measuring the width of the roadway she rode on slowly along the middle of it, pressing steadily and thoughtlessly forward, her eyes fixed on the far-off spaces of the world she used to know, towards a barrier of swirling twilight. He was quite near, slouching and thinking and silently talking, on and on. He was all right poor thing. She put forth all her strength and shot past him in a sharp curve, her eye just seeing that he turned and stood, swaying.
What a blessing he was drunk what a blessing he was drunk she chattered busily, trying to ignore her trembling limbs. Again and again as she steadied and rode sturdily and blissfully on came the picture of herself saying with confidential eagerness as she dismounted “I *say*﻿—make haste﻿—there’s a madman coming down the road﻿—get behind the hedge till he’s gone﻿—I’m going for the police.” A man would not have been afraid. Then men *are* more independent than women. Women can never go very far from the protection of men﻿—because they are physically inferior.

===Chinese===

它在她身后无形地伸出去，空空的。没有什么过来的声音；只有她的链罩吱呀吱呀；两侧是苦味的空田野，灰下去，退进暮色，树篱又利又黑，是敌人；前头只有光秃秃的路，载着那团暗影；一直在那里；并且必定要来。她照常的速度骑下去，挣扎着求一种足以使她隐形的专注，却被自己卡住——她恨自己，恨刚才那一下纳罕他看见她没有。那身影越来越清楚了。暗。从头到脚的暗。把那种在人人心里都藏得着的表情，像一件外衣一样公开穿在身上。她把他变成了敌人。太迟了。她心里那个宣告同情、认亲的声音，淡下去，远远地退到她里面去了…喂老伙计，这世界是不是血腥透了…他会知道，一切已经太迟。他一路走来，慢慢地走，像行列里的人，像一场快步的葬礼；像行列里的人，必须走下去。他被人群围着，被他们挤着压着，想用一眼杀死所有人，然后狂奔，去拔起树木、撕碎风景、逃到外面去…他就是我。…他站住了。她瞪着的眼睛把他照得那样清楚，她看见他那张定住的脸，就在他猛地伸出一条臂、踉跄着逼上来的前一刻。她目测路面的宽度，沿着路中央慢慢骑，稳稳地、不假思索地朝前压，眼睛钉在远处那片她从前认识的世界的空阔上，朝着一道旋卷的暮色的屏障。他离得很近了，拖着脚，想着，无声地说着，一路又一路。他没有事的，可怜的东西。她使出全身的力气，一记急弯从他身边射过，眼角刚看见他转过身、晃了晃地站着。

多亏他喝醉了多亏他喝醉了，她忙忙地絮叨着，想不理会自己发抖的四肢。一回又一回，随着她稳住、雄赳赳地、幸福地骑下去，那幅画面浮上来：她一面下车一面急切而知心地喊“我说——快——路上来了个疯子——先躲到树篱后头等他过去——我去叫警察。”男人就不会怕。那么男人确实比女人独立。女人永远离不开男人的保护——因为体力上弱。

===Original===

But men are afraid of mad bulls.﻿ ﻿… They have to resort to tricks. What was that I was just thinking? Something I ought to remember. Women have to be protected. But men explain it the wrong way. It was the same thing.﻿ ﻿… The polite protective man was the same; if he relied on his strength. The world is the most sickening hash.﻿ ﻿… I’m so sorry for you.
