I hate humanity too. *Isn’t* it a lovely day? *Isn’t* it? Just look.
---
The dim road led on into the darkness of what appeared to be a private estate. The light from the lamp fell upon wide gates fastened back. The road glimmered on ahead with dense darkness on either side. There had been no turning. The road evidently passed through the estate. She rode on and on between the two darknesses, her light casting a wobbling radiance along her path. Rustling sounded close at hand, and quick thuddings startled her making her heart leap. The hooting of an owl echoed through the hollows amongst the trees. Stronger than fear was the comfort of the dense darkness. Her own darkness by right of riding through the day. Leaning upon the velvety blackness she pushed on, her eyes upon the little circle of light, steady on the centre of the pathway, wobbling upon the feet of the trees emerging in slow procession on either side of the way.
---
The road began to slope gently downwards. Wearily back-pedalling she crept down the incline her hand on the brake, her eyes straining forward. Hard points of gold light﻿—of course. She had put them there herself. Marlborough﻿ ﻿… the prim polite lights of Marlborough; little gliding moving lights, welcoming, coming safely up as she descended. They disappeared. There must have been a gap in the trees. Presently she would be down among them.
---
“*Goode* Lord﻿—it’s a woman.”
She passed through the open gate into the glimmer of a descending road. Yes. Why not? Why that amazed stupefaction? Trying to rob her of the darkness and the wonderful coming out into the light. The man’s voice went on with her down the dull safe road. A young lady, taking a bicycle ride in a daylit suburb. That was what she was. That was all he would allow. It’s something in men.
---
“You don’t think of riding up over the downs at this time of night?” It was like an At home. Everybody in the shop was in it, but she was not in it. Marlborough thoughts rattling in all the heads; with Sunday coming.

===Chinese===

可男人怕疯牛。…他们只好使花招。我刚才在想什么来着？一件该记住的事。女人需要保护。可男人把这个解释错了。是同一回事。…那个客气而护花的男人也是一样；倘若他仗的是他的力气。这个世界是一锅最叫人作咽的杂烩。…我真替你难过。我也恨人类。今天多好啊，是不是？是不是？只管看。
---
昏暗的路通进一片像私家地产的黑里。车灯的光照在敞开固定的两扇大门上。路在前头微微发亮，两侧是浓密的黑暗。一直没有岔路。这条路显然要穿过这片地产。她在两团黑暗之间骑了又骑，她的灯在她的小径上投下一道摇晃的光。近旁有窸窣声，急促的奔踏声惊得她心一跳又一跳。一声鸮鸣在树间的空谷里回荡。比恐惧更强的，是这浓黑的安慰。她自己挣来的黑，凭她骑过了这一整天的白昼。她倚着这天鹅绒般的黑往前推，眼睛盯着那一小圈光，稳稳地钉在路的中央，摇摇晃晃地落在那两行缓缓列队现出身来的树的脚上。
---
路开始缓缓向下。她倒着踏，倦倦地漏下坡，手搭着刹车，眼睛竭力前望。坚硬的金色光点——当然了。是她自己把它们放在那儿的。马尔伯勒…马尔伯勒那些端方的、客气的灯；小小的、滑行的、移动的灯，迎候着，随着她下降安然升上来。它们不见了。树间准有个缺口。不多时她就要下到它们中间了。
---
“老天爷——是个女的。”

她穿过敞开的园门，驶入下坡路的微光。是啊。为什么不可以？何必那样惊得发懵？想抢走她的黑暗、和她那奇妙的走进光里来。那男人的嗓音陪着她沿这条呆板安全的路走下去。一位年轻小姐，在白天的郊外骑车兜兜。她就是这样一个人。他容许的全部。男人身上就是有这个东西。
---
“你不会想在这个时辰骑车翻过丘原吧？”像一场家庭招待会。铺子里人人都在其中，只有她不在其中。马尔伯勒的念头在所有的头脑里咔嗒作响；星期天就要来了。

===Original===

They had sick and dying relations. But it was all in Marlborough. Marlborough was all round them all the time, the daily look of it, the morning coming each day excitingly, all the people seeing each other again and the day going on. They did not know that that was it; or what it was they liked. Talking and thinking with the secret hidden all the time even from themselves. But it was that that made them talk and make such a to do about everything. They had to hide it because if they knew they would *feel* fat and complacent and wicked. They were fat and complacent because they did not know it.
“Oh yes I do,” said Miriam in feeble husky tones.
She stood squarely in front of the grating. The people became angry gliding forms; cheated; angry in an eternal resentful silence; pretending. The man began thoughtfully ticking off the words.
“How far have you come,” he said suddenly pausing and looking up through the grating.
“From London.”
“Then you’ve just come down through the Forest.”
“Is that a forest?”
“You must have come through Savernake.”
“I didn’t know it was a forest.”
“Well I don’t advise you to go on up over the downs at this time of night.”
If only she had not come in she could have gone on without knowing it was “the downs.”
“My front tyre is punctured,” she said conversationally, leaning a little against the counter.
The man’s face tightened. “There’s Mr. Drake next door would mend that for you in the morning.”
“Next door. Oh, thank you.” Pushing her sixpence under the rail she went down the shop to the door seeing nothing but the brown dusty floor leading out to the helpless night.
Why did he keep making such impossible suggestions? The tyre was absolutely flat. How much would a hotel cost? How did you stay in hotels﻿ ﻿… hotels﻿ ﻿… her hands went busily to her wallet. She drew out the repair outfit and Mr. Leyton’s voice sounded, emphatic and argumentative “You know where you are and they don’t rook you.” There was certain to be one in a big town like this. She swished back into the shop and interrupted the man with her eager singing question.
“Yes,” came the answer, “there’s a quiet place of that sort up the road, right up against the Forest.”
“Has my telegram gone? Can I alter it?”
“No, it’s not gone, you’re just in time.”
It was the loveliest thing that could have happened. The day was complete, from morning to night.

===Chinese===

他们有生病垂危的亲戚。可那全在马尔伯勒。马尔伯勒时时刻刻围着他们，它每天的模样，每个早晨兴冲冲地到来，人们彼此再见，日子继续下去。他们不知道那才是它；也不知道自己喜欢的是什么。谈着、想着，那秘密却一直藏着，连对他们自己也藏着。可正是那个，叫他们那样谈话、对什么都那样大惊小怪。他们不得不藏，因为他们一旦知道，就会觉得自己肥胖、自满、罪恶。他们肥胖而自满，正因为他们不知道。

“哦，我要想的，”米丽亚姆用微弱的沙嗓说。

她端端正正站在格栅前。人们变成一个个发怒的、滑行的形影；受了骗；在一种永恒的怨愤的沉默里发怒；装着。那男人开始若有所思地把词一个个数过去。

“你从哪儿来，”他突然停住，隔着格栅抬眼说。

“从伦敦（London）。”

“那你是刚穿过那片林子下来的。”

“那是片林子？”

“你准是穿萨弗纳克过来的。”

“我不知道那是片林子。”

“呢，我可不建议你在这个时辰再上丘原去。”

早知道不进来，她本可以一直走下去，不必知道那是“丘原”。

“我的前胎破了，”她搭话似的说，微微倚着柜台。

那男人的脸紧了紧。“隔壁的德雷克先生明早可以给你补。”

“隔壁。哦，谢谢。”她把六便士从栏杆下推进去，顺着铺子走向门口，眼里只有那条通向无助的黑夜的、棕色的积尘的地板。

他为什么老是出这些办不到的主意？胎瘪得死死的。住旅馆要多少钱？你怎么住旅馆…旅馆…她的手忙忙摸向皮夹。她抽出修补工具，莱顿先生的声音响起来，斩钉截铁、争辩似的：“你知道自己在哪儿，人家也宰不了你。”这样的大镇子肯定有一家。她嗖地转回铺子里，用她热切的、唱歌似的问话打断了那男人。

“有，”回答来了，“路那头有一家那类的清净地方，紧挨着林子。”

“我的电报发了没有？能改吗？”

“没发，还没发，你来得正巧。”

再没有比这更好的了。这一天圆满了，从早到晚。

===Original===

---
Someone brought in the meal and clattered it quietly down, going away and shutting the door without a word. A door opened and the sound of departing footsteps ceased. She was shut in with the meal and the lamp in the little crowded world. The musty silence was so complete that the window hidden behind the buff and white blinds and curtains must be shut. The silence throbbed. The throbbing of her heart shook the room. Something was telling the room that she was the happiest thing in existence. She stood up, the beloved little room moving as she moved, and gathered her hands gently against her breast, to﻿ ﻿… get through, through into the soul of the musty little room.﻿ ﻿… “Oh.﻿ ﻿…” She felt herself beating from head to foot with a radiance, but her body within it was weak and heavy with fever. The little scene rocked, crowding furniture, antimacassars, ornaments, wool mats. She looked from thing to thing with a beaming, feverish, frozen smile. Her eyes blinked wearily at the hot crimson flush of the mat under the lamp. She sank back again her heavy light limbs glowing with fever. “By Jove, I’m tired.﻿ ﻿… I’ve had nothing since breakfast m﻿—but a m-bath bun and an acidulatudd *drop*.”﻿ ﻿… She laughed and sat whistling softly﻿ ﻿… Jehoshophat﻿—Manchester﻿—Mesopotamia﻿—beloved﻿—you sweet sweet thing﻿—Veilchen, unter Gras versteckt﻿—out of it all﻿—here I am. I shall always stay in hotels.﻿ ﻿… Glancing towards the food spread out on a white cloth near the globed lamp she saw beyond the table a little stack of books. Ham and tea and bread and butter.﻿ ﻿… Leaning unsteadily across the table﻿ ﻿… battered and ribbed green binding and then a short moral story or natural history﻿—blue, large and fat, a “storybook” of some kind﻿ ﻿… she drew out one of the undermost volumes.﻿ ﻿… *Robert Elsmere*! Here, after all these years in this little outlandish place. She poured out some tea and hurriedly slid a slice of ham between two pieces of bread and butter and sat back with the food drawn near, the lamplight glaring into her eyes, the printed page in exciting shadow. Everything in the room was distinct and sharp﻿—morning strength descended upon her.
---
How he must have liked and admired. It must have amazed him; a woman setting forth and putting straight the muddles of his own mind. “Powerful,” he probably said.

===Chinese===

---
有人把饭菜端进来，轻轻哐啷一声放下，一言不发地走了，带上门。一扇门开过，离去的脚步声远了。她同饭菜和灯一起，关在这小小的、塞满的世界里。霉味的寂静那样彻底，藏在浅黄和白色的窗帘、布幔后面的窗子一定关着。寂静搏动着。她心的搏动摇着这房间。有什么在告诉这房间，她是存在的一切中最幸福的东西。她站起来，那可爱的小房间随她一动而动，她把双手轻轻拢在胸前，好…穿过去，穿进这霉味小屋的灵魂里。…“哦。…”她觉出自己从头到脚被一道光辉拍打着，可光辉里的身体又弱又沉，带着烧热。小小的场景摇着，挤挤的家具、椅背搭巾、摆设、毛线垫。她带着发亮的、发烧的、冻住的笑，从一件东西看到一件东西。她的眼睛倦倦地眨着，避开灯下垫子那团热的绯红。她重新沉下去，沉重又轻飘的四肢发着烧。“老天，我累了。…早饭以来我什么都没吃，m——不过一个 m-牛奶小圆面包和一颗酸酸甜甜的糖。”…她笑了，坐着轻轻吹起口哨…约沙法——曼彻斯特——美索不达米亚——亲爱的——你甜甜的小东西——紫罗兰，藏在青草底下（Veilchen, unter Gras versteckt）——从那一切里出来——我在这儿。我要永远住旅馆。…朝球形灯旁白布上摊开的食物瞥去，她看见桌子那边一小摞书。火腿、茶、黄油面包。…摇摇晃晃地隔着桌子俯过去…磨破的、起棱的绿封皮，随后是一篇短的道德故事或博物志——蓝的，又大又厚，某种“故事书”…她从最底下几本里抽出一本。…《罗伯特·埃尔斯米尔》（Robert Elsmere）！在这里，在这些年之后，在这个小小的偏乡。她斟了些茶，匆勿夹了一片火腿在两片黄油面包中间，重新靠好，把食物拉近，灯光晃进她的眼睛，印着的书页在撩人的暗影里。房间里每一样东西都清楚而锐利——清晨的力量降到了她身上。
---
他该多么喜爱、多么叹服。那一定惊着了他；一个女人动身出发，把他自己头脑里的乱麻理直。“有力量”，他多半这样说。

===Original===

It was a half jealous keeping to himself of a fine, good thing. If he could have known that it would have been, just at that very moment, the answer to my worry about Christ he would have been jealous and angry quite as much as surprised and pleased and sympathetic﻿ ﻿… he was afraid *himself* of the idea that anyone can give up the idea of the divinity of Christ and still remain religious and good. He ought to have let me read it.﻿ ﻿… If he could have stated it himself as well, that day by the gate he would have done so﻿ ﻿… “a very reasonable dilemma my dear.” He knew I was thinking about things. But he had not read Robert Elsmere then. He was jealous of a thunderbolt flung by a woman.﻿ ﻿…
---
And now I’ve got beyond *Robert Elsmere*.﻿ ﻿… That’s Mrs. Humphry Ward and Robert Elsmere; that’s gone. There’s no answering science. One must choose. Either science or religion. They can’t both be true. This is the same as Literature and Dogma.﻿ ﻿… Only in Literature and Dogma there is that thing that is perfectly true﻿—that thing﻿—what is it? What was that idea in Literature and Dogma?
---
I wonder if I’ve strained my heart. This funny feeling of sinking through the bed. Never mind. I’ve done the ride. I’m alive and alone in a strange place. Everything’s alive all round me in a new way. Nearer. As the flame of the candle had swelled and gone out under her blowing she had noticed the bareness of everything in the room﻿—a room for chance travellers, nothing that anyone could carry away. She could still see it as it was when she moved and blew out the candle, a whole room swaying sideways into darkness. The more she relinquished the idea of harm and danger, the nearer and more intimate the room became.﻿ ﻿… No one can prevent my being alone in a strange place, near to things and loving them. It’s more than worth half killing yourself. It makes you ready to die. I’m not going to die, even if I have strained my heart. “Damaged myself for life.” I am going to sleep. The dawn will come, no one knowing where I am. Because I have no money I must go on and stay with these people. But I have been alive here. There’s hardly any time. I *must* go to sleep.

===Chinese===

那是一种带着一半妒意的、把一件美好的好东西攬在自己手里的行为。倘若他能知道，恰恰就在那一刻，它正是我关于基督之忧的答案，他会又妒又恼，不亚于又惊又喜又同情…他自己也怕那个念头——人可以放弃基督神性的观念而仍然虔诚、仍然为善。他本该让我读的。…倘若那天在栅门边他自己也能把它说得这样清楚，他就说了…“一个十分合乎情理的两难，亲爱的。”他知道我在思考种种事情。可他那时还没读过《罗伯特·埃尔斯米尔》。他妒忌一道由一个女人掷出的雷霆。…
---
而如今我已经走出《罗伯特·埃尔斯米尔》了。…那是汉弗莱·沃德太太和《罗伯特·埃尔斯米尔》；那已经过去了。科学无从应答。人必须选择。要么科学，要么宗教。两者不可能同真。这同《文学与教义》是一回事。…只是《文学与教义》里有那样东西是全然真的——那样东西——是什么来着？《文学与教义》里那个观念是什么来着？
---
不知我是不是把心脏累坏了。这种往床底下沉的怪感觉。不要紧。这一趟骑下来了。我活着，独自在一个陌生的地方。我四周的一切以一种新的方式活着。更近了。烛火随着她一吹胀起来、熄掉时，她注意到了房间里一切的空落——一间给萍水相逢的旅人的屋子，没有一件谁拿得走的东西。她还能看见自己一动、吹熄蜡烛时它的样子，一整间屋子侧着晃进黑暗。她愈放下受害与危险的念头，房间就变得愈近、愈亲。…谁也拦不住我独自待在一个陌生的地方，亲近着万物、爱着万物。这抵得过半条命。它叫人准备好了去死。我不会死的，哪怕真累坏了心脏。“为这一生伤了自己。”我要睡了。天会亮，没有人知道我在哪儿。因为没有钱，我只好走下去，住到这些人家里。可我在这里活过了。几乎没有时间了。我必须睡了。

## XXVII / 二十七
===Original===

Being really happy or really miserable makes people like you and like being with you. They need not know the cause. Someone will speak now, in a moment.﻿ ﻿… Miriam tried to return to the falling rain, the soft light in it, the soft light on the greenery, the intense green glow everywhere﻿ ﻿… misty green glow. But her eyes fell and her thoughts went on. They would have seen. Her face must be speaking of their niceness in coming out on the dull day so that she might drive about once more in Lord Lansdowne’s estate. Someone will speak. Perhaps they had not found forgetfulness in the green through the rain under the grey. Moments came suddenly in the lanes between the hedges, like that moment that always came where the lane ran up and turned and the fields spread out in the distance. But usually you could not forget the chaise and the donkey and the people. In here amongst the green something always came at once and stayed. Perhaps they did not find it so, or did not know they found it, because of their thoughts about the “fine estate.” They seemed quite easy driving in the lanes, as easy as they ever seemed when one could not forget them. What were they doing when one forgot them? They knew one liked some things better than others; or suddenly liked everything very much indeed﻿ ﻿… she said you were apathetic﻿ ﻿… what does that mean﻿ ﻿… what did she mean﻿ ﻿… with her one could see nothing and sat waiting﻿ ﻿… I said I don’t think so, I don’t think she is apathetic at all. Then they understood when one sat in a heap.﻿ ﻿… They had been pleased this morning because of one’s misery at going away. They did not know of the wild happiness in the garden before breakfast nor that the garden had been so lovely because the strain of the visit was over, and London was coming. They did not know that the happiness of being in amongst the greenery today, pouring out one’s heart in farewell to the great trees had grown so intense because the feeling of London and freedom was there. They could not see the long rich winter, the lectures and books, out of which something was coming.﻿ ﻿…
“It’s a pity the rain came.”
Ah no, that is not rain. It is not raining. What is “raining”? What do people *think* when they say these things?
“We are like daisies, *drenched* in dew.” She pursed up her face towards the sky.
They laughed and silence came again. Heavy and happy.

===Chinese===

真正快乐或者真正痛苦，会让人喜欢你、喜欢和你待在一起。他们不必知道缘故。马上就要有人开口了，就在这一刻。……米丽亚姆想回到那正落着的雨里，回到雨中柔和的光，照在绿意上的柔和的光，还有那四处浓得发亮的绿……雾蒙蒙的绿光。可她的眼睛垂了下去，思绪继续往前走。他们会看出来的。她的脸一定正在说着他们的好——这样阴沉的天还肯出来，好让她再坐车把兰斯当勋爵的庄园兜上一圈。要有人说话了。也许他们并没有在灰色天幕下、透过雨水、在绿意里找到忘我。有些时刻会在树篱间的小巷里突然降临，就像那条巷子爬上坡、一拐弯、田野在远处铺展开来的地方总会降临的那种时刻。可通常你忘不掉那轻便马车、那头驴子和那些人。在这绿意之中，总有什么东西立刻就来了，并且留驻。也许他们并没有这样的体验，或者不知道自己体验到了，因为他们心里想的是那“气派的庄园”。他们行在小巷里显得相当自在，就像人们忘不掉他们时他们看上去那样自在。人们忘掉他们的时候，他们在做些什么呢？他们知道人喜欢某些东西胜过另一些；或者突然间当真喜欢上了一切……她说你麻木冷淡……那是什么意思……她当时是什么意思……和她在一起什么也看不见，只能坐着干等……我说我不这么想，我一点也不觉得她麻木冷淡。于是他们明白了，当一个人缩成一团坐着的时候。……今天早上他们很高兴，因为人要走了、一脸难过。他们不知道早饭前花园里那阵狂喜，也不知道那花园之所以那么可爱，是因为做客的紧绷已经过去，伦敦（London）就要来了。他们不知道今天置身绿意之中、向那些大树倾吐临别心曲的快乐之所以浓烈到那个地步，是因为伦敦和自由的感觉就在那里。他们看不见那漫长而丰饶的冬天，那些讲座和书，有什么东西正从中孕育出来。……
“可惜下雨了。”
啊不，那不是雨。没有在下雨。“下雨”是什么？人们说这些话的时候，脑子里*想*的是什么？
“我们像雏菊，*浸*在露水里。”她朝天空噘起了脸。
他们笑了，随后又是沉默。沉甸甸的、快活的。

===Original===

---
“I’m glad you came up. I want to ask you what is to be done about Hendie.”
Miriam looked about the boudoir. Mrs. Green had hardly looked at her. She was smiling at her fancy work. But if one did not say something soon she would speak again, going on into things from her point of view. Doctor and medicine. Eve liked it all. She *liked* Mrs. Green’s clever difficult fancy work and the boudoir smell of Turkish beans and the house and garden and the bazaars and village entertainments and the children’s endless expensive clothes and the excitements and troubles about that fat man. Down here she was in a curious flush of excitement all the time herself.﻿ ﻿…
“I think she wants a rest.”
“I told her so. But resting seems to make her worse. We all thought she was worse after the holidays.”
Miriam’s eyes fell before the sudden glance of Mrs. Green’s blue green eye. She must have seen her private vision of life in the great rich house﻿ ﻿… misery, death with no escape. But they had Eve. Eve did not know what was killing her. She liked being tied to people.
“She is very nervous.”
“Yes. I know it’s only nerves. I’ve told her that.”
“But you don’t know what nerves are. They’re not just nothing.﻿ ﻿…”
“*You’re* not nervous.”
“Don’t you think so?”
“Not in the way Hendie is. You’re a solid little person.”
Miriam laughed and thought of Germany and Newlands and Banbury Park. But this house would be a thousand times worse. There was no one in it who knew anything about anything. That was why when she was not too bad Eve thought it was good for her to be there.
“I think she’s very happy here.”
“I’m glad you think that. But something must be done. She can’t go on with these perpetual headaches and sleeplessness and attacks of weepiness.”
“I think she wants a long rest.”
“What does she do with her holidays? Doesn’t she rest then?”
“Yes, but there are always *worries*,” said Miriam desperately.
“You have had a good deal of worry﻿—how is your father?”
How much do you know about that.﻿ ﻿… How does it strike you.﻿ ﻿…
“He is all right, I think.”
“He lives with your eldest sister.”
“Yes.”
“That’s very nice for him. I expect the little grandson will be a great interest.”
“Yes.”
“And your youngest sister has a little girl?”
“Yes.”
“Do you like children?”
“Yes.”
“I expect you spend a good deal of your time with your sisters.”
“Well﻿—it’s a fearful distance.”

===Chinese===

---
“你上楼来我很高兴。我想问问你，亨迪该怎么办。”
米丽亚姆环顾这间闺房。格林太太几乎没有看她。她正对着自己的绣活微笑。可要是人不及早说点什么，她就要又开口了，顺着她的见解把事情一路说下去。请医生，吃药。伊芙喜欢这一切。她*喜欢*格林太太那精巧费神的绣活，闺房里土耳其咖啡豆的气味，这房子和花园，义卖会和村里的游艺会，孩子们没完没了的贵重衣裳，还有围着那个胖子转的热闹和麻烦。在这底下，她自己整个人始终处在一种奇异的亢奋之中。……
“我想她需要休息。”
“我跟她这么说过。可休息好像反倒让她更坏了。假期过后我们都觉得她坏了些。”
米丽亚姆在格林太太那只蓝绿色的眼睛蕦地扫来的一瞥前垂下了目光。她一定看出了她私下里对这种深宅大院生活的设想……苦难，无处可逃的死亡。可他们有伊芙。伊芙不知道是什么在耗她的命。她喜欢被拴在人身边。
“她神经很不好。”
“是啊。我知道那不过是神经的问题。这话我跟她说过。”
“可你不知道神经不好是什么。那不是小事。……”
“*你*就没有这毛病。”
“你不这么觉得？”
“不像亨迪那样。你是个结实的小人儿。”
米丽亚姆笑了，想起德国，想起纽兰兹，想起班伯里公园（Banbury Park）。可这座房子还要糟上一千倍。屋里没有一个人对任何事有半点见识。所以当她自己还不太坏的时候，伊芙就觉得她待在这儿是好的。
“我看她在这儿很快活。”
“你这么想我很高兴。可总得想点办法。她不能老这样头痛、失眠、一阵阵地哭。”
“我想她需要长期休息。”
“她假期都怎么过的？那时候不是休息吗？”
“是休息，可总有*烦心的事*。”米丽亚姆绝望地说。
“你也操了不少心——你父亲怎么样？”
这些事你知道多少。……这事在你听来是怎么回事。……
“我想他还好。”
“他跟你大姐住在一起。”
“是的。”
“那对他很好。我想那个小外孙会是他的一大乐事。”
“是的。”
“你最小的妹妹有个小女孩？”
“是的。”
“你喜欢孩子吗？”
“喜欢。”
“我想你大部分时间都跟姐姐们待在一起。”
“呃——路远得吓人。”

===Original===

Why didn’t you ask me all these things when I was staying with you. There’s no time now.﻿ ﻿…
“Do you like living alone in London?”
“Well﻿—I’m fearfully *busy*.”
“I expect you are. I think it’s wonderful. But you must be awfully lonely sometimes.”
Miriam fidgeted and wondered how to go.
“Well﻿—come down and see us again. I’m glad I had this chance of talking to you about Hendie.”
“Perhaps she’ll be better in the winter. I think she’s really better in the cold weather.”
“Well﻿—we’ll hope so,” said Mrs. Green getting up. “I can’t think what’s the matter with her. There’s nothing to worry her down here.”
“No,” said Miriam emphatically in a worldly tone of departure. “Thank you so much for having me,” she said feebly as they passed through the flower-scented hall the scent of the flowers hanging delicately within the stronger odour of the large wood-fire.
“I’m glad you came. We thought it would be nice for both of you.”
“Yes it was very kind of you. I’m sure she wants a complete rest.” Away from us away from you in some new place.﻿ ﻿…
In the open light of the garden Mrs. Green’s eyes were almost invisible points. She ought to do her hair smaller. The fashionable bundle of little sausages did not suit a large head. The eyes looked more sunken and dead than Eve’s with her many headaches. But she was strong﻿—a strong hard thundercloud at breakfast. Perhaps very unhappy. But wealthy. Strong, cruel wealth, eating up lives it did not understand. How did Eve manage to read *Music and Morals* and Olive Schreiner here?

===Chinese===

我住在你们家的时候，你们怎么不问这些。现在没有时间了。……
“你喜欢一个人住在伦敦吗？”
“呃——我忙得*要命*。”
“我想也是。我觉得真了不起。可你有时候一定孤单得厉害。”
米丽亚姆坐立不安，琢磨着怎么脱身。
“呃——以后再下来看我们吧。很高兴有这个机会同你谈谈亨迪的事。”
“也许到冬天她会好些。我觉得天冷的时候她确实好一点。”
“呃——但愿如此。”格林太太说着站起身。“我真想不出她到底怎么了。在这儿没有什么可让她烦心的。”
“是啊。”米丽亚姆用一种世故的送别口吻斩钉截铁地说。“多谢你们接待我。”穿过那花香浮动的门厅时她轻声说，花香轻悬在大炉柴火更浓的气味之中。
“你来了我很高兴。我们想这对你们俩都好。”
“是，你们太好了。我确信她需要彻底休息。”离开我们，离开你们，到一个新的地方去。……
花园敞亮的天光里，格林太太的眼睛几乎成了看不见的两个点。她的头发该梳得小些。那时髦的一小卷一小卷的香肠髻衬不起这么大的脑袋。这双眼睛看上去比头痛不断的伊芙那双更凹陷、更无生气。可她身子结实——早餐桌上一团又硬又烈的雷雨云。或许非常不幸。可她有钱。强横、残忍的财富，吞噬着它不懂的一条条性命。伊芙在这里是怎么读得进《音乐与道德》和奥利芙·施赖纳（Olive Schreiner）的？

## XXVIII / 二十八
===Original===

“Miss Dear to see you Miss.”
“Is there anyone else in the waiting room?”
“No miss﻿—nobody.”
Miriam went in briskly.﻿ ﻿… “Well? How is the decayed gentlewoman?” she said briskly from the doorway. She hardly looked. She had taken in the close-fitting bonnet and chin bow and the height-giving look of the long blue uniform cloak together with the general aspect of the heavily shaded afternoon room.﻿ ﻿…
“Oh, she’s very well.”
Miss Dear had stood quite still in her place halfway down the room between the sofa and the littered waiting room table. She made a small controlled movement with her right hand as Miriam approached. Miriam paused with her hand on a “Navy League,” absorbed in the low sweet even tone. She found herself standing reverently, pulled up a few inches from the dark figure. Suddenly she was alight with the radiance of an uncontrollable smile. Her downcast eyes were fixed upon a tall slender figure in a skimpy black dress, tendrils of fine gold hair dancing in the rough wind under a cornflower blue toque, a clear living rose-flush.﻿ ﻿… Something making one delicate figure more than the open width of the afternoon, the blue afternoon sea and sky. She looked up. The shy sweet flower pink face glowed more intensely under the cap of gold hair clasped flatly down by the blue velvet rim of the bonnet. The eyes, now like Weymouth Bay, now like Julia Doyle’s, now a clear expressionless blue, were fixed on hers; the hesitating face was breaking again into watchful speech. But there was no speech in the well-remembered outlines moulding the ominous cloak. Miriam flung out to stem the voice, rushing into phrases to open the way to the hall and the front door. Miss Dear stood smiling and laughing her little smothered obsequious laugh, just as she had done at Bognor, making one feel like a man.
“Well﻿—I’m most frightfully busy,” wound up Miriam cheerfully turning to the door. “That’s London﻿—isn’t it? One never has a minute.”
Miss Dear did not move. “I came to thank you for the concert tickets,” she said in the even thoughtful voice that dispersed one’s thoughts.
“Oh yes. Was it any good?”
“I enjoyed it immensely,” said Miss Dear gravely. “So did Sister North,” she added, shaking out the words in delicate laughter.
… *I* don’t know “Sister *North*.”﻿ ﻿… “Oh, good,” said Miriam opening the door.
“It was most kind of you to send them.

===Chinese===

“迪尔小姐（Miss Dear）来访，小姐。”

“候诊室里还有别人吗？”

“没有了，小姐——没有人。”

米丽亚姆轻快地走进去。…“怎么样？落魄淑女她可好？”她从门口轻快地说。她几乎没有看。她一眼收进那顶箍得紧紧的软帽和下巴底下的蝴蝶结，蓝色制服长披风那副显高的做派，连同这下午重帘深掩的房间的大致光景。…

“哦，她好得很。”

迪尔小姐在房间半途、沙发和摊满杂物的候诊桌之间，站在原地一动不动。米丽亚姆走近时，她右手作了一个小小的、克制的手势。米丽亚姆的手搭在一册《海军联盟》上停住，被那又低又甜又匀的调子吸住了。她发觉自己肃然站着，在那黑色的身影前几英寸处收住了脚。骤然间，她因一个忍不住的微笑而亮了。她低垂的眼睛盯住一个高挑纤细的身影，一身瘦小的黑裙，几绺细金发在粗野的风里舞动，在一顶矢车菊蓝的小帽下，一片清鲜活泛的玫瑰红。…有一样东西，使这娇柔的身影胜过下午那整个的敞开的宽阔，胜过那蓝色的下午的海和天。她抬起头。那羞怯的、甜美的、花粉色的小脸，在扣伏的金发下、在帽子的蓝丝绒檐下，灼灼地更亮了。那双眼睛，一会儿像韦茅斯湾，一会儿像茱莉亚·多伊尔的，一会儿又是一片清澄的无表情的蓝，正定定地望着她的眼睛；那张欲语还休的脸又一次绽成留神的言语。可那件不祥的披风塑出的、记忆犹新的轮廓里，并没有言语。米丽亚姆抢出去拦住那嗓音，急急涌出一串话，为通向门厅和前门开路。迪尔小姐站着，微笑着，发出她那小小的、捂住的、殷勤的笑，同在博格诺时一模一样，叫人觉得自己像个男人。

“好啦——我忙得要命，”米丽亚姆快活地收尾，转身朝门。“这就是伦敦——是不是？一分钟也没有。”

迪尔小姐没有动。“我来谢谢你的音乐会票，”她用那匀匀的、深思的、叫人心思涣散的嗓音说。

“哦，是的。还好看吗？”

“我非常喜欢，”迪尔小姐庄重地说。“诺斯护士（Sister North）也是，”她补了一句，把那几个字抖出来，抖成一串精巧的笑。

……我可不知道什么“诺斯护士”。…“哦，好，”米丽亚姆说着开了门。

“多谢你把票给我们。

===Original===

I’m going to a case tomorrow, but I shall hope to see you when I come back.”
“Sister North sported a swell new blouse,” said Miss Dear in clear intimate tones as she paused in the hall to take up her umbrella.
“I hope it won’t rain,” said Miriam formally, opening the front door.
“She was no *end* of a swell,” pursued Miss Dear, hitching her cloak and skirt from her heels with a neat cuffed gloved hand, quirked compactly against her person just under her waist and turned so that her elbow and forearm made a small compact angle against her person. She spoke over her shoulder, her form slenderly poised forward to descend the steps; “I told her she would knock them.” She was aglow with the afternoon sunlight streaming down the street.
Miriam spoke as she stepped down with delicate plunges. She did not hear and paused turning on the last step.
“It was too *bad* of you,” shouted Miriam smiling “to leave my sister alone at the Decayed Gentlewomen’s.”
“I couldn’t help myself,” gleamed Miss Dear. “My time was up.”
“Did you *hate* being there?”
Miss Dear hung, poised and swaying to some inner breeze. Miriam gazed, waiting for her words, watching the inturned eyes control the sweet lips flowering for speech.
“It was rather comical”﻿—the eyes came round, clear pure blue;﻿—“until your sister came.” The tall slender figure faced the length of the street; the long thin blue cloak flickering all over gave Miriam a foresight of the coming swift hesitating conversational progress of the figure along the pavement, the poise of the delicate surmounting head, slightly bent, the pure brow foremost, shading the lowered thoughtful eyes, the clear little rounded dip of the chin indrawn.
“I’m glad she gave me your address,” finished Miss Dear a little furrow running along her brow in control of the dimpling flushed oval below it. “I’ll say au revoir and not goodbye for the present.”
“Goodbye,” flung Miriam stiffly at the departing face. Shutting the neglected door she hurried back through the hall and resumed her consciousness of Wimpole Street with angry, eager swiftness.﻿ ﻿… Eve, getting mixed up with people﻿ ﻿… it is right﻿ ﻿… *she* would not have been angry if I had asked her to be nice to somebody.﻿ ﻿… I did not mean to do anything﻿ ﻿… I was proud of having the tickets to send﻿ ﻿…

我明天要出一个诊，回来再来看你。”

“诺斯护士秀了一件挺括的新罩衫，”迪尔小姐在门厅里停步拿伞时，用清亮而亲昵的声调说。

“希望别下雨，”米丽亚姆拘拘礼礼地说，开了前门。

“她可神气得没边儿了，”迪尔小姐接着说，用一只带罗纹袖口的手套的手利落地从脚跟摆起披风和裙子，紧凑地别在腰下身侧，转过身，使她的肘和小臂贴着身子折出一个小小的利落的角度。她偏过头说，纤纤的身形前倾着待下台阶；“我跟她说，她准把他们全镇住。”她在沿街倾泻下来的下午阳光里灼灼发亮。

米丽亚姆一面踩着娇巧的一顿一顿的步子下阶，一面说话。她没听见，在末一级台阶上停步转过身。

“你真是太不像话了，”米丽亚姆笑着喊道，“把我妹妹一个人摆在落魄淑女之家。”

“我身不由己，”迪尔小姐闪着光。“我的期限到了。”

“你在那儿待得恨不恨？”

迪尔小姐悬着，凭一阵内在的微风摆着、平衡着。米丽亚姆凝望，等她的话，看着那内敛的眼睛管束着那两瓣含苞欲语的甜唇。

“本来相当滑稽”——那双眼睛转过来，清纯粹蓝；——“直到你妹妹来。”那高挑纤细的身影面朝整条街的长；那件又长又薄的蓝披风通身闪动，使米丽亚姆预见到这身影沿人行道那即将到来的、迅疾而迟疑的谈话行进，那娇巧的头颅端踞其上的平衡，微微前俯，光洁的额头打头，遮着低垂的深思的眼睛，下巴那清丽的浅窝微微内收。

“幸亏她给了我你的地址，”迪尔小姐收尾，一道细细的纹沿额头走，管束着额头下面那张起着笑涡、泛着红晕的鹅蛋脸。“这回我说再见（au revoir），不说别离。”

“再见，”米丽亚姆硬邦邦地朝那张远去的脸抛去。关上被晾了半晌的门，她匆勿穿过门厅，带着愠怒的、急切的速度重新拾起她对温波尔街的意识。…伊芙，同人撘和在一起…这没有错…倘若我求她善待什么人，她可不会恼。…我没想做什么…我是为自己有票可送而得意…

if I had not sent them I should have had the thought of all those nurses, longing for something to do between cases. 倘若不送，我心里就得老惦着那一群护士，两桩案子中间巴望着点事做。她们正是学生音乐会的主顾…她要是再来。…“可惜我没法有社交生活，”说这话时我会多恼——“你瞧我满得吓人——每晚有讲座，每个周末都不在…再说这儿不兴我见人——”

## XXIX / 二十九
===Original===

Miriam had no choice but to settle herself on the cane-seated chair. When Miss Dear had drawn the four drab coloured curtains into place the small cubicle was in semidarkness.
“I hope the next time you come to tea with me it will be under rather more comfortable circumstances.”
“This is all right,” said Miriam in abstracted impatient continuation of her abounding manner. Miss Dear was arranging herself on the bed as if for a long sitting. The small matter of business would come now. Having had tea it would be impossible to depart the moment the discussion was over. How much did the tea cost here? That basement tearoom, those excited young women and middle-aged women watchful and stealthy and ugly with poverty and shifts, those teapots and shabby trays and thick bread and butter were like the Y.W.C.A. public restaurant at the other end of the street﻿—fourpence at the outside; but Miss Dear would have to pay it. She felt trapped﻿ ﻿… “a few moments of your time to advise me” and now half the summer twilight had gone and she was pinned in this prison face to face with anything Miss Dear might choose to present; forced by the presences audible in the other cubicles to a continuation of her triumphant tearoom manner.
“You must excuse my dolly.” She arranged her skirt neatly about the ankle of the slippered bandaged foot.
Anyone else would say what is the matter with your foot.﻿ ﻿… It stuck out, a dreadfully padded mass, dark in the darkness of the dreadful little enclosure in the dreadful dark hive of women, collected together only by poverty.
“Have you *left* your association?”
“Oh *no*, de‑er; not *permanently* of course,” said Miss Dear pausing in her tweakings and adjustments of draperies to glance watchfully through the gloom.
“I’m still a member there.”
“Oh yes.”
“But I’ve got to look *after* myself. They don’t give you a chance.”
“No﻿—”
“It’s rush in and rush out and rush in and rush out.”
“What are you going to do?﻿ ﻿… what do you want with me.﻿ ﻿…”
“What do you mean de‑er.”
“Well I mean, are you going on nursing.”
“Of *course* de‑er. I was going to tell you.”
Miriam’s restive anger would not allow her to attend fully to the long story.

===Chinese===

米丽亚姆没有别的法子，只好在藤座椅上安顿下来。迪尔小姐把四幅暗色帘子拉拢归位，小隔间便半暗了。

“但愿你下回来同我喝茶，情形能舒服些。”

“这儿就挺好，”米丽亚姆说，心不在焉、又不耐烦地延续着她那兴致勃勃的做派。迪尔小姐正在床上安排自己，仿佛要作一次长谈。那桩小小的正事现在总该来了。既然吃了茶，讨论一完就走是不可能的了。这儿的茶多少钱？那间地下室茶室，那些兴奋的年轻女人和警觉、鬼祟、被贫穷和零工磨丑了的中年女人，那些茶壶、寒酸的托盘、厚厚的黄油面包，同街那头的基督教女青年会公共食堂一个样——顶多四便士；可这钱得迪尔小姐付。她觉得中了圈套…“占您几分钟，给我出出主意”，如今半个夏天的暮光过去了，她被钉在这座牢里，同迪尔小姐高兴摆出的任何东西面对面；被相邻隔间里听得见的那些人在场逼着，只得延续她茶室里那副得胜的做派。

“你多包涵我这娃娃脚。”她把裙子在那只套着便鞋、缠着绷带的脚踝周围理得整整齐齐。

换了别人早问你的脚怎么了。…它伸在那儿，一团垫得可怕的鼓包，暗在这可怕的小围拢的暗里，在这座只靠贫穷把人聚到一起的、可怕的女人的暗巢里。

“你离开你的协会了？”

“哦没有，亲-爱的；当然不是‘永远’离开，”迪尔小姐说着，停下她对面料的拉拉弄弄，隔着幽暗留神瞟一眼。

“我还在那儿挂名。”

“哦，是。”

“可我得自己照管自己。她们不给你机会。”

“是——”

“冲进来冲出去，冲进来冲出去。”

“你打算怎么办呢？…你要我做什么呢。…”

“你什么意思呀，亲-爱的。”

“呢，我是说，你还继续做护理吗。”

“当然啦，亲-爱的。我正要告诉你呢。”

米丽亚姆那坐立不安的火气不容她全神贯注地听这个长长的故事。

===Original===

She wandered off with the dreadful idea of nursing a “semi-mental” sitting in a deck-chair in a country garden, the hopeless patient, the nurse half intent on a healthy life and fees for herself, and recalled the sprinkling of uniformed figures amongst the women crowded at the table, all in this dilemma, all eagerly intent; all overworked by associations claiming part of their fees or taking the risks of private nursing, all getting older; all anyhow as long as they went on nursing bound to live on illness; to live with illness knowing that they were living on it. Yet Mr. Leyton had said that no hospital run by a religious sisterhood was any good﻿ ﻿… these women were run by doctors.﻿ ﻿…
“You see de‑er it’s the best thing any sensible nurse can do as soon as she knows a sufficient number of influenchoo peopoo﻿—physicians and others.”
“Yes, I see.”﻿ ﻿… But what has all this to do with me.﻿ ﻿…
“I shall keep in correspondence with my doctors and friends and look *after* myself a bit.”
“Yes, I see,” said Miriam eagerly. “It’s a *splendid* plan. What did you want to consult me about?”
“Well you see it’s like this. I must tell you my little difficulty. The folks at thirty-three don’t know I’m here and I don’t want to go back there just at present. I was wondering if when I leave here you’d mind my having my box sent to your lodgings. I shan’t want my reserve things down there.”
“Well﻿—there isn’t much *room* in my room.”
“It’s a flat box. I got it to go to the Colonies with a patient.”
“*Oh*, did you go?﻿ ﻿…” Nurses did see life; though they were never free to see it in their own way. Perhaps some of them﻿ ﻿… but then they would not be good nurses.
“Well I didn’t *go*. It was a chance of a lifetime. Such a de‑er old gentleman﻿—one of the Fitz-Duff family. It would have been nurse companion. He didn’t want me in uniform. My word. He gave me a complete outfit, *took* me round, coats and skirts at Peters, gloves at Penberthy’s, a *lovely* gold-mounted umbrella, everything the heart could desire. He treated me just like a daughter.” During the whole of this speech she redeemed her words by little delicate bridling movements and adjustments, her averted eyes resting in indulgent approval on the old gentleman.
“Why didn’t you go?”
“He *died*, dear.”
“Oh I see.”
“It could go under your bed, out of the way.”
“I’ve got hatboxes and things.

===Chinese===

她的心思荡开去，想着那个可怕的念头——护理一位“半精神”的病人，坐在乡间花园的帆布椅里，无望的病人，护士一半的心思在自己的健康和诊金上——又回想起围桌拥挤的女人堆里那星星点点的制服身影，全都陷在这道两难里，全都热切专注；全都被那些抽走一部分诊金、或承担私人护理风险的协会折腾得劳碌不堪，全都在见老；只要还干护理这一行，不管怎样，全都注定靠疾病过活；同疾病住在一起，明知自己靠它过活。可是莱顿先生说过，凡宗教修女会办的医院没有一家顶用…而这些女人归医生管。…

“你明白么，亲-爱的，哪个明白的护士，一认识够多的大人物（influenchoo peopoo）——内科大夫等等——就该这么办，这是最好的。”

“是，我明白。”…可这一切同我有什么相干。…

“我要同我的医生们和朋友们保持通信，自己稍微照管照管自己。”

“是，我明白，”米丽亚姆热切地说。“这是个绝妙的计划。你要找我商量什么呢？”

“呢，是这样的。我得把我的小小难处告诉你。三十三号的人们不知道我在这里，眼下我也不想回那儿去。我在想，等我离开这儿，你介不介意我把我的箱子寄到你的住处。我那些备用的东西在那下头用不着。”

“呢——我屋里没有什么空地方。”

“是只扁箱子。我为了陪一位病人去殖民地置的。”

“哦，你去过？…”护士们到底见过世面；虽然她们永远不能按自己的方式去看。也许其中几位…可那样她们就不是好护士了。

“呢，我没能去成。那是千载难逢的机会。那样一位亲-爱的老先生——菲茨-达夫家的一位。本该是护理加陪伴。他不要我穿制服。天哪。他给我置了一整套行头，领着我，外套和裙子在彼得斯，手套在彭伯西，一把金顶的、可爱极了的伞，心所要的一应俱全。他待我就像亲生女儿。”

这整段话里，她用一个个娇巧的扬首和拉弄来赎回她的话，偏过去的眼睛宠溺地、赞许地落在那老先生身上。

“你怎么没去呢？”

“他去世了，亲爱的。”

“哦，我明白了。”

“它可以塞在你床底下，不碍事。”

“我有帽盒什么的。

===Original===

My room is full of things I’m afraid.”
“P’raps your landlady would let it stand somewhere.”
“I might ask her﻿—won’t they let you leave things here?”
“They *would* I daresay,” frowned Miss Dear, “but I have special reasons. I don’t wish to be beholden to the people here.” She patted the tendrils of her hair, looking about the cubicle with cold disapproval.
“I daresay Mrs. Bailey wouldn’t mind. But I hardly like to ask her, you know. There seems to be luggage piled up everywhere.”
“Of course I should be prepared to pay a fee.”
… What a wonderful way of living﻿ ﻿… dropping a trunk full of things and going off with a portmanteau; starting life afresh in a new strange place. Miriam regarded the limber capable form outstretched on the narrow bed. This dark little enclosure, the forced companionship of the crowd of competing adventuresses, the sounds of them in the near cubicles, the perpetual sound filling the house like a sea of their busy calculations﻿ ﻿… all this was only a single passing incident﻿ ﻿… beyond it were the wide well-placed lives of wealthy patients.
“Miss Younger is a sweet woman.”
Miriam’s eyes awoke to affronted surprise.
“You know de‑er; the wan yow was sitting by at teatime. I told you just now.”
“Oh,” said Miriam guiltily.
Miss Dear dropped her voice; “she’s told me her whole story. She’s a dear sweet Christian woman. She’s working in a settlement. She’s privately engaged to the Bishop. It’s not to be published yet. She’s a sweet woman.”
Miriam rose. “I’ve got to get back, I’m afraid.”
“Don’t hurry away, dear. I hoped you would stay and have some supper.”
“I really can’t,” said Miriam wearily.
“Well, perhaps we shall meet again before Thursday. You’ll ask Mrs. Bailey about my box,” said Miss Dear getting to her feet.
“Fancy your remembering her name,” said Miriam with loud cheerfulness, fumbling with the curtains.
Miss Dear stood beaming indulgently.
All the way down the unlit stone staircase they rallied each other about the country garden with the deck chairs.
“Well,” said Miriam from the street, “I’ll let you know about Mrs. Bailey.”
“All right dear, I shall expect to hear from you; au revoir,” cried Miss Dear from the door. In the joy of her escape into the twilight Miriam waved her hand towards the indulgently smiling form and flung away, singing.

我的屋里怕是堆满了东西。”

“贝利太太多半不会介意。可你知道，我不大好开口求她。到处好像都堆着行李。”

“当然，我准备付一点寄存费。”

…多妙的一种活法…摆下一箱子东西，提一只旅行包就走；在一个陌生的新地方重新开始生活。米丽亚姆端详那伸在窄床上柔软而能干的身形。这黑暗的小围拢，这一群互相竞争的女冒险家被迫的共处，她们在近旁隔间里的声响，那像一片忙于算计的海一样灌满整座屋子的不断的声响…这一切不过是一桩过眼的插曲…在它之外，是有钱病人们那安置妥帖的宽绰生活。

“扬格小姐是个可爱的女人。”

米丽亚姆的眼睛受了冒犯似的、吃惊地睁开。

“你知道的呀，亲-爱的；喝茶时坐在你旁边的那个。我方才告诉过你。”

“哦，”米丽亚姆心虚地说。

迪尔小姐压低了嗓音；“她把她的身世全讲给我听了。她是位亲爱的、甜蜜的基督徒女子。她在一处贫民服务所做工。她同主教私订了终身。还没有公开。她是个可爱的女人。”

米丽亚姆站起身。“恐怕我得回去了。”

“别急着走呀，亲爱的。我本想留下你吃点晚饭。”

“实在不行了，”米丽亚姆倦倦地说。

“那么，兴许星期四之前我们还会见面。你会替我问贝利太太我的箱子的事，”迪尔小姐说着站起身。

“想不到你竟记得她的名字，”米丽亚姆大声地、快活地说，一面摸索着帘子。

迪尔小姐站在那儿，宠溺地笑着。

一路下那段没有灯的石楼梯，两人拿那个摆满帆布椅的乡间花园互相打趣。

“好啦，”米丽亚姆在街上说，“贝利太太的事我会给你信儿。”

“好的，亲爱的，我等你的信儿；再见（au revoir），”迪尔小姐在门口喊。逃进暮色里的欢喜中，米丽亚姆朝那个宠溺微笑着的身影挥一挥手，一面唱着，扬长而去。

## XXX / 三十
===Original===

“Regular field-day, eh Miss Hens’n? Look here﻿—” Mr. Orly turned towards the light coming in above the front door to exhibit his torn waistcoat and broken watch-chain. “Came for me like a fury. They’ve got double strength y’know when they’re under. Ever seen anything like it?”
Miriam glanced incredulously at the portly frontage.
“Fancy breaking the *chain*,” she said, sickened by the vision of small white desperately fighting hands. He gathered up the hanging strings of bright links, his powerful padded musicianly hands finding the edges of the broken links and holding them adjusted with the discoloured ravaged fingers of an artisan. “A good tug would do it,” he said kindly. “A chain’s no stronger than the weakest link,” he added with a note of dreamy sadness, drawing a sharp sigh.
“Did you get the tooth out,” clutched Miriam automatically making a mental note of the remark that flashed through the world with a sad light, a lamp brought into a hopeless sickroom﻿ ﻿… keeping up her attitude of response to show that she was accepting the apology for the extremities of rage over the getting of the anaesthetist. Mrs. Orly appearing in the hall at the moment, still flushed from the storm, joined the group and outdid Miriam’s admiring amazement, brilliant smiles of relief garlanding her gentle outcry. “Hancock busy?” said Mr. Orly in farewell as he turned and swung away to the den followed by Mrs. Orly, her unseen face busy with an interrupted errand. He would not hear that her voice was divided.﻿ ﻿… No one seemed to be aware of the divided voices﻿ ﻿… no men. Life went on and on, a great oblivious awfulness, sliding over everything. Every moment things went that could never be recovered﻿ ﻿… on and on, and it was always too late, there was always some new thing obliterating everything, something that looked new, but always turned out to be the same as everything else, grinning with its sameness in an awful blank where one tried to remember the killed things﻿ ﻿… if only everyone would stop for a moment and let the thing that was always hovering be there, let it settle and intensify. But the whole of life was a conspiracy to prevent it. Was there something wrong in it? It could not be a coincidence the way life *always* did that﻿ ﻿… she had reached the little conservatory on the half landing, darkened with a small forest of aspidistra.

===Chinese===

“又是个大忙的日子吧，呢，亨德森小姐？瞧这儿——”奥利先生转身迎着前门上方进来的光，展示他撕破的背心和拉断的表链。“像复仇女神似的朝我扑过来。你知道的，她们麻醉过去的时候力气有双份。见过这样的阵仗吗？”

米丽亚姆难以置信地扫了一眼那堂堂的正面身躯。

“想想看，竟把链子拉断了，”她说，那双拼死挣扎的小白手的幻象叫她恶心。他把亮晶晶的链环一挂挂拢起来，那双有垫的、音乐家的有力大手找出断环的边，用一双工匠的、变色磨损的手指对合着。“好好一拽就成了，”他和和气气地说。“一条链子的强度，超不过它最弱的一环，”他添了一句，带着一缕梦似的哀伤，抽了一口尖利的气。

“牙拔出来了吗，”米丽亚姆机械地追问，心里暗暗记下那句闪过世界、带着悲哀的光的话，一盏提进无望病室的灯……维持着她应答的姿态，表示她接受了他为请麻醉师那场雷霆大怒所作的道歉。奥利太太此刻出现在门厅，风暴的红晕还没退，加入这一伙，把米丽亚姆的叹服惊异又翻高一层，解脱的灿笑给她的轻呼缀成一圈花环。“汉考克忙着呢？”奥利先生临别说，转身朝小书房荡去，奥利太太跟着，她那张看不见的脸正忙着一件被打断的差事。他不会听出她的嗓音是分裂的。…好像谁也没有觉察那些分裂的嗓音…男人没有一个。生活继续、继续，一桩浩大的、浑然不觉的可怕，从一切上面滑过去。时时刻刻都有东西逝去，永远追不回…继续、继续，而永远太迟，永远有一样新东西抹掉一切，一样看着新、其实回回都是同一样的东西，在一片可怕的空白里咧着嘴笑它的一致，人在那空白里竭力回想那些被杀死的东西…但愿人人能停上一刻，让那件永远悬着的东西在那里，让它落定、变浓。可整个生活就是一场不让它落定的共谋。这里面有什么错吗？生活总是那样干，不会是巧合…她已走到半楼梯平台那间小小花房，一小片蜘蛛抱冬的暗林。

===Original===

The dull dust-laden leaves identified themselves with her life. What had become of her autumn of hard work that was to lift her out of her personal affairs and lead somewhere? Already the holiday freshness and vigour had left her; and nothing had been done. Nothing was so strong as the desire that everything would stop for a moment and allow her to remember﻿ ﻿… wearily she mounted the remaining stairs to Mr. Hancock’s room. “I think,” said a clear high confident voice from the chair and stopped. Miriam waited with painful eagerness while the patient rinsed her mouth; “that that gentleman thinks himself a good deal cleverer than he is,” she resumed sitting back in the chair.
“I am afraid I’m not as familiar with his work as I ought to be, but I can’t say I’ve been very greatly impressed as far as I have gone.”
“Don’t go any further. There’s nothing there to go for.”
Who are you speaking of? How do you know? What have *you* got that makes you think he has nothing?﻿—Miriam almost cried aloud. Could she not see, could not both of them see that the quiet sheen of the green-painted window-frame cast off their complacent speech? Did they not hear it tinkle emptily back from the twined leaves and tendrils, the flowers and butterflies painted on the window in front of them? The patient had turned briskly to the spittoon again after her little speech. She would have a remark ready when the brisk rinsing was over. There could be no peace in her presence. Even when she was gagged there would be the sense of her sending out little teasing thoughts and comments. They could never leave anything alone﻿ ﻿… oh it was *that* woman﻿ ﻿… the little gold knot at the back of the cheerful little gold head; hair that curled tightly about her head when she was a baby and that had grown long and been pinned up, as the clever daughter of that man; getting to know all he had said about women. If she believed it she must loathe her married state and her children﻿ ﻿… how *could* she let life continue through her? Perhaps it was the sense of her treachery that *gave* her that bright brisk amused manner. It was a way of carrying things off, that maddening way of speaking of everything as if life were a jest at everybody’s expense﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

那些蒙尘的暗叶同她的生活认了亲。她那指望把她从私事里拔出来、引向某个去处的苦干的秋天，后来怎样了？假期的清新和元气已经离开了她；而什么也没有做成。什么都强不过这个愿望：但愿一切停上一刻，容她记一记…她倦倦地登上余下的楼梯，进汉考克的房间。“我认为，”椅子里一个清亮、高扬、笃定的嗓音开了头，又停了。米丽亚姆怀着疼的急切等着那病人漱口；“那位先生自以为，比他实际的能耐聪明得多，”她重新靠进椅背里说。

“恐怕我对他著作的熟悉不及应有的程度，但就我读过的而言，我不能说有很深的印象。”

“别再往下读了。那儿没有什么可读的。”

你说的是谁？你怎么知道？你有什么，使你认为他一无所有？——米丽亚姆几乎喊出来。她难道看不见，他们两个难道看不见，绿漆窗框那层安静的幽光正把他们自满的言语弹开？他们难道听不见它从缠卷的叶蔓上、从他们面前窗上画的花与蝴蝶上，空空地叮回？病人一番小演说后又利落地转向痰盂。利落的漱口一完，她准有一句评语备好。她在场就没有安宁。哪怕口里塞着东西，也有她在放出一条条逗弄的念头和议论的感觉。他们永远放不过任何东西…哦，是那个女人…那颗快活的小金脑袋后头的小金髻；婴儿时紧紧卷在头上的头发，如今长了、绾起了，作为那个人的聪明女儿；把他对女人的那套说法摸了个透。她倘若信了，必定又恨自己的婚姻又恨自己的孩子…她怎么能容生命从她身上过下去？也许正是那份背叛的意识，给了她那副明快、麻利、觉得好玩的神气。那是一种把事搪塞过去的法子，那种谈论一切的叫人发疯的方式，仿佛生活是拿人人取乐的一场玩笑…

===Original===

all “clever” women seemed to have that, *never* speaking what they thought or felt, but always things that sounded like quotations from men; so that they always seemed to flatter or criticise the men they were with according as they were as clever as some man they knew, or less clever. *What* was she like when she was alone and dropped that bright *manner*.﻿ ﻿… “Have you made any New Year resolutions? I don’t make any. My friends think me godless, *I* think *them* lacking in common sense”﻿ ﻿… exactly like a man; taking up a fixed attitude﻿ ﻿… having a sort of prepared way of taking everything﻿ ﻿… like the Wilsons﻿ ﻿… anything else was “unintelligent” or “absurd”﻿ ﻿… their impatience meant something. Somehow all the other people were a reproach. If some day everyone lived in the clear light of science, “waiting for the pronouncements of science in all the affairs of life,” waiting for the pronouncements of those sensual dyspeptic men with families who thought of women as existing only to produce more men﻿ ﻿… admirably fitted by Nature’s inexorable laws for her biological role﻿ ﻿… perhaps she agreed or pretended to think it all a great lark﻿ ﻿… the last vilest flattery﻿ ﻿… she had only two children﻿ ﻿… *si la femme avait plus de sensibilité elle ne retomberait pas si facilement dans la grossesse.﻿ ﻿… La femme, c’est peu galant de le dire, est la femelle de l’homme.* The Frenchman at any rate *wanted* to say something else. But why want to be gallant﻿ ﻿… and why not say man; it is not very graceful to say it, is the male of woman. If women had been the recorders of things from the beginning it would all have been the other way round﻿ ﻿… Mary. Mary, the Jewess, write something about Mary the Jewess; the Frenchman’s Queen of Heaven.
Englishmen; the English were “the leading race.” “England and America together﻿—the Anglo-Saxon peoples﻿—could govern the destinies of the world.” *What* world?﻿ ﻿… millions and millions of childbirths﻿ ﻿… colonial women would keep it all going﻿ ﻿… and religious people﻿ ﻿… and if religion went on there would always be all the people who took the Bible literally﻿ ﻿… and if religion were not true then there was only science. Either way was equally abominable﻿ ﻿… for women.

The far end of the ward was bright sunlight﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

所有“聪明”女人好像都有那一手，从来不说自己所想所感，说的永远像引自男人的语录；于是她们仿佛永远在同行的男人是奉承或贬抑，全看她们同某个认识的男人一样聪明、还是差一点。她独自一人、卸下那副明快做派时，是什么样子。…“你订新年决心了吗？我不订。我的朋友当我没有上帝，我当他们没有常识”…活脱一个男人；摆一个定死的立场…对一切有一套备好的接受法…同威尔逊家一样…别的统统“没头脑”或“荒唐”…他们的不耐烦是有含义的。不知怎么，其余一切人都是一种谴责。倘若有一天人人活在科学的清光里，“在生活一切事务中静候科学的裁断”，静候那些有家眷的、欲重胃滞的男人们的裁断——他们视女人只存在于多生产男人…由大自然无情的法则为她那生物学的角色安排得尽善尽美…也许她同意，也许装作觉得这一切是场大乐子…最后一种最下流的奉承…她只有两个孩子…倘若女人感觉更细腻些，便不会这样轻易地一再度怀孕。女人——说这话未免不雅——是人的雌性。法国人好歹还想说点别的。可为什么要想着献殷勤…又为什么不说：男人——说这话也不大雅观——是女人的雄性。倘若从开天辟地执笔记事的是女人，一切都会倒个个儿…玛丽。犹太女人玛丽，为犹太女人玛丽写点什么吧；那法国人的天后。

英国男人们；英国人是“领导种族”。“英国和美国携手——盎格鲁-撒克逊诸民族——能够主宰世界的命运。”哪个世界？…千百万次千百万次的分娩…殖民地的女人会把它一直撑下去…还有信教的人们…而宗教若延续，就永远有字面信《圣经》的那一大群…而宗教若不真，那就只剩科学。两条路对女人同样可憎。…
---
病房远端是一片明亮的日光…

===Original===

there she was enthroned, commanding the whole length of the ward, sitting upright, her head and shoulders already conversational, her hands busy with objects on the bed towards which her welcoming head was momentarily bent; like a hostess moving chairs in a small drawing room﻿ ﻿… chrysanthemums all down the ward﻿—massed on little tables﻿ ﻿… a *parrot* sidling and bobbing along its perch, great big funny solemn French grey, fresh clean living French grey pure in the sunlight, a pure canary coloured beak﻿ ﻿… clean grey and yellow﻿ ﻿… in the sun﻿ ﻿… a curious silent noise in the stillness of the ward.
“I couldn’t hear; I wasn’t near enough.”
“Better late than never, I *said*.”
“D’you *know* I thought you’d only been here a few days and today when I looked at your letter I was simply *astounded*. You’re sitting up.”
“I should hope I am. They kept me on my back, half starving for three weeks.”
“You look very pink and well now.”
“That’s what Dr. Ashley Densley said. You ought to have seen me when I came in. You see I’m on chicken now.”
“And you feel better.”
“Well﻿—you can’t really tell how you are till you’re up.”
“When are you going to get up?”
“Tomorrow, I hope, dear. So you see you’re just in time.”
“Do you mean you are going away?”
“They turn you out as soon as you’re strong enough to stand.”
“But﻿—*how* can you get about?”
“Dr. Ashley Densley has arranged all that. I’m going to a convalescent home.”
“*Oh*, that’s very nice.”
“Poor Dr. Ashley Densley, he was dreadfully upset.”
“You’ve had some letters to cheer you up.” Miriam spoke impatiently, her eyes rooted on the pale leisurely hands mechanically adjusting some neatly arranged papers.
“*No* de‑er. My friends have all left me to look after my*self* this time but since I’ve been sitting up, I’ve been trying to get my affairs in order.”
“I thought of bringing you some flowers but there was not a single shop between here and Wimpole Street.”
“There’s generally women selling them outside. But I’m glad you didn’t; I’ve too much sympathy with the poor nurses.”
Miriam glanced fearfully about. There were so many beds with forms seated and lying upon them﻿ ﻿… but there seemed no illness or pain. Quiet eyes met hers; everything seemed serene; there was no sound but the strange silent noise of the sunlight and the flowers. Halfway down the ward stood a large threefold screen covered with dark American cloth.

===Chinese===

她在那里端坐着，君临病房的全长，坐得笔直，头和肩膀已经进入交谈，双手忙着床上的物件，那迎客的头时时朝它们俯一下；像一位在小客厅里挪椅子的女主人…菊花沿病房一路——小桌上团团簇拥…一只鹦鹉在栖木上侧着身子颠颠地走、点着头，好大一只滑稽庄重的法国灰，新鲜干净、活生生的法国灰，在阳光里纯正，一只纯金丝雀色的喙…干净的灰和黄…在太阳里…病房的静寂中一阵古怪的无声的响。

“我听不见；我离得太远。”

“迟到总比不到好，我说。”

“你可知道，我一直当你只来了几天，今天一看你的信，我简直惊呆了。你都坐起来了。”

“我还不该坐起来。他们叫我躺了三个星期，半饿着。”

“你现在脸色红润得很。”

“阿什利·登斯利医生也这么说。你该看看我进来时的样子。瞧，我现在吃鸡了。”

“你也觉得好些了。”

“呢——人不起来，真说不清自己怎么样。”

“你什么时候下床？”

“明天，我希望，亲爱的。所以你看你来得正巧。”

“你是说你要走了？”

“你一站得稳，他们就把你打发出门。”

“可是——你怎么走动呢？”

“阿什利·登斯利医生全安排好了。我要去一家疗养院。”

“哦，那很好。”

“可怜的阿什利·登斯利医生，他难过得不得了。”

“你有几封信解解闷。”米丽亚姆不耐烦地说，眼睛钉住那双苍白、从容的手，它们正机械地理着一叠理得整整齐齐的纸。

“没有，亲-爱的。这回朋友们全撇下我，叫我照管我自己，可自打坐起来，我一直在把我的事情理出头绪。”

“我本想带些花来，可从这儿到温波尔街之间一家花店也没有。”

“外头通常有女人卖的。不过你没带我倒高兴；我太心疼那些可怜的护士了。”

米丽亚姆惶惶四顾。这么多病床上坐着、躺着人影…可好像没有病痛。安静的眼睛迎着她的眼睛；一切都安详；除了阳光和花的那种古怪的无声的响，没有一点声音。病房半途立着一架三折的大屏风，罩着深色的美国油布。

===Original===

“She’s unconscious today,” said Miss Dear; “she won’t last through the night.”
“Do you mean to say there is someone *dying* there?”
“*Yes de*-er.”
“Do you mean to say they don’t put them into a separate room to *die*?”
“They can’t dear. They haven’t got the *space*,” flashed Miss Dear.
---
Death shut in with one lonely person. Brisk nurses putting up the screen. Dying eyes cut off from all but those three dark surrounding walls, with death waiting inside them. Miriam’s eyes filled with tears. There, just across the room, was the end. It had to come somewhere; just that; on any summer’s afternoon﻿ ﻿… people did things; hands placed a screen, people cleared you away.﻿ ﻿… It was a relief to realise that there were hospitals to die in; worry and torture of mind could end here. Perhaps it might be easier with people all round you than in a little room. There were hospitals to be ill in and somewhere to die neatly, however poor you were. It was a relief﻿ ﻿… “she’s always the last to get up; still snoring when everybody’s fussing and washing.” That would be me﻿ ﻿… it lit up the hostel. Miss Dear liked that time of fussing and washing in company with all the other cubicles fussing and washing. To be very poor meant getting more and more social life with no appearances to keep up, getting up each day with a holiday feeling of one more day and the surprise of seeing everybody again; and the certainty that if you died somebody would do something. Certainly it was this knowledge that gave Miss Dear her peculiar strength. She was a nurse and knew how everything was done. She knew that people, all kinds of people, were *people* and would do things. When one was quite alone one could not believe this. Besides no one *would* do anything for me. I don’t want anyone to. I should hate the face of a nurse who put a screen round my bed. I shall not die like that. I shall die in some other way, out in the sun, with﻿—yes﻿—oh yes﻿—Tah-dee, t’*dee*, t’dee﻿—t’dee.
“It must be funny for a nurse to be in a hospital.”
“It’s a little too funny sometimes dear﻿—you know too much about what you’re in for.”
“Ilikeyourredjacket. Good *Heavens*!”
“That’s nothing dear. He does that all the afternoon.”
“How can you stand it?”
“It’s Hobson’s choice, madam.”
The parrot uttered three successive squawks fuller and harsher and even more shrill than the first.

===Chinese===

“她今天不省人事了，”迪尔小姐说；“她熬不过今夜。”

“你是说那里头有个人正在死？”

“是的，亲-爱的。”

“你是说他们不让病人单独一间房去死？”

“不能啊，亲爱的。他们没有那个地方，”迪尔小姐抢白道。
---
死，同唯一的一个孤独的人关在一起。麻利的护士支起屏风。垂死的眼睛被隔断，只剩那三面围拢的暗墙，死就候在墙里。米丽亚姆的眼睛涌满泪。那边，只隔一间屋子，就是终点。它总得在某处来临；就这个样子；随便哪个夏日的下午…人们做事；几双手支起一道屏风，人们把你清走。…意识到有医院可死，是一种宽解；心灵的忧惧和折磨可以在这儿了结。四周都是人，也许比独守一小间还容易些。有医院可病，有个地方干干净净地死，无论你多穷。这是一种宽解…“她永远是最后一个起床的；人人忙忙地洗脸时她还打着呼噜。”那说的就是我…它照亮了那座寄宿所。迪尔小姐喜欢那段人人忙忙洗脸的时刻，同所有别的小隔间里忙忙洗脸的人一起。一贫如洗意味着社交生活越来越多，再无排场要撑，每天带着“又多一天”的假日感起床，带着同人人再见一面的惊奇；还有那份确凿：你死了，总会有人做点什么。迪尔小姐那份独特的力量，正是来自这份认知，一定。她是护士，知道一切是怎么办的。她知道人，各式各样的人，都是人，都会做事。彻底孤身一人的时候，人信不了这个。再说也没有谁会为我做什么。我也不要谁做。我会恨那双围着我床支屏风的护士的脸。我不会那样死。我要换一种死法，死在外面太阳底下，带着——是——哦是——嗒嘀，嗒嘀，嗒嘀——嗒嘀。

“护士住进医院，一定很滑稽吧。”

“有时滑稽得过了头，亲爱的——你太清楚自己要挨的是什么了。”

“我喜欢你的红上衣。老天爷！”

“没什么，亲爱的。他整个下午都这样。”

“你怎么受得了？”

“别无选择呀，夫人。”

鹦鹉接连发出三声尖叫，比头一声更满、更糙、更尖。

===Original===

“He’s just tuning up; he always does in the afternoon just as everybody is trying to get a little sleep.”
“But I never *heard* of such a thing! It’s monstrous, in a hospital. Why don’t you all complain.”
“ ’Sh, dear; he belongs to Matron.”
“Why doesn’t she have him in her room? Shut *up*, polly.”
“He’d be rather a roomful in a little room.”
“Well﻿—what is he *here*? It’s the wickedest thing of its kind I’ve ever heard of; some great fat healthy woman﻿ ﻿… why don’t the *doctors* stop it?”
“Perhaps they hardly notice it, dear. There’s such a bustle going on in the morning when they all come round.”
“But hang it all, she’s here to look after you, not to leave her luggage all over the ward.”
---
The ripe afternoon light﻿ ﻿… even outside a hospital﻿ ﻿… the strange indistinguishable friend, mighty welcome, unutterable happiness. Oh death, where is thy sting? Oh grave, where is thy victory? The light has no end. I know it and it knows me, no misunderstanding, no barrier. I love you﻿—people say things. But nothing that anybody says has any meaning. Nothing that anybody says has any meaning. There is something more than anything that anybody says, that comes, first, before they speak﻿ ﻿… vehicles travelling along through heaven; everybody in heaven without knowing it; the sound the vehicles made all together, sounding out through the universe﻿ ﻿… life touches your heart like dew; that is *true*﻿ ﻿… the edge of his greasy knowing selfish hair touches the light; he brushes it; there is something in him that remembers. It is in everybody; but they won’t stop. How maddening. But they know. When people die they must stop. Then they remember. Remorse may be complete; until it is complete you cannot live. When it is complete something is burned away﻿ ﻿… ou-agh, flows out of you, burning, inky, acid, flows right out﻿ ﻿… purged﻿ ﻿… though thy sins are as *scarlet* they shall be white as snow. *Then* the light is there, nothing but the light, and new memory, sweet and bright; but only when you have been killed by remorse.
---
This is what is meant by a purple twilight. Lamps alight, small round lights, each in place, shedding no radiance, white day lingering on the stone pillars of the great crescent, the park railings distinct, the trees shrouded but looming very large and permanent, the air wide and high and purple, darkness alight and warm.

===Chinese===

“他这是调嗓子；每回下午人人都想睡一会儿，他都来这一套。”

“可我从来没听说过这样的事！太不像话了，在医院里。你们怎么不全体抗议。”

“嘘，亲爱的；他是护士长的。”

“她怎么不把他摆自己房里？闭嘴，波利。”

“小小一间房，他可太占地方了。”

“唉——可他在这儿算什么？这是我这辈子听说的最缺德的一类事；哪个又肥又壮的大女人…医生们怎么不制止？”

“兴许他们压根儿注意不到，亲爱的。早晨他们全体查房，乱哄哄的。”

“可是见鬼，她在这儿是照护你们的，不是把行李摊满病房的。”
---
熟透的下午的光…哪怕在一家医院之外…那认不出的、陌生的朋友，浩大的欢迎，说不出的幸福。死啊，你的毒钩在哪里？坟墓啊，你的胜利在哪里？光没有尽头。我认识它，它也认识我，没有误解，没有屏障。我爱你——人们总说话。可人说的任何话都没有意义。人说的任何话都没有意义。有一样东西超出人所说的一切，它先来，在人开口之前…车辆一路行过天庭；人人都在天庭里而自己不知道；所有车辆一齐发出的声响，响彻宇宙…生命像露水碰一碰你的心；那是真的…他那油亮的、自得的、自私的头发的一缘碰到光；他拂一拂它；他心里有一样还记得的东西。它人人心里都有；可他们不肯停。多叫人发疯。可他们是知道的。人到死，就得停了。那时他们就记起来了。悔恨可以圆满；不到圆满，人活不成。一旦圆满，有一样东西就被烧掉了…呕——啊，从你身上流出去，烧的，墨的，酸的，一径流尽…涤净了…你的罪虽朱红，必白如雪。那时光就在那里，只剩光，和新的记忆，又甜又亮；可只有在你被悔恨杀死之后。
---
这就是紫色的黄昏的意思了。灯亮起来，一盏盏小小的圆光，各就各位，不放光辉，白昼流连在大新月形街坊的石柱上，公园的栅栏清清楚楚，树木蒙着黑却庞大而永在，空气宽、高、紫，黑暗发着光、带着暖。

===Original===

Far far away beyond the length of two endless months is Christmas. This kind of day lived forever. It stood still. The whole year, funny little distant fussy thing, stood still in this sort of day. You could take it in your hand and look at it. Nobody could touch this. People and books and all those things that men had done, in the British Museum were a crackling noise, outside.﻿ ﻿… *Les yeux gris, vont au paradis.* That was the two poplars standing one each side of the little break in the railings, shooting up; the space between them shaped by their shapes, leading somewhere. I *must* have been through there; it’s the park. I don’t remember. It isn’t. It’s waiting. One day I will go through. *Les yeux gris, vont au paradis.* Going along, along, the twilight hides your shabby clothes. They are not shabby. They are clothes you go along in, funny; jolly. Everything’s here, any bit of anything, clear in your brain; you can look at it. What a terrific thing a person is; bigger than anything. How *funny* it is to be a person. You can never not have been a person. Bouleversement. It’s a *fait bouleversant*. *Christ*-how-rummy. It’s enough. *Du, Heilige, rufe dein Kind zurück, ich habe genossen dass irdische Glück; ich habe geleibt und gelebet.*﻿ ﻿… Oh let the solid earth not fail beneath my feet, until I am quite quite sure.﻿ ﻿… Hullo, old Euston Road, beloved of my soul, my own country, my native heath. There’ll still be a glimmer on the table when I light the lamp﻿ ﻿… how shall I write it down, the *sound* the little boy made as he carefully carried the milk jug﻿ ﻿… going along, trusted, *trusted*, you could see it, you could see his mother. His legs came along, little loose feet, looking after themselves, pottering, behind him. All his body was in the hand carrying the milk jug. When he had done carrying the milk jug he would run; running along the pavement amongst people, with cool round eyes not looking at anything. Where the crowd prevented his running he would jog up and down as he walked, until he could run again, bumping solemnly up and down amongst the people; boy.
---
The turning of the key in the latch was lively with the vision of the jumping boy. The flare of the match in the unlit hall lit up eternity. The front door was open, eternity poured in and on up the stairs.

===Chinese===

远远远远，隔着两个月没个头，才是圣诞。这样的日子永远活着。它停着不动。整整一年，那个滑稽的小小的、远方的、忙忙叨叨的东西，停在这一类日子里不动了。你可以把它拿在手里看。谁也碰不得这个。人、书、男人做过的一切那些东西，在大英博物馆里，是一阵噼啪的响，在外头。…灰眼睛，上天堂（Les yeux gris, vont au paradis）。那是两棵杨树，各立在栅栏豁口的一边，直往上窜；它们中间那道空，被它们的身形塑出，通向某个地方。我一定从那儿穿过去过的；那是公园。我不记得了。不对。它在等着。总有一天我要穿过去。灰眼睛，上天堂。一路走，一路走，黄昏掩住你寒酸的衣裳。它们并不寒酸。它们是你一路走着的衣裳，滑稽；快活。一切都在这儿，随便哪一件的随便一角，在你脑子里清清楚楚；你可以看它。一个人是件多么惊人的东西；比什么都大。做一个人多滑稽啊。你永远不可能不曾是一个人。天翻地覆。这是一桩翻天覆地的事（fait bouleversant）。天——哪——真妙。够了。你，圣洁的，唤回你的孩子吧，我已享尽尘世的幸福；我爱过，也活过。…哦，让坚实的大地别在我脚下塌陷，等我完全、完全确准了再说。…你好，老尤斯顿路，我灵魂的爱，我自己的国度，我的故乡荒原。等我点起灯，桌上还会剩一线微光…我该怎么写下来呢，那小男孩小心捧着牛奶罐时发出的那个声响…一路走着，被信托着，被信托着，你看得出来，你看得见他的母亲。他的两条腿跟在后头，两只松松的小脚，自己顾自己地磨蹭着。他整个身子都在那只捧着牛奶罐的手里。捧完了，他就要跑了；在人行道上、在人堆里跑，一双凉凉的圆眼睛什么也不看。人堆挡住他跑的地方，他就边走边一颠一颠，直到能再跑，在人堆里一本正经地颠上颠下；男孩。
---
钥匙旋进锁孔的那一旋，还带着蹦跳男孩的幻影。火柴在无灯的门厅里轰地一亮，照亮了永恒。前门开着，永恒涌进来，一路涌上楼梯。

At one of those great staircase windows where the last of the twilight stood a sudden light of morning would not be surprising. Of course a letter; curly curious statements on the hall-stand.
That is mother-of-pearl, nacre; twilight nacre; crépuscule nacre; I must wait until it is gone. It is a visitor; pearly freshness pouring in; but if I wait I may feel different. With the blind up the lamp will be a lamp in it; twilight outside, the lamp on the edge of it, making the room gold, edged with twilight.
---
I *can’t* go tonight. It’s all *here*; I *must* stay here. Botheration. It’s Eve’s fault. Eve would rather go out and see that girl than stay here. Eve *likes* getting tied up with people. I *won’t* get tied up; it drives everything away. Now I’ve read the letter I must go. There’ll be afterwards when I get back. No one has any power over me. I shall be coming back. I shall always be coming back.
